What is authorization?

Every app must answer one question many times a second. May this person do this thing to this object right now? This chapter explains that question, the common ways to answer it, and where Cedar fits.

  • Explain the difference between authentication and authorization with a real example.
  • Describe a request with its four parts: principal, action, resource and context.
  • Compare ACL, RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC, and write each one as a small Cedar policy.
  • Say why policy as code helps, and where Cedar comes from and is used today.
  • Compare Cedar with OPA and Rego, OpenFGA and hand-written if-statements in a fair way.

Two questions: who are you, and what may you do?

Think about an office building. At the door, a guard checks your badge photo. Inside, your badge opens some doors but not others. These are two different checks.

At the door

"Are you really Alice?" The guard compares your face with the photo. This is authentication.

At the server room

"May Alice open this room?" The lock checks a list of rules. This is authorization.

Software works the same way. First the app learns who you are. Then it decides what you may do. Engineers often write these two words as authn and authz.

  • Authentication uses passwords, passkeys, one-time codes or a login with another company. The result is an identity, like "this is user alice".
  • Authorization takes that identity and asks about one action on one object. The result is a decision: Allow or Deny.

HTTP has a status code for each failure. 401 Unauthorized really means "we do not know who you are". 403 Forbidden means "we know you, but you may not do this". The name 401 is confusing. It is about authentication.

Many teams think a valid login token is enough. It is not. A token proves who you are. It does not prove that you may delete this invoice. Every action still needs an authorization check.

Authentication: who are you? Authorization: what may you do? Cedar only answers the second question. Another system answers the first one.

The shape of every request

Here is a real question that an app asks Cedar. Bob wants to edit the budget document. He logged in with a second factor. His computer has the address 10.1.2.3.

{
  "principal": "User::\"bob\"",
  "action": "Action::\"edit\"",
  "resource": "Doc::\"budget\"",
  "context": { "mfa": true, "ip": "10.1.2.3" }
}

Every Cedar request has these four parts. People call the shape PARC, from the first letters.

  • P: the principal. Who asks? Here, User::"bob".
  • A: the action. What do they want to do? Here, Action::"edit".
  • R: the resource. On which thing? Here, Doc::"budget".
  • C: the context. In what situation? Here, MFA is on and the IP address is known.

The authorizer needs two more inputs. Policies are the rules. Entities are the facts, for example "bob is in the editor role". With all of this, Cedar gave this real answer:

decision: Allow
reason:   rbac-editors

The answer is not only Allow. It also names the policy that decided it. Here the policy rbac-editors allowed the request. You will build this test bench in Your first Cedar policy.

The word PARC is a community name. The Cedar documentation simply lists principal, action, resource and context as the parts of a request. The idea is the same.

Four ways to say who can do what

Look at one document, the budget. Three people want to use it. We can write the rules in four classic styles. Each style answers "why is this allowed?" in a different way.

  • An ACL keeps a list on the object. "These users may read this file."
  • RBAC gives rights to roles. People get roles. "Editors may edit."
  • ABAC compares attributes. "Users may read documents of their own department."
  • ReBAC follows links. "The owner of a document may delete it."

Cedar can write all four. Here are four real policies in one file. The text after // is a comment.

// ACL: the doc keeps a list of users who may read it.
@id("acl-readers")
permit (principal, action == Action::"read", resource is Doc)
when { resource.readers.contains(principal) };

// RBAC: every member of the editor role may edit.
@id("rbac-editors")
permit (principal in Role::"editor", action == Action::"edit", resource is Doc);

// ABAC: same department may read.
@id("abac-same-department")
permit (principal is User, action == Action::"read", resource is Doc)
when { principal.department == resource.department };

// ReBAC: the owner may delete.
@id("rebac-owner")
permit (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource is Doc)
when { resource.owner == principal };

The facts: alice works in sales and is on the reader list. Bob works in finance and has the editor role. Carol works in finance and owns the budget. We asked Cedar all nine questions. These are the real decisions and the policy that gave each Allow.

Nine requests on Doc::"budget", run with cedar-policy 4.14.1
Principalreadeditdelete
alice (sales, on list)Allow: acl-readersDenyDeny
bob (finance, editor)Allow: abac-same-departmentAllow: rbac-editorsDeny
carol (finance, owner)Allow: abac-same-departmentDenyAllow: rebac-owner

Real systems mix the styles. A company may use roles for most rights, attributes for limits, and owners for sharing. Cedar lets you put all of them in one policy set. The chapter Modeling RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC designs full models.

Do not choose one model for the whole system too early. Pure RBAC often leads to "role explosion": hundreds of roles like editor-finance-europe. One attribute rule can replace many roles.

Policy as code

Most apps start with authorization inside the code. The same four rules look like this in Python. The second tab repeats two of the four Cedar policies from above, for comparison. Both versions give the same answers.

# The same four rules as hand-written if-statements.
users = {
    "alice": {"department": "sales", "roles": []},
    "bob": {"department": "finance", "roles": ["editor"]},
    "carol": {"department": "finance", "roles": []},
}
budget = {"department": "finance", "owner": "carol", "readers": ["alice"]}


def can(user: str, action: str, doc: dict) -> bool:
    u = users[user]
    if action == "read" and user in doc["readers"]:
        return True  # ACL
    if action == "edit" and "editor" in u["roles"]:
        return True  # RBAC
    if action == "read" and u["department"] == doc["department"]:
        return True  # ABAC
    if action == "delete" and doc["owner"] == user:
        return True  # ReBAC
    return False  # nothing matched: deny


for user in users:
    print(user, [a for a in ("read", "edit", "delete") if can(user, a, budget)])
@id("rbac-editors")
permit (principal in Role::"editor", action == Action::"edit", resource is Doc);

@id("rebac-owner")
permit (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource is Doc)
when { resource.owner == principal };
alice ['read']
bob ['read', 'edit']
carol ['read', 'delete']

The Python is short here. But in a real app, checks like this live in hundreds of places. Some are in the web layer, some in the database layer, some in a background job. Nobody can see all the rules at once.

Policy as code moves the rules out of the app. The rules live in their own files. The app only asks "is this allowed?" and obeys the answer.

  • One place. A security reviewer reads the policy files, not the whole app.
  • Review and history. Policies go through pull requests and version control like other code.
  • Tests. You can test each rule with many requests. See Testing Cedar policies.
  • Same rules everywhere. A Rust service and a Python service can load the same files.
  • Tools can check them. Cedar policies are simple enough for a solver to analyze. See Analysis and formal verification.

A good test: can a new team member answer "who can delete invoices?" in five minutes? With policy as code, they search one folder. With scattered if-statements, they search the whole code base.

The user logs in

Bob signs in with a passkey. The login system gives the app a token. This is authentication, not Cedar.

The user clicks

Bob clicks "Edit" on the budget. The app receives an HTTP request.

The app builds a request

The app turns the click into principal, action, resource and context. It also collects the entities: bob, his roles, the budget.

Cedar decides

The authorizer checks every policy against the request. It returns Allow or Deny, the deciding policies and any errors.

The app obeys

On Allow, the app saves the edit. On Deny, it returns 403. Cedar never touches the data itself.

Where Cedar comes from and where it runs

Cedar was built at Amazon Web Services (AWS). AWS uses it inside its own products. Then it gave the language to the public.

  1. 2023-05-10 Open source. AWS releases the Cedar language and its engine under the Apache 2.0 license.
  2. 2023-06-13 Managed service. Amazon Verified Permissions becomes generally available. It runs Cedar policies for your apps.
  3. 2024-10 Research paper. The Cedar team presents its design paper at OOPSLA 2024. It describes the Lean proofs and the speed tests.
  4. 2025-10-08 CNCF. The Cloud Native Computing Foundation accepts Cedar as a Sandbox project.
  5. 2026-11-02 Today's release. The Rust crate cedar-policy 4.14.1 is the newest stable version. Its language version is 4.6.

The engine is written in Rust. The team also wrote a model of Cedar in Lean. They proved important properties there. They test the Rust code against the Lean model with many random inputs. This is called differential random testing.

Here are places that use Cedar today, checked on 2026-11-15:

  • Amazon Verified Permissions: a managed authorization service for your own apps. Its guide says it uses Cedar version 4.12. See Amazon Verified Permissions and Cedar in products.
  • AWS Verified Access: its access policies are written in Cedar.
  • Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Policy: checks AI agent tool calls with Cedar policies. See Cedar for AI agents and tool calls.
  • Cedar for Kubernetes: a project in the cedar-policy GitHub organization for Kubernetes authorization and admission checks.
  • Other companies: the AWS blog post about CNCF names Cloudflare, MongoDB, StrongDM and Cloudinary as users. Check their own pages for details.

Language bindings exist for Java, Go, JavaScript and Python. The chapter Cedar in Python and other languages checks who keeps each one.

Versions change fast. The cedar-policy crate had eleven releases in 2026, from 4.9.0 to 4.14.1. Check crates.io and the Cedar changelog before you pin a version.

Cedar and other tools: a fair comparison

Cedar is not the only choice. Three other options are common. Each one is good at different jobs.

  • OPA and Rego. OPA (Open Policy Agent) is a CNCF Graduated project since January 2021. Its language, Rego, was inspired by Datalog. OPA checks many kinds of data: Kubernetes objects, Terraform plans, API requests.
  • OpenFGA and Zanzibar. Zanzibar is Google's global authorization system, described in a 2019 paper. OpenFGA is an open-source system inspired by it. It moved to CNCF Incubating on 2025-10-28. It stores relationship tuples in a database.
  • Hand-written checks. If-statements in your own language. No new tool. Fine for very small apps.
Cedar compared with other common choices (November 2026)
QuestionCedarOPA / RegoOpenFGAIf-statements
Main jobApp authorization decisionsGeneral policy over any JSONRelationship checks at large scaleWhatever you write
OutputAllow or Deny plus reasonsAny JSON valueAllowed true or falseAny
Where facts liveSent with each requestSent in or loaded as dataStored in its own databaseYour database
Runs asLibrary (Rust, bindings) or managed serviceServer, sidecar or libraryServer, or library in GoInside the app
Loops and functionsNone on purposeYes, rich languageNo, a model languageYes
Proofs and analysisLean proofs, solver toolsUnit tests and static checksModel testsOnly your tests
Best whenYou want readable, checkable app rulesYou need one tool for many policy kindsYou have huge sharing graphs like Google DriveA tiny app with few rules

The Cedar paper compared Cedar with OpenFGA and Rego. The authors found Cedar about as readable or more readable, and much faster in their tests. Remember that the Cedar team wrote the paper. Measure with your own data before you decide.

A common question: "Where does the relationship data live?" In OpenFGA, the service stores it. In Cedar, your app sends the needed entities with each request, or a service like Verified Permissions helps you. This one difference shapes the whole design.

Cedar's promises

Cedar makes a few strong promises. Later chapters show each one with real runs. Here is the short list, so you know what to expect.

  • Deny by default. If no policy allows a request, the answer is Deny. See Your first Cedar policy.
  • Forbid wins. One matching forbid policy beats any number of permit policies. See Policy syntax and how decisions are made.
  • Order does not matter. You can shuffle the policies. The decision stays the same.
  • Every evaluation ends. There are no loops, so a policy cannot run forever. See Expressions, types and operators.
  • Types can be checked. With a schema, a validator finds mistakes before you deploy. See Schemas and validation.

Cedar is not a login system and not a database. It does not store users or check passwords. It does not fetch data. Your app gives it everything it needs, then it decides.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for thinking about authorization
PatternPurposeModelNote
Authn first, then authzSeparate "who are you" from "what may you do"Login gives User::"bob". Cedar decides edit on Doc::"budget".A valid token is not permission.
Principal + action + resource + contextDescribe any access questionbob, edit, budget, {mfa: true}Context is for facts about this one request only.
Type::"id"Name one entityDoc::"budget"The id needs double quotes.
ACL: list on the objectShare one object with named usersresource.readers.contains(principal)Long lists get hard to manage.
RBAC: principal in RoleGive rights to a jobprincipal in Role::"editor"Too many roles is a warning sign.
ABAC: compare attributesRules from propertiesprincipal.department == resource.departmentThe attributes must be in the entities.
ReBAC: follow a linkOwners, sharing, teamsresource.owner == principalThe link must point to an entity, not a string.
Ask, then obeyKeep rules out of app codeAllow: save. Deny: return 403.Check every action, not only the first page.
Decision + reasonsExplain and audit a decisionAllow, reason rbac-editorsLog the reasons. They answer "why?".

Practice

A user types a password and a one-time code. Which step is this?

  • Authentication
  • Authorization
  • Policy validation

Checking a password and a code proves who the user is. That is authentication.

The server knows the user, but the user may not delete this file. Which HTTP status fits best?

  • 401 Unauthorized
  • 403 Forbidden
  • 404 Not Found

403 means the identity is known but the action is not allowed. 401 is about missing or bad login data.

"Users may read documents of their own department." Which model is this?

  • ACL
  • RBAC
  • ABAC

The rule compares two attributes: the user's department and the document's department.

In a Cedar request, the user or service that asks is the ?.

The time of day and the IP address of one request go in the ?.

The four letters for the request shape are ?.

  1. Write the PARC parts for this sentence: "Carol wants to delete the budget document from her office network."
    Answer

    Principal: User::"carol". Action: Action::"delete". Resource: Doc::"budget". Context: a record with the network facts, for example { "ip": "10.1.2.3" }.

  2. In the nine-request table, why may bob read the budget? He is not on the reader list.
    Answer

    The ABAC policy abac-same-department matches. Bob works in finance, and the budget belongs to finance. One matching permit is enough for Allow.

  3. Your team has 300 roles like editor-finance-asia. What would you suggest?
    Answer

    Keep a few roles, like editor. Move the department and region into attributes. Then one ABAC condition, like principal.department == resource.department, replaces many roles.

  4. Name one situation where OpenFGA may fit better than Cedar, and one where Cedar fits better.
    Answer

    OpenFGA fits a huge sharing graph that a central service should store, like files shared between millions of users. Cedar fits apps that want readable rules with attributes and context, run as a fast library, and checked by a validator.

  5. A teammate says: "Cedar will check the user's password for us." What do you answer?
    Answer

    No. Cedar only does authorization. A login system checks the password first. Then the app sends the known principal to Cedar.

Key takeaways

  • Authentication asks "who are you?". Authorization asks "what may you do?". Cedar answers only the second question.
  • Every Cedar request has a principal, an action, a resource and a context.
  • The authorizer also needs policies (the rules) and entities (the facts). It returns Allow or Deny plus the deciding policies.
  • ACL, RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC are four ways to explain access. Cedar can express all four, and real systems mix them.
  • Policy as code puts rules in one place, so people can review, test and analyze them.
  • Cedar came from AWS, became open source on 2023-05-10, and joined the CNCF Sandbox on 2025-10-08.

Sources

  • Cedar terms and concepts. The official definitions of principal, action, resource, context and entities.
  • Introducing Cedar, an open-source language for access control. The AWS announcement from May 2023.
  • CNCF: Cedar project page. Shows the Sandbox level and the acceptance date.
  • Cedar joins CNCF as a Sandbox project. AWS blog with named users, the Lean proofs and random testing.
  • What is Amazon Verified Permissions?. The AWS guide, with the Cedar version it uses.
  • Cedar: A New Language for Expressive, Fast, Safe, and Analyzable Authorization. The OOPSLA 2024 paper, with the comparison to OpenFGA and Rego.

Your first Cedar policy

In this chapter you build a tiny test bench and ask Cedar real questions. You write one policy, one entities file and one request. Then you change one thing at a time and watch the decision change.

  • Set up a test bench with the Rust cedar-policy crate and with Python cedarpy.
  • Write a permit policy, an entities file and a request file that work together.
  • Read a decision, its reasons and its errors.
  • Explain default deny with a real example.
  • Recognize the most common first mistakes from their real error messages.

The lab folder

Everything in this chapter lives in one folder. Here is the layout. Open the tree to see each file.

A test bench reads the files, asks Cedar, and prints the answer. You need only one bench. This chapter shows both, so you can pick your language. Later chapters use the same files.

  • Rust: Rust 1.89 or newer. The cedar-policy crate 4.14.1 needs at least 1.89. This book used 1.99.0.
  • Python: Python 3.10 or newer and the uv tool. The package is cedarpy 4.12.2.

The cedar-policy crate is the official engine from the cedar-policy GitHub organization. The cedarpy package is a community project by k9securityio. It wraps the same Rust engine. Version 4.12.2 is built on engine 4.12.0, so it can be a little behind.

Build the Rust bench

Create a new project and add two crates.

mkdir -p /home/you/cedar-lab && cd /home/you/cedar-lab
cargo new cedar-bench
cd cedar-bench
cargo add cedar-policy@4.14.1 serde_json
[package]
name = "cedar-bench"
version = "0.1.0"
edition = "2024"

[dependencies]
cedar-policy = "4.14.1"
serde_json = "1.0.154"

Now replace src/main.rs with the bench. Read the comments. The schema file is optional. We use it at the end of the chapter.

// A tiny Cedar test bench.
// Usage: cedar-bench policy.cedar entities.json request.json [schema.cedarschema]
use cedar_policy::{Authorizer, Context, Entities, EntityUid, PolicyId, PolicySet, Request, Schema};
use std::{env, error::Error, fs, str::FromStr};

fn run(args: &[String]) -> Result<(), Box<dyn Error>> {
    let read = |i: usize| fs::read_to_string(&args[i]);
    let schema = match args.get(4) {
        Some(_) => Some(Schema::from_cedarschema_str(&read(4)?)?.0),
        None => None,
    };
    // Parse the policies. Use the @id annotation as the policy id.
    let mut policies = PolicySet::new();
    for p in PolicySet::from_str(&read(1)?)?.policies() {
        let id = p.annotation("id").map(PolicyId::new);
        policies.add(id.map_or(p.clone(), |id| p.new_id(id)))?;
    }
    let entities = Entities::from_json_str(&read(2)?, schema.as_ref())?;
    let req: serde_json::Value = serde_json::from_str(&read(3)?)?;
    let uid = |key: &str| EntityUid::from_str(req[key].as_str().unwrap_or_default());
    let action = uid("action")?;
    let ctx_schema = schema.as_ref().map(|s| (s, &action));
    let context = Context::from_json_value(req["context"].clone(), ctx_schema)?;
    let request = Request::new(uid("principal")?, action, uid("resource")?, context, schema.as_ref())?;
    let answer = Authorizer::new().is_authorized(&request, &policies, &entities);
    println!("decision: {:?}", answer.decision());
    for id in answer.diagnostics().reason() { println!("reason:   {id}"); }
    for err in answer.diagnostics().errors() { println!("error:    {err}"); }
    Ok(())
}

fn main() {
    let args: Vec<String> = env::args().collect();
    if let Err(e) = run(&args) {
        let mut cause: Option<&dyn Error> = Some(e.as_ref());
        while let Some(c) = cause { eprintln!("problem: {c}"); cause = c.source(); }
        std::process::exit(1);
    }
}

The important types are all in one crate:

  • PolicySet holds the rules. Entities holds the facts.
  • Request holds principal, action, resource and context.
  • Authorizer::is_authorized returns a Response with a decision and diagnostics.

The Rust crate names policies policy0, policy1 and so on. It does not read @id by itself. The loop in the bench copies @id into the policy id. The cedar command-line tool does the same thing.

Build the Python bench

The Python bench is shorter. uv downloads cedarpy for you when you run it.

# bench.py: a tiny Cedar test bench in Python.
# Usage: python3.14 bench.py policy.cedar entities.json request.json [schema.cedarschema]
import json
import sys

import cedarpy

policies = open(sys.argv[1]).read()
entities = open(sys.argv[2]).read()
request = json.load(open(sys.argv[3]))
schema = open(sys.argv[4]).read() if len(sys.argv) > 4 else None

result = cedarpy.is_authorized(request, policies, entities, schema)
print("decision:", result.decision.name)
# cedarpy names policies policy0, policy1, ... Show the @id too.
ids = result.diagnostics.id_annotations_by_reason
for reason in result.diagnostics.reasons:
    print("reason:  ", reason, "@id =", ids.get(reason))
for error in result.diagnostics.errors:
    print("error:   ", error)

cedarpy has a third decision value, NoDecision. It appears when the input is broken, for example a policy that does not parse. Treat NoDecision exactly like Deny in your app.

Three files and one question

Now write the Cedar files. Start with the rule. Alice may view the trip plan. Nobody else is mentioned.

// Alice may view the trip plan.
@id("alice-views-plan")
permit (
    principal == User::"alice",
    action == Action::"view",
    resource == Doc::"trip-plan"
);
  • permit is the effect. This policy can only allow.
  • The part in round brackets is the scope. It names who, what and which thing.
  • @id("...") is an annotation. It gives the policy a readable name.
  • The policy ends with a semicolon. Every policy needs one.

The next file holds the entities. Each entity has a uid, attrs and parents. Here they are all empty.

[
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "bob" },   "attrs": {}, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Doc",  "id": "trip-plan" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] }
]

The last file is the question. Inside JSON strings, the inner double quotes need a backslash.

{
  "principal": "User::\"alice\"",
  "action": "Action::\"view\"",
  "resource": "Doc::\"trip-plan\"",
  "context": {}
}

Run both benches from the cedar-lab folder:

cd cedar-bench
cargo run -q -- ../first/policy.cedar ../first/entities.json ../first/request.json
uv run --with cedarpy==4.12.2 python3.14 bench.py first/policy.cedar first/entities.json first/request.json
decision: Allow
reason:   alice-views-plan
decision: Allow
reason:   policy0 @id = alice-views-plan

Both say Allow. Both name the same policy. The decision is the answer. The reason list says why. An empty error list means every policy ran without a problem.

You can also install the official command-line tool: cargo install cedar-policy-cli --version 4.14.1 --locked. Then cedar authorize -v --policies first/policy.cedar --entities first/entities.json --request-json first/request.json prints ALLOW and the policy id. The exit code is 0 for Allow and 2 for Deny.

Change one thing at a time

Good testers change one thing, run again, and compare. Go through the slides. Each step changes one line. Every output is real.

The start

Alice asks to view the trip plan.

decision: Allow
reason:   alice-views-plan

Change the principal

Bob asks the same question. No policy mentions Bob.

decision: Deny

No reason and no error. Nothing matched, so the answer is Deny.

Change the action

Alice asks to edit the trip plan. The policy only covers view.

decision: Deny

Open the scope

Change principal == User::"alice" to just principal. Now any principal matches. Bob asks again.

decision: Allow
reason:   anyone-views-plan

Add a condition

Add when { context.mfa == true };. Bob's request still has an empty context.

decision: Deny
error:    error while evaluating policy `anyone-views-plan-with-mfa`: record does not have the attribute `mfa`

Fill the context

Send "context": { "mfa": false }. The result is Deny, with no error. Then send { "mfa": true }:

decision: Allow
reason:   anyone-views-plan-with-mfa

Here is the policy from steps 5 and 6. The when block is a condition.

// Anyone may view the trip plan, but only after a second login step.
@id("anyone-views-plan-with-mfa")
permit (
    principal,
    action == Action::"view",
    resource == Doc::"trip-plan"
)
when { context.mfa == true };

Look at step 5 again. The condition could not run, because the key mfa was missing. Cedar reported an error, skipped that policy, and answered Deny. The chapter Policy syntax and how decisions are made explains this rule. The chapter Context and requests shows safe ways to handle missing keys.

The six experiments, as a table
StepWhat changedDecisionReason or error
1nothingAllowalice-views-plan
2principal is bobDenynone
3action is editDenynone
4scope has any principalAllowanyone-views-plan
5condition on context.mfa, empty contextDenyerror: no attribute mfa
6context mfa false, then trueDeny, then Allownone, then anyone-views-plan-with-mfa

Default deny

Steps 2 and 3 show the most important rule in Cedar. You do not write "deny everything else". Cedar does it for you.

If no permit policy is satisfied by a request, the decision is Deny. In particular, an empty policy set denies every request.

In simple words: silence means no. Access exists only when a permit policy says yes.

  1. Use an empty policy.cedar file. Alice asks to view the trip plan. The bench prints decision: Deny. Zero permits match.
  2. Use the file with alice-views-plan. Bob asks. One permit exists, but zero permits match. The result is Deny.
  3. Alice asks. One permit matches. The result is Allow with one reason.
Why is this always true?

The authorizer has three rules. If a satisfied forbid exists, the answer is Deny. Otherwise, if a satisfied permit exists, the answer is Allow. Otherwise the answer is Deny. Allow appears in only one rule, and that rule needs a satisfied permit. So without a satisfied permit, Allow is impossible. A policy that hits an error is not satisfied, so it cannot create an Allow either.

Think of a locked door. Each permit policy is a key. No key, no entry. A broken key does not open the door either.

The four files you will always have

So far we used three files. Real projects add a fourth one, the schema. Here are the four jobs.

The four files of every Cedar project
FileJobChanges how often?
policy.cedarThe rules: permit and forbid policiesWhen the rules change
schema.cedarschemaThe shapes: entity types, attributes, actionsWhen the app model changes
entities.jsonThe facts: users, groups, documentsOften. Usually built from your database for each request
request.jsonThe question: principal, action, resource, contextEvery request

Here is a small schema for our files. It says there are users and docs, and that view applies to a user and a doc.

// The shapes the app knows about.
entity User;
entity Doc;
action view appliesTo {
    principal: User,
    resource: Doc,
};

Pass the schema as the fourth argument. The answer stays the same: decision: Allow, reason: alice-views-plan. But now Cedar checks the request too. Alice asks to edit, and edit is not in the schema:

problem: action `Action::"edit"` is not defined in the supplied schema
decision: NoDecision
error:    failed to build request: action `Action::"edit"` does not exist in the supplied schema

The schema also lets the validator check policies before they run. The chapter Schemas and validation covers it in full. You will see one validator message in the next section.

Common first mistakes

These mistakes happen to almost everyone in the first week. Each message below is real. The parse errors come from the cedar command-line tool, which shows the place in the file.

1. Missing semicolon at the end 
  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ unexpected end of input
   ╭─[5:2]
 4 │     resource == Doc::"trip-plan"
 5 │ )
   ·  ▲
   ·  ╰── expected `;` or identifier
   ╰────

Fix: end every policy with ;. The message says "end of input" because Cedar reached the end of the file while it still waited for ;.

2. Capital letter in the effect 
  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ invalid policy effect: Permit
   ╭─[1:1]
 1 │ Permit (
   · ──────
 2 │     principal == User::"alice",
   ╰────
  help: effect must be either `permit` or `forbid`

Fix: Cedar keywords are lower case. Write permit and forbid.

3. Entity id without quotes 
  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ expected an entity uid or matching template slot, found name
      `User::alice`
   ╭─[2:18]
 1 │ permit (
 2 │     principal == User::alice,
   ·                  ───────────
 3 │     action == Action::"view",
   ╰────
  help: try using `is` to test for an entity type or including an identifier
        string if you intended this name to be an entity uid

Fix: write User::"alice". The id is always a string in double quotes.

4. An entity without attrs or parents 
problem: error during entity deserialization
problem: missing field `attrs` at line 1 column 60

Fix: in Cedar 4.14.1, every entity needs "attrs" and "parents", even when they are empty. Leave out "parents", and you get missing field `parents`.

5. The uid written as one string 
problem: error during entity deserialization
problem: in uid field of <unknown entity>, expected a literal entity reference, but got `"User::\"alice\""`

Fix: in the entities file, write { "type": "User", "id": "alice" }. The string form User::"alice" is for policies and requests.

6. Request without the inner quotes 
  × failed to parse request
  ├─▶ failed to parse principal in request-bad.json as entity Uid
  ╰─▶ unexpected end of input
   ╭────
 1 │ User::alice
   ╰────

Fix: in request.json, write "User::\"alice\"", with backslashes before the inner quotes.

7. Comparing an entity with a string

The policy string-not-entity says when { principal == "alice" };. Without a schema there is no error at all. The answer is simply Deny, because an entity is never equal to a string. With the schema, the validator catches it:

  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ the types String and User are not compatible
   ╭─[3:8]
 2 │ permit (principal, action, resource)
 3 │ when { principal == "alice" };
   ·        ────────────────────
   ╰────
  help: for policy `string-not-entity`, both operands to a `==` expression
        must have compatible types. Types must be exactly equal to be
        compatible

Fix: write principal == User::"alice". Run the validator on every change.

8. Reading an attribute that does not exist

The policy owner-can-view says when { resource.owner == principal };, but the doc has no owner.

decision: Deny
error:    error while evaluating policy `owner-can-view`: `Doc::"trip-plan"` does not have the attribute `owner`

Fix: add the attribute to the entity, or test it first with resource has owner && ....

9. Wrong capital letter in a type name

The request says user::"alice". The policy says User::"alice". Type names are case sensitive, so nothing matches. The answer is a quiet Deny with no error. A schema turns this into a clear request error.

An entity that is missing from the entities file is not an error. Delete all entities and ask again: Alice still gets Allow. The policy only uses ==, and == compares the names. Cedar never looks up a list of "real" users. Your app must decide which principals exist.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for your first Cedar files
PatternPurposeModelNote
permit (principal == ..., action == ..., resource == ...);Allow one exact requestpermit (principal == User::"alice", action == Action::"view", resource == Doc::"trip-plan");End with a semicolon.
@id("name") before the effectGive the policy a readable name@id("alice-views-plan")The Rust crate needs your code to use it.
permit (principal, ...)Match any principalpermit (principal, action == Action::"view", resource == Doc::"trip-plan");Open scopes allow more than you think.
when { context.key == value }Add a condition from the requestwhen { context.mfa == true };A missing key is an error, and the policy is skipped.
{ "uid": {...}, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] }Write one entity{ "uid": { "type": "Doc", "id": "trip-plan" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] }attrs and parents are required.
"principal": "Type::\"id\""Write a request field in JSON"principal": "User::\"alice\""Escape the inner quotes.
Change one thing, run againLearn and debugalice, then bob, then editKeep every request file. They become tests.
No permit matched means DenyDefault denyempty policy set: DenyYou never write a "deny all" policy.
NoDecision means DenyHandle broken input safelycedarpy parse error: NoDecisionNever treat an error as Allow.

Practice

The policy file is empty. Alice asks to view a document. What is the decision?

  • Allow, because nothing forbids it
  • Deny
  • An error, because there are no policies

Default deny. With no satisfied permit, the answer is Deny. An empty policy set is not an error.

Which line is a correct entity in entities.json for Cedar 4.14.1?

  • { "uid": "User::\"bob\"", "attrs": {}, "parents": [] }
  • { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "bob" } }
  • { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "bob" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] }

The uid must be an object with type and id. The fields attrs and parents are required, even when empty.

cedarpy returns NoDecision. What should your app do?

  • Allow, and log a warning
  • Deny, and log the error
  • Retry until it says Allow

NoDecision means the input was broken. A safe app fails closed and treats it as Deny.

Every Cedar policy ends with a ?.

The cedar command-line tool exits with code ? when the decision is Deny.

  1. Write a policy that lets Bob edit the trip plan. Give it the id bob-edits-plan.
    Answer
    @id("bob-edits-plan")
permit (
    principal == User::"bob",
    action == Action::"edit",
    resource == Doc::"trip-plan"
);
  2. Your policy has when { context.mfa == true }. A request comes with "context": {}. Describe the decision and the diagnostics.
    Answer

    The decision is Deny. There is no reason. The errors list has one entry: the policy failed because the record does not have the attribute mfa. The policy was skipped.

  3. A teammate deletes Bob from entities.json to block him. Bob still gets Allow from permit (principal == User::"bob", ...). Why?
    Answer

    The == test compares names. It does not check that the entity is in the file. To block Bob, remove or change the policy, or add a forbid policy. Never rely on a missing entity.

  4. Write the request.json for: Carol wants to view the trip plan, and she used MFA.
    Answer
    {
  "principal": "User::\"carol\"",
  "action": "Action::\"view\"",
  "resource": "Doc::\"trip-plan\"",
  "context": { "mfa": true }
}
  5. Why does a schema turn mistake 9 (wrong capital letter) from a quiet Deny into a clear error?
    Answer

    With a schema, Cedar checks the request before it decides. The type user is not in the schema, so the request fails with an error that names the problem. Without a schema, Cedar accepts any type name. It just finds no matching policy.

Key takeaways

  • A test bench reads a policy file, an entities file and a request, then prints the decision, the reasons and the errors.
  • The Rust crate cedar-policy 4.14.1 and the Python package cedarpy 4.12.2 give the same decisions for the same files.
  • Default deny: without a satisfied permit, the answer is always Deny.
  • A policy that hits an error is skipped. The error appears in the diagnostics.
  • Real projects have four files: policies, schema, entities and request.
  • Many first mistakes give a quiet Deny, not an error. A schema and the validator make them visible.

Sources

  • cedar-policy 4.14.1 on docs.rs. The Rust API: PolicySet, Entities, Request, Authorizer, Response.
  • cedar-py on GitHub. The cedarpy package, its API and its engine version table.
  • Cedar policy syntax. Effect, scope, conditions and annotations.
  • Entities and context syntax. The JSON format for entities.
  • Cedar authorization. How the authorizer reaches Allow or Deny.

Policy syntax and how decisions are made

A Cedar policy has only a few parts: an effect, a scope, conditions and annotations. The authorizer combines many policies with three simple rules. This chapter teaches every part and every rule with real runs.

  • Write the effect, the scope and the conditions of a policy correctly.
  • Use ==, in, is and is ... in in the scope, and know where each one is allowed.
  • Add annotations and comments without breaking the parser.
  • Predict a decision from many policies: default deny, forbid wins, errors are skipped, order does not matter.
  • Read the diagnostics: the deciding policies and the errors.

The parts of a policy

Here is one complete policy. It uses every part you will learn in this chapter.

// Staff in the eng team may read and comment on project docs.
@id("eng-staff-read-projects")
@owner("platform-team")
permit (
    principal is User in Team::"eng",
    action in [Action::"view", Action::"comment"],
    resource is Doc in Folder::"projects",
)
unless { principal.contractor };
  • Comment: the line that starts with //. Cedar ignores it.
  • Annotations: @id(...) and @owner(...). Labels for people and tools.
  • Effect: permit. What happens when the policy matches.
  • Scope: the three lines in round brackets. Who, what action, which resource.
  • Condition: unless { ... }. An extra test.
  • Semicolon: the end of the policy.

A policy is satisfied when the scope matches and every condition passes. Only satisfied policies take part in the decision.

Build the same policy one step at a time. Each slide adds one part. The results come from three real users: alice (eng team), bob (eng team, contractor) and carol (not in eng).

The widest policy

permit (principal, action, resource);

Every request is allowed. Alice, bob and carol may view the roadmap. Alice may even delete it. Never ship this.

Add a scope

permit (
    principal is User in Team::"eng",
    action in [Action::"view", Action::"comment"],
    resource is Doc in Folder::"projects"
);

Alice and bob may view. Carol gets Deny. Delete gets Deny.

Add a condition

unless { principal.contractor };

Now bob gets Deny, because he is a contractor. Only alice may view.

Add a name and a comment

Add // ..., @id and @owner. The decisions stay the same. The reason now says eng-staff-read-projects instead of policy0.

The effect: permit or forbid

Every policy starts with one of two words.

permit

"This may happen." A satisfied permit can lead to Allow. Use permits for features: who may do what.

forbid

"This must never happen." A satisfied forbid always leads to Deny. Use forbids for guardrails.

// Nobody may delete a locked doc.
@id("no-delete-locked")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource is Doc)
when { resource.locked };

There is no third effect. You cannot write "deny" or "allow" as an effect. And you never need "deny everything else". Default deny does it, as you saw in Your first Cedar policy.

Do not use forbid to say "only admins may do X". A forbid without a matching permit changes nothing, because the answer was already Deny. Write a permit for admins instead. Use forbid when you must block something that some permit would allow.

The scope: principal, action, resource

The scope always has three parts, in this order: principal, action, resource. Each part can be open or have one constraint.

Scope forms in Cedar 4.14.1
FormMeaningprincipalactionresource
just the namematches anythingyesyesyes
== Eexactly this entityyesyesyes
in Ethis entity or anything inside ityesyesyes
in [E1, E2]inside any entity in the listnoyesno
is Tany entity of type Tyesnoyes
is T in Etype T and inside Eyesnoyes
  • == compares one exact entity UID, like User::"alice".
  • in follows the hierarchy. principal in Team::"eng" matches every member of eng, and also Team::"eng" itself.
  • is checks only the entity type. resource is Doc matches every Doc and nothing else.
  • is T in E combines both. It means the same as is T and in E together.

The parser checks these rules strictly. Here are three real errors from cedar check-parse:

  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ expected single entity uid or template slot, found set of entity uids
   ╭────
 1 │ permit (principal in [User::"a", User::"b"], action, resource);
   ·                      ──────────────────────
   ╰────
  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ `is` cannot appear in the action scope
   ╭────
 1 │ permit (principal, action is Action, resource);
   ·                    ────────────────
   ╰────
  help: try moving `action is ..` into a `when` condition
  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ found the variable `action` where the variable `principal` must be used
   ╭────
 1 │ permit (action, principal, resource);
   ·         ──────
   ╰────
  help: policy scopes must contain a `principal`, `action`, and `resource`
        element in that order

You also cannot leave a part out or add context to the scope. permit (principal, action); fails with "this policy is missing the `resource` variable in the scope".

Need a list of principals? Put them in a group entity and write principal in Group::"reviewers". Or move the test into a condition: when { principal in [User::"a", User::"b"] }. The condition form is allowed.

principal in User::"alice" is true for alice herself, because in includes the entity itself. This is useful. But it surprises people who expect "strictly inside". The chapter Entities and hierarchy shows the full rules of in.

Conditions: when and unless

The scope uses only the three entities. Conditions can use anything: attributes, the context, and many operators.

// Owners may view, edit and delete their own docs.
@id("owner-full-access")
permit (
    principal is User,
    action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit", Action::"delete"],
    resource is Doc
)
when { resource.owner == principal };
  • when { e }: the policy needs e to be true.
  • unless { e }: the policy needs e to be false.
  • A policy can have zero, one or many conditions, in any mix. All of them must pass.
  • The expression inside must give a Boolean. Otherwise the policy hits an error.

Two conditions mean "and". These two policies match exactly the same requests:

forbid (principal is User, action, resource is Doc)
when { principal.contractor }
when { resource.secret };
forbid (principal is User, action, resource is Doc)
when { principal.contractor && resource.secret };

Cedar checks the conditions in order. It stops at the first condition that fails, or at the first error. So a later condition may never run. The expression language itself is in Expressions, types and operators.

You can write principal == User::"alice" in the scope or in a condition. The decision is the same. Prefer the scope when you can. It is easier to read, and policy templates use slots only in the scope. Amazon Verified Permissions can also list policies by the principal or resource they name.

Annotations and comments

Annotations sit before the effect. Each one has a name and a string value. Cedar keeps them but ignores them when it decides.

@id("no-delete-locked")
@reason("Locked docs are under legal hold.")
@ticket("SEC-142")
@deprecated
forbid (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource is Doc)
when { resource.locked };
  • @id is a common convention for the policy name. The cedar command-line tool uses it as the policy id. The Rust crate does not, unless your code copies it, as in our bench.
  • A bare annotation like @deprecated is the same as @deprecated("").
  • The same name twice is an error: duplicate annotation: @id.
  • An annotation after the policy body fails. The parser says unexpected token `@`.

Comments use // and run to the end of the line. Cedar has no block comments. This is the real error for /* hi */:

  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ unexpected token `/`
   ╭────
 1 │ /* hi */ permit (principal, action, resource);
   · ┬
   · ╰── expected `@` or identifier
   ╰────

Since cedar-policy 4.5.0 (June 2025), a trailing comma after the last scope part is allowed. The policy at the top of this chapter uses one.

Comments disappear when you translate a policy to JSON with cedar translate-policy. Annotations stay. Put facts that tools need, like an owner or a ticket number, in annotations.

How the authorizer decides

Now we put four policies together and ask 18 questions. The policies are eng-views-projects, owner-full-access, no-delete-locked and contractors-no-secrets.

// Team members may view anything in the projects folder.
@id("eng-views-projects")
permit (
    principal in Team::"eng",
    action == Action::"view",
    resource in Folder::"projects"
);

// Owners may view, edit and delete their own docs.
@id("owner-full-access")
permit (
    principal is User,
    action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit", Action::"delete"],
    resource is Doc
)
when { resource.owner == principal };

// Nobody may delete a locked doc.
@id("no-delete-locked")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource is Doc)
when { resource.locked };

// Contractors may not see secret docs.
@id("contractors-no-secrets")
forbid (principal is User, action, resource is Doc)
when { principal.contractor }
when { resource.secret };
[
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" }, "attrs": { "contractor": false },
    "parents": [ { "type": "Team", "id": "eng" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "bob" }, "attrs": { "contractor": true },
    "parents": [ { "type": "Team", "id": "eng" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "carol" }, "attrs": { "contractor": false }, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Team", "id": "eng" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Folder", "id": "projects" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Doc", "id": "roadmap" },
    "attrs": { "owner": { "__entity": { "type": "User", "id": "carol" } },
               "locked": false, "secret": true },
    "parents": [ { "type": "Folder", "id": "projects" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Doc", "id": "notes" },
    "attrs": { "owner": { "__entity": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" } },
               "locked": true },
    "parents": [ { "type": "Folder", "id": "projects" } ] }
]

The roadmap belongs to carol and is secret. The notes belong to alice and are locked. The notes have no secret attribute at all. That detail matters soon.

18 real decisions (cedar-policy 4.14.1). E means the diagnostics also hold an error.
Requestroadmapnotes
alice viewAllow: eng-views-projectsAllow: eng-views-projects, owner-full-access
alice editDenyAllow: owner-full-access
alice deleteDenyDeny: no-delete-locked
bob viewDeny: contractors-no-secretsAllow: eng-views-projects, E
bob editDeny: contractors-no-secretsDeny, E
bob deleteDeny: contractors-no-secretsDeny: no-delete-locked, E
carol viewAllow: owner-full-accessDeny
carol editAllow: owner-full-accessDeny
carol deleteAllow: owner-full-accessDeny: no-delete-locked

The authorizer follows three steps. It evaluates every policy. Then it looks at the satisfied forbids, then at the satisfied permits.

The same rules as a decision table. A policy with an error counts as "not satisfied" in every row.

The decision table of Cedar
Any forbid satisfied?Any permit satisfied?DecisionReasons
yesyesDenythe satisfied forbids
yesnoDenythe satisfied forbids
noyesAllowthe satisfied permits
nonoDenynone

Rule 1: forbid wins

Look at "alice delete notes". Alice owns the notes, so owner-full-access is satisfied. But the notes are locked, so no-delete-locked is satisfied too. The answer is Deny, and only the forbid is listed.

If at least one forbid policy is satisfied by a request, the decision is Deny. This holds no matter how many permit policies are also satisfied.

In simple words: one "never" beats any number of "yes". In our table, three requests have a satisfied permit and a satisfied forbid at the same time. All three are Deny.

Why is this always true?

The authorizer checks forbids first. If one is satisfied, it returns Deny and never looks at the permits. Adding a permit cannot change the forbid's result, because each policy is evaluated on its own. So no permit can undo a satisfied forbid.

Rule 2: a policy with an error is skipped

Look at "bob view notes". Bob is a contractor, so the first condition of contractors-no-secrets is true. Then Cedar reads resource.secret. The notes have no such attribute. Here is the real output:

decision: Allow
reason:   eng-views-projects
error:    error while evaluating policy `contractors-no-secrets`: `Doc::"notes"` does not have the attribute `secret`

If evaluating a policy causes an error, that policy is treated as not satisfied. It does not affect the decision. The authorizer reports the policy id and the error in the diagnostics.

In simple words: a broken rule is ignored, but reported. One bad policy cannot crash all decisions. Here three of the 18 requests had this error. Their decisions came from the other three policies.

Why does Cedar work this way?

The alternative would be "any error makes the whole answer Deny". Then one typo in one policy could lock every user out of everything. Cedar keeps each policy independent. It gives you the errors, so your app can log them, raise an alarm, or choose to deny.

An error in a forbid is dangerous. The guardrail is skipped, so a permit can win. That is exactly why bob got Allow. Fix it in one of two ways. Guard the attribute: when { resource has secret && resource.secret }. Or make the rule fail closed: unless { resource has secret && resource.secret == false }. With the second version, bob gets Deny for both docs. A schema plus the validator catches this whole class of error before deploy.

Rule 3: order does not matter

We reversed the four policies in the file and asked all 18 questions again. The decisions, the reason sets and the errors were the same for every request.

The decision and the set of deciding policies depend only on which policies are in the policy set. They do not depend on the order of the policies in a file or in the set.

In simple words: a policy set is a bag, not a list. There is no "first match wins" like in a firewall. You can split policies across many files and load them in any order.

Why is this always true?

Each policy is evaluated alone, using only the request and the entities. The final step asks two yes or no questions: is any forbid satisfied, and is any permit satisfied? "Any" does not care about order. So the result cannot change when you shuffle the policies.

Expect a question like: "The first policy permits, the second forbids. What happens?" The answer is Deny if the forbid is satisfied, whatever the order. Position never matters in Cedar.

Reading the diagnostics

Every answer has three parts. Learn to read all three.

  1. Decision: Allow or Deny.
  2. Reasons: the deciding policies. For Allow, the satisfied permits. For Deny by forbid, the satisfied forbids. For default deny, nothing.
  3. Errors: each policy that failed, with its id and a message.

Here is "bob delete notes" in three tools. Compare them.

decision: Deny
reason:   no-delete-locked
error:    error while evaluating policy `contractors-no-secrets`: `Doc::"notes"` does not have the attribute `secret`
decision: Deny
reason:   policy2 @id = no-delete-locked
error:    error while evaluating policy `policy3`: `Doc::"notes"` does not have the attribute `secret`
DENY

error while evaluating policy `contractors-no-secrets`: `Doc::"notes"` does not have the attribute `secret`

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  no-delete-locked
  • The Python bench shows the internal names policy2 and policy3. They count from 0 in file order. Use the @id map to get readable names.
  • The CLI prints the decision in capitals and exits with code 2 for Deny.
  • The reasons are a set. Their order can change from run to run. Two runs of "alice view notes" printed them in different orders:
decision: Allow
reason:   eng-views-projects
reason:   owner-full-access
decision: Allow
reason:   owner-full-access
reason:   eng-views-projects

Log all three parts for every decision. "Deny, no reasons" means no permit matched: maybe a missing feature. "Deny, reason X" means a guardrail fired. "Errors not empty" means a bug in data or policies. They need different fixes. The chapter Running Cedar in production builds on this.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for policy syntax and decisions
PatternPurposeModelNote
permit (scope) when { ... };Allow a feature under a conditionpermit (principal is User, action == Action::"edit", resource is Doc) when { resource.owner == principal };All conditions must pass.
forbid (scope) when { ... };A guardrail that always winsforbid (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource is Doc) when { resource.locked };A forbid with an error is skipped.
principal in GroupMatch all members of a groupprincipal in Team::"eng"Also matches the group itself.
action in [A, B]Match a list of actionsaction in [Action::"view", Action::"comment"]Lists work only for action in the scope.
resource is T in EOne type inside a containerresource is Doc in Folder::"projects"is is not allowed for action.
unless { e }An exception to a ruleunless { principal.contractor }The policy needs e to be false.
has before readingAvoid errors on optional attributeswhen { resource has secret && resource.secret }Most important inside forbids.
unless { has && == false }A guardrail that fails closedunless { resource has secret && resource.secret == false }Missing data then means Deny.
@id("...") @key("...")Name and document a policy@id("no-delete-locked") @ticket("SEC-142")Before the effect, each name once.
// commentExplain a policy to people// Nobody may delete a locked doc.No /* */ block comments.
Decision + reasons + errorsDebug and auditDeny, no-delete-locked, one errorReasons are a set with no fixed order.

Practice

Which scope is valid Cedar?

  • permit (principal in [User::"a", User::"b"], action, resource);
  • permit (principal, action is Action, resource);
  • permit (principal is User, action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"], resource);

A list is allowed only for action. is is not allowed for action. The third scope follows both rules.

One permit and one forbid are both satisfied. The forbid comes first in the file. What is the decision?

  • Allow, because the permit comes last
  • Deny, with the forbid as the reason
  • Deny, with both policies as reasons

Forbid wins, and order does not matter. For a Deny by forbid, only the satisfied forbids are listed.

A request matches no policy, and one policy had an error. What does the response hold?

  • Deny, no reasons, one error
  • Deny, the failed policy as reason, one error
  • No decision at all, only the error

The failed policy is skipped and reported. Nothing is satisfied, so default deny gives Deny with an empty reason list.

To make a policy need a condition to be false, use ?.

is T in E means the same as is T together with in ?.

The only comment style in Cedar starts with two ?.

  1. Write a policy with the id reviewers-comment. Members of Group::"reviewers" may comment on any Doc in Folder::"drafts".
    Answer
    @id("reviewers-comment")
permit (
    principal in Group::"reviewers",
    action == Action::"comment",
    resource is Doc in Folder::"drafts"
);
  2. In the 18-request table, why is "bob view roadmap" Deny, although bob is in the eng team?
    Answer

    eng-views-projects is satisfied. But bob is a contractor and the roadmap is secret, so contractors-no-secrets is also satisfied. Forbid wins, so the answer is Deny with the forbid as the reason.

  3. Why is "bob view notes" Allow? Is that what the policy writer wanted?
    Answer

    The forbid failed on resource.secret, because the notes have no secret attribute. A failed policy is skipped, so the permit won. The writer probably wanted "contractors see only docs known to be not secret". Fix it with a fail-closed condition: unless { resource has secret && resource.secret == false }.

  4. A teammate wants "first match wins", like a firewall. They put a permit at the top to "override" a forbid below it. Explain what happens.
    Answer

    Nothing changes. Cedar has no first match. All policies are evaluated, and any satisfied forbid gives Deny. To make an exception, change the forbid itself, for example with an unless condition.

  5. Your log shows many "Deny, no reasons" for one action after a release. What do you check first?
    Answer

    No permit matched, and no forbid fired. Check that a permit exists for that action, that the action name in the request matches the policy, and that the entities still carry the needed parents and attributes. Also look at the errors list: a permit with an error is skipped too.

Key takeaways

  • A policy has an effect, a scope with principal, action and resource, optional conditions, optional annotations, and a semicolon.
  • The scope allows ==, in, is and is ... in. Lists are allowed only for action, and is is not allowed for action.
  • when needs true, unless needs false, and every condition must pass.
  • Default deny, forbid wins, a failed policy is skipped, and order does not matter.
  • An error inside a forbid can open access. Guard attributes with has, write fail-closed guardrails, and validate with a schema.
  • Read decision, reasons and errors together. Reasons are a set with no fixed order.

Sources

  • Basic policy construction in Cedar. Effect, scope forms, conditions and annotations.
  • Cedar authorization. Forbid overrides permit, default deny, and how errors are skipped and reported.
  • Cedar operators. The rules of in, is and has.
  • cedar-policy changelog. Trailing commas in 4.5.0 and the 4.14.1 release notes.
  • Diagnostics in cedar-policy 4.14.1. The reason set and the unordered error list.

Entities and hierarchy

Policies are rules. Entities are the facts the rules talk about: users, teams, folders, documents and actions. This chapter shows how to name entities, give them attributes, put them in a hierarchy, and send just enough of them with each request.

  • Write entity UIDs, with and without a namespace, and avoid the common naming mistakes.
  • Give entities attributes of every kind, in the correct JSON form.
  • Build groups, folders and organisations as hierarchies, and predict what in returns.
  • Use actions and action groups as entities.
  • Use entity tags with hasTag and getTag, and decide how much data to send with a request.

Entity types and entity UIDs

Every entity has a name made of two parts. Look at these expressions and the values that cedar evaluate printed for them:

User::"alice" == User::"Alice"   =>  false
User::"alice" == Admin::"alice"  =>  false
File::"/home/you/notes.txt"      =>  File::"/home/you/notes.txt"
Acme::Notes::User::"alice"       =>  Acme::Notes::User::"alice"

The part before the last :: is the entity type. The string in double quotes is the entity id. Together they form the entity UID.

  • Both parts are case sensitive. "alice" and "Alice" are two different users.
  • The type is part of the name. User::"alice" and Admin::"alice" are different entities.
  • The id can be any string: a path, a number as text, or a random key. Use \" for a quote inside it.
  • Type names can have a namespace in front: Acme::Notes::User.
  • The name __cedar is reserved. __cedar::User::"x" fails: "The name `__cedar::User` contains `__cedar`, which is reserved".

Do not use a changing value as an id, like an email address or a display name. People change emails. Then old policies point to a stranger, or to nobody. The Cedar docs recommend ids that are unique, never change and are never reused. Use a stable key such as a database UUID.

Attributes

Attributes are named facts about one entity. Here is a user with every common kind of attribute.

[
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" },
    "attrs": {
      "name": "Alice",
      "level": 5,
      "active": true,
      "skills": ["rust", "sql"],
      "address": { "city": "Ulaanbaatar", "zip": "14200" },
      "manager": { "__entity": { "type": "User", "id": "bob" } },
      "homeIp": { "__extn": { "fn": "ip", "arg": "10.0.0.7" } },
      "score": { "__extn": { "fn": "decimal", "arg": "0.75" } }
    },
    "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "bob" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] }
]

We asked cedar evaluate about these attributes, with alice as the principal. Every answer is real.

principal.skills.contains("rust")              =>  true
principal.address.city                         =>  "Ulaanbaatar"
principal.manager                              =>  User::"bob"
principal.homeIp.isInRange(ip("10.0.0.0/8"))   =>  true
principal.score.greaterThan(decimal("0.75"))   =>  false
principal has address.zip                      =>  true
How JSON values become Cedar values
JSONCedar typeExample
stringString"Alice"
integerLong5
true or falseBooltrue
arraySet["rust", "sql"]
objectRecord{ "city": "Ulaanbaatar" }
{ "__entity": {...} }entity referenceUser::"bob"
{ "__extn": {...} }extension valueip("10.0.0.7"), decimal("0.75")

The __entity and __extn forms are escapes. They tell Cedar "this object is not a record". The full list of value types and operators is in Expressions, types and operators.

Without a schema, "owner": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" } is a record, not an entity. We checked: resource.owner printed {"id": "alice", "type": "User"}. So resource.owner == principal was false, and the owner got a quiet Deny. With __entity, the same attribute printed User::"alice" and the owner got Allow. With a schema, Cedar knows the type, so the short form works too.

Parents and the in operator

Each entity can list its parents. Parents build a hierarchy. Here is a small company.

[
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" }, "attrs": { "name": "Alice", "level": 5 },
    "parents": [ { "type": "Team", "id": "platform" }, { "type": "Team", "id": "oncall" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "bob" }, "attrs": { "name": "Bob", "level": 3 },
    "parents": [ { "type": "Team", "id": "sales" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Team", "id": "platform" }, "attrs": {},
    "parents": [ { "type": "Dept", "id": "engineering" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Team", "id": "oncall" }, "attrs": {},
    "parents": [ { "type": "Dept", "id": "engineering" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Team", "id": "sales" }, "attrs": {},
    "parents": [ { "type": "Org", "id": "acme" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Dept", "id": "engineering" }, "attrs": {},
    "parents": [ { "type": "Org", "id": "acme" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Org", "id": "acme" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] }
]

Now ask questions with in. The principal is alice.

Real answers from cedar evaluate, principal = User::"alice"
ExpressionResultWhy
principal in Team::"platform"truea direct parent
principal in Dept::"engineering"truetwo steps up
principal in Org::"acme"truethree steps up
principal in User::"alice"trueevery entity is in itself
principal in Team::"sales"falseno path from alice to sales
Org::"acme" in User::"alice"falsein only goes up
principal in [Team::"sales", Team::"oncall"]truein any entity of the set
User::"zed" in Org::"acme"falsezed is not in the entities

Start at the principal

Is User::"alice" the same as Org::"acme"? No. Look at her parents.

One step up

Her parents are Team::"platform" and Team::"oncall". Neither is acme. Look at their parents.

Two steps up

Both teams have the parent Dept::"engineering". Not acme yet. Look at its parent.

Three steps up

The parent of engineering is Org::"acme". Found. The answer is true.

What really happens

Cedar does not walk at request time. When it loads the entities, it computes all ancestors once. Then in is a fast lookup.

For entities A, B and C: A in A is always true (reflexive). If A in B and B in C, then A in C (transitive). With a set on the right, A in S is true when A is in at least one entity of S.

In simple words: in means "is this entity, or is somewhere below it". Example with numbers: alice is 1 step below platform, platform is 1 step below engineering, and engineering is 1 step below acme. So alice is 3 steps below acme, and principal in Org::"acme" is true.

Why is this always true?

When Cedar loads entities, it computes the transitive closure of the parent links. Every entity then stores the full set of its ancestors. A in B checks "A equals B, or B is among A's ancestors". The first part gives reflexivity. The closure gives transitivity, because an ancestor of an ancestor is in the same stored set.

Two edge cases, both tested:

  • A cycle is an error. If team a lists b as parent and b lists a, loading fails: "input graph has a cycle containing vertex `Team::"b"`".
  • A missing middle entity breaks the chain. Alice lists Team::"platform", but platform is not in the file. Then principal in Team::"platform" is still true, because the link is on alice. But principal in Org::"acme" is false. Cedar cannot know platform's parents.

Groups, folders and organisations

The same parent links model many real things. Here are folders. Bob is in sales, and sales is in acme.

// Everyone in acme may view anything under the shared folder.
@id("acme-views-shared")
permit (
    principal in Org::"acme",
    action == Action::"view",
    resource in Folder::"shared"
);
-- bob view roadmap
decision: Allow
reason:   acme-views-shared
-- bob view salaries
decision: Deny
-- bob view folder q3
decision: Allow
reason:   acme-views-shared

One policy covers every document in every subfolder. This is inheritance. Move the roadmap to another folder, and its access changes with it. You change data, not policies.

  • Groups: User in Group. Groups can be in groups.
  • Folders: Doc in Folder, folder in folder.
  • Organisations: user in team, team in department, department in company.
  • Tenants: every resource in its Tenant. This is a base for tenant isolation, shown in Modeling RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC.

An entity can have many parents. Alice is in platform and in oncall. The hierarchy is a graph without cycles, not a strict tree. Use this for "a doc in a folder and also in a project".

Actions are entities too

Actions have the type Action. Like other entities, they can have parents. A parent action is an action group.

// Readers may do every action in the "read" group.
@id("readers-read")
permit (
    principal in Group::"readers",
    action in Action::"read",
    resource
);

Without a schema, you put the action hierarchy in the entities file:

[
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "bob" }, "attrs": {},
    "parents": [ { "type": "Group", "id": "readers" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Group", "id": "readers" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Action", "id": "view" }, "attrs": {},
    "parents": [ { "type": "Action", "id": "read" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Action", "id": "download" }, "attrs": {},
    "parents": [ { "type": "Action", "id": "read" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Action", "id": "edit" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Action", "id": "read" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] }
]
Real decisions for bob on Doc::"handbook"
ActionEntities file has actionsEntities file without actionsWithout actions, with schema
viewAllow: readers-readDenyAllow: readers-read
downloadAllow: readers-readDenyAllow: readers-read
editDenyDenyDeny

With a schema, you declare the groups there, and Cedar adds the action entities for you:

entity Group;
entity User in [Group];
entity Doc;
action read;
action view, download in [read] appliesTo {
    principal: User,
    resource: Doc,
};
action edit appliesTo {
    principal: User,
    resource: Doc,
};
  • The schema cannot give actions attributes. Actions only have a UID and parents.
  • If the entities file also lists an action, it must match the schema exactly. Otherwise: "definition of action `Action::"view"` does not match its schema declaration". The help text says to simply leave it out.
  • In this schema, read is only a group. Asking with Action::"read" fails request validation: "no principal types are valid for `Action::"read"`".

Without a schema, forgetting the action entities gives a quiet Deny. action in Action::"read" cannot be true if Cedar never learned that view is in read. Use a schema, so the action hierarchy lives in one place.

The entities JSON format in full

Here is every part of one entity in one place.

[
  {
    "uid": { "type": "Doc", "id": "launch-plan" },
    "attrs": {
      "title": "Launch plan",
      "owner": { "__entity": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" } },
      "reviewers": [ { "__entity": { "type": "User", "id": "bob" } } ],
      "limits": { "maxCopies": 3 },
      "officeNet": { "__extn": { "fn": "ip", "arg": "10.0.0.0/8" } }
    },
    "parents": [ { "type": "Folder", "id": "q3" } ],
    "tags": { "project": "apollo", "stage": "draft" }
  }
]
Fields of one entity (cedar-policy 4.14.1)
FieldRequired?Content
uidyes{ "type": ..., "id": ... }. The form { "__entity": { ... } } also works.
attrsyes, can be {}attribute names and values
parentsyes, can be []a list of entity references
tagsnotag names and values, same format as attrs
  • The whole file is one JSON array.
  • Write each entity once. Two different entries for one UID fail: "duplicate entity entry `User::"alice"`".
  • The context of a request uses the same value format as attrs. See Context and requests.
  • With a schema, Cedar also checks types. A wrong type fails with "entity does not conform to the schema".

With a schema you may drop the escapes. "owner": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" } and "officeNet": "10.0.0.0/8" then work, because the schema says which type each attribute has. The Cedar docs call this schema-based parsing.

Namespaces

Two apps may both have a User type. A namespace keeps them apart. Here everything lives in the namespace Notes.

namespace Notes {
    entity User;
    entity Doc {
        owner: User,
    };
    action edit appliesTo {
        principal: User,
        resource: Doc,
    };
}
@id("owner-edits")
permit (
    principal is Notes::User,
    action == Notes::Action::"edit",
    resource is Notes::Doc
)
when { resource.owner == principal };
[
  { "uid": { "type": "Notes::User", "id": "alice" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Notes::Doc", "id": "todo" },
    "attrs": { "owner": { "type": "Notes::User", "id": "alice" } },
    "parents": [] }
]

Inside the schema, names are short: User. Everywhere else, write the full name: Notes::User::"alice". Actions get the namespace too: Notes::Action::"edit". With the schema, alice editing Notes::Doc::"todo" gives Allow, reason owner-edits.

Forget the namespace on the action, and the validator helps you:

  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ for policy `x`, unrecognized action `Action::"edit"`
   ╭─[2:30]
 1 │ @id("x")
 2 │ permit (principal, action == Action::"edit", resource);
   ·                              ──────────────
   ╰────
  help: did you mean `Notes::Action::"edit"`?

Run this example without the schema, and alice gets Deny. The owner attribute uses the short form without __entity, so it becomes a record again. Schema and entities file must be used together.

Entity tags

Attributes have fixed names that the schema lists. Sometimes the names are not known in advance: a project label, a cost center, a customer key. Entity tags solve this. Tags arrived as an experiment in cedar-policy 4.1.0 and became stable in 4.2.0 (October 2024).

entity User {
    projects: Set<String>,
};
entity Doc tags String;
action view appliesTo {
    principal: User,
    resource: Doc,
};
// You may view a doc if you work on the project in its "project" tag.
@id("project-members-view")
permit (principal is User, action == Action::"view", resource is Doc)
when {
    resource.hasTag("project") &&
    principal.projects.contains(resource.getTag("project"))
};
[
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" },
    "attrs": { "projects": ["apollo", "gemini"] }, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Doc", "id": "launch-plan" },
    "attrs": {}, "parents": [],
    "tags": { "project": "apollo", "stage": "draft" } },
  { "uid": { "type": "Doc", "id": "lunch-menu" },
    "attrs": {}, "parents": [] }
]

Alice may view launch-plan: Allow, reason project-members-view. The lunch-menu has no tags: Deny. Here are the tag operators, tested on launch-plan:

resource.getTag("stage")   =>  "draft"
resource.hasTag("owner")   =>  false
resource has stage         =>  false
resource.getTag("owner")   =>  error: `Doc::"launch-plan"` does not have the tag `owner`
                               help: available tags: [project,stage]
  • e.hasTag(k) tests whether the tag exists. e.getTag(k) reads it.
  • The tag name can be any String expression, not only a fixed word.
  • has sees only attributes, never tags.
  • All tags of one entity type share one value type. In the schema above it is String. A Long tag value fails: "type mismatch: value was expected to have type string".
  • The validator demands hasTag before getTag. Without it: "unable to guarantee safety of access to tag `"project"` on entity type `Doc`".

Use attributes for fields every entity of a type has. Use tags for open-ended labels. A schema can declare both: entity Doc { title: String } tags String;.

How much data to send

Cedar knows only what you give it. It never calls your database. So each request needs the entities that the policies will read. Watch what happens when one parent link is missing.

@id("staff-view-wiki")
permit (principal in Org::"acme", action == Action::"view", resource in Folder::"wiki");

@id("blocked-users-nothing")
forbid (principal in Group::"blocked", action, resource);

Dave's entity lists both parents: Org::"acme" and Group::"blocked".

DENY

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  blocked-users-nothing

The app sent dave with only Org::"acme". The blocked group was left out.

ALLOW

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  staff-view-wiki

A blocked user got in, with no error at all. Missing data does not only cause wrong denials. With forbids, it can cause wrong Allows.

A safe rule of thumb: send the entity slice for the request.

  1. The principal and the resource, with all their attributes.
  2. Their parents, and the parents of those parents, up to the top.
  3. Every entity that the policies reach through an attribute, like resource.owner, with the attributes the policies read.
  4. The action hierarchy, unless a schema provides it.

Sending the whole database works for tests, but it is slow in production. Sending too little is a security bug. Cedar has an experimental feature called an entity manifest (RFC 74). It works out which data the policies need. It sits behind the entity-manifest flag, so check its status before you rely on it. The chapter Architecture: where authorization lives shows how to load entities fast.

A favourite question: "The forbid did not fire. Why?" Check the entities first. A missing parent, a missing attribute, or an entity reference written as a record each make the forbid silently miss.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for entities and hierarchies
PatternPurposeModelNote
Type::"id"Name one entityUser::"alice"Case sensitive. Use stable ids, not emails.
Namespace::Type::"id"Keep apps apartNotes::User::"alice"Actions need it too: Notes::Action::"edit".
{ "__entity": {type, id} }An entity reference in attrs"owner": { "__entity": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" } }Without a schema, the short form is a record.
{ "__extn": {fn, arg} }An extension value in attrs{ "__extn": { "fn": "ip", "arg": "10.0.0.7" } }The schema lets you drop the escape.
principal in GroupRights through membershipprincipal in Team::"eng"Works across many levels.
resource in FolderInheritance from containersresource in Folder::"shared"Every level must be in the entities.
A in AReflexive inprincipal in User::"alice" is true for alicein means "is or is below".
action in Action::"group"Rights for a set of actionsaction in Action::"read"Declare groups in the schema.
hasTag then getTagRead open-ended labelsresource.hasTag("project") && resource.getTag("project") == "apollo"getTag on a missing tag is an error.
Send the sliceGive Cedar what it needsprincipal, resource, ancestors, referenced entitiesMissing data can turn a forbid off.

Practice

Alice is in Team::"platform", which is in Dept::"engineering". What is User::"alice" in Dept::"engineering"?

  • true
  • false, because engineering is not a direct parent
  • an error, because the types are different

The in operator is transitive. It follows parents up any number of levels.

Without a schema, an attribute is "owner": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" }. What is resource.owner == User::"alice"?

  • true
  • false, because the value is a record
  • an error

Without a schema and without __entity, the object is a record with the keys id and type. A record is never equal to an entity.

Which entity file entry is wrong for Cedar 4.14.1?

  • { "uid": { "type": "Team", "id": "eng" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] }
  • { "uid": { "type": "Team", "id": "eng" }, "parents": [] }
  • { "uid": { "type": "Team", "id": "eng" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [], "tags": {} }

The field attrs is required, even when it is empty. Tags are optional.

principal in User::"alice" is true for alice, because in is ?.

To read a tag safely, call ? before getTag.

A parent link that points back to its own child makes a ?, and loading fails.

  1. Write the entities for: carol is in Group::"auditors", and auditors is in Group::"staff".
    Answer
    [
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "carol" }, "attrs": {},
    "parents": [ { "type": "Group", "id": "auditors" } ] },
