  { "uid": { "type": "Group", "id": "auditors" }, "attrs": {},
    "parents": [ { "type": "Group", "id": "staff" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Group", "id": "staff" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] }
]
  2. The app sends the roadmap with its parent Folder::"q3", but not the q3 entity. Does resource in Folder::"shared" still work?
    Answer

    No. resource in Folder::"q3" is true, because the link is on the roadmap. But Cedar does not know that q3 is in projects and projects is in shared. So resource in Folder::"shared" is false, and the result is a quiet Deny.

  3. Why is it dangerous to send only the principal's direct parents, when the policies contain forbid (principal in Group::"blocked", ...)?
    Answer

    The blocked group may be two levels up. If the app leaves out the middle group, the forbid cannot match, and a permit may give Allow. Always send all ancestors of the principal and the resource.

  4. Your schema is in namespace Shop { ... } with an action buy. Write the action UID for a request.
    Answer

    Shop::Action::"buy". In JSON: "action": "Shop::Action::\"buy\"".

  5. Customers can add any number of labels to an invoice, like region or costCenter. Attributes or tags? Write a condition that checks costCenter is "cc-42".
    Answer

    Tags, because the names are chosen at run time. Declare entity Invoice tags String; and write when { resource.hasTag("costCenter") && resource.getTag("costCenter") == "cc-42" }.

Key takeaways

  • An entity UID is a type plus an id, like User::"alice". Both parts are case sensitive.
  • Attributes hold facts. Use __entity and __extn escapes unless a schema tells Cedar the types.
  • Parents build a hierarchy without cycles. in is reflexive and transitive.
  • Actions are entities. Action groups give rights to many actions at once. Declare them in the schema.
  • Entity tags, stable since 4.2.0, hold labels whose names are not known in advance. Use hasTag before getTag.
  • Send every entity the policies may read. Missing data can turn a forbid off without any error.

Sources

  • Entities and context syntax. The JSON format: uid, attrs, parents, tags and the escapes.
  • Cedar terms and concepts. Entities, hierarchies, action groups and namespaces.
  • Cedar operators. The rules of in, hasTag and getTag.
  • Entities in policies. How entity UIDs are written in Cedar.
  • cedar-policy changelog. Entity tags in 4.1.0 and 4.2.0, the entity manifest flag.

Expressions, types and operators

The when and unless parts of a policy hold expressions. An expression is a small formula that gives one value. This chapter shows every kind of value in Cedar and every operator. It also shows the errors you can meet and why Cedar has no loops.

  • Name the six basic types and the four extension types of Cedar.
  • Read a long condition with the right operator precedence.
  • Use has, like and the set methods without run-time errors.
  • Work with IP addresses, decimals, dates and durations.
  • Explain why every Cedar evaluation finishes, and what that costs you.

Values and their types

We start with a tiny tool. The Cedar CLI can evaluate one expression. It needs a request, so we give it a dummy one. Save this as a script called ev.

#!/bin/sh
# ev: evaluate one Cedar expression with a dummy request
exec cedar evaluate -l 'User::"alice"' -a 'Action::"view"' -r 'Doc::"d1"' -- "$1"

Now we ask Cedar for some values. All output below is real. It comes from cedar-policy-cli 4.14.1, the newest release on 2026-11-15.

$ ev 'true'
true
$ ev '-9223372036854775808'
-9223372036854775808
$ ev '[1, "two", true]'
[true, 1, "two"]
$ ev '{name: "Ann", age: 31}'
{"age": 31, "name": "Ann"}
$ ev 'User::"alice" == User::"alice"'
true
$ ev 'decimal("1.23")'
decimal("1.23")

Every value has a type. Cedar has six basic types and four extension types.

The value types of Cedar
TypeExampleNote
Booltrue, falseConditions must give a Bool.
Long42, -7A whole number with 64 bits. No fractions.
String"report.pdf"Text in double quotes.
Set[1, 2, 3]No order and no duplicates.
Record{name: "Ann"}Named fields. The context is a record.
EntityUser::"alice"A reference to a principal, action or resource.
decimaldecimal("1.23")Up to four digits after the point.
ipaddrip("10.0.0.0/8")One IPv4 or IPv6 address, or a range.
datetimedatetime("2026-11-15T09:30:00Z")A moment in time, in milliseconds.
durationduration("1h30m")A length of time, in milliseconds.

Cedar is strongly typed. It never turns a string into a number for you. A Long is never equal to a String. Look at the set above. Cedar printed it in its own order, because a set has no order.

$ ev '1 == "1"'
false
$ ev '"5" + 1'
  × failed to evaluate the expression
  ╰─▶ type error: expected long, got string
   ╭────
 1 │ "5" + 1
   · ───
   ╰────

Your app sends "level": "4" as text. The policy says principal.level >= 3. Cedar does not convert the text. The comparison fails with a type error. Send numbers as JSON numbers, not as strings.

Operators and precedence

Here are three small sums. The answers show which operator Cedar does first.

$ ev '1 + 2 * 3'
7
$ ev '(1 + 2) * 3'
9
$ ev 'false && true || true'
true

Precedence decides the order. A higher level binds tighter. The table goes from the lowest level to the highest. It comes from the official grammar.

Operator precedence, lowest first
LevelOperatorsNote
1if ... then ... elseThe whole rest of the line belongs to else.
2||Or. Stops early when the left side is true.
3&&And. Stops early when the left side is false.
4== != < <= > >= in has like isOnly one per level. No chains.
5+ and -Long only. Left to right.
6*Long only. There is no division.
7! and unary -At most four in a row.
8.attr, ["attr"], .method()Member access binds tightest.

So Cedar reads a long condition like a tree. The lowest operator sits at the top.

Cedar refuses some shapes that other languages accept. These are parse errors.

$ ev '1 < 2 < 3'
  × failed to parse the expression
  ╰─▶ multiple relational operators (>, ==, in, etc.) must be used with
      parentheses to make ordering explicit
$ ev '5 / 2'
  × failed to parse the expression
  ╰─▶ division is not supported
$ ev '!!!!!true'
  × failed to parse the expression
  ╰─▶ too many occurrences of `!`
  help: cannot chain more the 4 applications of a unary operator

You write !context has mfa. The ! binds tighter than has, so Cedar reads (!context) has mfa. The result is type error: expected bool, got record. Write !(context has mfa) with brackets.

Attributes, has and safe access

This policy uses four kinds of values at once. Save it with the files below and run it.

// Staff can view a report inside the office network, in work hours.
@id("staff-view-office-hours")
permit (
  principal is User,
  action == Action::"view",
  resource is Report
)
when {
  principal.level >= 3 &&
  resource.labels.containsAny(["public", "internal"]) &&
  context.ip.isInRange(ip("10.0.0.0/8")) &&
  context.now.toTime() >= duration("9h") &&
  context.now.toTime() < duration("18h")
};
[
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" },
    "attrs": { "level": 4 }, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Report", "id": "q3" },
    "attrs": { "labels": ["internal", "finance"] }, "parents": [] }
]
{
  "ip":  { "__extn": { "fn": "ip", "arg": "10.20.30.40" } },
  "now": { "__extn": { "fn": "datetime", "arg": "2026-11-16T10:15:00Z" } }
}

Without a schema, an extension value in JSON needs the __extn wrapper. With a schema, a plain string is enough. The chapter Schemas and validation shows that.

$ cedar authorize -v --policies policy.cedar --entities entities.json \
    -l 'User::"alice"' -a 'Action::"view"' -r 'Report::"q3"' --context context.json
ALLOW

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  staff-view-office-hours

Now we remove the ip key from the context and run it again.

DENY

error while evaluating policy `staff-view-office-hours`: record does not have the attribute `ip`

note: no policies applied to this request

You read an attribute with a dot: principal.level. You can also use brackets: context["user-agent"]. Brackets work for any name, even with a dash. If the attribute is missing, evaluation stops with an error. The policy is then skipped.

The has operator asks first. It returns true or false and never fails on a missing name. Because && stops early, a has on the left protects the access on the right. Since Cedar 4.3.0 you can test a whole path at once.

$ ev '{device: {os: "ios"}} has device.os'
true
$ ev '{device: {}} has device.os'
false
$ ev 'context has ip && context.ip == 1'
false
$ ev 'context.ip == 1 && context has ip'
  × failed to evaluate the expression
  ╰─▶ record does not have the attribute `ip`

The has check is on the wrong side. Cedar reads && from left to right. The access on the left fails before has runs. Always put has first: context has ip && context.ip....

A missing attribute is not the only error. A missing entity is one too. principal.level fails with entity `User::"alice"` does not exist when the entities file has no alice. The chapter Entities and hierarchy shows what to load.

Strings, like and sets

Strings have one special operator: like. It compares a string with a pattern.

$ ev '"report.pdf" like "*.pdf"'
true
$ ev '"Report.PDF" like "*.pdf"'
false
$ ev '"report.pdf" like "report"'
false
$ ev '"a*b" like "a\*b"'
true
$ ev '"axb" like "a\*b"'
false

The star * is a wildcard. It matches zero or more characters. The pattern must match the whole string. Upper case and lower case are different. Write \* for a real star. There is no regex, no ? and no character class. The pattern must be a literal string, so you cannot build it at run time.

Strings have no other methods. There is no length, no concatenation and no <. A common surprise is contains:

$ ev '"alice@corp".contains("@")'
  × failed to evaluate the expression
  ╰─▶ type error: expected set, got string

Use like "*@*" instead. Now the sets. A set has four methods, and the in operator also accepts a set of entities.

Set operations
ExpressionResultMeaning
[1, 2, 3].contains(2)trueOne element is in the set.
[1, 2, 3].containsAll([1, 3])trueEvery element of the argument is in the set.
[1, 2, 3].containsAny([7, 3])trueAt least one element is shared.
[].isEmpty()trueThe set has no elements. Added in 4.3.0.
[1, 2] == [2, 1]trueOrder does not count.
[1, 1, 2] == [2, 1]trueDuplicates do not count.
User::"alice" in [Team::"a", User::"alice"]trueIn any of these entities.
[1, 2].containsAll([])trueThe empty set is inside every set.
[].containsAny([])falseNothing is shared.

You swap the two sides of containsAll. The rule "the user has all required roles" is principal.roles.containsAll(resource.requiredRoles). The other way round asks "the resource needs every role the user has". That is a very different rule. Say the sentence aloud before you write it.

Equality works on every type. Two records are equal when all fields are equal. Two entity references are equal when type and id are equal. The in and is operators work on entities and follow the hierarchy. The chapter Entities and hierarchy covers them in depth.

Extension types: decimal, ipaddr, datetime, duration

Each extension type has a constructor that takes a string. Each also has its own methods. The four slides show one type each.

decimal: money and scores

$ ev 'decimal("1.23").lessThan(decimal("1.5"))'
true
$ ev 'decimal("1.50") == decimal("1.5")'
true

At most four digits after the point. Compare with lessThan, lessThanOrEqual, greaterThan and greaterThanOrEqual. There is no arithmetic.

ipaddr: networks

$ ev 'ip("10.0.0.7").isInRange(ip("10.0.0.0/24"))'
true
$ ev 'ip("127.0.0.1").isLoopback()'
true

Methods: isIpv4, isIpv6, isLoopback, isMulticast, isInRange. A range can be inside a bigger range.

datetime: moments

$ ev 'datetime("2026-11-15T09:30:00+0900") < datetime("2026-11-15T09:30:00Z")'
true
$ ev 'datetime("2026-11-15T09:30:00Z").toTime() >= duration("9h")'
true

Formats: YYYY-MM-DD, with THH:MM:SS and Z, .SSSZ or an offset like +0700. Methods: offset, durationSince, toDate, toTime.

duration: lengths of time

$ ev 'duration("1h30m") == duration("90m")'
true
$ ev 'duration("1d2h30m").toMinutes()'
1590

Units: d, h, m, s, ms, with an optional leading minus. Methods: toMilliseconds, toSeconds, toMinutes, toHours, toDays.

The plain comparison operators < <= > >= work on three types only: Long, datetime and duration. Decimal needs its methods.

$ ev 'decimal("1.5") < decimal("2.0")'
  × failed to evaluate the expression
  ╰─▶ type error: expected one of [long, datetime, duration], got decimal
  help: Only types long, datetime, duration support comparison

datetime and duration came in 4.3.0 as an experiment. They became a normal, default feature in 4.4.0 (April 2025). The official docs also show isInRange with several ranges at once. That form is still behind an experimental flag in the released 4.14.1. The changelog lists it as stable for 4.15.0, which is not out yet. With 4.14.1 you get wrong number of arguments provided to extension function `isInRange`: expected 2, got 3. Join two calls with || for now.

A bad constructor string is an evaluation error. The help text tells you the right shape.

$ ev 'ip("10.0.0.300")'
  × failed to evaluate the expression
  ╰─▶ error while evaluating `ipaddr` extension function: invalid IP address:
      10.0.0.300
  help: valid IP strings are IPv4/IPv6 addresses or CIDR ranges like
        `127.0.0.1`, `127.0.0.1/24`, or `ffee::/64`
$ ev 'duration("1.5h")'
  × failed to evaluate the expression
  ╰─▶ error while evaluating `duration` extension function: invalid duration
      pattern
  help: valid duration strings are concatenated quantity-unit pairs with an
        optional leading `-`, for example `1d2h3m4s50ms`

A datetime is a point on one global clock. toTime() gives the time since midnight in UTC. We sent "2026-11-16T10:15:00+0900", which is 10:15 in Tokyo. The policy above gave DENY, because it is 01:15 in UTC. Check work hours in UTC, or send the local hour as a Long in the context.

if-then-else and evaluation errors

Cedar has one way to choose between two values. It is if ... then ... else, and it always needs the else part.

$ ev 'if 3 > 2 then "big" else "small"'
"big"
$ ev 'if false then 9223372036854775807 + 1 else 0'
0
$ ev '1 + (if true then 2 else 3)'
3
$ ev 'if 1 then 2 else 3'
  × failed to evaluate the expression
  ╰─▶ type error: expected bool, got long

Cedar only runs the branch it picks. The unused branch can hold an error, and nothing happens. Without brackets, 1 + if ... is a parse error, because if has the lowest precedence.

Now the full list of run-time errors. An evaluation error stops the policy. The authorizer then skips that policy. The chapter Policy syntax states this rule as a guarantee.

Evaluation errors with real messages from 4.14.1
KindExampleMessage
Type error"a" + "b"type error: expected long, got string
Missing attribute{name: "Ann"}.agerecord does not have the attribute `age`
Missing entityUser::"alice".levelentity `User::"alice"` does not exist
Overflow9223372036854775807 + 1integer overflow while attempting to add the values ...
Bad extension stringdecimal("1.23456")too many digits after the decimal in `1.23456`
Decimal out of rangedecimal("922337203685477.5808")overflow when converting to decimal

A Long goes from -2^{63} to 2^{63}-1. Addition, subtraction and multiplication check for overflow. They never wrap around like C does. They give an error.

An error in a permit gives no access. That fails safe. An error in a forbid removes the guardrail. That fails open. The chapter Context and requests shows a real case and the fix with has.

The validator finds type errors, missing attributes and unguarded optional fields before you deploy. It cannot find overflow. The chapter Schemas and validation explains what it can and cannot prove.

What Cedar leaves out, and why

Look at what you cannot write in Cedar:

  • No loops. There is no for and no while.
  • No functions of your own, and no recursion.
  • No variables. You cannot store a value and change it.
  • No division, no regex, and no string building.
  • No input or output. A policy cannot read a file or call a server.
  • No null. A value is there or it is not, and has tells you which.

This looks poor next to a real programming language. It is a choice. A small language gives three gifts: every evaluation ends, it ends fast, and tools can reason about it.

Take any policy, any request and any finite set of entities. Evaluating the policy ends after a finite number of steps. It ends with a value or with an error. It never runs forever, and it has no side effects.

Proof idea

Use induction on the size of the expression. A literal or a variable takes one step. Every operator first evaluates its parts, which are smaller expressions. Each part runs at most once, because there are no loops and no calls back to the policy.

The parts with data are finite too. A set has a finite number of elements, so containsAll ends. The entity hierarchy is finite and has no cycles. Cedar rejects a cycle when it loads the entities: input graph has a cycle containing vertex `Group::"a"`. So in looks up a finite list of ancestors. Each extension constructor reads one finite string. So every step ends, and there are finitely many steps.

The Cedar team also checks this by machine. Their Lean model of the evaluator must pass Lean's termination checker. Their paper lists termination as one of the proved properties.

In simple words: you can never write a policy that hangs your server. The cost of one check grows with the size of the policy and the size of its sets. It does not depend on luck. The security page of the Cedar docs says the same: bounded policies always end and have no effects.

  1. The policy says principal.roles.containsAny(resource.allowedRoles).
  2. The user has 5 roles. The resource allows 4 roles.
  3. At most 5 \times 4 = 20 comparisons are needed, even with a naive method.
  4. With 1000 roles on each side, it is still finite. But it is slower, so keep sets small.

Need a loop, a lookup or a calculation? Do it in your app before the request. Put the result in the context or in an entity attribute. For example, compute riskScore in your app and let the policy compare it.

Questions often ask why Cedar has no loops. Give three answers: guaranteed termination, predictable speed, and analysis by an SMT solver. The chapter Analysis and formal verification shows the third one.

Patterns to remember

Expression patterns
PatternPurposeModelNote
x has a && x.a ...Read an attribute that may be missingcontext has mfa && context.mfaThe has part must come first.
x has a.b.cTest a nested path in one stepcontext has device.osNeeds Cedar 4.3.0 or newer.
s like "pat*"Match a string shaperesource.name like "*.pdf"Case matters. Only * is a wildcard.
set.contains(v)One value is in a setresource.viewers.contains(principal)Works on sets only, never on strings.
a.containsAll(b)Every item of b is in aprincipal.roles.containsAll(resource.required)Read it as "a has all of b".
a.containsAny(b)At least one shared itemresource.labels.containsAny(["public"])Empty b always gives false.
ip.isInRange(ip("..."))Check a networkcontext.ip.isInRange(ip("10.0.0.0/8"))One range per call in 4.14.1.
d.lessThan(decimal("..."))Compare decimalscontext.score.lessThan(decimal("0.7"))< does not work on decimal.
t.toTime() >= duration("9h")Check the time of daycontext.now.toTime() < duration("18h")The clock is UTC.
if c then a else bChoose a valueif resource.public then 0 else 3Put brackets around it inside a sum.
!(x has a)Say "the field is missing"!(principal has suspended)Without brackets ! grabs x.

Practice

What does ev '2 + 3 * 4' print?

  • 20
  • 14
  • a parse error

* has a higher level than +, so Cedar computes 3 * 4 first.

Which expression is a parse error?

  • (1 < 2) == true
  • 1 < 2 == true
  • !(1 < 2)

Two relation operators on one level need brackets.

Which comparison works on decimals?

  • decimal("1.5") < decimal("2.0")
  • decimal("1.5").lessThan(decimal("2.0"))
  • decimal("1.5") - decimal("2.0") < 0

Plain < works on Long, datetime and duration only. Decimal has no arithmetic.

"Report.PDF" like "*.pdf" gives ?.

[1, 1, 2] == [2, 1] gives ?.

duration("2h").toMinutes() gives ?.

  1. Fix this condition so it never errors: context.device.os == "ios" && context has device.
    Answer

    context has device.os && context.device.os == "ios". The check comes first, and the path form also tests os.

  2. Write a condition: the resource name ends in .csv and the user has the role "analyst" in the set principal.roles.
    Answer

    resource.name like "*.csv" && principal.roles.contains("analyst").

  3. A policy allows access from 10.0.0.0/8 or 192.168.0.0/16. Write it for Cedar 4.14.1.
    Answer

    context.ip.isInRange(ip("10.0.0.0/8")) || context.ip.isInRange(ip("192.168.0.0/16")). The form with two ranges in one call needs 4.15.0 or the experimental flag.

  4. Your forbid policy reads principal.risk * 1000000000000 > context.limit. What can go wrong?
    Answer

    A big risk value overflows. The multiplication gives an error, the forbid is skipped, and the guardrail is gone. The validator does not catch overflow. Compute the number in your app instead.

  5. Explain in two sentences why a Cedar policy can never run forever.
    Answer

    Cedar has no loops, no recursion and no functions of your own, so each part of an expression runs at most once. The data is finite too: sets are finite and the entity hierarchy has no cycles.

  6. Hard: work hours are 09:00 to 17:00 in Tokyo (UTC+9). Write the check with context.now.
    Answer

    Tokyo 09:00 to 17:00 is 00:00 to 08:00 in UTC. So write context.now.toTime() < duration("8h"). The lower bound 0h is always true. A simpler and clearer way is to send localHour as a Long and test context.localHour >= 9 && context.localHour < 17.

Key takeaways

  • Cedar has six basic types and four extension types: decimal, ipaddr, datetime, duration.
  • Values never change type by themselves. 1 == "1" is false.
  • Precedence goes from if (lowest) to member access (highest). Relations never chain.
  • Put has before every access to a field that may be missing.
  • like has one wildcard, *. Strings have no other methods.
  • An evaluation error skips the policy. In a forbid, that removes protection.
  • No loops and no user functions, so every evaluation ends.

Sources

  • Cedar docs: data types. Ranges of Long, decimal, datetime and duration.
  • Cedar docs: operators and functions. Every operator and method.
  • Cedar docs: grammar. The source of the precedence table.
  • cedar-policy changelog. Versions of isEmpty, datetime and variadic isInRange.
  • Cedar docs: security. Bounded policies terminate and have no effects.
  • How We Built Cedar: A Verification-Guided Approach. The Lean model and the termination property.

Context and requests

Every Cedar decision answers one request. The request names who, what action, which thing, and it carries a context. The context holds facts about this one moment: the time, the network, the sign-in. This chapter shows what to put in the context, who may fill it, and how to stay safe when a fact is missing.

  • Build a request with principal, action, resource and context.
  • Decide if a fact belongs in the context or in an entity.
  • Write policies for time, IP address, MFA, device, risk score and request source.
  • Protect guardrails from missing or wrong context keys.
  • Design one context shape per action and check many requests in one batch.

The four parts of a request

Here is a complete request as one JSON file. Alice wants to view a report.

{
  "principal": "User::\"alice\"",
  "action": "Action::\"view\"",
  "resource": "Document::\"q3-report\"",
  "context": {
    "sourceIp": "203.0.113.7",
    "channel": "web",
    "riskScore": "0.25"
  }
}
$ cedar authorize -v --schema schema.cedarschema --policies policies.cedar \
    --entities entities.json --request-json req-view.json
ALLOW

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  staff-view

A request has four parts. The principal, the action and the resource are entity references. They are only names, like User::"alice". The context is a record with any fields you define. The chapter What is authorization? calls this shape PARC.

The request does not carry Alice's attributes. Her level and her teams live in the entities file. The authorizer gets two inputs: the request and the entities. The chapter Entities and hierarchy explains how much entity data to send.

The output is real. All runs in this chapter use cedar-policy-cli and the cedar-policy crate, version 4.14.1. The Python runs use cedarpy 4.12.2, which wraps the Cedar engine 4.12.0.

Context or entity?

Some facts can go in either place. This table puts each fact where it belongs.

Where each fact belongs
FactPlaceWhy
Time of the requestContextIt is different for every request.
Source IP addressContextIt belongs to this connection.
MFA used, sign-in timeContextIt belongs to this session.
Device managed or notContextThe same user may switch devices.
Risk score of the sessionContextA risk service computes it now.
Request source (web, mobile, API)ContextIt describes this call.
User level, department, teamsEntityIt stays the same for weeks.
Document owner, classificationEntityIt belongs to the document.

Ask one question: "Is this fact about the request, or about a thing?" A fact about the request goes in the context. A fact about a user, a document or a team goes in an entity. An attribute on an entity is the same for every request that uses that entity.

Your app computes "isOwner": true and puts it in the context. Now the real rule lives in your app code, not in a policy. Nobody can see it, test it or analyse it. Send the facts (resource.owner) and let the policy compare them.

Six kinds of context, in real policies

We now build a small document app. The schema gives each action its own context. The view action gets network, source and risk. The delete action gets time, network and sign-in facts.

entity User { level: Long };
entity Document { owner: User, classification: String };

type Device = { managed: Bool, os: String };

action view appliesTo {
  principal: User,
  resource: Document,
  context: {
    sourceIp: ipaddr,
    channel: String,      // "web", "mobile" or "partner-api"
    riskScore: decimal,   // 0.0 = safe, 1.0 = very risky
    device?: Device,      // only when the device agent reports
  }
};

action delete appliesTo {
  principal: User,
  resource: Document,
  context: {
    now: datetime,
    sourceIp: ipaddr,
    mfa: Bool,
    authTime: datetime,   // when the user last signed in
    reason: String,
  }
};

Two permits give the features. Five forbids add the guardrails. One policy file holds all seven. We show it in two parts.

// Feature: staff can view documents.
@id("staff-view")
permit (principal, action == Action::"view", resource)
when { principal.level >= 1 };

// Feature: the owner can delete a document.
@id("owner-delete")
permit (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource)
when { resource.owner == principal };

// Guardrail: a risky session cannot view anything.
@id("block-high-risk")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"view", resource)
when { context.riskScore.greaterThan(decimal("0.8")) };

// Guardrail: secret documents only on managed devices.
@id("secret-needs-managed-device")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"view", resource)
when { resource.classification == "secret" }
unless { context has device && context.device.managed };
// Guardrail: the partner API sees public documents only.
@id("partner-api-public-only")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"view", resource)
when { context.channel == "partner-api" && resource.classification != "public" };

// Guardrail: delete needs MFA from the last 15 minutes.
@id("delete-needs-fresh-mfa")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource)
unless {
  context.mfa &&
  context.now.durationSince(context.authTime) <= duration("15m")
};

// Guardrail: delete only from the office network.
@id("delete-from-office")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource)
unless { context.sourceIp.isInRange(ip("10.0.0.0/8")) };
[
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" }, "attrs": { "level": 3 }, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Document", "id": "q3-report" },
    "attrs": { "owner": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" },
               "classification": "internal" }, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Document", "id": "merger-plan" },
    "attrs": { "owner": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" },
               "classification": "secret" }, "parents": [] }
]

The validator accepts this set: policy set validation passed. Now we send eight requests. Alice is the principal every time. The time is always 2026-11-16T10:15:00Z.

Test matrix with real decisions from cedar 4.14.1
#Action and resourceContext that mattersDecisionPolicy
1view q3-reportweb, risk 0.25ALLOWstaff-view
2view q3-reportrisk 0.93DENYblock-high-risk
3view merger-planno device fieldDENYsecret-needs-managed-device
4view merger-plandevice managed: trueALLOWstaff-view
5view q3-reportchannel partner-apiDENYpartner-api-public-only
6delete q3-reportIP 10.1.2.3, MFA 10 minutes agoALLOWowner-delete
7delete q3-reportIP 10.1.2.3, MFA 60 minutes agoDENYdelete-needs-fresh-mfa
8delete q3-reportIP 203.0.113.7, MFA 10 minutes agoDENYdelete-from-office

Each kind of context has one job. The datetime fields measure how old the sign-in is. The ipaddr field checks the network. mfa and device describe the session. riskScore is a decimal from a risk service. channel names the source of the request. With a schema, these values are plain JSON strings. Cedar turns "10.1.2.3" into an IP address because the schema says ipaddr.

Read row 4 closely. The forbid did not fire, so the permit decided. A forbid never "allows". It only removes access that a permit gave.

Who builds the context, and why it must be trusted

Cedar believes every fact you give it. It cannot check that mfa is really true. So the question "who writes the context?" is a security question.

The part of your system that builds the request is the policy enforcement point, or PEP. The chapter Architecture: where authorization lives covers it in depth. Here are the trusted sources for each fact.

Trusted and untrusted sources
FactTrusted sourceNever take it from
nowThe server clockA time field in the request body
sourceIpThe TCP peer, or a header that your own proxy overwritesAn X-Forwarded-For header the client can set
mfa, authTimeClaims in a verified, signed tokenA cookie or a JSON flag
riskScoreYour risk serviceThe client app
channelWhich endpoint or API key received the callA user-agent string
 
def build_context(body: dict) -> dict:
    return body["context"]   # the client writes its own facts
import time
from datetime import datetime, timezone
from cedarpy import is_authorized

def iso(ts: float) -> str:
    return datetime.fromtimestamp(ts, timezone.utc).strftime("%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ")

def build_delete_context(claims: dict, peer_ip: str, reason: str) -> dict:
    """claims come from a token that your auth library has already verified."""
    return {
        "now": iso(time.time()),                 # the server clock
        "sourceIp": peer_ip,                     # the TCP peer, not a header
        "mfa": "mfa" in claims.get("amr", []),   # signed claim (RFC 8176)
        "authTime": iso(claims["auth_time"]),    # signed claim (OIDC)
        "reason": reason,                        # user text: only for audit
    }

claims = {"sub": "alice", "amr": ["pwd", "mfa"], "auth_time": time.time() - 300}
ctx = build_delete_context(claims, "10.1.2.3", "duplicate")
request = {"principal": 'User::"alice"', "action": 'Action::"delete"',
           "resource": 'Document::"q3-report"', "context": ctx}
result = is_authorized(request, open("policies.cedar").read(),
                       open("entities.json").read(),
                       schema=open("schema.cedarschema").read())
print(result.decision, result.diagnostics.reasons)
Decision.Allow ['policy1']

The amr and auth_time claims are standard OpenID Connect claims. policy1 is the second policy in the file, owner-delete. Python numbers policies by position. The Rust example below maps them back to the @id names.

The Cedar security guide says attackers must not be able to change policies, entities or context. A context copied from the request body breaks that rule. The user can send "mfa": true and pass every MFA guardrail.

Missing keys and safe defaults

This is the most important test in the chapter. We run the delete request without a schema, and the mfa key is missing.

{
  "principal": "User::\"alice\"",
  "action": "Action::\"delete\"",
  "resource": "Document::\"q3-report\"",
  "context": {
    "now":      { "__extn": { "fn": "datetime", "arg": "2026-11-16T10:15:00Z" } },
    "sourceIp": { "__extn": { "fn": "ip", "arg": "10.1.2.3" } },
    "authTime": { "__extn": { "fn": "datetime", "arg": "2026-11-16T10:05:00Z" } },
    "reason": "duplicate"
  }
}
$ cedar authorize -v --policies policies.cedar --entities entities-noschema.json \
    --request-json req-del-missing.json
ALLOW

error while evaluating policy `delete-needs-fresh-mfa`: record does not have the attribute `mfa`

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  owner-delete

The guardrail failed with an error. Cedar skipped it. The permit still applied, so the answer is ALLOW. Nobody did MFA, and the delete went through. This is a fail-open bug.

The fix is one has check. Now a missing key means "no MFA".

@id("delete-needs-fresh-mfa")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource)
unless {
  context has mfa && context.mfa &&
  context.now.durationSince(context.authTime) <= duration("15m")
};
DENY

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  delete-needs-fresh-mfa

The rule behind this is simple. An error in a permit removes access, which is safe. An error in a forbid removes a guardrail, which is not safe. The chapter Policy syntax states the rule that a policy with an error is skipped.

Write the forbid with unless

Say what must be true to pass: unless { context.mfa }. Then a false value blocks.

Guard with has

Add context has mfa && in front. Now a missing value also blocks.

Make the key required

In the schema, write mfa: Bool without ?. A request without it is rejected.

Validate every request

Pass the schema to the context parser and to Request::new. Never turn request validation off.

Layer 4 matters a lot. Look at what happens when mfa has the wrong type and request validation is off.

$ cedar authorize -v --schema schema.cedarschema --request-validation false \
    --policies policies.cedar --entities entities.json --request-json req-del-badtype.json
ALLOW

error while evaluating policy `delete-needs-fresh-mfa`: type error: expected bool, got string

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  owner-delete

With request validation on (the default), the same request never reaches the policies:

  × failed to parse request
  ╰─▶ context `{authTime: datetime("2026-11-16T10:05:00Z"), mfa: "yes", now:
      datetime("2026-11-16T10:15:00Z"), reason: "duplicate", sourceIp:
      ip("10.1.2.3")}` is not valid for `Action::"delete"`

Optional keys like device? are fine. The validator forces you to write context has device before you read it. So the schema and the validator work together to remove this whole class of bugs.

One context shape per action

Look at the schema again. view and delete have different contexts. This is a good design. Each action asks only for the facts its policies need.

The schema now protects every request. We tried three wrong contexts. These are the real errors.

A required key is missing 
  × failed to parse request
  ├─▶ failed to create a context from r.json
  ╰─▶ while parsing context, expected the record to have an attribute `mfa`,
      but it does not
The client adds an unknown key 
  × failed to parse request
  ├─▶ failed to create a context from r.json
  ╰─▶ while parsing context, record attribute `isAdmin` should not exist
      according to the schema
A view request gets the delete context 
  × failed to parse request
  ├─▶ failed to create a context from r.json
  ╰─▶ while parsing context, expected the record to have an attribute
      `channel`, but it does not
The principal has the wrong type (from Rust) 
  × principal type `Document` is not valid for `Action::"delete"`
   ╭────
 1 │ Document
   · ────────
   ╰────
  help: valid principal types for `Action::"delete"`: `User`

In Rust there are two separate checks. Context::from_json_value with the schema checks the keys and turns strings into IP addresses and datetimes. Request::new with the schema checks the types. In our test, the context parser accepted "mfa": "yes". Only Request::new rejected it. So always pass the schema to both.

Some design rules for request types:

  • Give each action exactly the keys its policies read.
  • Make keys required when the server always knows them.
  • Use ? only for facts that are truly sometimes absent, like a device report.
  • Put shared fields in a common type, like type Device.
  • Use the real types: ipaddr, datetime, decimal, Bool. Not strings for everything.
  • Keep user text, like reason, for the audit log. Do not base decisions on free text.

One giant context with forty optional keys for all actions. Every policy then needs many has checks, and the schema checks nothing. The chapter Schemas and validation shows the schema syntax in full.

Checking many requests at once

A document page shows buttons: View, Delete. The page must know which buttons to show. That is one check per action. Here is the batch in Rust and in Python.

use cedar_policy::*;
use serde_json::{json, Value};
use std::{fs, str::FromStr};
// Facts the server knows itself. None come from the request body.
struct Facts { now: String, ip: String, mfa: bool, auth_time: String, risk: String }
// Each action has its own context shape, as the schema says.
fn context_for(action: &str, f: &Facts) -> Value {
    match action {
        "view" => json!({ "sourceIp": f.ip, "channel": "web", "riskScore": f.risk }),
        "delete" => json!({ "now": f.now, "sourceIp": f.ip, "mfa": f.mfa,
                            "authTime": f.auth_time, "reason": "cleanup" }),
        _ => json!({}),
    }
}
fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let (schema, _) = Schema::from_cedarschema_str(&fs::read_to_string("schema.cedarschema")?)?;
    let policies = PolicySet::from_str(&fs::read_to_string("policies.cedar")?)?;
    let entities = Entities::from_json_str(&fs::read_to_string("entities.json")?, Some(&schema))?;
    let authorizer = Authorizer::new(); // parse and build once, check many times

    let facts = Facts { now: "2026-11-16T10:15:00Z".into(), ip: "10.1.2.3".into(), mfa: true,
                        auth_time: "2026-11-16T09:15:00Z".into(), risk: "0.25".into() };
    let alice = EntityUid::from_str(r#"User::"alice""#)?;
    let doc = EntityUid::from_str(r#"Document::"q3-report""#)?;

    for name in ["view", "delete"] {
        let action = EntityUid::from_str(&format!(r#"Action::"{name}""#))?;
        let ctx = Context::from_json_value(context_for(name, &facts), Some((&schema, &action)))?;
        let req = Request::new(alice.clone(), action, doc.clone(), ctx, Some(&schema))?;
        let resp = authorizer.is_authorized(&req, &policies, &entities);
        let why: Vec<&str> = resp.diagnostics().reason()
            .map(|id| policies.annotation(id, "id").unwrap_or(id.as_ref()))
            .collect();
        println!("{name:<7} {:?} {:?}", resp.decision(), why);
    }
    Ok(())
}
from cedarpy import is_authorized_batch, Schema, PolicySet, Entities

schema = Schema.from_str(open("schema.cedarschema").read())
policies = PolicySet.from_str(open("policies.cedar").read())
entities = Entities.from_json_str(open("entities.json").read(), schema=schema)

now, ip = "2026-11-16T10:15:00Z", "10.1.2.3"   # facts the server knows
contexts = {                                  # one context shape per action
    "view": {"sourceIp": ip, "channel": "web", "riskScore": "0.25"},
    "delete": {"now": now, "sourceIp": ip, "mfa": True,
               "authTime": "2026-11-16T09:15:00Z", "reason": "cleanup"},
}
requests = [
    {"principal": 'User::"alice"', "action": f'Action::"{name}"',
     "resource": 'Document::"q3-report"', "context": ctx,
     "correlation_id": name}
    for name, ctx in contexts.items()
]
for r in is_authorized_batch(requests, policies, entities, schema=schema):
    print(r.correlation_id, r.decision, r.diagnostics.id_annotations_by_reason)
view    Allow ["staff-view"]
delete  Deny ["delete-needs-fresh-mfa"]
view Decision.Allow {'policy0': 'staff-view'}
delete Decision.Deny {'policy5': 'delete-needs-fresh-mfa'}

The page shows View and hides Delete, or asks Alice to sign in again. The expensive part is parsing the policies, the schema and the entities. So parse them once and reuse them for every check. In Rust that is a plain loop. cedarpy has is_authorized_batch, which returns the results in the same order as the requests.

  • Amazon Verified Permissions has a BatchIsAuthorized call. It takes up to 30 requests. All of them must share the same principal or the same resource. See Amazon Verified Permissions.
  • The Rust crate also has batched and type-aware partial evaluation for questions like "which documents can Alice view?". In 4.14.1 these sit behind the experimental tpe feature. The chapter Architecture discusses partial evaluation.

The buttons say "allowed", so the server skips the real check when the user clicks. Never do that. Facts change between page load and click. A batch is for the user interface. The server checks again at the moment of the action.

Patterns to remember

Context patterns
PatternPurposeModelNote
Fact about the request goes in contextDecide where data livescontext.sourceIp, context.mfaFacts about things go in entities.
forbid ... unless { context has k && context.k }A guardrail that needs a flagunless { context has mfa && context.mfa }Without has, a missing key allows.
now.durationSince(t) <= duration("15m")Fresh sign-incontext.now.durationSince(context.authTime) <= duration("15m")now comes from the server clock.
ip.isInRange(ip("cidr"))Network rulecontext.sourceIp.isInRange(ip("10.0.0.0/8"))Take the IP from the TCP peer.
score.greaterThan(decimal("x"))Risk thresholdcontext.riskScore.greaterThan(decimal("0.8"))Declare it decimal in the schema.
context has device && context.device.managedOptional device factsecret documents need a managed deviceUse ? only for truly optional facts.
context.channel == "..."Limit a request sourcepartner API sees public onlySet the channel from the endpoint, not a header.
action X appliesTo { context: {...} }One context shape per actionview and delete differUnknown keys are rejected.
Parse once, check manyFast batchesis_authorized_batch(requests, ...)Check again on the real action.

Practice

Where does "the document is classified secret" belong?

  • In the context
  • In an attribute of the Document entity
  • In the action name

It is a fact about a thing, the same for every request.

A forbid reads unless { context.mfa }. The key is missing and no schema is used. What happens?

  • The forbid blocks the request.
  • The forbid errors, Cedar skips it, and a permit can allow the request.
  • Cedar treats the missing key as false.

We saw it in the real run: ALLOW with an error line. Add context has mfa.

Which source for sourceIp is safe?

  • The X-Forwarded-For header from the client
  • A field in the JSON body
  • The TCP peer address, or a header that your own proxy overwrites

The client can write any header or field it likes.

In the schema, an optional context key has a ? after its name.

One BatchIsAuthorized call in Verified Permissions takes up to ? requests.

  1. Write a forbid: the export action is blocked when the risk score is above 0.5.
    Answer

    forbid (principal, action == Action::"export", resource) when { context.riskScore.greaterThan(decimal("0.5")) }; Declare riskScore: decimal in the context of export.

  2. Your teammate wants one context type for all twelve actions. Give two reasons against it.
    Answer

    Most keys become optional, so every policy needs has checks and missing keys go unnoticed. Request validation also checks less, because a wrong request still fits the big shape.

  3. Why does the batch example parse the policies only once?
    Answer

    Parsing policies, schema and entities is the slow part. One check on parsed data is cheap. So you parse once and run many checks.

  4. Hard: the delete guardrail compares context.now with context.authTime. An attacker controls the client clock. Is the rule still safe? What must be true?
    Answer

    It is safe only if the server fills both values. now must come from the server clock. authTime must come from a signed claim, like auth_time in a verified OIDC token. If either comes from the client, the attacker can make any sign-in look fresh.

Key takeaways

  • A request has principal, action, resource and a context record. Entities come next to it.
  • Facts about this request go in the context. Facts about things go in entities.
  • Cedar trusts the context completely. Your server must build it from trusted sources.
  • An error in a forbid skips the guardrail. Guard every context read with has.
  • Required keys in the schema plus request validation reject bad requests early.
  • Give each action its own context shape.
  • Batch checks are for the user interface. Check again at the real action.

Sources

  • Cedar docs: entities and context syntax. The context record and the escapes.
  • Cedar docs: security. Policies, entities and context must be safe from attackers.
  • docs.rs: cedar_policy::Context 4.14.1. Context constructors with a schema.
  • docs.rs: cedar_policy::Request 4.14.1. Request validation with a schema.
  • cedarpy on PyPI. Version 4.12.2 and is_authorized_batch.
  • AWS API reference: BatchIsAuthorized. The 30-request limit.

Schemas and validation

A schema describes your app to Cedar: the entity types, their attributes, the actions and the context of each action. With a schema, the validator checks your policies before they reach production. This chapter shows both schema formats, the most common errors with fixes, and what validation really proves.

  • Write a schema in the Cedar format and read the same schema in JSON.
  • Use namespaces, memberOfTypes, optional attributes, common types, enums and action groups.
  • Run the validator from the CLI, Rust and Python, and fix its errors.
  • Validate requests and entities, not only policies.
  • State exactly what a passed validation guarantees, and what it does not.

Why a schema?

Here is a policy with a small typo. Can you see it?

@id("finance-view")
permit (principal, action == DocApp::Action::"view", resource)
when { principal.departmnet == "finance" };

Without a schema, Cedar accepts the file. The mistake shows up only at run time, and only as an error line.

DENY

error while evaluating policy `finance-view`: `DocApp::User::"alice"` does not have the attribute `departmnet`

note: no policies applied to this request

Finance staff simply lose access, and nobody knows why. Now we run the validator with a schema.

$ cedar validate --schema schema.cedarschema --policies typo.cedar
  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ for policy `finance-view`, attribute `departmnet` on entity type
      `DocApp::User` not found
   ╭─[3:8]
 2 │ permit (principal, action == DocApp::Action::"view", resource)
 3 │ when { principal.departmnet == "finance" };
   ·        ────────────────────
   ╰────
  help: did you mean `department`?

The error points at the exact place and suggests the fix. A schema makes this possible. It is optional for authorization. But it is the base of four checks: policy validation, request validation, entity validation and type-aware parsing of JSON.

All output in this chapter is real. It comes from cedar-policy-cli and the cedar-policy crate 4.14.1, and from cedarpy 4.12.2. The CLI exit code is 3 when validation fails and 0 when it passes.

The Cedar schema format

This schema describes a small document app. Read it once. Then read the list under it.

namespace DocApp {
  // A common type: one name for a shape we use twice.
  type RequestInfo = { sourceIp: ipaddr, mfa: Bool };

  entity Team in [Team];
  entity User in [Team] {
    level: Long,
    department: String,
    manager?: User,          // optional: not every user has one
  };
  entity Folder in [Folder];
  entity Classification enum ["public", "internal", "secret"];
  entity Document in [Folder] {
    owner: User,
    classification: Classification,
    labels: Set<String>,
  };

  action read;               // an action group, used only with "in"
  action view, comment in [read] appliesTo {
    principal: User,
    resource: Document,
    context: RequestInfo,
  };
  action delete appliesTo {
    principal: User,
    resource: Document,
    context: { mfa: Bool, reason?: String },
  };
}
  • namespace DocApp { ... } puts every name in a group. Policies write DocApp::User and DocApp::Action::"view".
  • entity User in [Team] says a user can have teams as parents. In JSON this is memberOfTypes. Team in [Team] allows teams inside teams.
  • { level: Long, ... } lists the attributes and their types. Every attribute is required unless it has ?.
  • manager?: User is an optional attribute. Policies must test it with has first.
  • type RequestInfo = ... is a common type. Two actions share it.
  • entity Classification enum [...] is an enumerated entity type. Only these three ids exist. Enums came in Cedar 4.4.0.
  • action read has no appliesTo. It is an action group. view and comment are in it.
  • appliesTo says which principal and resource types an action accepts, and the type of its context.

The schema also defines a graph of types. Parents use in. Attributes point to other types.

Two more features exist. An entity can declare tags with tags String after its attributes. Any declaration can carry annotations like @doc("who created it"). The chapter Entities and hierarchy covers tags.

List the entity types

Write every kind of principal and resource: User, Team, Folder, Document.

Draw the hierarchy

Add in [...] for parents: users in teams, documents in folders.

Add attributes

Only the attributes your policies read. Use the real types: Long, Bool, ipaddr, entity.

Mark the optional ones

Put ? on attributes that some entities lack. Keep this list short.

Add actions and groups

One action per operation. Group them with in [read].

Give each action a context

Declare the exact context keys. The chapter Context and requests shows why.

You add a namespace to the schema, but your policies still say User and Action::"view". The validator answers unrecognized entity type `User` with help: did you mean `DocApp::User`?. Inside a namespace, policies must use the full name.

The same schema in JSON

Cedar also reads a JSON schema format. Programs often generate it. The CLI translates between the two. Here is a short schema in both formats.

$ cedar translate-schema --direction cedar-to-json-with-resolved-types --schema mini.cedarschema
namespace DocApp {
  type RequestInfo = { sourceIp: ipaddr, mfa: Bool };
  entity Team in [Team];
  entity User in [Team] { level: Long, manager?: User };
  entity Document;
  action view appliesTo {
    principal: User,
    resource: Document,
    context: RequestInfo,
  };
}
{
  "DocApp": {
    "commonTypes": {
      "RequestInfo": {
        "type": "Record",
        "attributes": {"mfa": {"type": "Bool"}, "sourceIp": {"type": "ipaddr"}}
      }
    },
    "entityTypes": {
      "Document": {},
      "Team": {"memberOfTypes": ["DocApp::Team"]},
      "User": {
        "memberOfTypes": ["DocApp::Team"],
        "shape": {
          "type": "Record",
          "attributes": {
            "level": {"type": "Long"},
            "manager": {
              "type": "Entity",
              "name": "DocApp::User",
              "required": false
            }
          }
        }
      }
    },
    "actions": {
      "view": {
        "appliesTo": {
          "resourceTypes": ["DocApp::Document"],
          "principalTypes": ["DocApp::User"],
          "context": {"type": "DocApp::RequestInfo"}
        }
      }
    }
  }
}

I only changed the line breaks of the JSON output. Then I checked the file with cedar validate --schema-format json, and it passed.

Cedar format and JSON format
IdeaCedar formatJSON format
Parent typesentity User in [Team]"memberOfTypes": ["Team"]
Attributes{ level: Long }"shape": {"type": "Record", "attributes": ...}
Optionalmanager?: User"required": false
Common typetype RequestInfo = {...}"commonTypes": {...}
Action groupaction view in [read]"memberOf": [{"id": "read"}]
Applies toprincipal: User"principalTypes": ["User"]
Comments// like thisNone. Translation drops them.

Write the Cedar format by hand. It is shorter and allows comments. Generate the JSON format when a tool or a service needs it.

A JSON attribute without "required": false is required. People expect the opposite. If your entities lack the attribute, entity validation fails with expected entity ... to have attribute.

Running the validator

The validator reads policies and a schema. It reports errors and warnings. Here is the same check in three tools.

cedar validate --schema schema.cedarschema --policies typo.cedar
cedar validate --schema schema.cedarschema --policies policies.cedar --deny-warnings
use cedar_policy::*;
use std::{fs, str::FromStr};

fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let (schema, _) = Schema::from_cedarschema_str(&fs::read_to_string("schema.cedarschema")?)?;
    let policies = PolicySet::from_str(&fs::read_to_string("typo.cedar")?)?;
    let validator = Validator::new(schema);
    let result = validator.validate(&policies, ValidationMode::Strict);
    println!("passed: {}", result.validation_passed());
    for e in result.validation_errors() {
        println!("error in {}: {e}", e.policy_id());
    }
    for w in result.validation_warnings() {
        println!("warning in {}: {w}", w.policy_id());
    }
    Ok(())
}
from cedarpy import validate_policies

schema = open("schema.cedarschema").read()
policies = open("typo.cedar").read()
result = validate_policies(policies, schema)
print("passed:", result.validation_passed)
for e in result.errors:
    print(e)
passed: false
error in policy0: for policy `policy0`, attribute `departmnet` on entity type `DocApp::User` not found
passed: False
[policy0] for policy `policy0`, attribute `departmnet` on entity type `DocApp::User` not found

The library names policies by position: policy0, policy1. The CLI uses the @id annotation instead, so it printed finance-view. A warning does not fail validation. Add --deny-warnings in CI to make warnings fail too.

You validate once, then change the schema. Old policies may now be wrong. Validate the whole policy set again after every schema change, in the same CI job.

The most common validation errors

We broke the good policy set on purpose, one mistake at a time. These are the real messages. Open each one.

1. Unknown entity type 
  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ for policy `p1`, unrecognized entity type `DocApp::Uzer`
   ╭─[2:22]
 1 │ @id("p1")
 2 │ permit (principal is DocApp::Uzer, action, resource);
   ·                      ────────────
   ╰────
  help: did you mean `DocApp::User`?

Fix: correct the name. Unknown actions give the same kind of message: unrecognized action `DocApp::Action::"veiw"`.

2. Optional attribute without has 
  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ for policy `p3`, unable to guarantee safety of access to optional
      attribute `manager` on entity type `DocApp::User`
   ╭─[3:8]
 2 │ permit (principal, action == DocApp::Action::"view", resource)
 3 │ when { resource.owner.manager == principal };
   ·        ──────────────────────
   ╰────
  help: try testing for the attribute's presence with `e has manager && ..`

Fix: resource.owner has manager && resource.owner.manager == principal.

3. Wrong type for an operator 
  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ for policy `p4`, unexpected type: expected Long but saw String
   ╭─[3:8]
 2 │ permit (principal, action == DocApp::Action::"view", resource)
 3 │ when { principal.department > 3 };
   ·        ────────────────────
   ╰────

Fix: compare a Long with a Long. Use principal.level > 3.

4. == between different types 
  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ the types Long and String are not compatible
   ╭─[3:8]
 2 │ permit (principal, action == DocApp::Action::"view", resource)
 3 │ when { principal.level == "3" };
   ·        ──────────────────────
   ╰────
  help: for policy `p5`, both operands to a `==` expression must have
        compatible types. Types must be exactly equal to be compatible

Fix: principal.level == 3. Without validation this is not an error at run time. It is just always false.

5. An id that is not in the enum 
  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ for policy `p6`: entity `DocApp::Classification::"topsecret"` is of an
      enumerated entity type, but `"topsecret"` is not declared as a valid eid
  help: valid entity eids: "public", "internal", "secret"

Fix: use one of the listed ids, or add the new id to the schema.

6. A context key of another action 
  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ for policy `p7`, attribute `reason` in context for
      DocApp::Action::"view" not found
   ╭─[3:8]
 2 │ permit (principal, action == DocApp::Action::"view", resource)
 3 │ when { context.reason == "audit" };
   ·        ──────────────
   ╰────
  help: did you mean `mfa`?

Fix: reason exists only for delete. Change the action, or add the key to the context of view.

7. An empty set literal 
  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ for policy `p8`, empty set literals are forbidden in policies
   ╭─[3:36]
 2 │ permit (principal, action == DocApp::Action::"view", resource)
 3 │ when { resource.labels.containsAny([]) };
   ·                                    ──
   ╰────

Fix: the validator cannot know the element type of []. Remove the test or use .isEmpty(). A set with mixed types like [1, "a"] fails too.

8. An extension constructor on a non-literal 
  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ for policy `p9`, extension constructors may not be called with non-
      literal expressions
  help: consider applying extension constructors inside attribute values when
        constructing entity or context data

Fix: store the value as ipaddr in the schema, not as a String. Then ip(...) is not needed. A bad literal fails too: failed to parse as IP address: `"10.0.0.300/8"`.

9. Warnings: == instead of in, and impossible policies 
  ⚠ policy set validation passed
  ╰─▶ for policy `p10`, unable to find an applicable action given the policy
      scope constraints
  help: try replacing `==` with `in` in the principal clause

Warning:
  ⚠ for policy `p10`, policy is impossible: the policy expression evaluates to
  │ false for all valid requests

The policy said principal == DocApp::Team::"finance". A principal of view is always a User, never a Team. So the policy can never match. Fix: principal in DocApp::Team::"finance".

"Unable to find an applicable action" used to be an error. Since 4.13.0 (September 2026) it is a warning, because the policy cannot cause a run-time error. It only never applies. Use --deny-warnings if you want it to fail your build.

Strict mode and levels

The validator has a mode. The default and only stable mode is strict. Errors 4, 7 and 8 above come from strict rules: the two sides of == must have the same type, set literals must be non-empty and of one type, and constructors like ip() need literal strings. The CLI also lists permissive and partial modes. In 4.14.1 both are experimental feature flags.

The second option is the level. Look at this policy from our set.

@id("manager-view")
permit (principal, action == DocApp::Action::"view", resource is DocApp::Document)
when { resource.owner has manager && resource.owner.manager == principal };
$ cedar validate --schema schema.cedarschema --policies policies.cedar --level 1
  × policy set validation failed
  ╰─▶ for policy `manager-view`, this policy requires level 2, which exceeds
      the maximum allowed level (1)
    ╭─[15:38]
 14 │ permit (principal, action == DocApp::Action::"view", resource is DocApp::Document)
 15 │ when { resource.owner has manager && resource.owner.manager == principal };
    ·                                      ──────────────────────
    ╰────
$ cedar validate --schema schema.cedarschema --policies policies.cedar --level 2
  ☞ policy set validation passed
  ╰─▶ no errors or warnings

The policy goes from resource to its owner (step 1), then reads the owner's data (step 2). Each read of an entity's data costs one level. At level 1, a policy may read only the data of the request entities: principal, resource, action and entities named in the context. Why care? If all policies pass at level N, your app only needs to load entities within N steps of the request. That makes entity loading small and predictable.

Level validation came from RFC 76. It was experimental in 4.2.0 and became stable in 4.4.0 (April 2025).

Validating requests and entities

Policies are only one input. Requests and entities can be wrong too. With a schema, Cedar checks them when it parses them. Here is a good entities file for our schema. Note the plain {"type", "id"} form for entity references. The schema tells Cedar that owner is an entity.

[
  { "uid": { "type": "DocApp::User", "id": "bob" },
    "attrs": { "level": 2, "department": "finance",
               "manager": { "type": "DocApp::User", "id": "alice" } },
    "parents": [ { "type": "DocApp::Team", "id": "finance" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "DocApp::Document", "id": "budget" },
    "attrs": { "owner": { "type": "DocApp::User", "id": "bob" },
               "classification": { "type": "DocApp::Classification", "id": "internal" },
               "labels": ["2026", "plan"] },
    "parents": [ { "type": "DocApp::Folder", "id": "finance" } ] }
]

We check an entities file with cedar check-parse --schema schema.cedarschema --entities FILE. Then we break one thing at a time.

Entity validation errors from cedar 4.14.1
MistakeReal message (last line)
"level": "3"in attribute `level` on `DocApp::User::"carol"`, type mismatch: value was expected to have type long, but it actually has type string: `"3"`
No departmentexpected entity `DocApp::User::"carol"` to have attribute `department`, but it does not
Extra isAdminattribute `isAdmin` on `DocApp::User::"carol"` should not exist according to the schema
User inside a Folder`DocApp::User::"carol"` is not allowed to have an ancestor of type `DocApp::Folder` according to the schema
Type DocApp::Robotentity `DocApp::Robot::"r2"` has type `DocApp::Robot` which is not declared in the schema
Classification "topsecret"`"topsecret"` is not declared as a valid eid

In Rust, pass the schema to Entities::from_json_str(json, Some(&schema)). For requests, pass it to Request::new(..., Some(&schema)). The chapter Context and requests shows the request errors.

You expect entity validation to find broken links. It does not. A document with "owner": {"type": "DocApp::User", "id": "ghost"} passes, even when no user ghost exists. The schema checks types, not existence.

What validation proves, and what it does not

The Cedar team proved the main property of the validator in Lean, a proof assistant. Here it is in simple words.

Suppose the schema is well formed, the validator accepts the policies, and the request and the entities pass validation against the same schema. Then every policy evaluates to true or false, or it fails with one of only three errors: a missing entity, an extension error, or an arithmetic overflow. It never fails with a type error or a missing attribute.

Proof idea

The validator is a type checker. It gives every expression a type, using the schema. It uses the action in the scope to know the exact principal, resource and context types. Then it checks each operator. > must get two Longs. .level must be an attribute of that entity type. An optional attribute must be under a has check.

The proof shows one step at a time that the type is always correct. If an expression has type Long, then evaluation gives a Long or one of the three run-time errors. The base cases are the request and the entities. They match the schema because they passed validation. The theorem in Lean is called validation_is_sound.

The three left-over errors depend on run-time data, so no static check can stop them. We made two of them happen with policies that passed validation.

@id("big-level")
permit (principal, action == DocApp::Action::"view", resource)
when { principal.level * 4000000000000000000 > 10 };
DENY

error while evaluating policy `big-level`: integer overflow while attempting to multiply the values `3` and `4000000000000000000`
@id("owner-level")
permit (principal, action == DocApp::Action::"view", resource)
when { resource.owner.level >= 2 };
DENY

error while evaluating policy `owner-level`: entity `DocApp::User::"ghost"` does not exist

Validation also does not prove these things:

  • That the policies say what you mean. A valid policy can still give too much access. Tests check that. See Testing Cedar policies.
  • That requests and entities match the schema, unless you validate them too.
  • That two policy sets allow the same things. Symbolic analysis can answer that. See Analysis and formal verification.

A schema can now declare names with external, such as external entity User;. This allows a partial schema that is linked to others later. Policy validation refuses a partial schema: schema is incomplete: `User` is declared `external` but not defined. Link all parts first.

Learn the three left-over errors by heart: missing entity, extension error, overflow. A question may ask "the policies validated, so why did one fail?" The answer is always one of these three, or data that skipped validation.

Patterns to remember

Schema and validation patterns
PatternPurposeModelNote
entity A in [B] { ... };Type with parents and attributesentity User in [Team] { level: Long };JSON: memberOfTypes and shape.
attr?: TypeOptional attributemanager?: UserPolicies need has before reading it.
type Name = { ... };Reuse a shapetype RequestInfo = { sourceIp: ipaddr, mfa: Bool };JSON: commonTypes.
entity E enum [...]Fixed list of idsentity Classification enum ["public", "secret"];Since 4.4.0. Typos in ids are caught.
action a, b in [g] appliesTo {...}Actions with a group and contextaction view, comment in [read] appliesTo {...}A group without appliesTo is used with in.
cedar validate --schema S --policies PCheck policiesadd --deny-warnings in CIExit code 3 on failure.
Validator::new(schema).validate(&ps, ValidationMode::Strict)Check in Rustresult.validation_passed()Also read the warnings.
--level NLimit how far policies readcedar validate ... --level 1Then load only entities within N steps.
Parse entities with the schemaCatch bad dataEntities::from_json_str(json, Some(&schema))Does not check that references exist.
cedar translate-schemaSwitch formats--direction cedar-to-json-with-resolved-typesComments are lost.

Practice

In the Cedar schema format, how do you make an attribute optional?

  • manager: Optional<User>
  • manager?: User
  • manager: User = null

A question mark after the name. In JSON it is "required": false.

The policies validated. Which run-time error can still happen?

  • A missing attribute
  • A type error
  • An integer overflow

Overflow, missing entities and extension errors depend on data.

What does principal == DocApp::Team::"finance" give for the action view?

  • A validation error
  • A warning: the policy is impossible
  • It works for all finance members

The principal of view is a User, so == with a Team never matches. Use in.

The JSON key for parent types is ?.

The CLI flag that makes warnings fail is ?.

  1. Write a schema line: a Project has parents of type Org and attributes name (String) and optional budget (Long).
    Answer

    entity Project in [Org] { name: String, budget?: Long };

  2. The validator says unable to guarantee safety of access to optional attribute `budget`. Fix resource.budget > 1000.
    Answer

    resource has budget && resource.budget > 1000.

  3. Why does the validator refuse ip(context.addr) when addr is a String? What is the better design?
    Answer

    Strict mode allows extension constructors only on literal strings, because a bad string would fail at run time. Declare addr: ipaddr in the schema. Then the context parser turns the string into an IP address before evaluation.

  4. Hard: all policies pass at --level 1. Which entities must your app load for a request?
    Answer

    Only the principal, the resource, the action and entities named in the context, with their attributes and ancestors. Entities that their attributes point to are compared by identity only, never read. So you do not need to load them.

Key takeaways

  • A schema lists entity types, attributes, actions and the context of each action.
  • The Cedar format and the JSON format hold the same information. The CLI translates them.
  • Attributes are required unless marked with ? or "required": false.
  • The validator finds typos, wrong types and unsafe optional reads before deployment.
  • Strict mode is the default. Levels limit how far policies read into the entity graph.
  • Validate requests and entities too. Entity validation does not check that references exist.
  • After validation, only three errors remain: missing entity, extension error, overflow.

Sources

  • Cedar docs: schema format. Namespaces, entities, enums, common types and actions.
  • Cedar docs: JSON schema format. memberOfTypes, shape, required and appliesTo.
  • Cedar docs: policy validation. What the validator checks and its limits.
  • cedar-spec: Validation.lean. The Lean theorem validation_is_sound.
  • RFC 76: entity slice validation. The idea behind levels.
  • cedar-policy changelog. Enums, levels, the 4.13.0 warning change and external.

Policy templates and linked policies

Many apps have a Share button. Every click creates a new permission for one person on one thing. Cedar handles this with templates: a policy with holes that you fill in later. This chapter shows how to write a template, link it, unlink it, and build a safe share feature with it.

  • Write a template with the ?principal and ?resource slots.
  • Link and unlink a template with the CLI, Rust and Python.
  • Explain why templates are safer than building policy text with strings.
  • Build a share feature for people and for teams.
  • Choose between a template, an attribute and a normal policy.

The problem: one policy per click

Alice owns the document plan. She clicks "Share with Bob, read only". Your app now needs this policy:

permit (principal == User::"bob", action in [Action::"view", Action::"comment"], resource == Document::"plan");

Tomorrow she shares with Eve. Next week Bob shares another file. Each click needs one more policy with the same shape. The first idea is to build the text with string formatting. That idea is dangerous. Here is a real test with cedarpy. The "user id" comes from a web form.

from cedarpy import is_authorized, PolicySet

entities = open("entities.json").read()
evil = 'bob", action, resource);\npermit (principal == User::"x'  # a "user id" typed in a form

# Wrong: build policy text with string formatting.
text = f'permit (principal == User::"{evil}", action == Action::"view", resource == Document::"plan");'
print(text)
req = {"principal": 'User::"bob"', "action": 'Action::"delete"',
       "resource": 'Document::"payroll"', "context": {}}
print("string-built:", is_authorized(req, text, entities).decision)

# Right: a template. The slot value is only ever an entity id.
base = PolicySet.from_str('@id("share-read") permit (principal == ?principal, '
                          'action == Action::"view", resource == ?resource);')
linked = base.with_linked(template_id="share-read", new_id="share-1",
                          values={"?principal": {"type": "User", "id": evil},
                                  "?resource": 'Document::"plan"'})
print("template:    ", is_authorized(req, linked, entities).decision)
print(str(linked).splitlines()[-1])
permit (principal == User::"bob", action, resource);
permit (principal == User::"x", action == Action::"view", resource == Document::"plan");
string-built: Decision.Allow
template:     Decision.Deny
permit(principal == User::"bob\", action, resource);\npermit (principal == User::\"x", action == Action::"view", resource == Document::"plan");

The string version created a second policy. Bob may now do every action on every resource, so he can delete payroll. This is code injection. The template version stayed safe. The strange text became only an odd entity id. The Cedar security guide gives the same advice: never build policies by joining strings, use templates.

"I escape the quotes, so string building is fine." Escaping is easy to get wrong, and one mistake gives full access. A template has no such risk, because the slot can only hold an entity reference.

A template and its slots

A template is a policy with one or two slots. Here is our policy file. It has one normal policy and two templates.

// Static policy: the owner can share a document.
@id("owner-can-share")
permit (principal, action == Action::"share", resource is Document)
when { resource.owner == principal };

// Template: one person may read one document.
@id("share-read")
permit (
  principal == ?principal,
  action in [Action::"view", Action::"comment"],
  resource == ?resource
);

// Template: a team may view everything in a folder.
@id("team-folder-view")
permit (
  principal in ?principal,
  action == Action::"view",
  resource in ?resource
);

A normal policy is also called a static policy. The rules for slots are strict:

  • Only two slots exist: ?principal and ?resource. There is no ?action.
  • A slot can appear only in the scope, never in when or unless.
  • ?principal goes in the principal part, ?resource in the resource part.
  • Three forms work: == ?slot, in ?slot and is Type in ?slot.
  • Each slot holds exactly one entity, never a set.
  • A template alone allows nothing. Only its linked policies count.

The parser enforces these rules. These are the real messages from cedar check-parse.

A slot inside when 
  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ found template slot ?principal in a `when` clause
  help: slots are currently unsupported in `when` clauses
A slot for the action 
  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ expected single entity uid, found template slot
   ╭────
 1 │ permit (principal == ?principal, action == ?action, resource);
   ·                                            ───────
   ╰────
A slot after is 
  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ right hand side of an `is` expression must be an entity type name, but
      got `?principal`
  help: try using `==` to test for equality: `principal == ?principal`
A made-up slot name 
  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ expected an entity uid or matching template slot, found ?owner instead
      of ?principal
A slot inside a set 
  × failed to parse policy set
  ╰─▶ expected single entity uid or template slot, found set of entity uids

principal is User in ?principal parses fine. It means "a User who is in the group that fills the slot".

Linking a template

Linking fills the slots with real entities. The result is a template-linked policy. It has its own id. Here is the CLI. It writes each link to a JSON file.

$ cedar link --policies policies.cedar --template-linked links.json \
    --template-id share-read --new-id share-plan-bob \
    --arguments '{"?principal": "User::\"bob\"", "?resource": "Document::\"plan\""}'
Template-linked policy added: @id("share-read")
permit(principal == User::"bob", action in [Action::"view", Action::"comment"], resource == Document::"plan");
$ cedar link --policies policies.cedar --template-linked links.json \
    --template-id team-folder-view --new-id design-sees-brand \
    --arguments '{"?principal": "Team::\"design\"", "?resource": "Folder::\"brand\""}'
Template-linked policy added: @id("team-folder-view")
permit(principal in Team::"design", action == Action::"view", resource in Folder::"brand");
[
    {
        "template_id": "share-read",
        "link_id": "share-plan-bob",
        "args": {
            "?resource": "Document::\"plan\"",
            "?principal": "User::\"bob\""
        }
    },
    {
        "template_id": "team-folder-view",
        "link_id": "design-sees-brand",
        "args": {
            "?principal": "Team::\"design\"",
            "?resource": "Folder::\"brand\""
        }
    }
]

Now we ask five questions. Dana is in the team design. The document logo is in the folder brand.

$ cedar authorize -v --schema schema.cedarschema --policies policies.cedar \
    --template-linked links.json --entities entities.json \
    -l 'User::"bob"' -a 'Action::"view"' -r 'Document::"plan"'
ALLOW

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  share-plan-bob
Real decisions with the two links
PrincipalActionResourceDecisionPolicy
bobviewplanALLOWshare-plan-bob
bobshareplanDENYnone
aliceshareplanALLOWowner-can-share
danaviewlogoALLOWdesign-sees-brand
danaviewplanDENYnone

The decision names the link id, not the template. That is useful in an audit log. You can see the exact grant that allowed the request.

In code, you link in memory. Rust uses PolicySet::link with a map from SlotId to EntityUid. cedarpy uses with_linked or with_linked_batch.

use cedar_policy::*;
use std::{collections::HashMap, str::FromStr};

const SHARE: &str = r#"permit (principal == ?principal,
  action in [Action::"view", Action::"comment"], resource == ?resource);"#;

fn uid(s: &str) -> EntityUid { EntityUid::from_str(s).unwrap() }

fn check(ps: &PolicySet, ents: &Entities) {
    let req = Request::new(uid(r#"User::"bob""#), uid(r#"Action::"view""#),
        uid(r#"Document::"plan""#), Context::empty(), None).unwrap();
    let r = Authorizer::new().is_authorized(&req, ps, ents);
    let why: Vec<String> = r.diagnostics().reason().map(|id| id.to_string()).collect();
    println!("bob view plan: {:?} {:?}", r.decision(), why);
}

fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let ents = Entities::from_json_str(&std::fs::read_to_string("entities.json")?, None)?;
    let mut ps = PolicySet::new();
    ps.add_template(Template::parse(Some(PolicyId::new("share-read")), SHARE)?)?;
    check(&ps, &ents); // a template alone allows nothing

    // Alice clicks "Share with Bob": fill both slots.
    let vals = HashMap::from([(SlotId::principal(), uid(r#"User::"bob""#)),
                              (SlotId::resource(), uid(r#"Document::"plan""#))]);
    ps.link(PolicyId::new("share-read"), PolicyId::new("share-plan-bob"), vals)?;
    println!("{}", ps.policy(&PolicyId::new("share-plan-bob")).unwrap());
    check(&ps, &ents);

    // The template cannot go while a link uses it.
    if let Err(e) = ps.remove_template(PolicyId::new("share-read")) { println!("error: {e}"); }

    // Alice clicks "Stop sharing": unlink.
    ps.unlink(PolicyId::new("share-plan-bob"))?;
    check(&ps, &ents);
    Ok(())
}
from cedarpy import is_authorized, PolicySet, Schema, Entities
