schema = Schema.from_str(open("schema.cedarschema").read())
entities = Entities.from_json_str(open("entities.json").read(), schema=schema)
base = PolicySet.from_str(open("policies.cedar").read())

def can(ps, who, act, doc):
    req = {"principal": f'User::"{who}"', "action": f'Action::"{act}"',
           "resource": f'Document::"{doc}"', "context": {}}
    r = is_authorized(req, ps, entities, schema=schema)
    return r.decision.name, r.diagnostics.reasons

print("before:", can(base, "bob", "view", "plan"))
shared = base.with_linked_batch([
    {"template_id": "share-read", "new_id": "share-plan-bob",
     "values": {"?principal": 'User::"bob"', "?resource": 'Document::"plan"'}},
    {"template_id": "team-folder-view", "new_id": "design-sees-brand",
     "values": {"?principal": 'Team::"design"', "?resource": 'Folder::"brand"'}},
])
print("bob view plan:  ", can(shared, "bob", "view", "plan"))
print("bob share plan: ", can(shared, "bob", "share", "plan"))
print("dana view logo: ", can(shared, "dana", "view", "logo"))
revoked = shared.without_linked("share-plan-bob")
print("after revoke:   ", can(revoked, "bob", "view", "plan"))
for t in revoked.templates():
    print(t["id_annotation"], [link["id"] for link in t["links"]])
bob view plan: Deny []
permit(principal == User::"bob", action in [Action::"view", Action::"comment"], resource == Document::"plan");
bob view plan: Allow ["share-plan-bob"]
error: unable to remove policy template `share-read` because it has active links
bob view plan: Deny []
before: ('Deny', [])
bob view plan:   ('Allow', ['share-plan-bob'])
bob share plan:  ('Deny', [])
dana view logo:  ('Allow', ['design-sees-brand'])
after revoke:    ('Deny', [])
share-read []
team-folder-view ['design-sees-brand']

Linking can fail. These messages come from the Rust crate 4.14.1:

  • A slot value is missing: the following slots were not provided as arguments: ?resource
  • The new id is taken: template-linked policy id `l1` conflicts with an existing policy id
  • The template does not exist: failed to find a template with id `nope`
  • Unlinking an unknown id: unable to unlink policy `zzz` because it does not exist

Choose link ids that explain themselves, like share-plan-bob or a database row id. You will see them in every decision and every log line.

Unlinking and changing a template

To revoke a share, you unlink it. The Rust run above showed it: after unlink, Bob gets Deny again. The template stays, so other links keep working. Cedar refuses to remove a template while links still use it: unable to remove policy template `share-read` because it has active links.

A template-linked policy stays connected to its template. If the template changes, every link changes. In your own app, the links are rows in a table, and you rebuild the policy set from the template text and the rows. Here we remove comment from the template and rebuild.

from cedarpy import is_authorized, PolicySet

# Rows from your "shares" table: one row per click on "Share".
SHARES = [("share-read", "share-plan-bob", 'User::"bob"', 'Document::"plan"')]

def build(template_text: str) -> PolicySet:
    base = PolicySet.from_str(template_text)
    return base.with_linked_batch([
        {"template_id": t, "new_id": n, "values": {"?principal": p, "?resource": r}}
        for t, n, p, r in SHARES])

V1 = '''@id("share-read") permit (principal == ?principal,
  action in [Action::"view", Action::"comment"], resource == ?resource);'''
V2 = '''@id("share-read") permit (principal == ?principal,
  action == Action::"view", resource == ?resource);'''   # comment removed

req = {"principal": 'User::"bob"', "action": 'Action::"comment"',
       "resource": 'Document::"plan"', "context": {}}
for name, text in [("v1", V1), ("v2", V2)]:
    print(name, is_authorized(req, build(text), "[]").decision.name)
v1 Allow
v2 Deny

One edit changed every share at once. That is the power of templates, and also the risk. A bad edit hits every link. Review template changes as carefully as code.

The managed service stores templates and links for you. Its UpdatePolicyTemplate call changes all linked policies, after a short delay because the service is eventually consistent. It lets you change only the action and the when and unless parts. The effect, principal and resource must stay the same. See Amazon Verified Permissions.

Building a share feature

Now we put it together. A share feature needs a permission to share, a link per share, storage, and revocation. The slides go through it in order.

Check the right to share

Alice clicks Share. First ask Cedar: may Alice do Action::"share" on plan? The static policy owner-can-share answers ALLOW.

Pick the template

"Read only" means share-read. "Can edit" would be a second template, share-edit. One template per kind of share.

Store a row

Save (template, link id, principal, resource, who, when) in your database. The row is the truth. The policy set is rebuilt from it.

Link

Call link with the two entities. Check that the principal really is a User or a Team before you link.

Authorize as usual

Bob opens the document. The decision names share-plan-bob. Log it.

Revoke

Alice clicks Stop sharing. Unlink the policy and mark the row as revoked. Keep the row for the audit history.

The picture shows one template with many links. Each link points at one principal and one resource.

Sharing with a team uses principal in ?principal. One link covers every member, now and later. When Dana joins the team, she gets access with no new link. When she leaves, you change her parents in the entity data, not the policies. The chapter Entities and hierarchy explains in.

You let Bob, a viewer, create links too. Now Bob can share Alice's document with anybody. Always check the share action first, and keep a forbid guardrail for cases that must never be shared. A forbid beats every linked permit.

Templates and the validator

The validator checks templates too. It tries every entity type that the slot could hold. Our three policies pass against this schema.

entity Team;
entity User in [Team];
entity Folder in [Folder];
entity Document in [Folder] { owner: User };
action view, comment, share appliesTo { principal: User, resource: Document };

But link does not look at the schema. We linked the slots the wrong way round, with a Document as principal. The CLI accepted it:

Template-linked policy added: @id("share-read")
permit(principal == Document::"plan", action in [Action::"view", Action::"comment"], resource == User::"bob");

Validation with the links file then gives only a warning, not an error:

  ⚠ policy set validation passed
  ╰─▶ for policy `bad-link`, unable to find an applicable action given the
      policy scope constraints

Since 4.13.0 this case is a warning, because the policy can never match. It is harmless but useless. Run cedar validate --deny-warnings with the links file in CI. Also check the entity types in your app before you call link.

Template or normal policy?

Templates are one way to model "who may access what". The Cedar best-practice guide names a second way: keep the relationship in entity data and write one static policy.

Template-linked policies

permit (principal == ?principal,
  action == Action::"view",
  resource == ?resource);

Relationship in entity data

permit (principal,
  action == Action::"view",
  resource)
when { resource.viewers.contains(principal) };
Which way to choose
QuestionTemplate-linked policyAttribute in entities
Where is the grant stored?In the policy setIn your app database
Does the relationship have other uses?No. It exists only for access.Yes, for example "owner" or "account holder".
Who has access to this document?List the links of the template.Read the attribute.
Change the rule for all grantsEdit one template.Edit one policy.
Grows withThe number of policiesThe size of entity data per request
Main riskLinks pile up when users leave.Stale or missing entity data.

The guide's rule of thumb: if the relationship exists only to grant access, use a template. If your app already stores it for other reasons, use an attribute. A rule that is the same for everybody, like "staff can view internal documents", needs neither. Write one static policy.

Know the limits before you choose templates:

  • Only two slots, and no slot for the action. One template per kind of share.
  • No slots in conditions. You cannot write when { context.project == ?project }.
  • One entity per slot. Ten people need ten links, or one team and in.
  • Links live as long as you keep them. Offboarding must remove them.
  • Generalized templates with more slots and typed slots are only a proposal (RFC 98). Its pull request was still open at its last update in May 2026. Do not plan on it.

Remember three facts. Slots: only ?principal and ?resource, only in the scope. Linking: the decision names the link id. Changes: editing a template changes every linked policy.

Patterns to remember

Template patterns
PatternPurposeModelNote
principal == ?principal, ..., resource == ?resourceShare one thing with one personshare-readOne link per share.
principal in ?principalShare with a teamteam-folder-viewMembers change in entity data, not in policies.
resource in ?resourceShare a whole folderlink to Folder::"brand"New documents in the folder are covered.
principal is User in ?principalOnly users of a groupusers in Team::"design"The only is form with a slot.
cedar link --template-id T --new-id N --arguments '{...}'Link from the shellwrites links.jsonPass the file with -k.
ps.link(tid, new_id, HashMap::from([...]))Link in RustSlotId::principal()Every slot needs a value.
base.with_linked_batch([...])Link in Pythonrebuild from database rowsReturns a new policy set.
ps.unlink(id) / without_linked(id)Revoke a shareshare-plan-bobKeep the row for the audit.
Check Action::"share" firstOnly owners create linksowner-can-shareAdd a forbid for things that must never be shared.
Never build policy text from user inputStop code injectionuse slots insteadThe Cedar security guide says the same.

Practice

Which template is valid?

  • permit (principal, action == ?action, resource);
  • permit (principal, action, resource) when { principal == ?principal };
  • permit (principal in ?principal, action, resource == ?resource);

There is no action slot, and slots cannot be in when.

Bob is allowed through a link. Which id does the decision show?

  • The template id share-read
  • The link id share-plan-bob
  • No id, because templates are anonymous

The template-linked policy is a real policy with its own id.

You call remove_template while two links exist. What happens?

  • The template and both links are removed.
  • An error: the template has active links.
  • The links stay and keep working.

Unlink first, then remove the template.

A template can have at most ? slots.

To revoke one share in Rust, call ps.<span class="blank" data-answer="unlink">?</span>(id).

  1. Write a template that lets one person edit one document.
    Answer

    @id("share-edit") permit (principal == ?principal, action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"], resource == ?resource);

  2. Your teammate builds policies with f'permit (principal == User::"{name}", ...)'. Explain the risk in two sentences.
    Answer

    A name with a quote can end the string and add new policy text, as the injection demo showed. One bad name can give a user every action on every resource.

  3. Shares should end after 30 days. Slots cannot be in conditions. How can you do it?
    Answer

    Put the end date in your share table and unlink when it passes. Or keep the expiry in entity data, for example a share entity with an expires datetime, and check it in a static policy with context.now.

  4. Hard: your app has 2 million shares. Compare templates with a viewers attribute for this case.
    Answer

    With templates, the policy set holds 2 million linked policies. It must be loaded, kept in memory and rebuilt on change. Measure that cost. With an attribute, the policy set stays tiny, and each request loads only the viewers of one document. Many teams choose attributes or group entities at this size, and templates for smaller, audited grants.

Key takeaways

  • A template is a policy with slots. Only ?principal and ?resource exist, only in the scope.
  • Linking fills the slots and creates a template-linked policy with its own id.
  • Unlinking revokes one grant. A template with links cannot be removed.
  • Editing a template changes every linked policy at once.
  • Templates stop policy injection. Never build policy text from user input.
  • link does not check types. Validate with the links file and --deny-warnings.
  • Use templates for grants that exist only for access. Use attributes for relationships your app already stores.

Sources

  • Cedar docs: policy templates. Slots, linking and how links follow the template.
  • Cedar best practice: attributes or templates for relationships. When to choose each.
  • Cedar docs: security. Code injection and why templates are preferred.
  • docs.rs: cedar_policy::PolicySet 4.14.1. link, unlink and remove_template.
  • AWS API reference: UpdatePolicyTemplate. What a template update may change.
  • Cedar RFC 98: Generalized Cedar Templates. The open proposal for more slots.

The 15 Cedar patterns

Fifteen policy shapes cover most access rules in real apps. Each shape on this page ran in the real Cedar engine, with one allow case and one deny case. Learn the fifteen shapes, and you can write most policies by changing a few names.

  • Choose the right pattern for a new access rule.
  • Write each of the 15 patterns and test it with an allow case and a deny case.
  • Explain why guardrails use forbid and unless.
  • Spot the common mistake of each pattern from real validator messages.
  • Combine many patterns in one policy set and predict the decision.

One lab for all fifteen patterns

Here is pattern 1. Read the policy first. Then read the two test lines under it.

// Pattern 1: role through group membership
@id("editors.view-edit")
permit (
  principal in Group::"editors",
  action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"],
  resource is Document
);
validate (strict): ok, 1 policies
alice edit roadmap           Allow reasons=["editors.view-edit"] ok
bob edit roadmap             Deny  reasons=[] ok

Alice is in the editors group, so she may edit. Bob is not, so the answer is Deny. No policy says yes to Bob. This is default deny at work.

A policy pattern is a shape you can reuse. You keep the shape. You change the names. Every pattern has three parts:

  • The effect: permit or forbid.
  • The scope: who, which action and which thing.
  • The condition: an optional when or unless block.

The lab schema

All fifteen patterns share one small world. It has users, groups, folders, documents, tenants, audit logs, one AI agent and two tools. A tenant is one customer company in a shared app. The schema below passed strict validation with every pattern.

// One schema for all 15 patterns.
entity Tenant;
entity Group in [Group];
entity User in [Group] {
  department: String,
  tenant: Tenant,
  projects: Set<String>,
};
entity Folder in [Folder, Tenant] { owner: User };
entity Document in [Folder] {
  owner: User,
  department: String,
  sharedWith: Set<User>,
} tags String;
entity AuditLog in [Tenant];
entity Agent;
entity Tool { egress: Bool };

type DocContext = {
  mfa: Bool,
  sourceIp: ipaddr,
  now: datetime,
  breakGlass?: { reason: String, ticket: String },
};

action readActions, writeActions;
action view, comment in [readActions] appliesTo {
  principal: [User], resource: [Document, Folder], context: DocContext,
};
action edit, delete, share in [writeActions] appliesTo {
  principal: [User], resource: [Document, Folder], context: DocContext,
};
action readLog appliesTo {
  principal: [User], resource: [AuditLog], context: DocContext,
};
action deleteLog appliesTo {
  principal: [User], resource: [AuditLog], context: DocContext,
};
action call_tool appliesTo {
  principal: [Agent], resource: [Tool], context: { taint: String },
};

The line tags String gives documents entity tags. The two action groups, readActions and writeActions, collect the normal actions. The lab has 27 entities. Here are two of them, as they appear in entities.json:

[
  {
    "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" },
    "attrs": {
      "department": "design",
      "tenant": { "type": "Tenant", "id": "acme" },
      "projects": ["apollo"]
    },
    "parents": [{ "type": "Group", "id": "design-team" }]
  },
  {
    "uid": { "type": "Document", "id": "roadmap" },
    "attrs": {
      "owner": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" },
      "department": "design",
      "sharedWith": [{ "type": "User", "id": "bob" }]
    },
    "parents": [{ "type": "Folder", "id": "design" }],
    "tags": { "project": "apollo", "classification": "internal" }
  }
]

The cast is small. Keep this table open while you read.

NameGroupsTenantDepartment
alicedesign-team (inside editors)acmedesign
bobviewersacmesales
carolcontractorsacmedesign
daveeditorsglobexdesign
erinadminsacmeit
malloryeditors and denylistacmedesign
oscaroncallacmeit

The document roadmap sits in folder design, inside acme-root, inside tenant acme. Alice owns it. It is shared with Bob. The document pricing sits directly in acme-root, and Bob owns it.

How each pattern is tested

Every pattern has a test suite. A small Rust program reads it and calls the real authorizer of the cedar-policy crate, version 4.14.1. It prints one line per request: the name, the decision, the policy ids in the reason, and ok when the decision matches our expectation. Cedar in Rust shows how to build such a program.

Validator messages come from the cedar CLI, version 4.14.1. Long messages are shortened here. We keep the main lines and drop the code snippet.

A forbid alone never allows anything. To watch a forbid work, the test adds one wide helper policy, test.allow-all. It says permit (principal, action, resource);. It lives only in tests, never in production.

Five families

The fifteen patterns fall into five families. Each family answers a different question about the request. Open a branch to see its patterns.

Membership looks at your groups. Relationship looks at your link to one thing. Attributes compare facts. Context looks at the moment of the request. Guardrails are hard limits that no other rule can break.

Membership patterns: 1 and 2

Membership patterns give access because of the groups you are in. People call this RBAC. The group list lives in your entity data, not in the policy.

Pattern 1: Role through group membership

You saw it at the top of the chapter. Alice is not in editors directly. She is in design-team, and design-team is in editors. The in operator is transitive, so it follows the chain. Details are in Entities and hierarchy.

@id("editors.view-edit")
permit (principal == Group::"editors", action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"], resource is Document);
policy set validation passed
    Diagnostic severity: warning
    Caused by: for policy `editors.view-edit`, unable to find an applicable action given the policy scope constraints
diagnostic help: try replacing `==` with `in` in the principal clause
@id("editors.view-edit")
permit (principal in Group::"editors", action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"], resource is Document);
validate (strict): ok, 1 policies

With ==, the principal must be the group itself. A group never sends a request, so the policy can never match. The validator calls it impossible. Note the word passed: a warning does not fail validation unless you ask for it.

Pattern 2: Read-only role

// Pattern 2: read-only role
@id("viewers.read-only")
permit (
  principal in Group::"viewers",
  action in Action::"readActions",
  resource
);
bob comment pricing          Allow reasons=["viewers.read-only"] ok
bob edit pricing             Deny  reasons=[] ok

The action group does the work. readActions holds view and comment. Later you can add a new read action to the schema. Then every read-only policy covers it at once.

Writing action == Action::"readActions". Nobody ever asks for the group action itself, so the validator warns again: "unable to find an applicable action given the policy scope constraints". Use in with action groups.

Relationship patterns: 3 to 5

Relationship patterns give access because of a link between you and one resource. People call this ReBAC. The link can be an attribute, like owner, or a parent, like a folder.

Pattern 3: Owner can edit

// Pattern 3: owner can edit
@id("owner.full-control")
permit (
  principal is User,
  action in [Action::"readActions", Action::"writeActions"],
  resource is Document
)
when { resource.owner == principal };
alice delete roadmap         Allow reasons=["owner.full-control"] ok
bob delete roadmap           Deny  reasons=[] ok

One policy covers every document. The owner is data, so a new document needs no new policy. Compare entities, as here, and never names. A user can change a display name. An entity id stays the same.

Pattern 4: Shared with a user

// Pattern 4: shared with a user
@id("share.direct-view")
permit (
  principal is User,
  action == Action::"view",
  resource is Document
)
when { resource.sharedWith.contains(principal) };
bob view roadmap             Allow reasons=["share.direct-view"] ok
carol view roadmap           Deny  reasons=[] ok

The share list is an attribute: a set of users. A share button in your app adds a user to the set. contains checks exact members only. It does not look inside groups. To share with a team, store a set of groups and write principal in resource.sharedWithTeams. Modeling RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC tests both forms. Another way to build sharing uses policy templates.

Pattern 5: Folder inheritance

// Pattern 5: folder inheritance
@id("design-team.read-design-folder")
permit (
  principal in Group::"design-team",
  action in Action::"readActions",
  resource in Folder::"design"
);
alice view roadmap           Allow reasons=["design-team.read-design-folder"] ok
alice view pricing           Deny  reasons=[] ok

A grant on a folder flows down to everything inside it, at any depth. The roadmap is in the design folder. Pricing is not.

The app sends the roadmap entity without its parent folder.

alice view roadmap, no parent Deny  reasons=[] MISMATCH

The app sends each entity with its parents, up to the top.

alice view roadmap           Allow reasons=["design-team.read-design-folder"] ok

Cedar only knows the parents you send. A missing parent makes the document look foreign. The result is a Deny. That is safe, but users see a bug.

Attribute patterns: 6 and 7

Attribute patterns compare facts about the principal and the resource. People call this ABAC.

Pattern 6: Attribute match

// Pattern 6: attribute match
@id("dept.read-own-department")
permit (
  principal is User,
  action in Action::"readActions",
  resource is Document
)
when { principal.department == resource.department };
carol view roadmap           Allow reasons=["dept.read-own-department"] ok
carol view pricing           Deny  reasons=[] ok

Carol works in design. The roadmap belongs to design, and pricing belongs to sales. String comparison in Cedar is exact. Here is the CLI on one small expression:

cedar evaluate -l 'User::"a"' -a 'Action::"view"' -r 'Document::"d"' '"Design" == "design"'
false

So normalize the data before you call Cedar. Pick lower case, for example, and use it everywhere.

Pattern 7: Tag-based access

// Pattern 7: tag-based access
@id("tags.project-members-view")
permit (
  principal is User,
  action == Action::"view",
  resource is Document
)
when {
  resource.hasTag("project") &&
  principal.projects.contains(resource.getTag("project"))
};
alice view roadmap           Allow reasons=["tags.project-members-view"] ok
alice view pricing           Deny  reasons=[] ok

The roadmap has the tag project = apollo, and Alice works on apollo. Tags suit labels that users invent at run time. Attributes suit fixed fields that you know when you write the schema. Entity tags are stable since cedar-policy 4.2.0.

@id("tags.project-members-view")
permit (principal is User, action == Action::"view", resource is Document)
when { principal.projects.contains(resource.getTag("project")) };
policy set validation failed
    Diagnostic severity: error
    Caused by: for policy `tags.project-members-view`, unable to guarantee safety of access to tag `"project"` on entity type `Document`
diagnostic help: try testing for the tag's presence with `.hasTag("project") && ..`
@id("tags.project-members-view")
permit (principal is User, action == Action::"view", resource is Document)
when {
  resource.hasTag("project") &&
  principal.projects.contains(resource.getTag("project"))
};
validate (strict): ok, 1 policies

Cedar has no loops. You cannot say "every tag of the user matches every tag of the document". Name each tag key you want to check.

Context patterns: 8 to 10

Context patterns look at the moment of the request: the time, the network and the login. Your app puts these facts in the context. Context and requests explains who fills it and why you must trust it.

Pattern 8: Time window

// Pattern 8: time window (times are UTC)
@id("contractors.office-hours")
permit (
  principal in Group::"contractors",
  action in Action::"readActions",
  resource is Document
)
when {
  context.now.toTime() >= duration("9h") &&
  context.now.toTime() < duration("17h")
};
carol view 10:00Z            Allow reasons=["contractors.office-hours"] ok
carol view 20:00Z            Deny  reasons=[] ok
carol view 10:00+0900        Deny  reasons=[] ok

Look at the third line. Ten in the morning in Tokyo is one in the morning in UTC. The method toTime() works in UTC. The CLI shows it:

cedar evaluate -l 'User::"a"' -a 'Action::"view"' -r 'Document::"d"' 'datetime("2026-11-16T10:00:00+0900").toTime()'
duration("3600000ms")

That is 3,600,000 milliseconds, or one hour after midnight UTC. Write your office hours in UTC. The datetime type has been stable since version 4.4.0.

Pattern 9: IP range

// Pattern 9: IP range
@id("guard.writes-from-office-network")
forbid (principal, action in Action::"writeActions", resource)
unless { context.sourceIp.isInRange(ip("10.0.0.0/8")) };
alice edit from 10.1.2.3     Allow reasons=["test.allow-all"] ok
alice edit from 203.0.113.9  Deny  reasons=["guard.writes-from-office-network"] ok

10.0.0.0/8 is a CIDR range. This pattern is a guardrail, so it uses forbid ... unless. Read it as: "Forbid writes, unless the request comes from the office network." Every write permit, today and in the future, now needs the office network.

The docs show isInRange with several ranges. In cedar-policy 4.14.1 it takes exactly one. With two ranges the CLI says: "wrong number of arguments provided to extension function `isInRange`: expected 2, got 3". The changelog lists the many-range form for 4.15.0. Until then, join ranges with ||.

Pattern 10: MFA required for sensitive actions

// Pattern 10: MFA required for sensitive actions
@id("guard.mfa-for-delete-and-share")
forbid (principal, action in [Action::"delete", Action::"share"], resource)
unless { context.mfa };
alice delete, mfa            Allow reasons=["test.allow-all"] ok
alice delete, no mfa         Deny  reasons=["guard.mfa-for-delete-and-share"] ok

MFA means the user passed a second login step. Put the check in one forbid, not in every permit. If you put it in the permits, someone will forget it in the next new permit.

Guardrail patterns: 11 to 15

A guardrail is a forbid that sets a hard limit. In Cedar, forbid overrides permit. So a guardrail holds even when some permit matches.

Pattern 11: Deny list with forbid

// Pattern 11: deny list with forbid
@id("guard.denylist")
forbid (principal in Group::"denylist", action, resource);
alice edit roadmap           Allow reasons=["editors.view-edit"] ok
mallory edit roadmap         Deny  reasons=["guard.denylist"] ok

This test runs pattern 1 and pattern 11 together. Mallory is an editor, so pattern 1 matches her. She is also on the deny list, and the forbid wins.

// WRONG: guardrail reads an attribute that may be missing
@id("guard.suspended")
forbid (principal, action, resource)
when { principal.suspended };
alice edit roadmap           Allow reasons=["test.allow-all"] errors=["error while evaluating policy `guard.suspended`: `User::\"alice\"` does not have the attribute `suspended`"] MISMATCH

Use group membership, as in pattern 11. Or make the attribute required in the schema and validate. Or guard it: when { principal has suspended && principal.suspended }.

This run had no schema. Alice has no suspended attribute, so the forbid hit an error. A policy that errors is skipped. The guardrail was silently off. A schema and strict validation catch this before it ships.

Pattern 12: Tenant isolation

// Pattern 12: tenant isolation
@id("guard.tenant-isolation")
forbid (principal is User, action, resource)
unless { resource in principal.tenant };
alice view roadmap           Allow reasons=["test.allow-all"] ok
dave view roadmap            Deny  reasons=["guard.tenant-isolation"] ok

Every folder sits under a tenant, so resource in principal.tenant asks: "Is this thing inside my company?" Dave works for globex. The roadmap belongs to acme.

// WRONG: tenant check written as a permit
@id("tenant.same-tenant")
permit (principal is User, action, resource)
when { resource in principal.tenant };
dave view roadmap (wrong)    Allow reasons=["editors.view-edit"] MISMATCH
@id("guard.tenant-isolation")
forbid (principal is User, action, resource)
unless { resource in principal.tenant };
dave view roadmap (right)    Deny  reasons=["guard.tenant-isolation"] ok

Both runs also contain pattern 1, and Dave is an editor. A permit cannot stop another permit. Only a forbid can.

Pattern 13: Admin override with a forbid guardrail

// Pattern 13: admin override with a forbid guardrail
@id("admins.full-access")
permit (principal in Group::"admins", action, resource);

@id("guard.audit-log-append-only")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"deleteLog", resource);
erin delete roadmap          Allow reasons=["admins.full-access"] ok
erin deleteLog acme-log      Deny  reasons=["guard.audit-log-append-only"] ok

Admins may do almost anything. Nobody may delete the audit log, not even an admin. Write the wide permit and the narrow forbid as two policies. Each one then shows up by name in the reason.

Pattern 14: Break-glass with a reason

// Pattern 14: break-glass with a reason
@id("oncall.break-glass-read")
permit (
  principal in Group::"oncall",
  action in [Action::"view", Action::"readLog"],
  resource
)
when {
  context has breakGlass &&
  context.breakGlass.reason != "" &&
  context.breakGlass.ticket like "INC-*"
};
oscar readLog, reason        Allow reasons=["oncall.break-glass-read"] ok
oscar readLog, no reason     Deny  reasons=[] ok
oscar readLog, bad ticket    Deny  reasons=[] ok

Break-glass access is for emergencies. The on-call engineer must give a reason and an incident ticket. The like operator matches the ticket with a wildcard. Log every allowed break-glass request, and review the log later.

Leaving out context has breakGlass. The field is optional, so strict validation stops you: "unable to guarantee safety of access to optional attribute `breakGlass` in context for Action::"readLog"". The help line says: try context has breakGlass && ...

Pattern 15: The AI agent egress rule

// Untrusted input may never drive a tool that sends data out.
@id("taint.no-untrusted-egress")
forbid (principal is Agent, action == Action::"call_tool", resource is Tool)
when { context.taint == "untrusted" && resource.egress };
helper send_email, trusted   Allow reasons=["agents.call-tools"] ok
helper send_email, untrusted Deny  reasons=["taint.no-untrusted-egress"] ok
helper read_file, untrusted  Allow reasons=["agents.call-tools"] ok

This is your own rule. Egress means data goes out, like an email. Taint marks input from an untrusted place, like a web page. The test adds one permit, agents.call-tools, that lets the agent call any tool. The forbid then blocks one dangerous mix: untrusted input plus an outbound tool. Reading a file stays allowed. Cedar for AI agents and tool calls explains every token and builds a full model around it.

All fifteen patterns, tested

Each slide shows one pattern with its two key requests. Every decision below came from the real engine. All 33 requests in the 15 suites matched what we expected.

Role through group membership

principal in Group::"editors"

Alice edits the roadmap: Allow (editors.view-edit).

Bob edits the roadmap: Deny (no policy says yes).

Read-only role

action in Action::"readActions"

Bob comments on pricing: Allow (viewers.read-only).

Bob edits pricing: Deny (no policy says yes).

Bob deletes the roadmap: Deny (not the owner).

Bob views the roadmap: Allow (share.direct-view).

Carol views the roadmap: Deny (not in the set).

resource in Folder::"design"

Alice views the roadmap: Allow (design-team.read-design-folder).

Alice views pricing: Deny (other folder).

principal.department == resource.department

Carol views the roadmap: Allow (dept.read-own-department).

Carol views pricing: Deny (sales, not design).

Alice views the roadmap: Allow (tags.project-members-view).

Alice views pricing: Deny (tag zeus).

Alice (acme) views the roadmap: Allow.

Trusted input, send_email: Allow (agents.call-tools).

Untrusted input, send_email: Deny (taint.no-untrusted-egress).

Expert level: combining patterns

Real apps load many patterns at once. So we loaded all fifteen patterns and the agent permit into one policy set. That makes 17 policies. Strict validation passed. Here are seven requests:

validate (strict): ok, 17 policies
alice view roadmap           Allow reasons=["dept.read-own-department", "design-team.read-design-folder", "editors.view-edit", "owner.full-control", "tags.project-members-view"] ok
bob view roadmap             Allow reasons=["share.direct-view", "viewers.read-only"] ok
erin delete, no mfa          Deny  reasons=["guard.mfa-for-delete-and-share"] ok
erin edit from 203.0.113.9   Deny  reasons=["guard.writes-from-office-network"] ok
dave view roadmap            Deny  reasons=["guard.tenant-isolation"] ok
mallory view roadmap         Deny  reasons=["guard.denylist"] ok
oscar readLog globex, bg     Deny  reasons=["guard.tenant-isolation"] MISMATCH

Read the lines one by one:

  1. For Alice, five permits matched. The reason lists all of them. One match is enough for Allow.
  2. Erin is an admin, but the MFA guardrail and the network guardrail still stop her.
  3. Mallory matches four permits. One forbid beats all of them.
  4. The last line is a real design bug. Oscar used break-glass for a globex log during an outage. The tenant guardrail blocked him.

You cannot fix this with a new permit, because forbid always wins. Put the exception inside the forbid:

// Pattern 12, version 2: on-call staff with a break-glass reason may cross tenants.
@id("guard.tenant-isolation")
forbid (principal is User, action, resource)
unless {
  resource in principal.tenant ||
  (principal in Group::"oncall" && context has breakGlass)
};
validate (strict): ok, 17 policies
oscar readLog globex, bg     Allow reasons=["oncall.break-glass-read"] ok
alice view globex-plan       Deny  reasons=["guard.tenant-isolation"] ok
oscar edit globex-plan, bg   Deny  reasons=[] ok
dave view roadmap, bg        Deny  reasons=["guard.tenant-isolation"] ok

Look at the third line. The exception only lifts the guardrail. It grants nothing by itself. Oscar still needs a permit, and no permit lets him edit. Dave sends a break-glass field too, but he is not on call.

Three facts decide most combination questions. Any matching forbid gives Deny. Otherwise any matching permit gives Allow. Otherwise the answer is Deny. The order of the policies never matters.

How to choose a pattern

  1. Is the rule a hard limit that must hold for every user? Use a guardrail: forbid ... unless.
  2. Does access come from a group or role? Use pattern 1 or 2.
  3. Does it come from a link to one resource? Use pattern 3, 4 or 5.
  4. Does it compare two facts? Use pattern 6, or pattern 7 for run-time labels.
  5. Does it depend on the moment of the request? Add a context condition, as in patterns 8 to 10.
  6. Write one allow test and one deny test before you merge the policy.

A solver can also check combinations for you. It can answer questions like "Can a globex user ever read an acme document?"

Patterns to remember

The 15 Cedar policy patterns, each tested with an allow and a deny case
PatternPurposeModelNote
1 Role through group membershipGive a role its actionspermit (principal in Group::"editors", action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"], resource is Document);Use in, not ==. A group never sends a request.
2 Read-only roleLet a role read, never writepermit (principal in Group::"viewers", action in Action::"readActions", resource);Use in with an action group. New read actions join in the schema.
3 Owner can editOwners control their own thingspermit (principal is User, action in [Action::"readActions", Action::"writeActions"], resource is Document) when { resource.owner == principal };Compare entities, never display names.
4 Shared with a userA share buttonpermit (principal is User, action == Action::"view", resource is Document) when { resource.sharedWith.contains(principal) };contains sees exact members only. Use in for groups.
5 Folder inheritanceA folder grant covers everything insidepermit (principal in Group::"design-team", action in Action::"readActions", resource in Folder::"design");Send every parent in the entities, or the answer is Deny.
6 Attribute matchSame department, same regionpermit (principal is User, action in Action::"readActions", resource is Document) when { principal.department == resource.department };Strings must match exactly. Normalize the data first.
7 Tag-based accessLabels that users create at run timewhen { resource.hasTag("project") && principal.projects.contains(resource.getTag("project")) }Always hasTag before getTag. Name each key, Cedar has no loops.
8 Time windowAccess only in working hourswhen { context.now.toTime() >= duration("9h") && context.now.toTime() < duration("17h") }toTime() is UTC. Convert local hours.
9 IP rangeWrites only from the office networkforbid (principal, action in Action::"writeActions", resource) unless { context.sourceIp.isInRange(ip("10.0.0.0/8")) };In 4.14.1, isInRange takes one range. Join more with ||.
10 MFA for sensitive actionsSecond login step before delete or shareforbid (principal, action in [Action::"delete", Action::"share"], resource) unless { context.mfa };One forbid, not a check in every permit.
11 Deny listBlock named users everywhereforbid (principal in Group::"denylist", action, resource);A guardrail on a missing attribute errors and is skipped. Use groups or a schema.
12 Tenant isolationOne customer never sees anotherforbid (principal is User, action, resource) unless { resource in principal.tenant };Never write it as a permit. Another permit could bypass it.
13 Admin with a guardrailWide power with one hard limitpermit (principal in Group::"admins", action, resource); plus forbid (principal, action == Action::"deleteLog", resource);No permit can undo a forbid, not even an admin permit.
14 Break-glass with a reasonEmergency access that leaves a trailwhen { context has breakGlass && context.breakGlass.reason != "" && context.breakGlass.ticket like "INC-*" }Check has first. A guardrail exception must live inside the forbid.
15 AI agent egressUntrusted input never sends data outforbid (principal is Agent, action == Action::"call_tool", resource is Tool) when { context.taint == "untrusted" && resource.egress };It only works if the gateway sets taint honestly.

Practice

Mallory is in editors and in denylist. Patterns 1 and 11 are loaded. She asks to edit the roadmap. What is the decision?

  • Allow, because the editors permit matches.
  • Deny, because the deny-list forbid matches.
  • It depends on which policy comes first in the file.

A matching forbid always wins. Policy order never matters.

Which line gives the validator warning "policy is impossible"?

  • principal in Group::"editors"
  • principal == Group::"editors"
  • principal is User

Users send requests, groups do not. With == the principal must be the group itself, which never happens.

A request has now = 2026-11-16T10:00:00+0900. Pattern 8 allows 9h to 17h. What happens?

  • Allow, because it is ten in the morning.
  • Deny, because toTime() gives one in the morning UTC.
  • An error, because of the time zone.

The real run printed Deny. Time zones are accepted, but toTime() works in UTC.

Before you call getTag("project"), check ? first.

Guardrails use forbid with the keyword ?, so that every future permit is covered.

  1. Write a policy: members of Group::"auditors" may use readLog on any audit log.
    Answer

    This is pattern 2 with other names: @id("auditors.read-logs") permit (principal in Group::"auditors", action == Action::"readLog", resource is AuditLog);. We tested it with a new user, Ann, in the auditors group. "ann readLog" gave Allow with reason auditors.read-logs. "ann deleteLog" gave Deny.

  2. A teammate writes tenant isolation as permit ... when { resource in principal.tenant }. Explain the bug in two sentences.
    Answer

    A permit only adds access. Any other permit, like the editors permit, can still allow a user from another tenant, as Dave showed. Write it as forbid ... unless { resource in principal.tenant }.

  3. Why did the forbid when { principal.suspended } fail to block Alice?
    Answer

    Alice has no suspended attribute. Reading it raised an error. Cedar skips a policy that errors, so the forbid did nothing and test.allow-all allowed the request.

  4. Allow writes from two networks, 10.0.0.0/8 and 192.168.0.0/16, in cedar-policy 4.14.1.
    Answer

    unless { context.sourceIp.isInRange(ip("10.0.0.0/8")) || context.sourceIp.isInRange(ip("192.168.0.0/16")) }. The many-range call fails in 4.14.1 with "expected 2, got 3".

  5. The security team wants break-glass users to cross tenants, but only for reading. Where do you put the exception, and why is it safe?
    Answer

    Put it inside the tenant forbid: unless { resource in principal.tenant || (principal in Group::"oncall" && context has breakGlass) }. It is safe because the exception only lifts the forbid. Access still needs a permit, and the break-glass permit lists only view and readLog. The real run showed Oscar's edit as Deny.

  6. Design a pattern 16 for your own app: "contractors may never share documents". Give the policy and two tests.
    Answer

    forbid (principal in Group::"contractors", action == Action::"share", resource);. Give it the id guard.contractors-no-share. We ran it with test.allow-all. Carol sharing the roadmap gave Deny with reason guard.contractors-no-share. Alice sharing it gave Allow. The shape is pattern 11 with a narrower action.

Key takeaways

  • Fifteen shapes cover most rules: membership, relationship, attributes, context and guardrails.
  • Every pattern needs two tests: one Allow and one Deny. All 33 lab requests matched.
  • Guardrails are forbid ... unless. No permit can undo them.
  • Exceptions to a guardrail live inside the forbid. They lift the block but grant nothing.
  • Guard optional data with has and hasTag. An error skips the policy, which can switch a guardrail off.
  • Check versions: toTime() is UTC, and isInRange takes one range in 4.14.1.

Sources

  • Cedar design patterns. Membership, relationship and discretionary patterns in the official docs.
  • Cedar operators. hasTag, getTag, isInRange, toTime and the other methods used here.
  • Populate the policy scope. Why patterns put roles and tenants in the scope.
  • cedar-policy changelog. Versions for entity tags (4.2.0), datetime (4.4.0) and the many-range isInRange (4.15.0).
  • cedar-policy on crates.io. Version 4.14.1, released 2026-11-02, used for every test on this page.

Modeling RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC

Good policies start with a good entity model. This chapter builds one real model step by step: an issue tracker that many companies share. You will add roles, then attributes, then relationships, then guardrails, and test all of it with a 42-cell matrix.

  • Turn written access rules into entity types, actions, attributes and parents.
  • Write RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC policies, and say what each one cannot do alone.
  • Translate a Zanzibar or OpenFGA model into Cedar templates and policies.
  • Build a multi-tenant model with a schema, policies and a tested decision matrix.
  • Choose between the models by their trade-offs.

Design an entity model step by step

Here are the rules for Tracker, an issue tracker for many companies. A product manager wrote them in plain words.

  1. R1. Nobody may see data from another company.
  2. R2. Admins may do anything inside their company.
  3. R3. Teams on a project may read its issues and create new ones.
  4. R4. Maintainers of a project may read, edit and close its issues.
  5. R5. The reporter and the assignee of an issue may edit it.
  6. R6. Staff may read issues. Inactive users may do nothing. Nobody reads an issue above their clearance.

An entity model turns rules like these into data that Cedar understands. Follow six steps. Do them in this order.

  1. Find the nouns. Company, user, team, role, project, issue. Each noun that a rule talks about becomes an entity type.
  2. Find the verbs. View, comment, edit, close, create, delete. Each verb becomes an action. Group similar actions into action groups.
  3. Find the "inside" links. An issue is inside a project. A project is inside a company. These become parents, so in can follow them.
  4. Find the facts. Clearance, active, sensitivity. These become attributes.
  5. Find the named links. Reporter, assignee, maintainers, member teams. These become attributes that point to other entities.
  6. Write the schema, the policies and a test matrix. One policy per rule.

The graph shows the result. Solid "in" arrows are parents. The other arrows are attributes that point to entities. Drag the nodes to see it better.

Use a parent when the link means "is inside" and you want in to follow it, step after step. Use an attribute when the link has a name, like reporter or owner. An issue has one reporter, but it is not inside the reporter.

The schema

Here is the whole schema. It passed strict validation with all nine policies in this chapter.

// Tracker: an issue tracker shared by many companies (tenants).
entity Tenant;
entity Role;
entity Team in [Team];
entity User in [Team, Role] {
  tenant: Tenant,
  active: Bool,
  clearance: Long,
};
entity Project in [Tenant] {
  members: Set<Team>,
  maintainers: Set<User>,
};
entity Issue in [Project] {
  project: Project,
  reporter: User,
  assignee?: User,
  sensitivity: Long,
};

action readActions, writeActions;
action viewIssue, comment in [readActions] appliesTo {
  principal: User, resource: Issue,
};
action editIssue, closeIssue in [writeActions] appliesTo {
  principal: User, resource: Issue,
};
action createIssue appliesTo { principal: User, resource: Project };
action deleteProject appliesTo { principal: User, resource: Project };

Notice two choices. An issue has the parent Project and also the attribute project. The parent lets in reach the tenant. The attribute lets a policy read resource.project.members. Second, assignee is optional, because new issues have none.

Modeling only what today's rules need. Then a new rule needs a schema change, a data change and a policy change at once. Spend ten more minutes on the nouns and links. A real company tracker always has projects and teams.

Pure RBAC with groups

Start with the easiest model. RBAC gives access through roles. In Cedar, a role is a group entity, and a user lists it as a parent.

// RBAC: access comes from the role groups a user is in.
@id("rbac.admin-everything")
permit (principal in Role::"admin", action, resource);

@id("rbac.staff-read")
permit (principal in Role::"staff", action in Action::"readActions", resource is Issue);

We ran three requests with only these two policies:

validate (strict): ok, 2 policies
ana viewIssue GLX-1          Allow reasons=["rbac.admin-everything"]
ben viewIssue ISS-1          Deny  reasons=[]
fay viewIssue ISS-2          Allow reasons=["rbac.staff-read"]

All three answers are wrong for our rules:

  • Ana is an admin at acme. GLX-1 belongs to globex. RBAC let her in, which breaks R1.
  • Ben is on the team that works on ISS-1. RBAC has no idea about teams on projects, which breaks R3.
  • Fay has clearance 0. ISS-2 has sensitivity 2. RBAC does not look at facts, which breaks R6.

RBAC answers "what is your job?". It cannot answer "which company?", "which project?" or "how secret?". The Cedar docs call this pattern membership permissions.

Role explosion

You could fix Ben with more roles: Role::"apollo-reader", Role::"hermes-reader", and so on. That is one role per project per job. With 500 projects and 4 jobs, you get 2,000 roles. This is role explosion. The usual fixes are relationships (this chapter) or policy templates.

Keep roles for real jobs that apply to the whole company: admin, staff, auditor. Use relationships for "this person on this project".

ABAC with attributes

ABAC compares facts. Rule R6 has two facts: active and clearance. Both are limits, so we write them as forbids.

// ABAC guardrails: facts about the user and the issue.
@id("abac.guard.inactive-user")
forbid (principal is User, action, resource)
unless { principal.active };

@id("abac.guard.clearance")
forbid (principal is User, action, resource is Issue)
unless { principal.clearance >= resource.sensitivity };
validate (strict): ok, 4 policies
ana viewIssue GLX-1          Allow reasons=["rbac.admin-everything"]
ben viewIssue ISS-1          Deny  reasons=[]
fay viewIssue ISS-2          Deny  reasons=["abac.guard.clearance"]

Fay is fixed. Her role still says yes, but the clearance forbid says no, and forbid wins. Notice that both attributes are required in the schema. A missing attribute would make the forbid error, and Cedar would skip it. Required attributes plus strict validation stop that.

ABAC is flexible. One policy covers every issue and every user. But it hides the "who" inside the condition. A reader of the policy cannot list who has access without the data.

Using ABAC for roles: when { principal.role == "admin" }. This works, but the scope is empty, so every request must check the policy. The Cedar best-practice guide says to put roles in the scope instead: principal in Role::"admin".

ReBAC: relationships to projects and issues

ReBAC gives access through a link between the user and the resource. Rules R3, R4 and R5 are all links.

// ReBAC: access comes from links between the user and the project or issue.
@id("rebac.project-team-read")
permit (principal is User, action in Action::"readActions", resource is Issue)
when { principal in resource.project.members };

@id("rebac.project-create")
permit (principal is User, action == Action::"createIssue", resource is Project)
when { principal in resource.members || principal in resource.maintainers };

@id("rebac.maintainer-read-write")
permit (
  principal is User,
  action in [Action::"readActions", Action::"writeActions"],
  resource is Issue
)
when { principal in resource.project.maintainers };

@id("rebac.reporter-or-assignee-edit")
permit (principal is User, action == Action::"editIssue", resource is Issue)
when {
  resource.reporter == principal ||
  (resource has assignee && resource.assignee == principal)
};
validate (strict): ok, 8 policies
ana viewIssue GLX-1          Allow reasons=["rbac.admin-everything"]
ben viewIssue ISS-1          Allow reasons=["rebac.project-team-read"]
fay viewIssue ISS-2          Deny  reasons=["abac.guard.clearance"]

Ben is fixed. He is in Team web, and Team web is in the members set of project apollo. Look at the expression principal in resource.project.members. When the right side of in is a set, Cedar asks: "Is the principal in any of these?" It follows parents, so team members count.

contains does not follow parents

In pattern 4 we used contains for a set of users. Can we use it for a set of teams? Try it:

@id("demo.contains")
permit (principal is User, action == Action::"viewIssue", resource is Issue)
when { resource.project.members.contains(principal) };
policy set validation failed
    Diagnostic severity: error
    Caused by: the types Team and User are not compatible
diagnostic help: for policy `demo.contains`, elements of the first operand and the second operand to a `contains` expression must have compatible types. Different entity types are never compatible even when their attributes would be compatible
@id("rebac.project-team-read")
permit (principal is User, action in Action::"readActions", resource is Issue)
when { principal in resource.project.members };
ben viewIssue ISS-1          Allow reasons=["rebac.project-team-read"]

The validator stops the wrong version. If you skip validation, the policy runs and simply says no. We tried it: Ben got Deny, because contains checks exact equality only. A set of teams never equals a user.

Where relationship data lives

The Cedar docs give two ways to store a relationship:

  • As attributes, like maintainers here. Choose this when your app database already stores the link for other reasons.
  • As template-linked policies. Each link becomes one small policy. Choose this when the link exists only for permissions, like a share button.

The next section uses the second way.

Translating a Zanzibar or OpenFGA model

Zanzibar is Google's authorization system, described in a 2019 USENIX paper. OpenFGA is an open-source system in the same style. Both store relationship tuples and compute access from them. Here is a small OpenFGA model for folders and documents. We wrote it for this chapter. We did not run OpenFGA here, so treat it as a picture of the idea.

model
  schema 1.1

type user

type team
  relations
    define member: [user]

type folder
  relations
    define parent: [folder]
    define viewer: [user, team#member] or viewer from parent

type doc
  relations
    define parent: [folder]
    define owner: [user]
    define blocked: [user]
    define editor: ([user, team#member] or owner) but not blocked
    define viewer: ([user, team#member] or editor or viewer from parent) but not blocked

Each OpenFGA idea has a Cedar partner. Learn this table, and you can translate most models.

OpenFGACedar
type docentity Doc
tuple: user ana is member of team webUser ana has the parent Team web
define parent: [folder]entity Doc in [Folder], and the parent in the entities
define viewer: [user, team#member]a template principal in ?principal, one link per tuple
viewer from parentresource in ?resource, because in follows every folder level
or editornest the action groups: viewer actions inside editor actions
or owneran attribute owner and one static permit
but not blockeda forbid

Here is the Cedar version. The schema nests the action groups, so "editor" includes "viewer".

// The OpenFGA model, translated to Cedar.
entity Team;
entity User in [Team];
entity Folder in [Folder];
entity Doc in [Folder] {
  owner: User,
  blocked: Set<User>,
};

action editorActions;
action viewerActions in [editorActions];
action read in [viewerActions] appliesTo {
  principal: User, resource: [Doc, Folder],
};
action write in [editorActions] appliesTo {
  principal: User, resource: [Doc, Folder],
};
// One template per relation. One link per relationship tuple.
@id("rel.viewer")
permit (principal in ?principal, action in Action::"viewerActions", resource in ?resource);

@id("rel.editor")
permit (principal in ?principal, action in Action::"editorActions", resource in ?resource);

// "or owner": the owner is an attribute, so one static policy covers it.
@id("rel.owner")
permit (principal is User, action in Action::"editorActions", resource is Doc)
when { resource.owner == principal };

// "but not blocked": a forbid.
@id("rel.blocked")
forbid (principal is User, action, resource is Doc)
when { resource.blocked.contains(principal) };

Two tuples become two links. The format is the one the cedar CLI reads with --template-linked:

[
  {"template_id": "rel.viewer", "link_id": "tuple-1",
   "args": {"?principal": "Team::\"web\"", "?resource": "Folder::\"eng\""}},
  {"template_id": "rel.editor", "link_id": "tuple-2",
   "args": {"?principal": "User::\"bo\"", "?resource": "Doc::\"api\""}}
]

The data: Ana and Dee are in Team web. Folder specs is inside folder eng. Doc api is inside specs. Cy owns api, and Dee is blocked on it. We asked the same questions that an OpenFGA check would ask:

validate (strict): ok, 4 policies
ana read api                 Allow reasons=["tuple-1"] ok
ana write api                Deny  reasons=[] ok
bo read api                  Allow reasons=["tuple-2"] ok
bo write api                 Allow reasons=["tuple-2"] ok
cy write api                 Allow reasons=["rel.owner"] ok
dee read api                 Deny  reasons=["rel.blocked"] ok
eli read api                 Deny  reasons=[] ok

Ana reads api through two folder levels, from one tuple on folder eng. Bo is an editor, so he may also read. Dee is in Team web, but the forbid blocks her. The CLI gives the same answer for Ana:

cedar authorize --schema schema.cedarschema --policies policies.cedar --template-linked links.json --entities entities.json -l 'User::"ana"' -a 'Action::"read"' -r 'Doc::"api"' -v
ALLOW

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  tuple-1

OpenFGA stores all tuples on its server and walks the graph for you. Cedar stores no data. Your app must send the entities for each request: the user with all its parent teams, and the document with all its parent folders. The links live in the policy store. This is the biggest practical difference.

Not every OpenFGA model translates this easily. An OpenFGA relation can depend on a relation of an object that the tuples point to, many levels deep. Cedar has no loops, so you must turn such chains into parents or attributes before the request. Test each translated relation, as we did here.

The multi-tenant model, end to end

Back to Tracker. One rule is still broken: Ana, an acme admin, can read a globex issue. Add the tenant guardrail from pattern 12:

// Guardrail: nobody leaves their own tenant.
@id("tenant.guard.isolation")
forbid (principal is User, action, resource)
unless { resource in principal.tenant };
validate (strict): ok, 9 policies
ana viewIssue GLX-1          Deny  reasons=["tenant.guard.isolation"]
ben viewIssue ISS-1          Allow reasons=["rebac.project-team-read"]
fay viewIssue ISS-2          Deny  reasons=["abac.guard.clearance"]

All three answers are now right. The global role "admin" plus the tenant guardrail gives "admin of my own company". That works because each user here belongs to one tenant. If one person can work for two companies, give each company its own role group, like Role::"acme-admin".

The data

The test data has two tenants, six users, two projects and three issues:

UserTenantParentsActiveClearanceLinks
anaacmeRole adminyes2
benacmeTeam webyes1assignee of ISS-1
cleoacmeTeam mobileyes2maintainer of apollo, reporter of ISS-2
danglobexRole admin, Team coreyes2reporter of GLX-1
eveacmeTeam webno1
fayacmeTeam mobile, Role staffyes0reporter of ISS-1

Project apollo (acme) has member team web and maintainer cleo. Project hermes (globex) has member team core. ISS-1 and ISS-2 are in apollo, with sensitivity 0 and 2. GLX-1 is in hermes, with sensitivity 0.

The test matrix

A test matrix crosses principals with actions and resources. Before running it, we wrote the expected answer for each cell from rules R1 to R6. Then the engine filled in the real answers. All 42 cells matched:

ben viewIssue ISS-1          Allow reasons=["rebac.project-team-read"] ok
ben editIssue ISS-1          Allow reasons=["rebac.reporter-or-assignee-edit"] ok
ben closeIssue ISS-1         Deny  reasons=[] ok
ben viewIssue ISS-2          Deny  reasons=["abac.guard.clearance"] ok
ben createIssue apollo       Allow reasons=["rebac.project-create"] ok
ben deleteProject apollo     Deny  reasons=[] ok
ben viewIssue GLX-1          Deny  reasons=["tenant.guard.isolation"] ok
expected decisions: 42 ok, 0 mismatch

That is Ben's row of the real output, plus the summary line. Here is the full matrix. Each cell shows the decision and the policy that decided it. "none" means no permit matched, so default deny answered.

Userview ISS-1edit ISS-1close ISS-1view ISS-2create in apollodelete apolloview GLX-1
anaAllow, adminAllow, adminAllow, adminAllow, adminAllow, adminAllow, adminDeny, tenant
benAllow, teamAllow, assigneeDeny, noneDeny, clearanceAllow, teamDeny, noneDeny, tenant
cleoAllow, maintainerAllow, maintainerAllow, maintainerAllow, maintainerAllow, maintainerDeny, noneDeny, tenant
danDeny, tenantDeny, tenantDeny, tenantDeny, tenantDeny, tenantDeny, tenantAllow, admin and team
eveDeny, inactiveDeny, inactiveDeny, inactiveDeny, clearance and inactiveDeny, inactiveDeny, inactiveDeny, inactive and tenant
fayAllow, staffAllow, reporterDeny, noneDeny, clearanceDeny, noneDeny, noneDeny, tenant

Read the matrix in three ways:

  1. By row. Each row is one person's whole access. Eve's row is all Deny, as R6 wants.
  2. By column. Each column is one question. Only Dan may see GLX-1.
  3. By reason. "none" cells are default deny. Named forbids are guardrails at work. Several names in one cell mean several forbids matched.

When a rule changes, add or update its cells first. Then change the policy until the matrix matches again. Testing Cedar policies turns this matrix into golden files and CI checks.

How much data per request

To answer "may Ben view ISS-1?", Cedar needs a small slice of the data:

  • Ben, with his parents: Team web and anything above it.
  • ISS-1, with its parents: project apollo, then tenant acme.
  • Every entity that a policy reads through an attribute: here, project apollo with its members and maintainers.

Your app loads this slice before each call. The part of your system that loads it is the policy information point. Deep attribute chains, like resource.project.members, mean more data to load. Keep chains short.

Trade-offs of each model

Most real systems mix all three models, plus guardrails. The Cedar docs say each policy should follow one pattern, but a policy store can mix patterns. The table helps you choose for each rule.

QuestionRBACABACReBAC
Where does access come from?Role groupsFacts on entities and contextLinks between user and resource
Where is the data?Group parents in the entitiesAttributesAttributes, parents or template links
Who has access to X?Easy: list the groupHard: test every userMedium: follow the links
Scope of the policyFull: principal in Role::...Often empty, logic in whenPartly in scope, partly in when
Main riskRole explosionMissing or dirty attributesLoading the link data for every request
Best forCompany-wide jobsLimits, levels, flags, timeOwnership, sharing, project teams

RBAC

Two policies. Admins do anything. Staff read.

Ana reads a globex issue. Ben cannot read his own project.

ABAC guardrails

Forbid inactive users. Forbid reading above your clearance.

Fay can no longer read the sensitive issue.

ReBAC

Teams on the project read. Maintainers write. Reporter and assignee edit.

Ben reads ISS-1 through Team web.

Tenant guardrail

Forbid everything outside your own tenant.

Ana is now stopped at the globex border.

Test matrix

Six users times seven questions. Expected answers written first.

42 of 42 cells match.

When a question describes a rule, name its model first. "Because of your job" is RBAC. "Because of a fact" is ABAC. "Because of your link to this thing" is ReBAC. "Must never happen" is a forbid guardrail.

Patterns to remember

Modeling patterns for RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC in Cedar
PatternPurposeModelNote
Role as a parent groupCompany-wide jobspermit (principal in Role::"staff", action in Action::"readActions", resource is Issue);One role per job, not per project. Else roles explode.
Fact as a forbidLimits like clearance or activeforbid (principal is User, action, resource is Issue) unless { principal.clearance >= resource.sensitivity };Make the attributes required, or the forbid can error and be skipped.
Team set on the resourceTeams work on a projectwhen { principal in resource.project.members }Use in with a set of groups. contains does not follow parents.
Named link attributeReporter, assignee, ownerwhen { resource.reporter == principal }Guard optional links with has.
Parent for "is inside"Issue in project in tenantentity Issue in [Project];Send every parent in the entities, or in says false.
Relation as a templateTranslate an OpenFGA relationpermit (principal in ?principal, action in Action::"viewerActions", resource in ?resource);One link per tuple. resource in covers "from parent".
Nested action groups"editor implies viewer"action viewerActions in [editorActions];Put the smaller group inside the bigger one.
"but not" as forbidExclusions like blocked usersforbid (...) when { resource.blocked.contains(principal) };Exclusions are always forbids, never missing permits.
Global role plus tenant guardAdmin of my own companyforbid (principal is User, action, resource) unless { resource in principal.tenant };Works when each user has one tenant. Else use per-tenant roles.
Expected-first test matrixProve the model meets the rules6 users x 7 questions, 42 of 42 okWrite the expected answers from the rules before you run.

Practice

"A user may delete a document because they created it." Which model is this?

  • RBAC
  • ABAC
  • ReBAC

It is a link between this user and this one resource: the creator or owner link.

Ben is in Team web. Project apollo has members = [Team::"web"]. Which condition is true for Ben?

  • resource.project.members.contains(principal)
  • principal in resource.project.members
  • Both of them

in follows parents. contains checks exact equality, and the validator rejects it here because the types differ.

How does the OpenFGA phrase viewer from parent translate to Cedar?

  • A loop over all parent folders in a when block.
  • resource in ?resource in a template, with folders as parents.
  • It cannot be translated.

in follows the parent chain at every depth. Cedar has no loops.

In OpenFGA, but not blocked becomes a Cedar ? policy.

Too many roles, one for each job on each project, is called role ?.

  1. Dan is an admin at globex. Why can he not delete project apollo?
    Answer

    Apollo is inside tenant acme, and Dan's tenant is globex. The forbid tenant.guard.isolation matches, and forbid wins over rbac.admin-everything. The matrix cell says "Deny, tenant".

  2. The product manager adds R7: "Guests may comment on issues of projects they are invited to." Which entities, attributes and policy would you add?
    Answer

    Add guests: Set<User> to Project. Add one permit: @id("rebac.guest-comment") permit (principal is User, action == Action::"comment", resource is Issue) when { principal in resource.project.guests };. We tested it with a new guest, Gus, on apollo. "gus comment ISS-1" gave Allow with reason rebac.guest-comment. "gus editIssue ISS-1" and "gus viewIssue ISS-1" gave Deny. Add these cells to the matrix.

  3. Why does Eve's cell for GLX-1 list two policies?
    Answer

    Two forbids matched: Eve is inactive, and GLX-1 is in another tenant. The reason lists every policy that decided the answer. One forbid would be enough for Deny.

  4. Your app lets one consultant work for acme and globex with different roles. What breaks, and how do you fix the model?
    Answer

    The user has one tenant attribute and global roles, so the model cannot say "admin at acme, staff at globex". Change three things. First, replace tenant: Tenant with tenants: Set<Tenant> and use unless { resource in principal.tenants } in the guardrail. Second, make roles per tenant, like Role::"acme-admin". Third, grant each role with a template link: permit (principal in ?principal, action, resource in ?resource); linked to Role::"acme-admin" and Tenant::"acme". We tested this with Kim. She could delete project apollo and read GLX-1, but not delete project hermes.

  5. Translate this OpenFGA line into Cedar: define commenter: [user, team#member] or viewer on type doc.
    Answer

    "Or viewer" means every viewer is also a commenter. So the commenter group goes inside the viewer group: action commenterActions in [viewerActions]; and action comment in [commenterActions] appliesTo { ... };. Add a template for direct tuples: permit (principal in ?principal, action in Action::"commenterActions", resource in ?resource);. We tested it. Ana (a viewer) and Bo (an editor) could comment. Fred, a direct commenter, could comment but not read. Eli could not comment.

  6. Explain to a teammate, in four sentences, the biggest difference between OpenFGA and Cedar for ReBAC.
    Answer

    OpenFGA stores the relationship graph and walks it on its server. Cedar stores no data at all. Your app must load the entities for each request: the user with its groups and the resource with its parents. In exchange, Cedar policies are small, fast to evaluate and easy to analyze.

Key takeaways

  • Model in six steps: nouns, verbs, "inside" links, facts, named links, then schema, policies and tests.
  • RBAC answers "what is your job", ABAC answers "what facts", ReBAC answers "what links". Real systems mix them.
  • Use in with sets of groups. contains only checks exact members.
  • An OpenFGA relation maps to a template, a tuple maps to a link, and "but not" maps to a forbid.
  • A global role plus a tenant forbid gives "admin of my own company".
  • Write the expected test matrix from the rules first. Ours matched 42 of 42 cells.

Sources

  • Cedar design patterns. Membership, relationship and discretionary permissions, and how to mix them.
  • Use attributes or templates to represent relationships. When to store links as attributes or as linked policies.
  • Groups and resource-specific roles. Roles as groups, and roles per resource set.
  • OpenFGA configuration language. Direct types, or, but not, from, and grouping with parentheses.
  • Zanzibar: Google's Consistent, Global Authorization System. The 2019 USENIX ATC paper behind relationship tuples.

Policy design and governance

A policy set grows. Ten policies become two hundred, written by many teams over many years. This chapter shows how to keep them clear, small and safe: good names, one rule per policy, narrow scopes, a tidy repository, reviews and versions.

  • Name entities, actions and policies with clear, stable conventions.
  • Split rules into one policy each, with permits for features and forbids for guardrails.
  • Structure a policy repository with a build script that formats, validates and documents it.
  • Review a policy change with a checklist and approve it with versions and owners.
  • Recognize eight anti-patterns and fix each one.

Names and @id conventions

Compare two answers to the same request. Bob asks to view the pricing document.

permit (principal in Group::"viewers", action in Action::"readActions", resource);
ALLOW

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  policy0
@id("roles.viewers-read-only")
permit (principal in Group::"viewers", action in Action::"readActions", resource);
ALLOW

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  roles.viewers-read-only

"policy0" means nothing in a log, an alert or a review. "roles.viewers-read-only" tells you the area and the rule. Good names pay off every day.

What to name, and how

The Cedar best-practice guide gives these conventions. We add one for policy ids.

ThingStyleExample
Entity typePascalCaseUser, AuditLog
Entity idan opaque id or camelCase"fcaf664d4f89fec0cda8"
AttributecamelCaseresource.sharedWith
Context keycamelCasecontext.sourceIp
Policy id (our rule)area.rule-nameguard.tenant-isolation

Choose one style for action names and keep it. This book uses camelCase, like readLog, but keeps call_tool from your AI rule. Mixing styles in one app makes policies harder to read.

Rules for @id

  • Start with the area: guard. for guardrails, roles., docs., agents. for features.
  • Make it unique in the whole policy set. The CLI refuses duplicates: "duplicate template or policy id `docs.owner-edit`".
  • Never change it and never reuse it. Logs, tests and dashboards point to it.
  • One @id per policy. Two give "duplicate annotation: @id".

The docs say it clearly: @id is not special in the Cedar language. The cedar CLI uses it as the policy id. The Rust and Python libraries give ids like policy0 unless your loader renames them. Cedar in Rust shows how.

Stable ids inside policies

Names inside policies matter too. Look at principal in Group::"TeamExample". If someone renames the team, the policy breaks. If someone deletes it and a new team takes the name, the new team gets old access. The docs call these mutable identifiers. Use ids that never change and never return, like a UUID. Put the human name in a comment.

One policy per rule

Here is one policy with three rules joined by "or":

@id("docs.access")
permit (principal is User, action == Action::"view", resource is Document)
when {
  resource.owner == principal ||
  resource.sharedWith.contains(principal) ||
  principal.department == resource.department
};

And here are the same three rules as three policies:

@id("docs.view-owner")
permit (principal is User, action == Action::"view", resource is Document)
when { resource.owner == principal };

@id("docs.view-shared")
permit (principal is User, action == Action::"view", resource is Document)
when { resource.sharedWith.contains(principal) };

@id("docs.view-same-department")
permit (principal is User, action == Action::"view", resource is Document)
when { principal.department == resource.department };

Bob views the roadmap. It is shared with him. Both sets say Allow, but the reasons differ:

ALLOW

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  docs.access
ALLOW

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  docs.view-shared

The split version tells you why. Each rule can also have its own owner, ticket and tests. You can delete one rule without touching the others. Cedar is fast with many small policies, so there is no speed reason to merge them.

Permits for features, forbids for guardrails

Sort every rule into one of two kinds:

  • A feature says who may do something. "Editors may edit." Write it as a permit. Product teams own features.
  • A guardrail says what must never happen. "Nobody crosses tenants." Write it as a forbid. A security team owns guardrails.

The two kinds change at different speeds. Features change every sprint. Guardrails change rarely, and each change needs extra review. Keep them in different folders, as the repository below does.

Permits open doors. Forbids are walls. Many teams may add doors. Only the security team builds or moves walls.

Meta-permissions

Who may change who has access? That is a meta-permission. The Cedar docs advise you to model it as a normal action, like editPermissions, and protect it with policies too. Then "who can share this folder?" has a tested answer, like any other question.

Least privilege and narrow scopes

Least privilege means each principal gets only what it needs. Cedar starts from default deny, which helps. But one wide permit can undo that. Look at this policy:

@id("it.read-everything")
permit (principal, action, resource)
when { principal.department == "it" };
policy set validation failed
    Diagnostic severity: error
    Caused by: for policy `it.read-everything`, attribute `department` on entity type `Agent` not found

The author meant "IT users may read". The empty scope says "any principal, any action, any resource". So the validator also checked it for AI agents, which have no department. The name says "read", but the scope includes delete and share. Fix it by filling the scope:

@id("it.read-documents")
permit (principal in Group::"it", action in Action::"readActions", resource is Document);

Now the scope matches the name. Group membership replaces the string test. The Cedar guide calls this populate the policy scope. A full scope has three more benefits:

  • Stores can index policies by principal and resource, so lookups are faster.
  • Readers see the "who, what, which" without reading the condition.
  • Analysis tools give clearer answers, as Analysis and formal verification shows.

Using action with no constraint in a feature permit. Only admin permits should cover all actions, and only next to forbid guardrails. List the actions, or use an action group.

Structuring a policy repository

Treat policies like code: one Git repository, small files, owners, tests and a build. Here is a layout that works. Open the folders.

Each policy file holds one area. Every policy carries four annotations: @id, @owner, @ticket and @description. Here is the guardrail file, after cedar format:

@id("guard.tenant-isolation")
@owner("platform-security")
@ticket("SEC-112")
@description("Users never touch data outside their own tenant.")
forbid (
  principal is User,
  action,
  resource
)
unless { resource in principal.tenant };

The cedar CLI reads one policy file. Give it a directory and it fails: "Is a directory (os error 21)". So the build joins the files into one bundle first. The tests folder holds the tests from Testing Cedar policies.

#!/bin/sh
# Check and bundle the policy repository. Stops at the first failure.
set -e
CEDAR=${CEDAR:-cedar}
echo "1. format check"
for f in policies/*/*.cedar; do
  "$CEDAR" format --check -p "$f" > /dev/null || { echo "not formatted: $f"; exit 1; }
done
echo "2. bundle"
cat policies/*/*.cedar > build/policies.cedar
echo "3. validate (strict, warnings fail)"
"$CEDAR" validate --schema schema/lab.cedarschema \
  --policies build/policies.cedar --deny-warnings -f plain
echo "4. docs"
uv run --quiet --with cedarpy==4.12.2 python3.14 tools/policy_docs.py > build/POLICIES.md
echo "5. fingerprint"
shasum -a 256 schema/lab.cedarschema build/policies.cedar
1. format check
2. bundle
3. validate (strict, warnings fail)
policy set validation passed
    Diagnostic severity: advice
    Caused by: no errors or warnings

4. docs
5. fingerprint
8c13973a9d022c80c6282fc8c03bfdd716dc69b949179c0762e92aeeedff333c  schema/lab.cedarschema
06cdf64be91f70d404c296ec179f295b463bb95a4eed405c48d2d23a0605746f  build/policies.cedar

The flag --deny-warnings turns warnings into failures. Without it, an impossible policy still "passes". The fingerprint identifies this exact bundle. Step 4 is the next section.

A build that catches a bad pull request

A teammate adds a reviewers policy with principal == Group::"reviewers". The build stops at step 3. Here is the output, shortened:

3. validate (strict, warnings fail)
policy set validation failed
    Diagnostic severity: warning
    Caused by: for policy `roles.reviewers-comment`, unable to find an applicable action given the policy scope constraints
Begin snippet starting at line 41, column 1
diagnostic help: try replacing `==` with `in` in the principal clause

Notice "line 41". That is a line in the bundle, not in the teammate's file. The @id in the message is what tells you which policy is wrong. This is one more reason for good ids.

Documentation with annotations
