An annotation has no effect on decisions. The docs say so directly. But your tools can read it. That makes annotations the best place for documentation, because they live next to the rule.

This script reads every policy file with cedarpy, turns it into Cedar's JSON format and prints a table. It also fails the build when a policy has no owner, ticket or description.

# Print a Markdown table of every policy, built from its annotations.
import glob, json, sys
import cedarpy

rows = []
for path in sorted(glob.glob("policies/*/*.cedar")):
    doc = json.loads(cedarpy.policies_to_json_str(open(path).read()))
    for p in doc["staticPolicies"].values():
        a = p.get("annotations", {})
        missing = [k for k in ("id", "owner", "ticket", "description") if k not in a]
        if missing:
            sys.exit(f"{path}: missing annotation(s) {missing}")
        rows.append((a["id"], p["effect"], a["owner"], a["ticket"], a["description"]))

print("| id | effect | owner | ticket | description |")
print("|---|---|---|---|---|")
for r in rows:
    print("| " + " | ".join(r) + " |")
| id | effect | owner | ticket | description |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| agents.call-tools | permit | ai-platform | AI-31 | Agents may call tools. Guardrails narrow this. |
| taint.no-untrusted-egress | forbid | ai-platform | AI-32 | Untrusted input may never drive a tool that sends data out. |
| docs.owner-full-control | permit | docs-team | DOC-7 | Owners can read and change their own documents. |
| docs.share-direct-view | permit | docs-team | DOC-9 | A user in the share list may view the document. |
| roles.editors-view-edit | permit | docs-team | DOC-2 | Editors may view and edit documents. |
| roles.viewers-read-only | permit | docs-team | DOC-3 | Viewers may only use read actions. |
| guard.denylist | forbid | platform-security | SEC-120 | Users in the deny list can do nothing. |
| guard.mfa-for-delete-and-share | forbid | platform-security | SEC-118 | Delete and share need a second login step. |
| guard.tenant-isolation | forbid | platform-security | SEC-112 | Users never touch data outside their own tenant. |

When the reviewers policy came back with only an @id, step 4 stopped the build:

4. docs
policies/features/reviewers.cedar: missing annotation(s) ['owner', 'ticket', 'description']

Annotations in the schema

Since cedar-policy 4.3.0, the schema accepts annotations too. Use @doc to explain each type, attribute and action. This schema passed validation with the CLI:

@doc("A company that uses the app.")
entity Tenant;

@doc("A person who signs in.")
entity User {
  @doc("The company this user works for.")
  tenant: Tenant,
};

@doc("A text file inside one tenant.")
entity Document in [Tenant];

@doc("Read a document.")
action view appliesTo { principal: User, resource: Document };

A value-less annotation like @reviewed is short for @reviewed(""). It works well as a flag for your own tools.

A review checklist for policy changes

Every pull request that touches policies gets the same questions. Reviewers tick them in order.

  1. One rule? Each new policy says one thing, and its @id says the same thing.
  2. Right effect? Features are permits. Hard limits are forbids, written with unless.
  3. Full scope? Principal, action and resource are as narrow as the rule. No string tests for roles.
  4. Safe access? Optional attributes and tags have has or hasTag guards, especially in forbids.
  5. Stable ids? No display names or reusable names inside the policy.
  6. Annotations? @id, @owner, @ticket, @description are all present.
  7. Tests? At least one new allow test and one new deny test. The full matrix still passes.
  8. Build green? Format check, strict validation with --deny-warnings, docs.
  9. Wider or narrower? The author states whether the change adds access, removes access or neither. A solver can check this claim.
  10. Right approver? Guardrail changes need a security owner. Schema changes need every team that uses the schema.

The most important review question is number 9. Every change either adds access, removes access, or keeps it the same. The author must say which one, and a test or a solver must prove it.

Versioning and change approval

Policies and the schema change together. A new attribute in a policy needs the same attribute in the schema and in the entity data. So version them as one bundle.

What goes into a version

  • The schema file and the joined policy file, with their fingerprints.
  • The Cedar language version. cedar language-version printed "Cedar language version: 4.6" for CLI 4.14.1.
  • A version number. One simple rule: a major bump when the schema removes or renames something, a minor bump for new policies, a patch bump for fixes.

Log the version tag with every decision. During an incident you can then answer "which rules said yes?". Rolling out with shadow mode and rolling back are in Running Cedar in production.

Who approves

Git hosts can require approval from file owners. On GitHub, a CODEOWNERS file maps folders to teams. GitHub reads this file. We did not run it here.

# Folder                 Owners who must approve
/policies/guardrails/    @your-org/platform-security
/policies/agents/        @your-org/ai-platform @your-org/platform-security
/policies/features/      @your-org/docs-team
/schema/                 @your-org/platform-security @your-org/docs-team

Guardrails get a two-person rule: the author plus one security owner. For high-risk changes, add a solver check: "Does the new policy set allow anything the old one denied?" Analysis and formal verification shows how to ask that question.

Ticket

A product rule arrives: "Reviewers may comment." It gets ticket DOC-12.

Tests first

Add a deny test and an allow test for a reviewer. They fail.

Policy

Write one permit with four annotations in policies/features.

Build

Run build.sh. Format, validate with warnings as errors, docs, fingerprint.

Review

A docs-team owner uses the checklist. The author says: "This only adds access for reviewers."

Tag

Merge, then tag the bundle, for example v1.8.0. Save the fingerprints.

Shadow, then enforce

Log new decisions next to old ones. Compare. Then switch.

Anti-patterns and their fixes

An anti-pattern is a habit that looks fine but causes trouble later. Here are eight from real policy sets. Open each one.

1. Many rules in one policy

Symptom: one policy with three or more || branches and a vague id like docs.access. The reason says "docs.access" and nothing more. Fix: split it, one policy per rule, as shown above.

2. Empty scope, logic in the condition

Symptom: permit (principal, action, resource) when { ... }. It covers every action and every principal type, and validation may fail on types you forgot. Fix: fill the scope with in, is and action groups.

3. Roles as strings

Symptom: when { principal.role == "admin" }. Typos like "Admin" silently deny, and stores cannot index the policy. Fix: principal in Role::"admin", with the role as a parent group.

4. Display names inside policies

Symptom: Group::"TeamExample". A rename breaks access, and a reused name leaks it. Fix: stable opaque ids, with the name in a comment.

5. A guardrail written as a permit

Symptom: "same tenant" or "office network" in a permit condition. Any other permit can bypass it. Fix: forbid ... unless, as pattern 12 showed with Dave.

6. A guardrail that can error

Symptom: a forbid reads an optional attribute with no has, and the set runs without strict validation. An error skips the forbid, so the wall disappears. Fix: required attributes, strict validation in CI, and has guards.

7. Entity facts in the context

Symptom: context.userDepartment or context.documentOwner. The docs advise against it: facts about principal and resource belong on the entities. Fix: move them to attributes. Keep context for facts about this one request, like time, IP address and MFA.

8. Policies with no id or owner

Symptom: logs say policy0, and nobody knows who may change a rule. Fix: the four required annotations, checked by the build, as the docs script does.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for policy design and governance
PatternPurposeModelNote
Area-prefixed @idReadable logs and reviews@id("guard.tenant-isolation")Unique, stable, never reused. The CLI refuses duplicates.
One policy per ruleClear reasons and small changesthree policies instead of one with ||The reason then names the exact rule that decided.
Permit for a featureSay who may do somethingpermit (principal in Group::"editors", action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"], resource is Document);Product teams own features.
Forbid for a guardrailSay what must never happenforbid (principal is User, action, resource) unless { resource in principal.tenant };Security owns guardrails. Two-person approval.
Populated scopeLeast privilege and fast lookupspermit (principal in Group::"it", action in Action::"readActions", resource is Document);An empty scope covers all types, even agents.
Four required annotationsDocs that live with the rule@id @owner @ticket @descriptionAnnotations never change decisions. The build checks them.
Folder per ownerClear approval pathspolicies/guardrails/, policies/features/Map folders to teams in CODEOWNERS.
Bundle buildOne checked file to deploycat policies/*/*.cedar > build/policies.cedarError line numbers point into the bundle. Read the @id.
Warnings as errorsStop impossible policiescedar validate ... --deny-warningsWithout the flag, an impossible policy still passes.
Versioned bundleKnow which rules decidedtag v1.8.0 plus SHA-256 of schema and policiesLog the tag with every decision. Roll back by tag.

Practice

Which change does NOT change any authorization decision?

  • Changing principal == to principal in
  • Adding @owner("docs-team") to a policy
  • Adding unless { context.mfa } to a forbid

Annotations have no effect on evaluation. Tools read them, the authorizer does not.

Your build ran cedar validate without --deny-warnings. A new policy is impossible. What happens?

  • Validation passes with a warning, and the useless policy ships.
  • Validation fails with exit code 3.
  • The CLI deletes the policy.

Warnings do not fail validation by default. Our reviewers example only failed because of the flag.

Where does "the user's department" belong?

  • In the context, because it changes sometimes.
  • In an attribute of the user entity.
  • In the policy, as a string.

Facts about the principal belong on the principal. Context is for facts about this one request.

To turn validator warnings into failures, add the flag ?.

A permission about changing permissions is called a ?.

  1. Rename these policy ids with our convention: p1 (forbid users on the deny list), adminStuff (admins may do anything), shareRule (shared users may view).
    Answer

    guard.denylist, roles.admins-full-access, docs.share-direct-view. The area comes first. The rest says the rule in a few words.

  2. Review this policy with the checklist and list every problem: permit (principal, action, resource) when { principal.role == "support" || resource.public };
    Answer

    Two rules in one policy. Empty scope, so it covers every action, including delete. A role as a string. No annotations, so the id is policy0. No tests are named. Fix: two policies. The first is permit (principal in Group::"support", action in Action::"readActions", resource is Document);, which passed strict validation in the lab schema. The second is a separate public-read permit. Give each one four annotations and two tests.

  3. The build says the error is at "line 41". Your files are short. What is going on, and how do you find the policy?
    Answer

    The CLI validated the joined bundle, so line 41 is a line in build/policies.cedar. Read the policy id in the message, here roles.reviewers-comment, and search the policies folder for it.

  4. Design a version rule for your team. When is a change major, minor or patch?
    Answer

    One good answer: major when the schema removes or renames a type, attribute or action, because old entity data breaks. Minor when policies or optional schema fields are added. Patch when a policy is fixed without new schema. Whatever you pick, write it down, tag every bundle, and log the tag with decisions.

  5. Your security lead asks: "Can the docs team weaken a guardrail without us noticing?" Describe three controls from this chapter.
    Answer

    First, guardrails live in their own folder, and CODEOWNERS requires a security approval for it. Second, the build runs strict validation, and the test suite has deny tests for every guardrail, so a weaker guardrail breaks tests. Third, a solver check compares the old and new policy sets and reports any request that is now allowed but was denied before.

Key takeaways

  • Name policies area.rule-name. The CLI uses @id as the id. Libraries need a loader that does the same.
  • One policy per rule. The reason then names the exact rule.
  • Permits are features, owned by product teams. Forbids are guardrails, owned by security.
  • Populate the scope. An empty scope covers every principal type and every action.
  • A build formats, validates with --deny-warnings, checks annotations and fingerprints the bundle.
  • Version the schema and policies together, and log the version with every decision.

Sources

  • Cedar best practices. The official list of design practices used in this chapter.
  • Define and use naming conventions. PascalCase types, camelCase attributes and context keys.
  • Populate the policy scope. Why roles and types belong in the scope.
  • Avoid mutable identifiers in policies. Stable ids instead of names.
  • Cedar policy syntax. Annotations, value-less annotations, and why @id is not special in the language.
  • RFC 48: schema annotations. The design behind @doc in schemas.

Testing Cedar policies

A policy without tests is a guess. This chapter builds a full test kit for one policy repository: unit tests in the CLI, Rust and Python, a decision matrix with a golden file, property tests that hunt for bugs, format and validation checks, and one CI script that runs everything.

  • Write allow and deny unit tests with the cedar run-tests command.
  • Test policies from Rust with cargo test and from Python with pytest.
  • Keep a golden decision matrix and read its diff in a review.
  • Write property tests that search many random cases for a broken rule.
  • Run format, parse, validation and tests together in CI.

What a policy test is

Here is a real run of four tests against the policy repository from Policy design and governance:

cedar run-tests --schema schema/lab.cedarschema --policies build/policies.cedar --tests tests/documents.json
running 4 test(s)
  test owner-can-delete-with-mfa ... ok
  test owner-cannot-delete-without-mfa ... ok
  test other-tenant-editor-is-denied ... ok
  test viewer-cannot-edit-shared-doc ... ok
results: 4 passed, 0 failed

Each test is a small JSON object. Here are the first two:

[
  {
    "name": "owner-can-delete-with-mfa",
    "request": {
      "principal": "User::\"alice\"",
      "action": "Action::\"delete\"",
      "resource": "Document::\"roadmap\"",
      "context": {"mfa": true, "sourceIp": "10.1.2.3", "now": "2026-11-16T10:00:00Z"}
    },
    "entities": "fixtures/entities.json",
    "decision": "allow",
    "reason": ["docs.owner-full-control"],
    "num_errors": 0
  },
  {
    "name": "owner-cannot-delete-without-mfa",
    "request": {
      "principal": "User::\"alice\"",
      "action": "Action::\"delete\"",
      "resource": "Document::\"roadmap\"",
      "context": {"mfa": false, "sourceIp": "10.1.2.3", "now": "2026-11-16T10:00:00Z"}
    },
    "entities": "fixtures/entities.json",
    "decision": "deny",
    "reason": ["guard.mfa-for-delete-and-share"],
    "num_errors": 0
  }
]

A policy test has four parts:

  1. A request: principal, action, resource and context.
  2. The entities: the data Cedar may look at. Here it is a file name. Inline JSON works too.
  3. The expected decision: allow or deny.
  4. The expected reasons and the expected number of errors.

The data and the policies are fixed, so the answer is always the same. A Cedar test never needs a server, a clock or a network. The run-tests command arrived in CLI version 4.6.0. In 4.14.1 it also accepts entity file paths, as above.

A test that only checks "deny" passes for the wrong reason too. Maybe the user was denied by default, not by the guardrail you meant to test. Listing guard.mfa-for-delete-and-share in reason proves the right rule fired.

Unit tests for allow and deny

Every rule needs at least two unit tests. One allow test proves the rule gives access. One deny test proves it does not give too much. Deny tests matter more. A missing permit annoys a user. An extra permit leaks data.

When a test fails

Our first version of the fourth test was different. It said "Bob, a viewer, cannot edit pricing". It failed:

  test viewer-cannot-edit ... fail: expected Deny, got Allow
results: 3 passed, 1 failed

We asked the CLI why. The file ctx.json holds the same context as the test:

cedar authorize --schema schema/lab.cedarschema --policies build/policies.cedar --entities tests/fixtures/entities.json -l 'User::"bob"' -a 'Action::"edit"' -r 'Document::"pricing"' -c ctx.json -v
ALLOW

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  docs.owner-full-control

Bob owns the pricing document. The policy was right, and the test was wrong. We changed the test to the roadmap, which Bob may only view. Then all four tests passed. A failing test is a question: is the rule wrong, or is my picture of the data wrong?

Three more failure messages

We broke tests on purpose to collect the real messages:

  test wrong-reason ... fail: missing reason(s): `roles.editors-view-edit`
  test no-num-errors ... error:
  missing field `num_errors`
  • The expected reasons must be a subset of the real reasons. Extra real reasons are fine.
  • The fields reason and num_errors are required. Use [] and 0 when there are none.
  • Entity files must sit inside the test file's folder. Our first try used ../fixtures and got this error (paths shortened): "entities file `/home/you/policy-repo/fixtures/entities.json` is outside of the test file directory `/home/you/policy-repo/tests`".

Passing --schema and forgetting that it changes the rules of the run. With a schema, the CLI validates the policy set for every test, and any warning fails the test. That is good. But a teammate's impossible policy can then fail all your tests at once. Read the first error first.

Tests in Rust and in Python

The CLI is good for policy authors. Application developers often prefer tests in their own language. The same four ideas work everywhere: load the bundle, build a request, call the authorizer, check decision and reasons.

use cedar_policy::*;
use std::str::FromStr;

// Load the bundle once per test. Rename policies by their @id, like the CLI.
fn setup() -> (PolicySet, Schema, Entities) {
    let (schema, _) = Schema::from_cedarschema_str(include_str!("../policy/lab.cedarschema")).unwrap();
    let parsed = PolicySet::from_str(include_str!("../policy/policies.cedar")).unwrap();
    let mut pset = PolicySet::new();
    for p in parsed.policies() {
        let id = p.annotation("id").expect("every policy needs @id");
        pset.add(p.new_id(PolicyId::new(id))).unwrap();
    }
    let ents = Entities::from_json_str(include_str!("../policy/entities.json"), Some(&schema)).unwrap();
    (pset, schema, ents)
}

fn decide(principal: &str, action: &str, resource: &str, mfa: bool) -> (Decision, Vec<String>) {
    let (pset, schema, ents) = setup();
    let a = EntityUid::from_str(action).unwrap();
    let ctx = serde_json::json!({"mfa": mfa, "sourceIp": "10.1.2.3", "now": "2026-11-16T10:00:00Z"});
    let ctx = Context::from_json_value(ctx, Some((&schema, &a))).unwrap();
    let req = Request::new(principal.parse().unwrap(), a, resource.parse().unwrap(), ctx, Some(&schema)).unwrap();
    let resp = Authorizer::new().is_authorized(&req, &pset, &ents);
    let mut why: Vec<String> = resp.diagnostics().reason().map(|id| id.to_string()).collect();
    why.sort();
    (resp.decision(), why)
}
#[test]
fn policies_pass_strict_validation() {
    let (pset, schema, _) = setup();
    let res = Validator::new(schema).validate(&pset, ValidationMode::Strict);
    assert!(res.validation_passed_without_warnings(), "{res}");
}

#[test]
fn owner_can_delete_with_mfa() {
    let (d, why) = decide(r#"User::"alice""#, r#"Action::"delete""#, r#"Document::"roadmap""#, true);
    assert_eq!(d, Decision::Allow);
    assert_eq!(why, ["docs.owner-full-control"]);
}

#[test]
fn owner_cannot_delete_without_mfa() {
    let (d, why) = decide(r#"User::"alice""#, r#"Action::"delete""#, r#"Document::"roadmap""#, false);
    assert_eq!(d, Decision::Deny);
    assert_eq!(why, ["guard.mfa-for-delete-and-share"]);
}

#[test]
fn table_of_denials() {
    let cases = [
        (r#"User::"dave""#, r#"Action::"view""#, r#"Document::"roadmap""#), // other tenant
        (r#"User::"mallory""#, r#"Action::"view""#, r#"Document::"roadmap""#), // deny list
        (r#"User::"bob""#, r#"Action::"edit""#, r#"Document::"roadmap""#), // viewer
    ];
    for (p, a, r) in cases {
        assert_eq!(decide(p, a, r, true).0, Decision::Deny, "{p} {a} {r}");
    }
}
running 4 tests
test owner_cannot_delete_without_mfa ... ok
test owner_can_delete_with_mfa ... ok
test policies_pass_strict_validation ... ok
test table_of_denials ... ok

test result: ok. 4 passed; 0 failed; 0 ignored; 0 measured; 0 filtered out; finished in 0.06s
import json
import cedarpy
import pytest

POLICIES = open("build/policies.cedar").read()
SCHEMA = open("schema/lab.cedarschema").read()
ENTITIES = json.load(open("tests/fixtures/entities.json"))
# cedarpy names policies policy0, policy1, ... Map them to their @id.
IDS = {k: v["annotations"]["id"]
       for k, v in json.loads(cedarpy.policies_to_json_str(POLICIES))["staticPolicies"].items()}

def decide(principal, action, resource, mfa=True):
    request = {"principal": principal, "action": action, "resource": resource,
               "context": {"mfa": mfa, "sourceIp": "10.1.2.3", "now": "2026-11-16T10:00:00Z"}}
    r = cedarpy.is_authorized(request, POLICIES, ENTITIES, schema=SCHEMA)
    assert r.diagnostics.errors == []
    return r.decision.value, sorted(IDS[p] for p in r.diagnostics.reasons)

def test_validates_strictly():
    assert cedarpy.validate_policies(POLICIES, SCHEMA).validation_passed

@pytest.mark.parametrize("principal, action, resource, mfa, decision, reason", [
    ('User::"alice"', 'Action::"delete"', 'Document::"roadmap"', True, "Allow", ["docs.owner-full-control"]),
    ('User::"alice"', 'Action::"delete"', 'Document::"roadmap"', False, "Deny", ["guard.mfa-for-delete-and-share"]),
    ('User::"dave"', 'Action::"view"', 'Document::"roadmap"', True, "Deny", ["guard.tenant-isolation"]),
    ('User::"mallory"', 'Action::"view"', 'Document::"roadmap"', True, "Deny", ["guard.denylist"]),
    ('User::"bob"', 'Action::"edit"', 'Document::"roadmap"', True, "Deny", []),
])
def test_decision(principal, action, resource, mfa, decision, reason):
    assert decide(principal, action, resource, mfa) == (decision, reason)
......                                                                   [100%]
6 passed in 0.68s

The Rust tests use cedar-policy 4.14.1. The Python tests use cedarpy 4.12.2 from PyPI, run with uv run --with cedarpy==4.12.2 --with pytest. Both libraries name policies policy0, policy1 and so on. So each test maps the names back to the @id annotations. The Python tests also assert that there were no evaluation errors, like num_errors: 0 in the CLI.

Use pytest.mark.parametrize or a Rust array of cases for tables of tests. One line per case keeps a long list easy to review.

The test matrix and golden files

Unit tests check the cases you thought of. A test matrix checks every combination. This script asks 7 users times 4 actions times 2 documents: 56 questions. It uses is_authorized_batch, which sends all requests in one call.

# Print one line per (principal, action, resource): the decision and the reasons.
import itertools, json, sys
import cedarpy

policies = open(sys.argv[1]).read()
schema = open("schema/lab.cedarschema").read()
entities = json.load(open("tests/fixtures/entities.json"))
ids = {k: v["annotations"]["id"]
       for k, v in json.loads(cedarpy.policies_to_json_str(policies))["staticPolicies"].items()}

users = ["alice", "bob", "carol", "dave", "erin", "mallory", "oscar"]
actions = ["view", "edit", "delete", "share"]
docs = ["roadmap", "pricing"]
ctx = {"mfa": True, "sourceIp": "10.1.2.3", "now": "2026-11-16T10:00:00Z"}
cells = list(itertools.product(users, actions, docs))
requests = [{"principal": f'User::"{u}"', "action": f'Action::"{a}"',
             "resource": f'Document::"{d}"', "context": ctx} for u, a, d in cells]
for (u, a, d), r in zip(cells, cedarpy.is_authorized_batch(requests, policies, entities, schema=schema)):
    why = ",".join(sorted(ids[p] for p in r.diagnostics.reasons)) or "-"
    print(f"{u:8} {a:7} {d:8} {r.decision.value:5} {why}")

We saved its output as a golden file. Here are its first eight lines. 11 of the 56 lines say Allow.

alice    view    roadmap  Allow docs.owner-full-control,roles.editors-view-edit
alice    view    pricing  Allow roles.editors-view-edit
alice    edit    roadmap  Allow docs.owner-full-control,roles.editors-view-edit
alice    edit    pricing  Allow roles.editors-view-edit
alice    delete  roadmap  Allow docs.owner-full-control
alice    delete  pricing  Deny  -
alice    share   roadmap  Allow docs.owner-full-control
alice    share   pricing  Deny  -

Reading a golden diff in review

Now a pull request changes one line. It adds delete to the editors policy:

<   action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"],
---
>   action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit", Action::"delete"],

CI runs the matrix again and compares it with the golden file:

@@ -2,8 +2,8 @@
 alice    view    pricing  Allow roles.editors-view-edit
 alice    edit    roadmap  Allow docs.owner-full-control,roles.editors-view-edit
 alice    edit    pricing  Allow roles.editors-view-edit
-alice    delete  roadmap  Allow docs.owner-full-control
-alice    delete  pricing  Deny  -
+alice    delete  roadmap  Allow docs.owner-full-control,roles.editors-view-edit
+alice    delete  pricing  Allow roles.editors-view-edit
 alice    share   roadmap  Allow docs.owner-full-control
 alice    share   pricing  Deny  -

The reviewer sees the exact effect: one decision flips from Deny to Allow. Alice may now delete Bob's pricing document. Dave and Mallory are editors too, but their lines did not change. Their guardrails still hold. If the flip is wanted, the author updates the golden file in the same pull request. If not, the change is rejected.

The golden file is a photo of all decisions. Every change takes a new photo. The diff shows what changed in the photo, line by line.

Updating the golden file without reading the diff. Then the golden file only records what the code does, not what it should do. Make the diff a required part of the review.

Property-style tests

A matrix covers fixed data. Bugs often hide in data you did not think of: a user in three groups, a document owned by someone in another tenant. A property test states a rule that must always hold. Then a library makes hundreds of random inputs and tries to break it. We used Hypothesis.

First, a generator of random small worlds. Each world has one to three users with random tenants and groups, one document in a random tenant, and one random request.

import json, os
import cedarpy
from hypothesis import given, settings, strategies as st

POLICIES = open(os.environ.get("POLICIES", "build/policies.cedar")).read()
SCHEMA = open("schema/lab.cedarschema").read()
GROUPS = ["editors", "viewers", "design-team", "denylist"]
ACTIONS = ["view", "comment", "edit", "delete", "share"]
E = lambda t, i: {"type": t, "id": i}

@st.composite
def worlds(draw):
    """A random small world: users, one document, one request."""
    names = draw(st.lists(st.sampled_from(["u1", "u2", "u3"]), min_size=1, unique=True))
    users = {n: (draw(st.sampled_from(["acme", "globex"])),
                 draw(st.lists(st.sampled_from(GROUPS), unique=True))) for n in names}
    doc_tenant = draw(st.sampled_from(["acme", "globex"]))
    ents = [{"uid": E("Tenant", t), "attrs": {}, "parents": []} for t in ("acme", "globex")]
    ents += [{"uid": E("Group", g), "attrs": {}, "parents": []} for g in GROUPS]
    ents += [{"uid": E("Folder", "root-" + t), "attrs": {"owner": E("User", names[0])},
              "parents": [E("Tenant", t)]} for t in ("acme", "globex")]
    ents += [{"uid": E("User", n), "parents": [E("Group", g) for g in gs],
              "attrs": {"department": "design", "tenant": E("Tenant", t), "projects": []}}
             for n, (t, gs) in users.items()]
    ents.append({"uid": E("Document", "d"), "parents": [E("Folder", "root-" + doc_tenant)],
                 "attrs": {"owner": E("User", draw(st.sampled_from(names))), "department": "design",
                           "sharedWith": [E("User", n) for n in draw(st.lists(st.sampled_from(names), unique=True))]},
                 "tags": {}})
    who = draw(st.sampled_from(names))
    req = {"principal": f'User::"{who}"', "action": f'Action::"{draw(st.sampled_from(ACTIONS))}"',
           "resource": 'Document::"d"',
           "context": {"mfa": draw(st.booleans()), "sourceIp": "10.1.2.3", "now": "2026-11-16T10:00:00Z"}}
    return ents, req, users[who], doc_tenant

Then two properties. Each one runs on 300 random worlds:

@settings(max_examples=300)
@given(worlds())
def test_no_access_across_tenants(world):
    ents, req, (tenant, groups), doc_tenant = world
    r = cedarpy.is_authorized(req, POLICIES, ents, schema=SCHEMA)
    if tenant != doc_tenant:
        assert r.decision.value == "Deny", (req, groups)

@settings(max_examples=300)
@given(worlds())
def test_denylist_always_denied(world):
    ents, req, (tenant, groups), doc_tenant = world
    r = cedarpy.is_authorized(req, POLICIES, ents, schema=SCHEMA)
    if "denylist" in groups:
        assert r.decision.value == "Deny", (req, groups)
..                                                                       [100%]
2 passed in 8.85s

Catching a real bug

Now someone "improves" the tenant guardrail. They want editors to help other companies:

< unless { resource in principal.tenant };
---
> unless { resource in principal.tenant || principal in Group::"editors" };

The unit tests still pass, because none of them has an editor in another tenant who owns a document there. The property test fails at once. Here is the key part of the output:

>           assert r.decision.value == "Deny", (req, groups)
E           AssertionError: ({'principal': 'User::"u1"', 'action': 'Action::"view"', 'resource': 'Document::"d"', 'context': {'mfa': False, 'sourceIp': '10.1.2.3', 'now': '2026-11-16T10:00:00Z'}}, ['editors'])
E           Failing test case: test_no_access_across_tenants(
E                ('acme', ['editors']),
FAILED test_properties.py::test_no_access_across_tenants - AssertionError: ({...
1 failed, 1 passed in 1.31s

Hypothesis also shrinks the failing world. The world it reported is tiny: one user, u1, at acme, in the editors group, and one globex document that u1 owns. That is the smallest story of the bug.

A property test tries many cases, not all cases. It found this bug quickly, but it can miss a rare one. To prove a property for every possible request, use a solver. Analysis and formal verification asks the same question, "can anyone cross tenants?", for all inputs.

Good properties for Cedar policies:

  • Isolation: nobody reads data from another tenant.
  • Guardrails win: a deny-listed user is always denied, whatever else is true.
  • Monotonic grants: adding a user to a viewer group never removes access.
  • No errors: valid random data never makes a policy raise an evaluation error.

Formatting, parsing and validation

Before any test runs, cheap checks catch most mistakes. The cedar CLI 4.14.1 has these commands for policy work:

CommandWhat it doesExit code we saw on failure
check-parseChecks that policies, a schema or entities parse1
format --checkChecks the layout. --write fixes it1
validateType-checks policies against the schema. Add --deny-warnings3
run-testsRuns JSON test cases1
authorizeAnswers one request. Use -v for reasons2 for DENY, 0 for ALLOW
evaluateEvaluates one expression

The CLI also has new, which creates a starter project with a policy, a JSON schema and entities but no tests. It has symcc for solver checks, covered in the analysis chapter.

Here is a parse error. The policy has no final semicolon:

failed to parse policy set
    Diagnostic severity: error
    Caused by: unexpected end of input
Begin snippet starting at line 1, column 1

snippet line 1: @id("roles.viewers-read-only")
snippet line 2: permit (principal in Group::"viewers", action in Action::"readActions", resource)
    label at line 2, column 82: expected `;` or identifier

format --check works differently from what many people expect. When a file is not formatted, it prints the formatted text and exits with code 1. It prints no message. So the build script in the governance chapter adds its own "not formatted" line. In all our files, the formatter put each scope part on its own line.

Know the order of the cheap checks: parse, then format, then validate, then tests. Each step needs the step before it to pass. Validation needs a schema. Tests need valid policies.

Running everything in CI

The picture shows the layers of checks. Lower layers are fast and catch simple mistakes. Higher layers are slower and catch deeper ones.

One script runs everything, so CI and your laptop do the same thing:

#!/bin/sh
# Everything CI runs, in order. Any failure stops the script.
set -e
CEDAR=${CEDAR:-cedar}
PY="uv run --quiet --python 3.14 --with cedarpy==4.12.2 --with pytest --with hypothesis"
./build.sh
echo "6. cedar run-tests"
"$CEDAR" run-tests --schema schema/lab.cedarschema \
  --policies build/policies.cedar --tests tests/documents.json
echo "7. pytest: unit and property tests"
$PY python -m pytest -q -p no:cacheprovider tests
echo "8. golden decision matrix"
$PY python tests/matrix.py build/policies.cedar > build/decisions.txt
diff -u tests/golden/decisions.txt build/decisions.txt
echo "all checks passed"

2. bundle
3. validate (strict, warnings fail)

    Diagnostic severity: advice
    Caused by: no errors or warnings

........                                                                 [100%]

Steps 1 to 5 come from build.sh. The Rust tests live in the application repository and run there with cargo test.

The CI service only has to install the tools and call the script. Here is a GitHub Actions workflow. We checked that it is valid YAML, but we did not run it on GitHub.

# .github/workflows/policies.yml
name: policies
on: [pull_request, push]
jobs:
  check:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v7
      - name: Install the Cedar CLI
        run: cargo install cedar-policy-cli --version 4.14.1 --locked
      - name: Install uv
        run: python3 -m pip install uv
      - name: Run every policy check
        run: ./ci.sh

We ran the same cargo install line on this machine, and it built CLI 4.14.1. Building from source takes minutes. Since CLI 4.12.0, the project also publishes ready-made cedar binaries on its GitHub releases page. Check the asset names there before you use them.

Parse and format

Every file parses and matches cedar format. Seconds.

Validate

Strict mode, warnings fail. Catches typos, wrong types and impossible policies.

Unit tests

CLI tests, plus pytest. Each rule has an allow and a deny case.

Property tests

600 random worlds check isolation and the deny list.

Golden matrix

56 decisions compared with the saved file. Any flip shows in the diff.

Review

A human reads the golden diff and the checklist. Then merge and tag the bundle.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for testing Cedar policies
PatternPurposeModelNote
Allow plus deny per ruleProve access and its limitsowner-can-delete-with-mfa and owner-cannot-delete-without-mfaDeny tests catch leaks. Never skip them.
Expected reasonProve the right rule fired"reason": ["guard.mfa-for-delete-and-share"]Expected reasons must be a subset of the real reasons.
Expected error countCatch policies that error and are skipped"num_errors": 0Required in run-tests. Use 0 for normal tests.
Shared fixturesOne entity file for many tests"entities": "fixtures/entities.json"The file must sit inside the test file's folder.
Map ids in librariesReadable reasons in Rust and Pythonp.new_id(PolicyId::new(id)) or a dict from policies_to_json_strLibraries name policies policy0, policy1.
Parametrized testsMany cases in a short table@pytest.mark.parametrize(...)One line per case keeps reviews easy.
Golden matrixSee every decision changediff -u tests/golden/decisions.txt build/decisions.txtRead the diff before you update the golden file.
Property testFind bugs in data you did not imagineif tenant != doc_tenant: assert decision == "Deny"Many cases, not all. Use a solver for proof.
Warnings as errorsStop impossible policies earlycedar validate --deny-warningsWith --schema, run-tests also fails on warnings.
One CI scriptSame checks on laptop and server./ci.shThe CI file only installs tools and calls it.

Practice

A test expects "reason": ["docs.owner-full-control"]. The real reasons are docs.owner-full-control and roles.editors-view-edit. Does the test pass?

  • Yes, because the expected reasons are a subset of the real ones.
  • No, because the lists are not equal.
  • No, because two policies matched.

The CLI only reports reasons that you expected but did not get.

Which test catches a permit that gives too much access?

  • An allow test.
  • A deny test.
  • A format check.

Only a deny test notices an extra Allow.

The unit tests passed but the property test failed after the guardrail change. Why?

  • Property tests are slower.
  • No unit test had an editor who owned a document in another tenant.
  • Hypothesis uses a different Cedar engine.

Unit tests only check the cases you wrote. The property test generated the missing case.

cedar authorize exits with code ? when the decision is DENY.

The CLI command that runs JSON test cases is cedar ?.

  1. Write a CLI test: Mallory views the roadmap and is denied by the deny list.
    Answer

    Use the same shape as the tests above: request with principal User::"mallory", action Action::"view", resource Document::"roadmap" and the usual context. Then "entities": "fixtures/entities.json", "decision": "deny", "reason": ["guard.denylist"], "num_errors": 0. We ran exactly this test with the CLI, and it printed "test denylisted-user-is-denied ... ok".

  2. The golden diff shows ten lines that flip from Deny to Allow, but the pull request says "rename a policy id". What do you do?
    Answer

    Reject it, or ask why. A rename changes only reason columns, never decisions. Ten flipped decisions mean the change also widened access. Read the policy diff line by line and ask the author for tests that explain each flip.

  3. Why does test_policies.py assert that r.diagnostics.errors is empty?
    Answer

    An evaluation error makes Cedar skip that policy. If the skipped policy is a forbid, the decision can still look right in one test while the guardrail is off. Checking for zero errors is the Python version of "num_errors": 0.

  4. Write the property "a viewer never gets edit, delete or share" in words, and say which entities the generator needs.
    Answer

    For every world where the principal is only in viewers and is not the owner, actions edit, delete and share give Deny. The generator needs users with random group sets that can include viewers, and documents with random owners, so that the test can skip owners. Without that filter the property is false, because owners may edit.

  5. Your matrix has 56 cells. The app grows to 2,000 users, 40 actions and 100,000 documents. What do you change?
    Answer

    Do not test every real combination. Keep the matrix on a small set of fixture entities: one user of each kind and one document of each kind. Cover the size problem with property tests on random small worlds, and with solver checks for the important guarantees. Large real data belongs in shadow-mode comparisons in production.

Key takeaways

  • A policy test is a request, entities, an expected decision, expected reasons and an error count.
  • Every rule needs an allow test and a deny test. Check the reason, not only the decision.
  • The same tests work in the CLI, in Rust and in Python. Map policy0 names back to @id.
  • A golden decision matrix turns every change into a readable diff.
  • Property tests search random worlds and found a guardrail bug that unit tests missed.
  • One CI script runs format, validation, unit, property and golden checks, in that order.

Sources

  • cedar-policy-cli on GitHub. The CLI with authorize, validate, format, check-parse and run-tests.
  • Cedar CLI changelog. run-tests in 4.6.0, schema option in 4.9.0, entity files in 4.14.1, release binaries in 4.12.0.
  • Cedar policy validation. What strict validation checks before you run tests.
  • cedarpy on PyPI. The Python package, version 4.12.2, used for the pytest and Hypothesis tests.
  • Hypothesis documentation. Property-based testing for Python, with composite strategies and shrinking.

Analysis and formal verification

Tests check the cases you thought of. Analysis checks every case, even the ones you did not think of. Cedar was built so that a computer can answer questions like "does this change allow more?" for all users and all resources at once.

  • Explain why a passing test matrix is not a proof.
  • List the design choices that make Cedar analyzable.
  • Describe how the Lean model, the proofs and differential testing protect the Rust engine.
  • Turn a policy change into an SMT question and read the formula Cedar produces.
  • Name the questions analysis can answer, and its limits.

Testing is not proof

Start with a small change. Someone edits team-view. They remove the folder check, "to make sharing easier".

 @id("team-view")
 permit (
   principal in Team::"eng",
   action == Action::"view",
-  resource in Folder::"shared"
+  resource
 );

Does the change allow more than before? We can test it. This script asks the real engine 36 questions, before and after. The store has Alice, Bob, and three documents: plan (shared, Bob's), notes (private, Alice's) and salary (private, Bob's).

from itertools import product
from pathlib import Path
from cedarpy import Entities, PolicySet, Schema, is_authorized_batch

schema = Schema.from_str(Path("data/schema.cedarschema").read_text())
entities = Entities.from_json_str(Path("data/all.json").read_text(), schema=schema)
old = PolicySet.from_str(Path("data/policy.cedar").read_text())
new = PolicySet.from_str(Path("data/policy-new.cedar").read_text())

grid = list(product(["alice", "bob"], ["view", "edit", "delete"],
                    ["plan", "notes", "salary"], [False, True]))
reqs = [{"principal": {"type": "User", "id": u}, "action": {"type": "Action", "id": a},
         "resource": {"type": "Document", "id": d}, "context": {"mfa": m}}
        for u, a, d, m in grid]
before = is_authorized_batch(reqs, old, entities, schema=schema)
after = is_authorized_batch(reqs, new, entities, schema=schema)
changed = [(g, b.decision.value, a.decision.value)
           for g, b, a in zip(grid, before, after) if b.decision != a.decision]
print(f"checked {len(grid)} requests, {len(changed)} changed")
for (u, a, d, m), b, n in changed:
    print(f"  {u} {a} {d} mfa={m}: {b} -> {n}")
checked 36 requests, 2 changed
  alice view salary mfa=False: Deny -> Allow
  alice view salary mfa=True: Deny -> Allow

Good: the test found the problem. Alice can now read Bob's salary file. But it found it only because we put salary in the test store. A real company has a million documents and changes every day. No test store holds them all.

Formal analysis asks the question for every user, document and context that fits the schema. Then "no difference" really means no difference.

Do not say "all tests pass, so the change is safe". Tests show the cases you wrote. Analysis shows all cases. Use both: tests for what you expect, analysis for what you forgot.

Why Cedar was designed to be analyzable

Most programming languages are too powerful to analyze fully. A loop might run forever. A function might call the network. Cedar leaves those things out on purpose.

Design choiceWhy it helps analysis
No loops, no recursion, no user functionsEvery evaluation finishes. Every policy becomes a formula of fixed size.
No side effects and no I/OA policy cannot read a file or call a service. All facts come in with the request.
A schema with typesEach value has a known type, so the solver knows what kind of thing it is.
Limited operatorslike has only the * wildcard, not full regular expressions. Sets have contains, not loops.
64-bit integers, overflow is an errorNumbers fit a fixed-size model.
Simple combining rulesDefault deny, forbid wins, order does not matter. The whole policy set is one clean formula.

The validator has a mode for this. In the Rust API, ValidationMode::Strict checks types and also a restricted form that suits analysis. It is the default. The chapter Schemas and validation covers it in depth.

You already met one small analysis. The validator in CLI 4.14.1 printed this warning for a policy with a typo in its action:

Warning: for policy `typo-action`, policy is impossible: the policy expression evaluates to false for all valid requests

"For all valid requests" is the key phrase. The validator did not try examples. It reasoned about every request the schema allows.

Cedar gave up some power to gain certainty. You cannot write a loop. In return, a computer can read your policies and tell you exactly what they allow.

Proof and testing behind the engine: Lean and DRT

Analysis is only useful if the engine does what the analysis assumes. The Cedar team checks this in two ways. Both live in the cedar-spec repository.

  1. A model in Lean with proofs. Lean is a language where you can write programs and also prove facts about them. The team wrote a simple, readable Cedar evaluator, authorizer and validator in Lean. Then they proved properties about it.
  2. Differential random testing. Differential random testing (DRT) creates millions of random policies, entities and requests. It runs each one through the Lean model and through the Rust engine. Any difference is a bug.

The cedar-lean README lists the proved properties. In our words:

  • Authorization. A matching forbid gives Deny. Allow needs a matching permit. Order and duplicates do not change the result.
  • Typechecking. A well-typed expression gives a value of the right type, or only a few allowed errors.
  • Slicing. Using a correct slice of the policies gives the same answer as the full set. Slicing entities at the checked level also keeps the answer.
  • Symbolic compilation. The formula from the symbolic compiler is exact, and compilation always works for well-typed expressions.
  • Verification. The checks built on the symbolic compiler are sound and complete.

The DRT folder, cedar-drt, has many fuzz targets. Some compare the authorizer, the evaluator and the validator. Others check round trips, like parse then format then parse again.

  1. 2024-03 OOPSLA paper on arXiv. "Cedar: A New Language for Expressive, Fast, Safe, and Analyzable Authorization". It describes a sound and complete logical encoding.
  2. 2024-04 AWS open source blog. Millions of DRT cases run every night. Lean took about 5 microseconds per input, Rust about 7.
  3. 2024-07 FSE industry paper. "How We Built Cedar". Proofs found four validator soundness bugs. DRT and property tests found 21 more bugs.
  4. 2025-11 cedar-policy-symcc 0.1.0. The symbolic compiler is published on crates.io.
  5. 2026 SymCert paper at FMCAD 2026. A Lean framework for verified SMT-based policy analyses.
  6. 2026-11 cedar-policy-symcc 0.8.1. Released with cedar-policy 4.14.1.

The 2024 paper also says a new Cedar version is not released until its model, proofs and differential tests are up to date. The bug numbers above come from that 2024 paper. They are not live numbers.

Symbolic analysis with an SMT solver

An SMT solver takes a logic formula and answers one question: can it be true? If yes, it gives a model, an example that makes it true. The answer "no" is called unsatisfiable.

The trick is to ask the opposite of what you want. Want to prove "the new policies never allow more than the old ones"? Ask the solver: "is there a request the new policies allow and the old ones deny?" If the answer is unsatisfiable, your claim is proved. If it is satisfiable, the model is a counterexample.

Take a schema, one request environment (a principal type, an action and a resource type), and policies that pass strict validation. Cedar's symbolic compiler turns the authorization result into one finite SMT formula. The formula is exact: it is true for exactly the requests and entity stores, fitting the schema, that the policies allow. So "unsatisfiable" proves a property for all such inputs, and "satisfiable" comes with a real counterexample.

In simple words: for each kind of request, the whole policy set becomes one formula, with nothing lost and nothing added. Example with numbers: our schema has 3 request environments (view, edit and delete on a Document). So one full check means 3 solver questions. Our "allows more?" question for view became a 48-line formula of 1,171 bytes.

Proof idea

Every Cedar feature maps to a theory the solver understands. Entities and records become data types. Attributes and ancestor sets become unknown functions. Booleans, strings, 64-bit integers and finite sets map to built-in theories. Because Cedar has no loops and no user functions, each policy becomes a formula of fixed size. Strict validation makes sure every value has a known type, so the translation never gets stuck. The Lean files SymbolicCompilation.lean and Verification.lean prove that the result over- and under-approximates the policy at the same time. That means it is exact. The SymCert paper describes this proof framework.

The tools, checked on 2026-11-15:

ToolWhat it isNeeds
cedar-policy-symcc 0.8.1The Rust library: compile, check, and get counterexamples.The cvc5 1.3.1 solver, path in the CVC5 variable. Uses tokio.
cedar symcc ...CLI subcommands, in the CLI built with the analyze feature. Releases also ship an "experimental" CLI archive with it.The cvc5 solver at run time.

Do not think "the solver runs my app". It never sees your real users or documents. It reasons about every store the schema allows. That is its strength, and it is also why the schema must match your real data.

Questions you can ask

Each business question maps to one check. These are the CLI subcommands in version 4.14.1.

Business questionCheckWhat "holds" means
Can this policy ever crash at run time?never-errorsNo well-formed request makes it error.
Is this policy dead code?never-matchesIt matches no request at all.
Does this policy match everything?always-matchesIt matches every request. Often a sign of a too-broad scope.
Does the new version allow more?implies (new, old)Everything new allows, old allowed too.
Is my refactoring safe?equivalentBoth sets allow exactly the same requests.
Can anyone ever do this action?always-deniesNobody is ever allowed, for this request shape.
Is everyone always allowed?always-allowsEvery request is allowed. Usually a red flag.
Do two teams' permissions overlap?disjointNo request is allowed by both sets.
Do these two single policies match the same requests?matches-equivalent, matches-implies, matches-disjointSame checks, for single policies.

Learn the direction of implies. "A implies B" means every request A allows, B allows too. So B is at least as permissive. To prove "the change adds nothing", check that new implies old.

A worked example: does this change allow more?

We now ask the real symbolic compiler our question. The slides show the plan.

Two versions

Old and new policy files, plus the schema.

Pick a request shape

User, Action::"view", Document. Repeat for every shape.

Compile

The symbolic compiler writes one SMT formula: "new allows and old denies".

Solve

cvc5 answers unsatisfiable (safe) or satisfiable (with a model).

Confirm

Turn the model into a request and run it through the normal authorizer.

Decide

Was the extra access wanted? If not, fix the policy and check again.

Steps 1 to 3: compile, for real

This machine has no cvc5. But the crate has a WriterSolver. It writes the SMT script and answers "unknown". So we can run the real compiler and look at its output.

[dependencies]
cedar-policy = "=4.14.1"
cedar-policy-symcc = "=0.8.1"
tokio = { version = "1", features = ["macros", "rt-multi-thread"] }
use cedar_policy::{EntityTypeName, EntityUid, PolicySet, RequestEnv, Schema};
use cedar_policy_symcc::{CedarSymCompiler, CompiledPolicySet, solver::WriterSolver};
use std::{error::Error, fs, str::FromStr};

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn Error>> {
    let read = |f: &str| fs::read_to_string(format!("data/{f}"));
    let (schema, _) = Schema::from_cedarschema_str(&read("schema.cedarschema")?)?;
    let old = PolicySet::from_str(&read("policy.cedar")?)?;
    let new = PolicySet::from_str(&read("policy-new.cedar")?)?;

    // One request shape: a User views a Document.
    let env = RequestEnv::new(EntityTypeName::from_str("User")?,
        EntityUid::from_str(r#"Action::"view""#)?, EntityTypeName::from_str("Document")?);
    let old_c = CompiledPolicySet::compile(&old, &env, &schema)?;
    let new_c = CompiledPolicySet::compile(&new, &env, &schema)?;

    // Question: is everything NEW allows also allowed by OLD?
    // WriterSolver only writes the SMT-LIB script. It never answers.
    let mut compiler = CedarSymCompiler::new(WriterSolver { w: Vec::<u8>::new() })?;
    let result = compiler.check_implies_opt(&new_c, &old_c).await;
    println!("result without a solver: {result:?}");
    let script = String::from_utf8(compiler.solver().w.clone())?;
    println!("SMT-LIB script: {} lines, {} bytes", script.lines().count(), script.len());
    fs::write("implies.smt2", &script)?; // a real solver could read this file
    Ok(())
}
result without a solver: Err(SolverUnknown)
SMT-LIB script: 48 lines, 1171 bytes

The result is SolverUnknown, as expected: nobody solved it. Here is the main part of the script. The first 14 lines only declare the types User, Document and the owner record.

; "resource"
(declare-const t0 E2)
; attrs[Document]
(declare-fun f0 (E2) R1)
(define-fun t1 () R1 (f0 t0))
(define-fun t2 () E0 (R1_a0 t1))
; "principal"
(declare-const t3 E0)
(define-fun t4 () Bool (= t2 t3))
; Team
(declare-datatype E3 (
  (E3 (E3_eid String))))
(define-fun t5 () E3 (E3 "eng"))
; ancs[User, Team]
(declare-fun f1 (E0) (Set E3))
(define-fun t6 () (Set E3) (f1 t3))
(define-fun t7 () Bool (set.member t5 t6))
(define-fun t8 () Bool (or t4 t7))
(define-fun t9 () Bool (not t8))
; Folder
(declare-datatype E4 (
  (E4 (E4_eid String))))
(define-fun t10 () E4 (E4 "shared"))
; ancs[Document, Folder]
(declare-fun f2 (E2) (Set E4))
(define-fun t11 () (Set E4) (f2 t0))
(define-fun t12 () Bool (set.member t10 t11))
(define-fun t13 () Bool (and t7 t12))
(define-fun t14 () Bool (or t4 t13))
(define-fun t15 () Bool (or t9 t14))
(define-fun t16 () Bool (not t15))
(assert true)
(assert t16)
(check-sat)

It looks hard, but it reads in small steps.

NameMeaning in Cedar words
t0, t3Some unknown resource and some unknown principal.
f0, f1, f2Unknown functions: a document's attributes, a user's teams, a document's folders.
t4resource.owner == principal
t7principal in Team::"eng"
t8The new set allows: owner, or any eng member.
t12resource in Folder::"shared"
t14The old set allows: owner, or eng member and shared folder.
t15"new allows" implies "old allows".
t16The opposite: new allows and old denies. This is asserted.

Notice what is missing. The forbid delete-needs-mfa is not in the formula. Its scope says action == Action::"delete", and this request shape is view. The compiler removed it.

Step 4: solve (not run here)

With cvc5 installed, you would run one CLI command. Here is the command, and the real error on this machine, which has no cvc5.

cedar symcc --principal-type User --action 'Action::"view"' --resource-type Document \
  --schema schema.cedarschema \
  implies --policies1 policy-new.cedar --policies2 policy.cedar
  × Analysis failed
  ├─▶ CVC5 solver not found or failed to start
  ├─▶ IO error during a solver operation
  ╰─▶ No such file or directory (os error 2)

Not run here. The CLI source shows the two possible reports. A property that holds prints a line ending in VERIFIED. A property that fails prints DOES NOT HOLD and then the counterexample. We cannot show a real solver answer, because we could not run cvc5.

Step 5: confirm by hand

We can still reason like the solver. To make t16 true, we need: the principal is in team eng (t7), the principal does not own the document (t4 false), and the document is not in the shared folder (t12 false). Alice viewing salary fits all three. The real authorizer agrees. It is the same row our test found in the first section.

  alice view salary mfa=False: Deny -> Allow

So a solver would answer "satisfiable": the change allows more. The difference is what comes next. The solver would find such a case with no test store at all.

Run the analysis in CI on every pull request that touches policies. Fail the build when implies (new, old) does not hold, unless the pull request says "this change grants new access" and a reviewer agrees.

Limits of analysis

Analysis is strong, but it answers only the question you ask, about the inputs it models.

  • One request shape at a time. Each check covers one principal type, one action and one resource type. Loop over all of them. In Rust, schema.request_envs() lists them. For our schema it printed three lines.
  • The schema is the world. The proof covers stores that fit the schema. If your real data breaks the schema, the proof says nothing about it. Validate entities too.
  • Policies only. Analysis does not see your PEP. A route with no middleware, a wrong entity loader or a fake context value are outside it. See Architecture: where authorization lives.
  • Intent is yours. The solver can say "the change allows more". It cannot say whether the business wanted that.
  • Trust in the solver. The Lean proofs cover the formula. You still trust cvc5 to answer it correctly. A solver can also give up or run out of time.
  • Experimental surface. The CLI README says the symcc commands may change between releases. In 4.14.1 the CLI checks policy sets without template links. Pin versions in CI.
  • Models are not the code. The proofs are about the Lean model. DRT gives strong evidence, not a proof, that the Rust engine matches it.

Do not stop testing because you have analysis. Analysis proves properties you wrote down. Tests check the decisions your users expect, with real names and real data. The chapter Testing Cedar policies shows how to keep both in CI.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for analyzing Cedar policies
PatternPurposeModelNote
Ask the oppositeProve a claim"Is there a request new allows and old denies?"Unsatisfiable means the claim holds.
New implies oldNo new accesscedar symcc ... implies --policies1 new --policies2 oldMind the direction.
Equivalent refactorSafe clean-upcedar symcc ... equivalent --policies1 a --policies2 bRun it for every request shape.
Dead policy checkFind policies that never matchnever-matches on one policyThe validator also warns "policy is impossible".
No-crash checkPolicies never errornever-errors on one policyAn erroring forbid is skipped, so this matters.
Loop over shapesFull coveragefor env in schema.request_envs()One check covers one shape only.
Script without a solverInspect the formulaCedarSymCompiler::new(WriterSolver { w: Vec::new() })Always answers unknown.
Confirm the counterexampleTrust but verifyRun the model as a normal requestThe normal authorizer must agree.
Finite diff as a testCheap early warningOld vs new over a grid of requestsCovers only your test store.
Strict validation firstMake policies analyzableValidationMode::StrictCompilation needs well-typed policies.

Practice

The solver answers "unsatisfiable" for "new allows and old denies". What does it mean?

  • The solver failed
  • The new policies never allow anything the old ones denied
  • The new policies allow more

No request can make the formula true. So the bad case cannot happen, for any store that fits the schema.

What does differential random testing compare?

  • Two versions of your policy file
  • The Lean model and the Rust engine on the same random inputs
  • Cedar and OPA

DRT runs random policies, entities and requests through both and reports any difference.

Why is the forbid delete-needs-mfa missing from the view formula?

  • Forbids are never analyzed
  • It has an error
  • Its scope only matches the delete action, and the formula is for view

Each formula is for one request shape. Policies whose scope cannot match are removed.

"A implies B" means every request A allows, ? allows too.

The symcc library needs the solver called ?.

  1. Name three Cedar design choices that make analysis possible, and say why each helps.
    Answer

    No loops: every policy becomes a formula of fixed size. No I/O: all facts come with the request, so nothing is hidden. A typed schema: the solver knows the type of every value. Also good answers: limited like, 64-bit integers, simple combining rules.

  2. Your test matrix passes. A teammate says analysis is not needed. What do you answer?
    Answer

    The matrix checks only the users and documents in it. Our diff found the salary leak only because salary was in the store. Analysis checks every store that fits the schema, so it finds cases nobody wrote down.

  3. Write the CLI check that proves a refactored file v2.cedar allows exactly what v1.cedar allows, for users editing documents.
    Answer

    cedar symcc --principal-type User --action 'Action::"edit"' --resource-type Document --schema schema.cedarschema equivalent --policies1 v1.cedar --policies2 v2.cedar. Repeat for every action, and install cvc5 first.

  4. The analysis says "VERIFIED" for all shapes, but a user still saw a private file. Give two possible causes outside the policies.
    Answer

    A route had no PEP, so Cedar was never asked. Or the PIP loaded wrong data, for example a wrong parent folder, or the context said mfa: true without checking. Analysis covers policies and the schema, not your code or data.

  5. Explain in your own words why the symbolic formula must be exact, not just "close".
    Answer

    If the formula allowed more than the policies, the solver could report false problems. If it allowed less, it could miss real problems and say "verified" wrongly. Exact means both directions hold, so "unsatisfiable" is a real proof and every model is a real counterexample. The Lean proofs show this.

Key takeaways

  • Tests cover the cases you wrote. Analysis covers every case that fits the schema.
  • Cedar left out loops, I/O and user functions so policies become exact logic formulas.
  • The Lean model has proofs, and differential random testing checks the Rust engine against it.
  • cedar-policy-symcc 0.8.1 and cedar symcc ask cvc5 questions like implies, equivalent and never-errors.
  • Ask the opposite of your claim. Unsatisfiable proves it. A model is a counterexample you can replay.
  • Analysis covers policies per request shape, against the schema. Your PEP, your data and your intent stay your job.

Sources

  • cedar-spec on GitHub. The Lean model, the proofs and the DRT framework.
  • cedar-policy-symcc 0.8.1 on docs.rs. The symbolic compiler, checks, solver needs.
  • Cedar: A New Language for Expressive, Fast, Safe, and Analyzable Authorization. The OOPSLA 2024 paper.
  • How We Built Cedar: A Verification-Guided Approach. The FSE 2024 industry paper about Lean and DRT.
  • Lean Into Verified Software Development. AWS blog, 2024-04-08.
  • SymCert: Verifying SMT-based policy analyses. FMCAD 2026 paper.

Cedar in Rust

Cedar itself is written in Rust. So Rust gives you the engine with nothing in between. This chapter shows how to load policies, ask questions, handle errors, measure speed and protect a web service with one middleware.

  • Set up the cedar-policy crate and choose the right features.
  • Load policies, a schema and entities, and read a decision with its reasons.
  • Validate policies and turn your own database rows into Cedar entities.
  • Handle every kind of error and fail closed.
  • Write and test an axum middleware that checks every request, with no server.

The crate and your project

Here is the whole Cargo.toml of the project in this chapter. Every program below ran with it on 2026-11-15.

[package]
name = "cd4"
version = "0.1.0"
edition = "2024"

[dependencies]
cedar-policy = "4.14.1"   # the Cedar engine
axum = "0.8.9"            # only for the web part
tokio = { version = "1", features = ["macros", "rt-multi-thread"] }
tower = { version = "0.5.3", features = ["util"] }   # gives oneshot()
serde_json = "1"
miette = "7.6.0"          # the error trait Cedar uses

A crate is a Rust package. The Cedar crate is cedar-policy. Version 4.14.1 came out on 2026-11-02. It needs Rust 1.89 or newer. We use Rust 1.99.

Two version numbers exist. The SDK version is the crate version, 4.14.1. The language version is the version of the Cedar language itself. You can print both.

fn main() {
    println!("sdk {}, language {}",
        cedar_policy::get_sdk_version(), cedar_policy::get_lang_version());
}
sdk 4.14.1, language 4.6.0

A feature flag turns optional parts of a crate on or off. The table shows the Cedar flags.

FlagOn by default?What it adds
ipaddr, decimal, datetimeYesThe extension types: IP addresses, decimals, dates and durations.
tpeNoType-aware partial evaluation and batched checks. Experimental.
partial-evalNoThe older partial evaluation. Experimental. The docs suggest tpe instead.
experimentalNoTurns on every experimental flag at once.
wasmNoBindings for WebAssembly builds.

Do not turn on experimental in production just to try one thing. Experimental parts can change in any release. Turn on only the one flag you need, and pin the exact version.

The main types and how they connect

You will use about ten types. Each one has one job. The picture shows how they fit together.

TypeIts job
PolicySetAll your policies and templates, parsed.
SchemaThe types of your entities, actions and context.
EntitiesThe users, groups and resources, with parents and attributes.
EntityUidOne entity name, like User::"alice".
ContextExtra facts about this one request, like MFA.
RequestPrincipal, action, resource and context together.
AuthorizerThe engine. It answers Allow or Deny.
ResponseThe decision plus Diagnostics: reasons and errors.
ValidatorChecks policies against the schema before you ship them.

Files go in at the start. Parsed objects stay in memory. Each request only builds a small Request and asks the Authorizer.

Load files and ask one question

We use the same small document app in this chapter. Bob owns a plan. The plan is in a shared folder. Alice is in the eng team.

entity Team;
entity User in [Team] {
  department: String,
};
entity Folder;
entity Document in [Folder] {
  owner: User,
};
action view, edit, delete appliesTo {
  principal: User,
  resource: Document,
  context: { mfa: Bool },
};
// Members of the eng team can view documents in the shared folder.
@id("team-view")
permit (
  principal in Team::"eng",
  action == Action::"view",
  resource in Folder::"shared"
);

// The owner of a document can view, edit and delete it.
@id("owner-access")
permit (
  principal,
  action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit", Action::"delete"],
  resource
)
when { resource.owner == principal };

// Nobody can delete without multi-factor login.
@id("delete-needs-mfa")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource)
unless { context.mfa };
[
  { "uid": { "type": "Team", "id": "eng" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "alice" },
    "attrs": { "department": "eng" },
    "parents": [ { "type": "Team", "id": "eng" } ] },
  { "uid": { "type": "User", "id": "bob" },
    "attrs": { "department": "sales" }, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Folder", "id": "shared" }, "attrs": {}, "parents": [] },
  { "uid": { "type": "Document", "id": "plan" },
    "attrs": { "owner": { "__entity": { "type": "User", "id": "bob" } } },
    "parents": [ { "type": "Folder", "id": "shared" } ] }
]

Now the program. It loads the three files and asks one question: can Alice view the plan?

use cedar_policy::{Authorizer, Context, Entities, EntityUid, PolicySet, Request, Schema};
use std::{error::Error, fs, str::FromStr};

fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn Error>> {
    // 1. Load the three files once.
    let policies = PolicySet::from_str(&fs::read_to_string("data/policy.cedar")?)?;
    let (schema, _warnings) =
        Schema::from_cedarschema_str(&fs::read_to_string("data/schema.cedarschema")?)?;
    let entities =
        Entities::from_json_str(&fs::read_to_string("data/entities.json")?, Some(&schema))?;

    // 2. Build one request: can alice view the plan?
    let action = EntityUid::from_str(r#"Action::"view""#)?;
    let context = Context::from_json_value(
        serde_json::json!({ "mfa": false }),
        Some((&schema, &action)),          // check the context type
    )?;
    let request = Request::new(
        EntityUid::from_str(r#"User::"alice""#)?,
        action,
        EntityUid::from_str(r#"Document::"plan""#)?,
        context,
        Some(&schema),                     // check the request too
    )?;

    // 3. Ask the authorizer.
    let response = Authorizer::new().is_authorized(&request, &policies, &entities);
    println!("decision: {:?}", response.decision());
    for id in response.diagnostics().reason() {
        let name = policies.policy(id).and_then(|p| p.annotation("id"));
        println!("reason:   {id} (@id = {name:?})");
    }
    for err in response.diagnostics().errors() {
        println!("error:    {err}");
    }
    Ok(())
}
decision: Allow
reason:   policy0 (@id = Some("team-view"))

Look at the reason. The policy id is policy0, not team-view. When you parse text, Cedar numbers the policies by position. The @id is only an annotation. Positions change when you add a policy. So logs with policy0 are hard to read later.

The fix is small. Rename each policy to its @id when you load it.

/// Parse policies and use each @id annotation as the policy id.
pub fn load_policies(src: &str) -> Result<PolicySet, Box<dyn Error>> {
    let parsed = PolicySet::from_str(src)?;
    let renamed: Vec<Policy> = parsed
        .policies()
        .map(|p| match p.annotation("id") {
            Some(name) => p.new_id(PolicyId::new(name)),
            None => p.clone(),
        })
        .collect();
    Ok(PolicySet::from_policies(renamed)?) // fails if two policies share an @id
}

From now on, the reasons say team-view and owner-access. A bonus: two policies with the same @id now fail at load time.

Do not read only decision() and throw the Response away. Always log the reasons and the errors. They tell you why. You need that during an incident.

Validate policies before you ship

Here are two broken policies. One has a typo in the action. One uses an attribute that does not exist.

@id("typo-action")
permit (principal, action == Action::"veiw", resource);

@id("typo-attr")
permit (principal, action == Action::"edit", resource)
when { resource.owner == principal.name };

Both files parse without error. The engine would run them. The first policy would never match. The second would error on every edit request. The validator finds both problems before you ship.

use cedar_policy::{Schema, ValidationMode, Validator};
use miette::Diagnostic; // gives .help() on errors
use std::{error::Error, fs};

fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn Error>> {
    let (schema, _) =
        Schema::from_cedarschema_str(&fs::read_to_string("data/schema.cedarschema")?)?;
    let validator = Validator::new(schema);
    for file in ["data/policy.cedar", "data/bad.cedar"] {
        let policies = cd4::load_policies(&fs::read_to_string(file)?)?;
        let result = validator.validate(&policies, ValidationMode::Strict);
        println!("{file}: passed = {}", result.validation_passed());
        for e in result.validation_errors() {
            println!("  error: {e}");
            if let Some(help) = e.help() {
                println!("  help:  {help}");
            }
        }
    }
    Ok(())
}
data/policy.cedar: passed = true
data/bad.cedar: passed = false
  error: for policy `typo-action`, unrecognized action `Action::"veiw"`
  help:  did you mean `Action::"edit"`?
  error: for policy `typo-attr`, attribute `name` on entity type `User` not found
  help:  did you mean `department`?

The help line comes from miette. Cedar errors use the miette::Diagnostic trait. It adds help text and source positions. ValidationMode::Strict is the default and the stable mode. The chapter Schemas and validation explains every validation error. Run the validator in your tests and at startup.

Validate at startup too. If validation fails, refuse to start. A service with no policies is safer than a service with wrong ones.

Build entities from your own data

In a real app, entities come from your database, not a JSON file. You turn each row into an Entity.

use cd4::uid;
use cedar_policy::{Entities, Entity, RestrictedExpression as Expr, Schema};
use std::collections::HashMap;

// Rows as your database returns them.
struct UserRow { name: &'static str, department: &'static str, teams: Vec<&'static str> }
struct DocRow { id: &'static str, owner: &'static str, folder: &'static str }

fn user_entity(u: &UserRow) -> Result<Entity, Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let attrs = HashMap::from([(
        "department".to_string(),
        Expr::new_string(u.department.to_string()),
    )]);
    let parents = u.teams.iter().map(|t| uid("Team", t)).collect();
    Ok(Entity::new(uid("User", u.name), attrs, parents)?)
}

fn doc_entity(d: &DocRow) -> Result<Entity, Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let attrs = HashMap::from([(
        "owner".to_string(),
        Expr::new_entity_uid(uid("User", d.owner)), // a link, not a copy
    )]);
    let parents = [uid("Folder", d.folder)].into();
    Ok(Entity::new(uid("Document", d.id), attrs, parents)?)
}
// main(): build both lists, then
// Entities::from_entities(list, Some(&schema))?

A restricted expression is a plain value: a string, a number, an entity link, a set or a record. It cannot hold variables. The program also tries a user whose department is a number.

built 5 entities: Action::"delete", Action::"edit", Action::"view", Document::"plan", User::"alice"
rejected: entity does not conform to the schema
  because: in attribute `department` on `User::"carol"`, type mismatch: value was expected to have type string, but it actually has type long: `42`

Two things to see here. First, we gave two entities but got five. With a schema, Cedar adds the action entities for you. Second, the short error message hides the detail. The detail is in the error's source() chain. Walk the chain when you log it.

The helper uid builds an entity UID safely.

/// Build an entity UID safely from a type name and a raw id.
pub fn uid(type_name: &str, id: &str) -> EntityUid {
    let t = EntityTypeName::from_str(type_name).expect("type names are fixed in code");
    EntityUid::from_type_name_and_id(t, EntityId::new(id))
}

Build a Cedar string from user input with format!, then parse it.

// id comes from the URL. A quote or backslash breaks the string.
let doc = EntityUid::from_str(&format!(r#"Document::"{id}""#))?;
// id = a"b  gives  Err("invalid token")

Keep the type fixed in code. Pass the raw id as an EntityId.

// Any text is a safe id. Nothing is parsed.
let doc_type = EntityTypeName::from_str("Document")?;
let doc = EntityUid::from_type_name_and_id(doc_type, EntityId::new(id));

Send only the entities a request needs. For one document check, that is the user, their groups, the document and its folders. The chapter Architecture: where authorization lives shows how to load them fast.

Handle errors well

Errors come from two places. Most come back as a Rust Result. But errors inside a policy come back inside the Response.

StepError typeWhere you see it
Parse policiesParseErrors, PolicySetErrorResult
Parse schemaCedarSchemaError, SchemaErrorResult
Load entitiesEntitiesErrorResult
Build contextContextJsonErrorResult
Build requestRequestValidationErrorResult
Evaluate a policyAuthorizationErrorresponse.diagnostics().errors()

Our Engine::check wraps the request steps. It returns Err for bad input, and logs policy errors.

pub fn check(&self, principal: EntityUid, action: EntityUid, resource: EntityUid,
             context: serde_json::Value) -> Result<(Decision, Vec<String>), CheckError> {
    let bad = |e: &dyn std::fmt::Display| CheckError::BadInput(e.to_string());
    let ctx = Context::from_json_value(context, Some((&self.schema, &action)))
        .map_err(|e| bad(&e))?;
    let req = Request::new(principal, action, resource, ctx, Some(&self.schema))
        .map_err(|e| bad(&e))?;
    let resp = self.authorizer.is_authorized(&req, &self.policies, &self.entities);
    for err in resp.diagnostics().errors() {
        eprintln!("policy error (skipped): {err}"); // log, never ignore
    }
    let ids = resp.diagnostics().reason().map(|id| id.to_string()).collect();
    Ok((resp.decision(), ids))
}

Now we feed it four bad inputs. Here is the real output.

parse: unexpected token `}`
context: Err(BadInput("context `{mfa: \"yes\"}` is not valid for `Action::\"delete\"`"))
action:  Err(BadInput("action `Action::\"print\"` is not defined in the supplied schema"))
type:    Err(BadInput("principal type `Document` is not valid for `Action::\"view\"`"))

The first line is a parse error for when { principal.level > }. The other three are request errors. The schema caught them before Cedar evaluated anything.

To fail closed means: when in doubt, deny. Every error path in your code must end in "no access". Inside Cedar, a policy that errors is skipped. A skipped permit cannot grant access. But a skipped forbid cannot block either. So validate your policies, and log every policy error. The chapter Policy syntax and how decisions are made explains this rule.

Do not write .unwrap_or(Decision::Allow) or return "allow" from an error branch. Also do not call unwrap() on request data. A bad header would crash the whole service.

Performance and caching

How fast is Cedar? We measured it on this machine, an Apple M2 Pro, with a release build. Each line is an average over many runs.

/// Run f n times and return the average time in microseconds.
fn micros<T>(n: u32, mut f: impl FnMut() -> T) -> f64 {
    let start = Instant::now();
    for _ in 0..n { black_box(f()); }
    start.elapsed().as_secs_f64() * 1e6 / n as f64
}
// 1,000 extra policies, one per user:
// @id("u7") permit (principal == User::"u7", action == Action::"view",
//                   resource in Folder::"f7");
println!("is_authorized, 3  {:>7.1} us",
