         micros(20_000, || auth.is_authorized(&req, &small, &entities)));
parse 3 policies              72.8 us
parse 1003 policies         8544.1 us
parse schema                 219.6 us
parse entities (5)            26.6 us
build request + context        5.5 us
is_authorized, 3               3.3 us
is_authorized, 1003          444.4 us

A microsecond is one millionth of a second. We ran the program three times. The numbers changed by less than ten percent.

Three lessons follow from these numbers.

  1. Parse once. Parsing three policies costs about 20 decisions. Keep the parsed PolicySet and Schema in memory.
  2. Share, do not copy. Put the parsed objects in an Arc. All Cedar types here are safe to share between threads.
  3. Watch the policy count. Time grew with the number of policies, from 3 to 444 microseconds. Many per-user policies are slow. One policy over groups or attributes is fast.

Policies change while the service runs. A hot reload swaps in new policies without a restart.

/// Hot reload: readers get the current engine, a writer swaps in a new one.
pub struct Shared(std::sync::RwLock<std::sync::Arc<Engine>>);

impl Shared {
    /// Cheap: clone the Arc and drop the lock at once.
    pub fn current(&self) -> std::sync::Arc<Engine> {
        self.0.read().expect("lock").clone()
    }
    /// Load new files first. Swap only if they load without error.
    pub fn reload(&self, dir: &str) -> Result<(), Box<dyn Error>> {
        let fresh = Engine::from_files(dir)?; // a bad file never replaces a good one
        *self.0.write().expect("lock") = std::sync::Arc::new(fresh);
        Ok(())
    }
}
before: (Allow, ["team-view"])
reload failed, old engine kept: No such file or directory (os error 2)
after:  (Allow, ["team-view"])

Do not parse the policy files inside the request handler. It works in a demo. Under load it makes every request 20 times slower. Also, do not swap in new policies before they parse and validate.

Your numbers will differ. Measure on your own machine with your own policies. Use a release build: debug builds are much slower.

An axum middleware for every request

A middleware runs before every handler. It is the right place for one shared check. The slides show what our middleware does, step by step.

The request arrives

DELETE /docs/plan with the header x-user: bob.

Find the principal

Read the user. No user means 401. A real app reads a verified token here.

Map to Cedar

The method gives the action: GET is view, PUT is edit, DELETE is delete. The path gives the resource.

Build the context

Add facts like mfa. They must come from a trusted place.

Ask Cedar

Call engine.check. It takes a few microseconds.

Act on the answer

Allow: run the handler. Deny: 403. Error: 400. Never run the handler on an error.

In axum, an extractor pulls data out of the request. Our middleware uses two: State and Path.

/// Middleware: runs before every /docs/{id} handler.
async fn authorize(
    State(engine): State<Arc<Engine>>,
    Path(doc_id): Path<String>,
    req: Request,
    next: Next,
) -> Response {
    // In a real app this comes from a verified token, not a raw header.
    let Some(user) = req.headers().get("x-user").and_then(|v| v.to_str().ok()) else {
        return StatusCode::UNAUTHORIZED.into_response();
    };
    let mfa = req.headers().get("x-mfa").is_some_and(|v| v == "true");
    let action = match *req.method() {
        Method::GET => "view",
        Method::PUT => "edit",
        Method::DELETE => "delete",
        _ => return StatusCode::METHOD_NOT_ALLOWED.into_response(),
    };
    let result = engine.check(
        uid("User", user),
        uid("Action", action),
        uid("Document", &doc_id),
        serde_json::json!({ "mfa": mfa }),
    );
    match result {
        Ok((Decision::Allow, _)) => next.run(req).await,
        Ok((Decision::Deny, _)) => StatusCode::FORBIDDEN.into_response(),
        Err(_) => StatusCode::BAD_REQUEST.into_response(), // fail closed
    }
}
async fn read_doc(Path(id): Path<String>) -> String { format!("content of {id}") }
async fn edit_doc(Path(id): Path<String>) -> String { format!("saved {id}") }
async fn delete_doc(Path(id): Path<String>) -> String { format!("deleted {id}") }

pub fn app(engine: Arc<Engine>) -> Router {
    Router::new()
        .route("/docs/{id}", get(read_doc).put(edit_doc).delete(delete_doc))
        // route_layer: only matched routes are checked, so Path works.
        .route_layer(middleware::from_fn_with_state(engine, authorize))
}

Axum 0.8 writes path parameters as {id}. Older versions used :id. We use route_layer, not layer. So the middleware runs only after a route matches, and Path has a value.

Now the test. We never start a server and never open a port. The oneshot method from tower::ServiceExt sends one request straight into the router.

use axum::{body::Body, http::Request};
use cd4::{Engine, web::app};
use std::sync::Arc;
use tower::ServiceExt; // gives .oneshot()

#[tokio::test]
async fn http_matrix() {
    let engine = Arc::new(Engine::from_files("data").expect("files load"));
    // method, path, user, mfa, expected status
    let cases = [
        ("GET", "/docs/plan", Some("alice"), false, 200), // team-view
        ("PUT", "/docs/plan", Some("alice"), false, 403), // not the owner
        ("PUT", "/docs/plan", Some("bob"), false, 200),   // owner-access
        ("DELETE", "/docs/plan", Some("bob"), false, 403), // forbid: no mfa
        ("DELETE", "/docs/plan", Some("bob"), true, 200), // owner + mfa
        ("GET", "/docs/plan", None, false, 401),          // no user header
        ("GET", "/docs/nope", Some("bob"), false, 403),   // unknown document
    ];
    for (method, path, user, mfa, want) in cases {
        let mut req = Request::builder().method(method).uri(path);
        if let Some(u) = user { req = req.header("x-user", u); }
        if mfa { req = req.header("x-mfa", "true"); }
        let resp = app(engine.clone())
            .oneshot(req.body(Body::empty()).unwrap()) // no server, no port
            .await
            .unwrap();
        println!("{method:6} {path:10} user={:5} mfa={mfa:5} -> {}",
                 user.unwrap_or("-"), resp.status().as_u16());
        assert_eq!(resp.status().as_u16(), want);
    }
}
CARGO_TARGET_DIR=/home/you/cargo-target cargo test -q --test web -- --nocapture
running 1 test
GET    /docs/plan user=alice mfa=false -> 200
PUT    /docs/plan user=alice mfa=false -> 403
PUT    /docs/plan user=bob   mfa=false -> 200
DELETE /docs/plan user=bob   mfa=false -> 403
DELETE /docs/plan user=bob   mfa=true  -> 200
GET    /docs/plan user=-     mfa=false -> 401
policy error (skipped): error while evaluating policy `owner-access`: entity `Document::"nope"` does not exist
GET    /docs/nope user=bob   mfa=false -> 403
.
test result: ok. 1 passed; 0 failed; 0 ignored; 0 measured; 0 filtered out; finished in 0.02s

Read the last case slowly. The document nope does not exist. So resource.owner fails, and Cedar skips owner-access. No policy grants access, so the answer is 403. The log line shows the skipped policy. A real app would check that the document exists first and return 404.

Never trust a raw x-user or x-mfa header from the internet. Anyone can send them. Put an authentication layer in front. It checks the token and then sets the principal and the MFA fact. The chapter Context and requests explains why the context must be trusted.

Know the three outcomes and their status codes. No identity: 401. Identity but Deny: 403. Bad input or error: deny too, never run the handler.

Expert notes

  • Async. is_authorized is plain CPU work with no waiting. A few microseconds is fine inside an async handler. For very large policy sets, measure first. Then consider tokio::task::spawn_blocking.
  • Partial evaluation. The tpe flag can answer "which documents may Alice view?" with unknown parts. It is experimental. See Architecture: where authorization lives.
  • Templates. PolicySet::link and unlink manage linked policies in Rust. See Policy templates and linked policies.
  • Tests. Put the decision matrix in cargo test, like above. The chapter Testing Cedar policies adds golden files and the CLI.
  • Proof. The Rust engine is tested against a Lean model of Cedar. See Analysis and formal verification.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for Cedar in Rust
PatternPurposeModelNote
Parse once, decide many timesKeep requests fastArc::new(Engine::from_files("data")?)Parsing costs about 20 decisions.
Rename to @id at loadReadable reasons in logsp.new_id(PolicyId::new(name))Text parsing gives policy0, policy1 by position.
Validate in strict modeCatch typos before shippingvalidator.validate(&ps, ValidationMode::Strict)Parsing alone does not find a wrong action name.
Schema on every inputReject bad requests earlyRequest::new(p, a, r, ctx, Some(&schema))Passing None skips all type checks.
Safe UID from partsNo string injectionEntityUid::from_type_name_and_id(t, EntityId::new(id))Never format! user input into Cedar text.
Row to entityUse your own databaseEntity::new(uid, attrs, parents)Links use new_entity_uid, not a string.
Read the whole responseKnow whyresp.diagnostics().reason() and .errors()Policy errors are not in a Result.
Walk the error chainSee the real causewhile let Some(c) = cause { ... c.source() }The top message is often short.
Fail closedErrors never grant accessErr(_) => StatusCode::BAD_REQUESTNever map an error to Allow.
Swap after loadHot reload safelylet fresh = Engine::from_files(dir)?; then writeA broken file must not replace a good one.
route_layer middlewareOne check for all routes.route_layer(from_fn_with_state(engine, authorize))Use {id} paths in axum 0.8.
Test with oneshotHTTP tests with no serverapp(engine).oneshot(req).awaitNeeds tower with the util feature.

Practice

You parse a policy file with PolicySet::from_str. What is the id of the first policy?

  • Its @id annotation
  • policy0
  • The first word of its comment

Text parsing numbers policies by position. The @id is only an annotation, unless you rename the policies yourself.

Where do you find an error that happened while a policy was evaluated?

  • In the Err of is_authorized
  • In response.diagnostics().errors()
  • Cedar panics

is_authorized returns a Response, not a Result. The erroring policy is skipped and listed in the diagnostics.

Which choice makes a busy service fastest?

  • Parse the policy file in each handler
  • Parse the policies once per user
  • Parse once at startup and share with Arc

On our machine, parsing three policies took about 73 microseconds. One decision took about 3.

To check policies against the schema, you create a ? from the schema.

To send one request into an axum router with no server, you call ? from tower::ServiceExt.

In axum 0.8 a path parameter is written as ? in the route.

  1. Why does the request for /docs/nope return 403 and not 500?
    Answer

    The entity does not exist, so resource.owner errors. Cedar skips that policy and lists the error in the diagnostics. No other policy allows the request, so the decision is Deny. Our middleware maps Deny to 403.

  2. Your colleague writes Request::new(p, a, r, ctx, None). What do you lose?
    Answer

    With None, Cedar does not check the request against the schema. A wrong principal type or a wrong context type is not caught at the start. Pass Some(&schema).

  3. Write the match arm list for the middleware result so that every error denies.
    Answer

    Ok((Decision::Allow, _)) => next.run(req).await, then Ok((Decision::Deny, _)) => 403, then Err(_) => 400. Only the Allow arm runs the handler.

  4. You have 50,000 users. Each has a personal policy like permit (principal == User::"u7", ...). What problem do you expect, and what is a better model?
    Answer

    Decision time grows with the number of policies. With 1003 policies we already saw about 444 microseconds. A better model uses groups or attributes: one policy such as principal in Team::"eng", or a template linked per grant, plus a store that sends only the needed policies.

  5. Design a safe hot reload. What must happen before the swap?
    Answer

    Read the new files. Parse the policies, schema and entities. Run the validator in strict mode. Only if all steps pass, take the write lock and swap the Arc. Readers keep the old engine until then. Log the new version.

Key takeaways

  • The crate is cedar-policy, version 4.14.1 on 2026-11-15, Cedar language 4.6.
  • Parse PolicySet, Schema and Entities once. Build only a small Request per call.
  • Rename policies to their @id, and log reasons and errors from the diagnostics.
  • Validate in strict mode, and pass the schema to entities, context and requests.
  • Build UIDs with EntityId::new, never with format!.
  • Every error path denies. A middleware with route_layer checks every route, and oneshot tests it with no server.

Sources

  • cedar-policy 4.14.1 on docs.rs. The API: PolicySet, Schema, Entities, Authorizer, Validator, feature flags.
  • cedar-policy on crates.io. Versions and release dates.
  • cedar-policy changelog. What changed in each release, and the language version.
  • Cedar docs: Authorization. How the authorizer combines policies.
  • axum 0.8.9: from_fn_with_state. Writing middleware with state.
  • tower 0.5.3: ServiceExt. The oneshot method used in the tests.

Cedar in Python and other languages

Most teams do not write Rust. Good news: you can run the same Cedar engine from Python, JavaScript, Go and Java. This chapter shows each one with real output, then a FastAPI check, the command line from scripts, and how to choose.

  • Authorize requests from Python with cedarpy and read the result safely.
  • Make Python calls fast with parsed handles and batches.
  • Protect FastAPI routes with one dependency and test them with no server.
  • Use Cedar from JavaScript, Go and Java, and call the CLI from a script.
  • Choose the right binding for a project, and explain why.

Your choices at a glance

Cedar's main engine is written in Rust. Other languages reach it in three ways.

  • A binding calls the Rust engine from your language. Python, Java and JavaScript work this way.
  • A reimplementation rewrites the engine in your language. Go works this way.
  • A subprocess runs the cedar command line tool and reads its answer.
LanguagePackageOwnerVersion on 2026-11-15Engine
Pythoncedarpy (PyPI)k9 Security (community)4.12.2Rust Cedar 4.12.0
JavaScript@cedar-policy/cedar-wasm (npm)Cedar team (official)4.14.0 (see the warning below)Rust compiled to WebAssembly
Gogithub.com/cedar-policy/cedar-goCedar team (official)v1.9.2Pure Go
Javacom.cedarpolicy:cedar-java (Maven)Cedar team (official)4.11.0Rust through a native library

We checked every version on the package sites on 2026-11-15. All four projects had new commits in the last month.

Do not assume all bindings have the same features. They follow the Rust engine at different speeds. For example, cedarpy 4.12.2 uses engine 4.12.0, while the Rust crate is at 4.14.1. Go has no validator yet.

Python with cedarpy

cedarpy wraps the Rust engine for Python. A company called k9 Security makes it. Its README says it is not officially supported by AWS or the Cedar team. It ships ready-made wheels for Linux, Mac and Windows, for Python 3.10 to 3.14. On a Mac it needs Python 3.11 or newer.

We use the same document app as in Cedar in Rust. Bob owns a plan in the shared folder. Alice is in the eng team. Here is the policy file again.

@id("team-view")
permit (principal in Team::"eng", action == Action::"view",
        resource in Folder::"shared");

@id("owner-access")
permit (principal, action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit", Action::"delete"],
        resource)
when { resource.owner == principal };

@id("delete-needs-mfa")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"delete", resource)
unless { context.mfa };

The schema and the entities are the same files as in the Rust chapter. Now the first Python call.

from pathlib import Path
from cedarpy import is_authorized

policies = Path("data/policy.cedar").read_text()
schema = Path("data/schema.cedarschema").read_text()
entities = Path("data/entities.json").read_text()  # a JSON string or a list

request = {
    "principal": 'User::"alice"',
    "action": 'Action::"view"',
    "resource": 'Document::"plan"',
    "context": {"mfa": False},
}
result = is_authorized(request, policies, entities, schema=schema)
print("decision:", result.decision)
print("allowed: ", result.allowed)
print("reasons: ", result.diagnostics.reasons)
print("by @id:  ", result.diagnostics.id_annotations_by_reason)
print("errors:  ", result.diagnostics.errors)
uv run --with cedarpy==4.12.2 python3.14 first.py
decision: Decision.Allow
allowed:  True
reasons:  ['policy0']
by @id:   {'policy0': 'team-view'}
errors:   []

The reason is policy0: Cedar numbers policies by position. cedarpy helps here. The map id_annotations_by_reason turns each reason into its @id.

The result has a third decision value. Look at this table.

result.decisionMeaningresult.allowed
Decision.AllowA permit matched and no forbid matched.True
Decision.DenyNo permit matched, or a forbid matched.False
Decision.NoDecisionCedar could not even build the request.False

Do not write if result.decision == Decision.Deny: block(). Then NoDecision slips through as "not denied". Always test for the good case: if result.decision != Decision.Allow: block(), or use result.allowed.

Parse once: handles and batches

The first example passed text. So cedarpy parsed all three files on every call. That is slow. We measured it on an Apple M2 Pro.

schema = Schema.from_str(text["schema.cedarschema"])
handles = (PolicySet.from_str(text["policy.cedar"]),
           Entities.from_json_str(text["entities.json"], schema=schema), schema)
strings = (text["policy.cedar"], text["entities.json"], text["schema.cedarschema"])

for name, (p, e, s) in [("strings every call", strings), ("parsed handles", handles)]:
    n = 5000
    t = timeit.timeit(lambda: is_authorized(request, p, e, schema=s), number=n)
    print(f"{name:20} {t / n * 1e6:7.1f} us per call")
strings every call     362.7 us per call
parsed handles          39.2 us per call

A handle keeps parsed data in memory. cedarpy has three: PolicySet, Entities and Schema. You can pass a handle anywhere you passed text before.

A batch is the other speed tool. is_authorized_batch takes a list of requests. It returns the results in the same order. Each request can carry a correlation_id, so you can match results to requests.

from pathlib import Path
from cedarpy import PolicySet, Schema, Entities, is_authorized_batch

# Parse once, reuse many times.
policies = PolicySet.from_str(Path("data/policy.cedar").read_text())
schema = Schema.from_str(Path("data/schema.cedarschema").read_text())
entities = Entities.from_json_str(Path("data/entities.json").read_text(), schema=schema)

def req(user, action, doc, mfa=False):
    return {"principal": f'User::"{user}"', "action": f'Action::"{action}"',
            "resource": f'Document::"{doc}"', "context": {"mfa": mfa},
            "correlation_id": f"{user}-{action}-{doc}-{mfa}"}

requests = [req("alice", "view", "plan"), req("alice", "edit", "plan"),
            req("bob", "delete", "plan"), req("bob", "delete", "plan", mfa=True),
            req("bob", "view", "nope")]
results = is_authorized_batch(requests, policies, entities, schema=schema)
for r in results:
    names = [r.diagnostics.id_annotations_by_reason.get(p, p) for p in r.diagnostics.reasons]
    print(f"{r.correlation_id:22} {r.decision.value:5} {names} {r.diagnostics.errors}")
alice-view-plan-False  Allow ['team-view'] []
alice-edit-plan-False  Deny  [] []
bob-delete-plan-False  Deny  ['delete-needs-mfa'] []
bob-delete-plan-True   Allow ['owner-access'] []
bob-view-nope-False    Deny  [] ['error while evaluating policy `policy1`: entity `Document::"nope"` does not exist']

Read the last line. The document nope does not exist. So owner-access errored and was skipped. The error message uses the position id policy1. Keep the @id map at hand when you read logs.

The GIL matters for threads. By default a cedarpy call holds the GIL until it returns. validate_policies and the from_... constructors accept release_gil=True. Use it for slow work, like validating a large policy set in asyncio.to_thread.

The f-strings in req() are fine for fixed test data. Never build entity strings from user input this way. A quote in the input breaks the string. Use the dict form instead: {"type": "User", "id": user}.

Errors and validation in Python

This script sends a good request, two bad ones, a broken policy and a policy file with typos.

from pathlib import Path
from cedarpy import PolicySet, is_authorized, validate_policies

policies = Path("data/policy.cedar").read_text()
schema = Path("data/schema.cedarschema").read_text()
entities = Path("data/entities.json").read_text()

def ask(action, context):
    r = is_authorized({"principal": {"type": "User", "id": "bob"},  # dict form: safe
                       "action": {"type": "Action", "id": action},
                       "resource": {"type": "Document", "id": "plan"},
                       "context": context}, policies, entities, schema=schema)
    print(f"{action:6} {context!s:16} -> {r.decision.value}  {r.diagnostics.errors}")

ask("delete", {"mfa": True})
ask("delete", {"mfa": "yes"})   # wrong type
ask("print", {})                # not in the schema

try:
    PolicySet.from_str("permit (principal, action, resource) when { principal.level > };")
except ValueError as e:
    print("parse:", e)

v = validate_policies(Path("data/bad.cedar").read_text(), schema)
print("valid:", v.validation_passed)
for err in v.errors:
    print(" ", err)
delete {'mfa': True}    -> Allow  []
delete {'mfa': 'yes'}   -> NoDecision  ['failed to build request: context `{mfa: "yes"}` is not valid for `Action::"delete"`']
print  {}               -> NoDecision  ['failed to build request: action `Action::"print"` does not exist in the supplied schema']
parse: unexpected token `}`
valid: False
  [policy0] for policy `policy0`, unrecognized action `Action::"veiw"`
  [policy0] for policy `policy0`, unable to find an applicable action given the policy scope constraints
  [policy1] for policy `policy1`, attribute `name` on entity type `User` not found

Three rules come from this output.

  1. Bad requests do not raise. They return NoDecision with the reason in errors. Treat them as deny.
  2. Bad policy text raises. PolicySet.from_str raises ValueError. Let it crash at startup.
  3. Validation returns a result. Check validation_passed and print every error.

Messages can differ between versions. The same bad.cedar in the Rust crate 4.14.1 and the CLI 4.14.1 shows "unable to find an applicable action" as a warning, not an error. cedarpy 4.12.2 lists it as an error. Pin your versions, and do not test exact message text across versions.

A FastAPI dependency

In FastAPI, a dependency runs before the route. If it raises HTTPException, the route never runs. That is the perfect place for one Cedar check.

from pathlib import Path
from typing import Annotated
from fastapi import APIRouter, Depends, FastAPI, Header, HTTPException, Request
from cedarpy import Decision, Entities, PolicySet, Schema, is_authorized

DATA = Path(__file__).parent / "data"
# Parse once when the module loads. A bad file stops the app from starting.
SCHEMA = Schema.from_str((DATA / "schema.cedarschema").read_text())
POLICIES = PolicySet.from_str((DATA / "policy.cedar").read_text())
ENTITIES = Entities.from_json_str((DATA / "entities.json").read_text(), schema=SCHEMA)
ACTIONS = {"GET": "view", "PUT": "edit", "DELETE": "delete"}

def authorize(
    request: Request,
    doc_id: str,                                     # the path parameter
    x_user: Annotated[str | None, Header()] = None,  # real apps: a verified token
    x_mfa: Annotated[str | None, Header()] = None,
) -> str:
    if x_user is None:
        raise HTTPException(status_code=401, detail="who are you?")
    result = is_authorized(
        {"principal": {"type": "User", "id": x_user},
         "action": {"type": "Action", "id": ACTIONS[request.method]},
         "resource": {"type": "Document", "id": doc_id},
         "context": {"mfa": x_mfa == "true"}},
        POLICIES, ENTITIES, schema=SCHEMA)
    for err in result.diagnostics.errors:
        print("cedar error:", err)                   # log, never ignore
    if result.decision != Decision.Allow:            # Deny AND NoDecision block
        raise HTTPException(status_code=403, detail="not allowed")
    return x_user
docs = APIRouter(prefix="/docs", dependencies=[Depends(authorize)])

@docs.get("/{doc_id}")
def read_doc(doc_id: str): return {"doc": doc_id}

@docs.put("/{doc_id}")
def edit_doc(doc_id: str): return {"saved": doc_id}

@docs.delete("/{doc_id}")
def delete_doc(doc_id: str): return {"deleted": doc_id}

app = FastAPI()
app.include_router(docs)

The dependency sits on the router. So every route under /docs gets the check, and nobody can forget it on a new route. FastAPI fills doc_id from the path, and the headers from the request.

Now the test. TestClient calls the app inside the same process. No server starts and no port opens.

import pytest
from fastapi.testclient import TestClient
from app import app

client = TestClient(app)  # calls the app in-process: no server, no port

@pytest.mark.parametrize("method,path,headers,want", [
    ("GET", "/docs/plan", {"x-user": "alice"}, 200),
    ("PUT", "/docs/plan", {"x-user": "alice"}, 403),
    ("PUT", "/docs/plan", {"x-user": "bob"}, 200),
    ("DELETE", "/docs/plan", {"x-user": "bob"}, 403),
    ("DELETE", "/docs/plan", {"x-user": "bob", "x-mfa": "true"}, 200),
    ("GET", "/docs/plan", {}, 401),
    ("GET", "/docs/nope", {"x-user": "bob"}, 403),
])
def test_matrix(method, path, headers, want):
    resp = client.request(method, path, headers=headers)
    print(f"{method:6} {path:10} {headers} -> {resp.status_code} {resp.json()}")
    assert resp.status_code == want
uv run --with cedarpy==4.12.2 --with fastapi==0.163.1 --with httpx2 --with pytest \
  python3.14 -m pytest -q -s test_app.py
GET    /docs/plan {'x-user': 'alice'} -> 200 {'doc': 'plan'}
.PUT    /docs/plan {'x-user': 'alice'} -> 403 {'detail': 'not allowed'}
.PUT    /docs/plan {'x-user': 'bob'} -> 200 {'saved': 'plan'}
.DELETE /docs/plan {'x-user': 'bob'} -> 403 {'detail': 'not allowed'}
.DELETE /docs/plan {'x-user': 'bob', 'x-mfa': 'true'} -> 200 {'deleted': 'plan'}
.GET    /docs/plan {} -> 401 {'detail': 'who are you?'}
.cedar error: error while evaluating policy `policy1`: entity `Document::"nope"` does not exist
GET    /docs/nope {'x-user': 'bob'} -> 403 {'detail': 'not allowed'}
.
7 passed in 0.50s

The dots are pytest's marks for passed tests. All seven cases pass. The versions were Python 3.14.3, FastAPI 0.163.1, Starlette 1.10.0 and pytest 9.2.0.

With Starlette 1.10, TestClient printed a deprecation warning when it ran on httpx. The warning asked for httpx2. With httpx2 the warning went away. Check the Starlette release notes: this can change.

Do not read x-user from the internet in a real app. Anyone can send that header. Put token checking in front, and set the principal from the verified token. See Context and requests.

JavaScript and WebAssembly

WebAssembly lets Rust code run in Node and in the browser. The official package is @cedar-policy/cedar-wasm. It has three builds: esm, nodejs and web.

import { readFileSync } from "node:fs";
import * as cedar from "@cedar-policy/cedar-wasm/nodejs";

const read = (f) => readFileSync(`data/${f}`, "utf8");
console.log("cedar-wasm", cedar.getCedarSDKVersion(), "language", cedar.getCedarLangVersion());

function check(user, action, mfa) {
  const answer = cedar.isAuthorized({
    principal: { type: "User", id: user },
    action: { type: "Action", id: action },
    resource: { type: "Document", id: "plan" },
    context: { mfa },
    schema: read("schema.cedarschema"),          // Cedar text works here
    policies: { staticPolicies: read("policy.cedar") },
    entities: JSON.parse(read("entities.json")),
  });
  if (answer.type === "failure") {               // bad input: treat as deny
    return "failure: " + answer.errors.map((e) => e.message).join("; ");
  }
  const { decision, diagnostics } = answer.response;
  return `${decision} ${JSON.stringify(diagnostics.reason)}`;
}
console.log(check("alice", "view", false));
console.log(check("bob", "delete", false));
console.log(check("bob", "delete", "yes"));
cedar-wasm 4.14.0 language 4.6
allow ["policy0"]
deny ["policy2"]
failure: context `{mfa: "yes"}` is not valid for `Action::"delete"`

The answer is a tagged object. type is "success" or "failure". Only on success is there a response with decision and diagnostics. Decisions are lowercase strings: "allow" and "deny".

The JavaScript package can parse once too. preparsePolicySet and preparseSchema store parsed data in a cache inside the module. Then statefulIsAuthorized uses the cache by name.

cedar.preparsePolicySet("docs-v1", { staticPolicies: policies });
cedar.preparseSchema("docs-schema", schema);

time("text every call", () => cedar.isAuthorized({ ...req, schema,
  policies: { staticPolicies: policies }, entities }));
time("preparsed (stateful)", () => cedar.statefulIsAuthorized({ ...req,
  preparsedSchemaName: "docs-schema", preparsedPolicySetId: "docs-v1", entities }));
text every call        714.0 us per call
preparsed (stateful)   74.2 us per call

Again, parsing once made each call about ten times faster.

We first installed version 4.14.1. npm printed a warning: the 4.14.1 package is deprecated because it is missing all its wasm, JavaScript and type files. Its message says to use 4.14.0. So we pinned 4.14.0. Lesson: read install warnings, and pin exact versions.

The Cedar team also has an official Express middleware, authorization-for-expressjs. It is a separate project with its own README.

Go and Java

cedar-go is a full rewrite in Go. It needs no C compiler and no WebAssembly. It is tested against the shared Cedar test suite. Its README lists what is missing: no CLI, no formatter, no templates, no partial evaluation, and the validator is only experimental.

package main

import (
    "encoding/json"
    "fmt"
    "log"
    "os"

    cedar "github.com/cedar-policy/cedar-go"
)

func main() {
    src, _ := os.ReadFile("data/policy.cedar")
    ps, err := cedar.NewPolicySetFromBytes("policy.cedar", src) // ids: policy0, policy1, ...
    if err != nil {
        log.Fatal(err)
    }
    var entities cedar.EntityMap
    raw, _ := os.ReadFile("data/entities.json")
    if err := json.Unmarshal(raw, &entities); err != nil {
        log.Fatal(err)
    }
    for _, c := range []struct{ user, action string; mfa bool }{
        {"alice", "view", false}, {"bob", "delete", false}, {"bob", "delete", true},
    } {
        req := cedar.Request{
            Principal: cedar.NewEntityUID("User", cedar.String(c.user)),
            Action:    cedar.NewEntityUID("Action", cedar.String(c.action)),
            Resource:  cedar.NewEntityUID("Document", "plan"),
            Context:   cedar.NewRecord(cedar.RecordMap{"mfa": cedar.Boolean(c.mfa)}),
        }
        decision, diag := cedar.Authorize(ps, entities, req)
        for _, r := range diag.Reasons {
            fmt.Printf("%s %s mfa=%v -> %s by %s (line %d)\n",
                c.user, c.action, c.mfa, decision, r.PolicyID, r.Position.Line)
        }
    }
}
alice view mfa=false -> allow by policy0 (line 2)
bob delete mfa=false -> deny by policy2 (line 19)
bob delete mfa=true -> allow by policy1 (line 10)

Same policies, same answers as Python and Rust. Go also gives the line number of each deciding policy. Line 2 is the @id line, where the policy starts. Notice: Go has no schema here, so nothing checks the request types.

Go is fast. In a loop of 200,000 calls, one Authorize took 0.75 microseconds on our machine. That loop does less work than our Rust and Python tests: no schema checks at all. So do not compare the numbers too closely.

cedar-java calls the Rust engine through a native library. It needs JDK 17 or newer. Use the uber jar: it bundles the native library. The code below follows the 4.11.0 Javadoc. We did not run it here.

// Gradle: implementation 'com.cedarpolicy:cedar-java:4.11.0:uber'
import com.cedarpolicy.BasicAuthorizationEngine;
import com.cedarpolicy.model.AuthorizationRequest;
import com.cedarpolicy.model.AuthorizationResponse;
import com.cedarpolicy.model.entity.Entities;
import com.cedarpolicy.model.policy.PolicySet;
import com.cedarpolicy.value.EntityUID;
import com.cedarpolicy.value.PrimBool;
import java.nio.file.Path;
import java.util.Map;

public class Check {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        PolicySet policies = PolicySet.parsePolicies(Path.of("data/policy.cedar"));
        Entities entities = Entities.parse(Path.of("data/entities.json"));
        AuthorizationRequest request = new AuthorizationRequest(
            EntityUID.parse("User::\"alice\"").orElseThrow(),
            EntityUID.parse("Action::\"view\"").orElseThrow(),
            EntityUID.parse("Document::\"plan\"").orElseThrow(),
            Map.of("mfa", new PrimBool(false)));
        AuthorizationResponse r =
            new BasicAuthorizationEngine().isAuthorized(request, policies, entities);
        // r.type is Success or Failure. On Success, read r.success.
        boolean allowed = r.success.map(s -> s.isAllowed()).orElse(false); // fail closed
        System.out.println(allowed);
    }
}

Java was not run because this machine has no Maven or Gradle. All class and method names come from the cedar-java 4.11.0 Javadoc. Check the Javadoc before you copy it.

Calling the CLI from scripts

Sometimes there is no binding for your language. Or you only need checks in a build script. Then call the cedar command line tool. Its exit code tells you the answer.

Exit codeMeaning
0Success. For authorize: ALLOW.
1Failure: bad input or bad files.
2authorize said DENY.
3validate found problems.
 
import json, subprocess, tempfile

CEDAR = "cedar"                      # or a full path to the binary
EXIT = {0: "ALLOW", 2: "DENY"}        # 1 = failure, 3 = validation failure

def authorize(principal, action, resource, context):
    with tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile("w", suffix=".json") as ctx:
        json.dump(context, ctx)
        ctx.flush()
        run = subprocess.run(
            [CEDAR, "authorize", "-v", "--error-format", "plain",
             "--policies", "data/policy.cedar", "--schema", "data/schema.cedarschema",
             "--entities", "data/entities.json", "--principal", principal,
             "--action", action, "--resource", resource, "--context", ctx.name],
            capture_output=True, text=True)    # a list of args: no shell
    decision = EXIT.get(run.returncode, f"ERROR (exit {run.returncode})")
    return decision, run.stdout.strip().splitlines()[-1], run.stderr.strip()[:70]

print(authorize('User::"bob"', 'Action::"delete"', 'Document::"plan"', {"mfa": False}))
print(authorize('User::"bob"', 'Action::"delete"', 'Document::"plan"', {"mfa": True}))
print(authorize('User::"bob"', 'Action::"delete"', 'Document::"plan"', {"mfa": "yes"}))
('DENY', '  delete-needs-mfa', '')
('ALLOW', '  owner-access', '')
('ERROR (exit 1)', '    Caused by: context `{mfa: "yes"}` is not valid for `Action::"delete"`', '')

With -v, the CLI prints the deciding policies by their @id. We used CLI 4.14.1. Each call starts a new process, and that costs time. On our machine one call took 6.0 ms. A parsed cedarpy call took 0.04 ms. So use the CLI for scripts and CI, not for every web request. The chapter Testing Cedar policies shows the other CLI commands.

Do not build the command as one string with shell=True. A quote in an id could run other commands. Pass a list of arguments, as above.

How to choose

Follow these questions in order. Stop at the first "yes".

Is your service in Rust?

Use cedar-policy. You get every feature first, and no extra layer.

Do you need Cedar on AWS with no engine to run?

Consider Amazon Verified Permissions. See Amazon Verified Permissions and Cedar in products.

Is it Java, JavaScript or a browser?

Use the official cedar-java or cedar-wasm. They run the Rust engine inside.

Is it Go and you want no C or Wasm?

Use cedar-go. Validate policies elsewhere, for example with the CLI in CI.

Is it Python?

Use cedarpy. Pin the version, and check it follows the engine version you need.

None of these?

Call the CLI from scripts, or run Cedar as a separate service. See Architecture: where authorization lives.

NeedRustPythonJS/WasmGoCLI
Authorizeyesyesyesyesyes
Validate with a schemayesyesyesexperimentalyes
Templatesyesyesyesnoyes
Partial evaluationexperimentalis_authorized_partialisAuthorizedPartialnoexperimental build
Cost per call, parsed once (our machine)about 3 usabout 39 usabout 74 usabout 0.75 usabout 6 ms

Know the three ways to reach Cedar from another language: a binding to the Rust engine, a reimplementation, or a subprocess. Know one strength and one weakness of each.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for Cedar outside Rust
PatternPurposeModelNote
Allow-only checkBlock Deny and NoDecisionif result.decision != Decision.Allow: raise ...Never test only for Deny.
Parse once (any language)Fast callsPOLICIES = PolicySet.from_str(text) or preparsePolicySet(id, ps)About ten times faster than text per call.
Entities handle with schemaCheck entities at loadEntities.from_json_str(js, schema=SCHEMA)Type errors show up at startup, not per request.
Batch with idsMany checks in one callis_authorized_batch(reqs, ps, ents)Add correlation_id to match results.
Dict form for idsNo string injection{"type": "User", "id": user}Never f-string user input into Cedar text.
@id lookupReadable reasonsdiagnostics.id_annotations_by_reasonError messages still use policy0, policy1.
Router-level dependencyEvery route is checkedAPIRouter(dependencies=[Depends(authorize)])Per-route checks are easy to forget.
In-process HTTP testTest with no serverTestClient(app).request(m, path, headers=h)Use httpx2 with Starlette 1.10.
Tagged answer in JSHandle failureif (answer.type === "failure") deny()Decisions are lowercase strings.
CLI exit codeScripts and CI{0: "ALLOW", 2: "DENY"}About 6 ms per call: not for hot paths.
Argument list, no shellSafe subprocesssubprocess.run([CEDAR, "authorize", ...])Never shell=True with ids.
Pin exact versionsStable behaviourcedarpy==4.12.2, cedar-wasm@4.14.0One release can be broken, like cedar-wasm 4.14.1.

Practice

A cedarpy request has a context value of the wrong type. What comes back?

  • A ValueError is raised
  • A result with Decision.NoDecision and an error message
  • A result with Decision.Allow

Request problems do not raise. They return NoDecision, with the reason in diagnostics.errors.

Which Cedar engine is a full rewrite, not a binding to the Rust engine?

  • cedar-java
  • cedar-wasm
  • cedar-go

cedar-go is pure Go. Java uses a native Rust library, and Wasm is Rust compiled to WebAssembly.

The CLI command cedar authorize exits with code 2. What does it mean?

  • The files could not be read
  • The decision was DENY
  • Validation failed

0 is ALLOW, 1 is failure, 2 is DENY, 3 is validation failure.

In cedarpy, the map from position ids to @id names is diagnostics.?.

In cedar-wasm, a bad request returns an answer whose type is ?.

  1. Your teammate writes if result.decision == Decision.Deny: raise HTTPException(403). Show the bug with one example.
    Answer

    Send a context with mfa: "yes". The decision is NoDecision, not Deny. The check does not raise, so the route runs. Fix: raise unless the decision is Allow.

  2. Why did the parsed-handle version run about nine times faster?
    Answer

    With text, cedarpy parses the policies, the schema and the entities on every call. With handles, it parses them once. Each call then only builds the request and evaluates.

  3. Your Go service uses cedar-go. How do you still catch policy typos before deploy?
    Answer

    cedar-go has only an experimental validator. Run cedar validate --policies ... --schema ... in CI with the official CLI, and fail the build on exit code 3. Also keep a test matrix in Go.

  4. A team wants Cedar checks in a Bash deploy script that runs 20 checks. Is the CLI a good choice? What about a web API with 2,000 requests per second?
    Answer

    For 20 checks, yes: 20 times 6 ms is about 0.1 seconds. For 2,000 requests per second, no: process start-up alone would need about 12 seconds of work per second. Use an in-process binding or a long-running service.

  5. Design the FastAPI check for a route /teams/{team_id}/docs/{doc_id}. What changes?
    Answer

    The dependency takes both path parameters. The resource is still the document. The team can be checked in the policy with resource in Team::"..." if documents have teams as parents. Load the document's entity with its parents, so the in check works.

Key takeaways

  • Official engines: Rust, Java, JavaScript/Wasm and Go. Python's cedarpy is a well-kept community binding.
  • In Python, allow only on Decision.Allow. NoDecision means a bad request.
  • Parse once with PolicySet, Entities and Schema handles. Use batches for many checks.
  • Put the Cedar check in a router-level FastAPI dependency, and test it with TestClient.
  • The CLI is good for scripts and CI. It costs milliseconds per call.
  • Pin exact versions and read install warnings. Bindings can lag behind, and a release can be broken.

Sources

  • cedarpy on PyPI. Versions, engine mapping, handles, batches and the GIL notes.
  • cedar-awesome. The official list of bindings, tools and integrations.
  • @cedar-policy/cedar-wasm on npm. The WebAssembly package and its versions.
  • cedar-go on GitHub. The Go implementation and its list of missing features.
  • cedar-java 4.11.0 Javadoc. The Java classes used in the example.
  • FastAPI: Testing. How TestClient works.

Architecture: where authorization lives

Cedar decides. But somebody must ask the question, collect the data and act on the answer. This chapter names those parts, shows where to run them, and covers speed, caching, logs and failures.

  • Name the PEP, PDP, PIP and PAP, and map each one to real Cedar code.
  • Compare a library, a sidecar and a central service, with numbers.
  • Load entities fast, and use partial evaluation to answer "which documents can I see?".
  • Cache decisions safely and write a decision log for audit.
  • Build a latency budget and make every failure deny.

Four boxes: PEP, PDP, PIP and PAP

Start with one click. Bob presses "Delete" on the plan. Here is what happens.

  1. The web app gets DELETE /docs/plan. It stops the request before the handler.
  2. It loads Bob, his groups and the plan from the database. It reads "MFA: yes" from Bob's token.
  3. It asks Cedar: may User::"bob" do Action::"delete" on Document::"plan"?
  4. Cedar answers Allow, because of owner-access.
  5. The app runs the handler and deletes the plan. It writes one log line.

Each step belongs to a part with a standard name. The names come from older standards like XACML and NIST's guide to attribute-based access control. The OpenID AuthZEN API uses them too.

PartFull nameJob in simple wordsIn a Cedar app
PEPPolicy Enforcement PointStops the request, asks, and obeys the answer.Your axum middleware or FastAPI dependency.
PDPPolicy Decision PointDecides Allow or Deny from policies and data.Authorizer::is_authorized, cedarpy, cedar-wasm.
PIPPolicy Information PointGives the facts: users, groups, resources, context.Your database, the identity provider, the token.
PAPPolicy Administration PointWrites, reviews, tests and ships policies.A Git repo, code review, CI with the validator.

A PEP never decides by itself. A PDP never touches the request. A PIP only supplies facts. A PAP is where people change the rules.

Cedar's PDP does not fetch data. The caller must pass in the entities and the context. So in a Cedar app, the PEP usually calls the PIP, then the PDP. (An experimental Rust API can load entities on demand, through an EntityLoader.)

Do not add extra if checks in the handler "just to be sure", like if user.is_admin { allow }. Now two places decide, and they will drift apart. Put every rule in Cedar. The PEP only enforces.

Where the PDP runs: library, sidecar or central service

You have three common shapes. All three can run the same Cedar policies.

ShapeHow it worksGoodWatch out
LibraryCedar runs inside your process, like in Cedar in Rust.Fastest: a few microseconds. No network. Nothing extra to run.Every app must load and update policies itself.
SidecarA small process next to each app. The app calls it on localhost.Any language. One place to update policies per machine.One more process to run, watch and upgrade.
Central serviceOne PDP service for many apps. Amazon Verified Permissions is a managed one.One copy of policies. One decision log.A network hop on every check. If it is down, everyone denies.

For a sidecar or a central PDP, you need an API between PEP and PDP. The OpenID Foundation approved the AuthZEN Authorization API 1.0 as a final standard on 2026-01-12. Its default evaluation path is /access/v1/evaluation. A request has subject, action, resource and optional context. The answer has a boolean decision. It also defines a batch path and search paths.

Building blocks you can check today:

  • cedar-local-agent (official, Rust, version 3.0.0). It reloads policy and entity files in the background. If a reload fails, it logs an error and keeps the old data. It can log decisions in the OCSF format. Its README calls it experimental.
  • Cedarling (community, Janssen project). It has a sidecar that speaks AuthZEN.
  • Amazon Verified Permissions. A managed central PDP. See Amazon Verified Permissions and Cedar in products.

Start with the library shape. Move to a sidecar or a central PDP only when you have a clear reason. Good reasons: many languages, one audit log, or a team that owns policies for many apps.

Loading entities fast (the PIP)

Cedar can only use the entities you pass in. If you pass too few, policies error or miss a group. If you pass too many, every request is slow. For one check, you usually need these.

  • The principal, and all its ancestors (groups, teams, the organisation).
  • The resource, and all its ancestors (folders, projects).
  • Entities that policies reach through attributes, like resource.owner.
  • The action entities. With a schema, Rust adds these for you.

How deep do policies reach? Level-based validation tells you. Level 1 means: policies read attributes of the principal, the resource and the context, but not attributes of entities found through them. The policy below needs level 2: it reads resource.owner.department.

let deep = r#"@id("owner-dept")
permit (principal, action == Action::"view", resource)
when { resource.owner.department == principal.department };"#;
for (name, src) in [("policy.cedar", fs::read_to_string("data/policy.cedar").unwrap()),
                    ("owner-dept", deep.to_string())] {
    let ps = cd4::load_policies(&src).unwrap();
    for level in [1, 2] {
        let r = validator.validate_with_level(&ps, ValidationMode::Strict, level);
        println!("{name:12} level {level}: passed = {}", r.validation_passed());
        for e in r.validation_errors() { println!("    {e}"); }
    }
}
policy.cedar level 1: passed = true
policy.cedar level 2: passed = true
owner-dept   level 1: passed = false
    for policy `owner-dept`, this policy requires level 2, which exceeds the maximum allowed level (1)
owner-dept   level 2: passed = true

So our real policies pass at level 1. The loader needs the request entities and their ancestors, but not the attributes of the owner. Make the level a rule in CI. Then nobody adds a deep policy that your loader cannot feed. The Lean proofs in cedar-spec cover this idea: slicing entities at the checked level does not change the answer.

More ways to keep the PIP fast:

  • Store ancestors with the entity. Keep group membership in a table you can read in one query. Cedar needs the parents list, not a graph walk at request time.
  • Cache stable entities. Users and groups change slowly. Documents change often. Cache the first kind with a short expiry.
  • Build the store once, add per request. In Python, Entities.with_added_json_str adds the request's entities to a parsed base. In Rust, Entities::upsert_entities does a similar job.

Do not load the whole database for each request "to be safe". It works with ten users and fails with ten thousand. Load the slice the policies need, and test the slice with level validation.

Partial evaluation and residual policies

A list page asks a different question: "which documents may Alice view?" Asking Cedar once per document is slow when there are millions. Partial evaluation helps. You leave the resource unknown. Cedar simplifies each policy as far as it can. What is left is a residual policy.

[dependencies]
cedar-policy = { version = "=4.14.1", features = ["tpe"] }  # experimental
serde_json = "1"
// Question: alice wants to view... which document? The resource is unknown.
let alice = EntityUid::from_str(r#"User::"alice""#)?;
let view = EntityUid::from_str(r#"Action::"view""#)?;
let doc_type = EntityTypeName::from_str("Document")?;
let ctx = Context::from_json_value(serde_json::json!({"mfa": false}), Some((&schema, &view)))?;
let request = PartialRequest::new(
    PartialEntityUid::from_concrete(alice.clone()),
    view.clone(),
    PartialEntityUid::new(doc_type.clone(), None), // unknown id
    Some(ctx.clone()),
    &schema,
)?;
// We load only alice and her team. No documents at all.
let users = Entities::from_json_str(&read("users.json")?, Some(&schema))?;
let partial = PartialEntities::from_concrete(users, &schema)?;
let answer = policies.tpe(&request, &partial, &schema)?;
println!("decision: {:?}", answer.decision());
for p in answer.residual_policies() {
    println!("residual {}: {}", p.id(), p);
}
decision: None
residual team-view: @id("team-view")
permit(principal, action, resource) when { resource in Folder::"shared" };
residual owner-access: @id("owner-access")
permit(principal, action, resource) when { resource.owner == User::"alice" };

Read the residuals. Cedar already knew Alice is in the eng team, so that part is gone. What is left reads like a filter: "the document is in the shared folder, or its owner is Alice". You can turn that into a database query yourself, for example:

SELECT id FROM documents
WHERE folder_id = 'shared'      -- from team-view
   OR owner_id = 'alice';       -- from owner-access

Cedar does not write the SQL for you. Your code maps each residual shape to a query. Keep that mapping small and well tested.

Sometimes there is no residual at all. If a forbid is already true, the answer is known without the resource. The same program also has a ready-made permission query, query_resource.

// Same question for delete without MFA: the forbid is already true.
let del = EntityUid::from_str(r#"Action::"delete""#)?;
let ctx_del = Context::from_json_value(serde_json::json!({"mfa": false}), Some((&schema, &del)))?;
let req_del = PartialRequest::new(PartialEntityUid::from_concrete(alice.clone()), del,
    PartialEntityUid::new(doc_type.clone(), None), Some(ctx_del), &schema)?;
let answer = policies.tpe(&req_del, &partial, &schema)?;
println!("delete without mfa: {:?}", answer.decision());

// Permission query: list the documents alice may view.
let all = Entities::from_json_str(&read("all.json")?, Some(&schema))?;
let q = ResourceQueryRequest::new(alice, view, doc_type, ctx, &schema)?;
let docs: Vec<String> = policies.query_resource(&q, &all, &schema)?.map(|u| u.to_string()).collect();
println!("alice may view: {docs:?}");
delete without mfa: Some(Deny)
alice may view: ["Document::\"notes\"", "Document::\"plan\""]

The store all.json has three documents. salary is Bob's and is private, so it is not in the list.

Type-aware partial evaluation (TPE, RFC 95) is behind the tpe feature. It arrived in cedar-policy 4.6.0 and is still experimental. Its API changed in 4.8, 4.12 and 4.13. In 4.14.1 the method nontrivial_residual_policies is deprecated. The older partial-eval feature also exists, but the crate docs suggest tpe instead. Pin the exact version if you use either.

A query_resource call still looks at every document you pass in. It is great for small sets. For millions of rows, use the residuals as a database filter instead.

Caching decisions safely

A decision cache saves work when the same question comes again. But a wrong cache key gives wrong answers. Here is a real bug and its fix.

POLICY_VERSION, ENTITY_VERSION = "p-42", "e-7"   # bump these on every change
bad_cache, good_cache = {}, {}

def decide_bad(user, action, doc, context):        # key forgets the context
    key = (user, action, doc)
    if key not in bad_cache:
        bad_cache[key] = decide(user, action, doc, context)
    return bad_cache[key]

def decide_good(user, action, doc, context):       # key has every input
    key = (POLICY_VERSION, ENTITY_VERSION, user, action, doc,
           json.dumps(context, sort_keys=True))
    if key not in good_cache:
        good_cache[key] = decide(user, action, doc, context)
    return good_cache[key]

for ctx in [{"mfa": True}, {"mfa": False}]:
    print(f"bob delete plan {ctx}: bad cache -> {decide_bad('bob', 'delete', 'plan', ctx)}, "
          f"good cache -> {decide_good('bob', 'delete', 'plan', ctx)}")
bob delete plan {'mfa': True}: bad cache -> True, good cache -> True
bob delete plan {'mfa': False}: bad cache -> True, good cache -> False

The bad cache remembered "allowed" from the MFA request. Then it allowed a delete without MFA. The forbid policy never ran.

Rules for a safe decision cache:

  1. The key holds every input: principal, action, resource, the full context, the policy version and the entity version.
  2. Change a version number whenever policies or entities change. Old keys then stop matching.
  3. Use a short expiry. Seconds, not hours. People leave groups.
  4. Never cache an error as Allow. Do not cache errors at all.
  5. Do not cache requests with a time in the context. Every second is a new key.

Cedar is already fast: a few microseconds in Rust. Often the slow part is loading entities, not deciding. Cache the entities first. Cache decisions only if you measure a real need.

Decision logging and audit

An audit needs to answer: who asked, for what, what was the answer, and why? So the PEP writes one log line per decision. This PEP also fails closed when the PDP is down.

def enforce(pdp, user, action, doc, mfa):
    req = {"principal": {"type": "User", "id": user}, "action": {"type": "Action", "id": action},
           "resource": {"type": "Document", "id": doc}, "context": {"mfa": mfa}}
    log = {"id": str(uuid.uuid4())[:8], "ts": round(time.time()), "policy_version": POLICY_VERSION,
           "principal": user, "action": action, "resource": doc, "mfa": mfa}
    try:
        r = pdp(req)
        allowed = r.decision == Decision.Allow
        log |= {"decision": r.decision.value,
                "reasons": [r.diagnostics.id_annotations_by_reason.get(p, p)
                            for p in r.diagnostics.reasons],
                "errors": r.diagnostics.errors}
    except Exception as e:                      # fail closed: any error is a deny
        allowed = False
        log |= {"decision": "Deny", "reasons": [], "errors": [f"pdp failure: {e}"]}
    print(json.dumps(log))                      # one JSON line per decision
    return allowed

enforce(local_pdp, "bob", "delete", "plan", False)
enforce(local_pdp, "bob", "delete", "plan", True)
enforce(broken_pdp, "bob", "delete", "plan", True)   # raises "connection refused"
{"id": "f5e07da0", "ts": 1794745254, "policy_version": "p-42", "principal": "bob", "action": "delete", "resource": "plan", "mfa": false, "decision": "Deny", "reasons": ["delete-needs-mfa"], "errors": []}
{"id": "19fb598a", "ts": 1794745254, "policy_version": "p-42", "principal": "bob", "action": "delete", "resource": "plan", "mfa": true, "decision": "Allow", "reasons": ["owner-access"], "errors": []}
{"id": "a3582ef6", "ts": 1794745254, "policy_version": "p-42", "principal": "bob", "action": "delete", "resource": "plan", "mfa": true, "decision": "Deny", "reasons": [], "errors": ["pdp failure: connection refused"]}

What a good decision log holds:

  • A request id, so you can join it with other logs.
  • Principal, action, resource, and the context fields that matter.
  • The decision, the deciding policy ids, and every error.
  • The policy set version. Without it, "why was this allowed?" has no answer next month.

Do not log whole tokens, passwords or secret context values. Log the facts you checked, like mfa: true, not the raw token. Also protect the log itself: it shows who can do what.

The chapter Running Cedar in production uses these logs for shadow mode and incident work.

A latency budget

A latency budget splits your allowed time across the steps. First, the numbers we measured on an Apple M2 Pro with our small policy set.

  1. Set the page budget: 50 ms for the whole request.
  2. Load entities from the database. Measure it. Say it is 4 ms in your system.
  3. Network to a central PDP and back. Measure it. Say it is 2 ms in your region.
  4. Cedar decides: about 0.04 ms with cedarpy, from our test.
  5. Write the log line: say 0.1 ms with a buffered writer.
  6. Total for authorization: about 6 ms, which is 12% of the page. The handler gets the other 44 ms.

Steps 2, 3 and 5 are assumptions. Only you can measure them. But the example shows the usual result: the decision itself is the cheap part. Entity loading and the network cost more.

Set a timeout for every remote call to the PDP or PIP. If the timeout fires, deny. Cedar's own evaluation always finishes, because the language has no loops. See Expressions, types and operators.

When asked "where does the time go?", list four parts: loading entities, transport, the decision, and logging. Say which ones you measured.

Failure modes: fail closed

Things break. The rule is simple: when in doubt, deny. To fail closed keeps data safe. To fail open keeps the app usable but unsafe.

The PDP is down, so we allow everything until it is back.

try:
    allowed = pdp(req).allowed
except Exception:
    allowed = True          # an outage now opens every door

The PDP is down, so we deny and alert.

try:
    allowed = pdp(req).decision == Decision.Allow
except Exception as e:
    allowed = False         # safe: users see 403 or 503
    alert("pdp failure", e)
What breaksWhat to do
The PDP is down or too slowTime out and deny. Return 503 for outages, so clients know to retry.
A new policy file does not parse or validateKeep the last good policy set. Alert. Never run with no policies.
The entity store is downDeny. Missing groups would make answers wrong.
A policy errors at run timeCedar skips it. A skipped forbid cannot block, so alert on every policy error.
The cache is staleShort expiry, and version numbers in every key.
The context is missing a factTreat it as the unsafe value. For example, missing MFA means "no MFA".

"We will fail open only for reads" sounds safe. It is not. Reads leak data. If the business truly needs a fallback, write it as a Cedar policy, review it, and log it as an emergency.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for Cedar architecture
PatternPurposeModelNote
Thin PEPOne place decidesMiddleware asks Cedar, then obeysNo extra if-checks in handlers.
PEP loads, PDP decidesFeed Cedar the factsLoad entities, then is_authorizedCedar does not fetch data itself.
Library firstLowest latencyCedar inside the app processMove to a sidecar or service only with a reason.
Standard PEP-PDP APISwap PDPs laterPOST /access/v1/evaluation (AuthZEN)Final standard since 2026-01-12.
Level-checked loaderLoad just enoughvalidate_with_level(&ps, Strict, 1)Make the level a CI rule.
Residual to filterList pagespolicies.tpe(&request, &partial, &schema)Experimental: pin the version.
Permission querySmall listspolicies.query_resource(&q, &all, &schema)Still checks every entity you pass.
Full cache keyNo wrong cached answers(policy_v, entity_v, p, a, r, context)Forgetting context skipped our MFA forbid.
One log line per decisionAudit{"decision": ..., "reasons": [...], "policy_version": ...}No tokens or secrets in logs.
Timeout then denyFail closedexcept Exception: allowed = FalseNever allowed = True in an error branch.
Keep last good policiesSafe reloadSwap only after parse and validate passAn empty policy set denies everything.

Practice

What does a PEP do?

It stops the request, asks the PDP, and obeys the answer.

Does Cedar fetch entities from your database?

No. The caller loads them and passes them in.

What is a residual policy?

The part of a policy that still depends on an unknown input.

What must a decision cache key hold?

Every input: principal, action, resource, context, policy version, entity version.

The PDP times out. What is the answer?

Deny. Fail closed, and alert.

Which part writes and reviews policies?

  • PEP
  • PIP
  • PAP

The Policy Administration Point is where people manage policies: the repo, review and CI.

In a Cedar app, who usually loads the entities for a request?

  • The Cedar PDP fetches them by itself
  • The PEP (or code next to it) loads them from the PIP and passes them in
  • The PAP sends them with the policies

Cedar's normal API takes entities as input. It does not call your database.

A cache key is (user, action, doc). What can go wrong?

  • Nothing, Cedar is pure
  • A decision made with one context is reused for another context
  • The cache uses too much memory

Our test reused an MFA Allow for a request without MFA. The key must hold the context and versions.

The standard default path for one AuthZEN evaluation is ?.

A policy that reads resource.owner.department needs level ?.

  1. Name the four parts for this system: a FastAPI app, cedarpy, a Postgres table of users and groups, and a Git repo with policies.
    Answer

    PEP: the FastAPI dependency. PDP: cedarpy's is_authorized. PIP: the Postgres tables (and the login token). PAP: the Git repo with review and CI.

  2. The TPE residuals were resource in Folder::"shared" and resource.owner == User::"alice". Why does the team check not appear?
    Answer

    We passed Alice and her team as known entities. So principal in Team::"eng" was already true, and Cedar removed it. Only the parts about the unknown resource are left.

  3. Your company has services in Rust, Python and Go, and auditors want one decision log. Which shape do you pick, and what is the risk?
    Answer

    A central PDP, or a sidecar on each machine that ships logs to one place. Both give one log format. The central PDP adds a network hop and becomes a shared point of failure, so use timeouts, fail closed and run several copies.

  4. Write a latency budget for authorization in a 20 ms API, with a library PDP in Rust.
    Answer

    Build request: about 5.5 microseconds. Decide: about 3.3 microseconds (our numbers). Entity load from a cache: measure, say 0.5 ms. Log write: say 0.1 ms. Total about 0.6 ms, about 3% of 20 ms. The assumed parts must be measured.

  5. The new policy file fails validation during a hot reload at 3 a.m. What should the service do?
    Answer

    Keep the last good policy set and keep serving. Raise an alert with the validation errors. Do not swap in the broken set and do not run with an empty set.

Key takeaways

  • PEP asks and enforces, PDP decides, PIP supplies facts, PAP manages policies.
  • Cedar does not fetch data: load the right slice of entities, and check the slice with level validation.
  • A library PDP costs microseconds. A sidecar or central PDP adds a network hop but gives one place to manage.
  • Partial evaluation (tpe) turns "which documents?" into residuals you can map to a query. It is experimental.
  • Cache keys need every input and version numbers. Log every decision with reasons and the policy version.
  • Every failure denies. Keep the last good policies when a reload fails.

Sources

  • AuthZEN Authorization API 1.0. The PEP and PDP terms, the evaluation, batch and search paths.
  • Authorization API 1.0 Final Specification Approved. The approval notice of 2026-01-12.
  • NIST SP 800-162. The ABAC guide that describes the PEP, PDP, PIP and PAP parts.
  • Cedar RFC 95: type-aware partial evaluation. Why TPE exists and how residuals work.
  • Cedar docs: policy level validation. Levels and entity slicing.
  • cedar-local-agent. Official building block for a local PDP with file reload and decision logs.

Amazon Verified Permissions and Cedar in products

Amazon Verified Permissions runs Cedar for you as an AWS service. You store policies there and ask it for decisions over an API. This chapter shows how it works, what it costs today, and where else Cedar runs.

  • Describe policy stores, schemas, templates and identity sources.
  • Map a Cognito or OIDC token to a Cedar principal and its groups.
  • Choose between IsAuthorized, IsAuthorizedWithToken and the batch calls.
  • Estimate the monthly price from the AWS pricing page.
  • Decide when a managed service is better than the library in your app.

A managed Cedar service

Your app wants to know: may Ben edit the handbook? With the library, your app runs Cedar itself. With Amazon Verified Permissions, your app sends the question to AWS. AWS answers ALLOW or DENY, plus the ids of the deciding policies.

The short name is AVP. AWS released its user guide on 13 June 2023. It uses the same Cedar language as this book. All policies in this chapter were checked with the local cedar CLI 4.14.1 and cedarpy 4.12.2.

"AVP protects my API." It does not. AVP returns a decision. Your code, or an API Gateway authorizer, must refuse the request. AWS calls this step policy enforcement, and it lives outside the service.

Policy stores, schemas and validation

A policy store holds everything for one app. You can also make one store per tenant. That gives strong isolation in a multi-tenant system. Each request names one store.

A store has these settings today:

  • Validation mode. STRICT rejects any policy that fails the schema. OFF skips the check.
  • Schema. One schema in Cedar JSON format. Up to 100,000 bytes.
  • Cedar version. The field cedarVersion shows CEDAR_2 or CEDAR_4.
  • Deletion protection. When it is ENABLED, the store cannot be deleted.
  • Aliases and names. A policy store alias can replace the long id. Policies and templates can have names too.
  1. Jun 2023 Launch. First release of the AVP user guide.
  2. Jun 2024 OIDC. Identity sources from any OIDC provider, not only Cognito.
  3. Feb 2025 Aliases and Cedar JSON. Policy store aliases, and entities in Cedar's own JSON format.
  4. Aug 2025 Cedar 4.5. AWS announced support for Cedar 4.5, including the is operator.
  5. Apr 2026 Cedar 2 ends. Authorization calls start to fail for stores still on Cedar 2.
  6. May 2026 Namespaces. AVP now supports multiple namespaces in one schema.

Here is the schema for a small document app, DocShare. We wrote it in the human format and validated it locally.

namespace DocShare {
  entity Group;
  entity User in [Group] {
    email?: String,
    "custom:department"?: String,   // an ID token claim with a colon
  };
  entity Folder;
  entity Document in [Folder] {
    owner: User,
    classification: String,         // "general" or "finance"
  };
  type RequestContext = {
    token?: {                       // access token claims land here
      client_id: String,
      scope: Set<String>,
    },
  };
  action view, edit, share appliesTo {
    principal: [User],
    resource: [Document],
    context: RequestContext,
  };
}

The AVP API stores a schema as a Cedar JSON string. The cedar CLI translates it for you:

cedar translate-schema --direction cedar-to-json-with-resolved-types \
  --schema schema.cedarschema > schema.json
{
 "DocShare": {
  "entityTypes": {
   "User": {
    "memberOfTypes": ["DocShare::Group"],
    "shape": {
     "type": "Record",
     "attributes": {
      "custom:department": {"type": "String", "required": false},
      "email": {"type": "String", "required": false}
     }
    }
   }
  }
 }
}

The full file is 1,063 bytes. The limit is 100,000 bytes, so we have plenty of room. The validator accepted our policies against the JSON version too.

Some AVP quotas (default values on 2026-11-15)
QuotaDefaultCan you raise it?
Policy stores per Region per account30,000Yes
Policy templates per policy store40Yes
Identity sources per policy store1No
Size of one policy10,000 bytesYes
Size of one authorization request1 MBNo
Transitive parents per principal100No
IsAuthorized requests per second per store200Yes
BatchIsAuthorized requests per second per store30Yes

Turning on STRICT before the store has a schema. With no schema, every policy fails validation and AVP rejects it. Add the schema first, then switch to STRICT.

Policies and templates in a store

These are the DocShare policies. Look at the group id. It has the form user-pool-id|group-name. The next section explains why.

// Members of the Cognito group "editors" may view and edit.
@id("editors-view-edit")
permit (
  principal in DocShare::Group::"us-east-1_EXAMPLE|editors",
  action in [DocShare::Action::"view", DocShare::Action::"edit"],
  resource
);

// Owners may do anything with their own documents.
@id("owner-all")
permit (principal, action, resource is DocShare::Document)
when { resource.owner == principal };

// Finance documents are only for the finance department.
@id("finance-only")
forbid (principal, action, resource is DocShare::Document)
when { resource.classification == "finance" }
unless {
  principal has "custom:department" &&
  principal["custom:department"] == "finance"
};

// Callers whose access token has the docs/read scope may view public documents.
@id("scope-read-public")
permit (principal, action == DocShare::Action::"view", resource in DocShare::Folder::"public")
when { context has token && context.token.scope.contains("docs/read") };

// Template behind the "Share" button: one person, one document, view only.
@id("share-view")
permit (principal == ?principal, action == DocShare::Action::"view", resource == ?resource);

The name custom:department has a colon. A dot cannot read it. So we use bracket notation: principal["custom:department"]. The has test also takes a quoted name.

We ran six requests with the local CLI. Ana is in the editors group and in finance. Ben is in sales and owns q3-report.

DocShare decisions (cedar 4.14.1)
RequestDecisionDeciding policies
Ana edit q3-reportAlloweditors-view-edit
Ben view q3-report (he owns it)Denyfinance-only
Ben view handbookDeny(none)
Ben view handbook, after the share linkAllowshare-ben-handbook
Ben edit handbook, after the share linkDeny(none)
Ben view handbook, token with docs/read, no shareAllowscope-read-public

The share link came from the template:

Template-linked policy added: @id("share-view")
permit(principal == DocShare::User::"us-east-1_EXAMPLE|2222-bbbb", action == DocShare::Action::"view", resource == DocShare::Document::"handbook");

One template rule is stricter in AVP

Cedar allows a template where the principal is open. AVP does not. In AVP, a template must constrain both the principal and the resource. Our local validator accepts this template:

@id("bad-template")
permit (principal, action == DocShare::Action::"view", resource == ?resource);
  ☞ policy set validation passed
  ╰─▶ no errors or warnings

AWS documents that AVP rejects this kind of template. We did not send it to AWS. So test new templates against the AVP rules, not only against the local validator.

Editing a template "just for one user". A template-linked policy follows its template. If you change the template, every linked policy changes at once. To change one user's access, delete that link and make a new one.

Identity sources: Cognito and OIDC

An identity source connects a policy store to your login system. Each store can have one. It can be an Amazon Cognito user pool or any OIDC provider.

Then you can send a JWT instead of building the principal yourself. AVP turns the token into a Cedar principal. The rules, from the AWS documentation:

  • Principal id. For Cognito it is user-pool-id|sub, for example us-east-1_EXAMPLE|2222-bbbb.
  • Principal type. You choose it when you create the identity source, for example DocShare::User.
  • Groups. The cognito:groups claim becomes parent entities of your group type, like us-east-1_EXAMPLE|editors.
  • ID token. Other claims become attributes of the principal.
  • Access token. Claims go into the context, under context.token.
  • OIDC. You set an entityIdPrefix such as MyOIDCProvider. Ids then look like MyOIDCProvider|Carlos.

To see this mapping work, we wrote our own small version of it in Python. AVP does the real mapping inside the service. Our sketch only follows the documented rules, then asks the local engine:

"""Our sketch of the documented ID-token mapping, run with the local engine."""
import json, cedarpy

POOL = "us-east-1_EXAMPLE"
claims = {"sub": "2222-bbbb", "cognito:groups": ["editors"], "token_use": "id",
          "email": "ben@example.com", "custom:department": "sales"}

def principal_from_id_token(c):
    # User id is "<pool id>|<sub>". Groups become parents. Other claims become attributes.
    uid = {"type": "DocShare::User", "id": f"{POOL}|{c['sub']}"}
    groups = [{"type": "DocShare::Group", "id": f"{POOL}|{g}"} for g in c.get("cognito:groups", [])]
    attrs = {k: c[k] for k in ("email", "custom:department") if k in c}
    return uid, [{"uid": uid, "attrs": attrs, "parents": groups}] + \
                [{"uid": g, "attrs": {}, "parents": []} for g in groups]

def load_policies(path):                     # use @id as the policy id
    j = json.loads(cedarpy.policies_to_json_str(open(path).read()))
    for part in ("templates", "staticPolicies"):
        j[part] = {p["annotations"]["id"]: p for p in j[part].values()}
    return cedarpy.PolicySet.from_json_str(json.dumps(j))

uid, principal_entities = principal_from_id_token(claims)
docs = [e for e in json.load(open("entities.json")) if e["uid"]["type"].startswith(("DocShare::Doc", "DocShare::Folder"))]
reqs = [{"principal": uid, "action": {"type": "DocShare::Action", "id": a},
         "resource": {"type": "DocShare::Document", "id": d}, "context": {}}
        for d in ("handbook", "q3-report") for a in ("view", "edit", "share")]
results = cedarpy.is_authorized_batch(reqs, load_policies("policies.cedar"),
                                      principal_entities + docs, open("schema.cedarschema").read())
for q, r in zip(reqs, results):
    print(f"{q['action']['id']:<5} {q['resource']['id']:<9} -> {r.decision.value:<5} {r.diagnostics.reasons}")
view  handbook  -> Allow ['editors-view-edit']
edit  handbook  -> Allow ['editors-view-edit']
share handbook  -> Deny  []
view  q3-report -> Deny  ['finance-only']
edit  q3-report -> Deny  ['finance-only']
share q3-report -> Deny  ['finance-only']

This time the token says Ben is in the editors group. So he can view and edit the handbook. He still cannot open the finance report. The forbid wins over his group and over his ownership.

Long-lived tokens. AVP checks the token's signature and expiry. It does not ask the provider whether the token was revoked. AWS warns that a revoked token can still work until it expires. Keep token lifetimes short.

The four authorization calls

AVP has four calls that return decisions. Pick by two questions. Do you have a token? Do you need many decisions at once?

Choosing a call
CallWho builds the principal?Decisions per call
IsAuthorizedYou, in the request and entities1
IsAuthorizedWithTokenAVP, from an ID or access token1
BatchIsAuthorizedYou. All requests share one principal or one resourceUp to 30
BatchIsAuthorizedWithTokenAVP, from one tokenUp to 30

Here is an IsAuthorized request body for "Ben views q3-report". The entities use Cedar's own JSON format, so we could test the same entities locally first. Locally the answer was DENY, by finance-only.

{
  "policyStoreId": "policy-store-alias/docshare",
  "principal": {"entityType": "DocShare::User", "entityId": "us-east-1_EXAMPLE|2222-bbbb"},
  "action": {"actionType": "DocShare::Action", "actionId": "view"},
  "resource": {"entityType": "DocShare::Document", "entityId": "q3-report"},
  "context": {"cedarJson": "{}"},
  "entities": {
    "cedarJson": "[{\"uid\":{\"type\":\"DocShare::User\",\"id\":\"us-east-1_EXAMPLE|2222-bbbb\"},\"attrs\":{\"email\":\"ben@example.com\",\"custom:department\":\"sales\"},\"parents\":[]},{\"uid\":{\"type\":\"DocShare::Folder\",\"id\":\"finance\"},\"attrs\":{},\"parents\":[]},{\"uid\":{\"type\":\"DocShare::Document\",\"id\":\"q3-report\"},\"attrs\":{\"owner\":{\"__entity\":{\"type\":\"DocShare::User\",\"id\":\"us-east-1_EXAMPLE|2222-bbbb\"}},\"classification\":\"finance\"},\"parents\":[{\"type\":\"DocShare::Folder\",\"id\":\"finance\"}]}]"
  }
}

The response has three fields: decision (ALLOW or DENY), determiningPolicies and errors. The policy ids are ids that AVP created, like SPEXAMPLEabcdefg111111. They are not your @id values. Keep a table from AVP ids to your names, or give each policy a policy name.

The user signs in

Cognito or your OIDC provider gives the app an ID token or an access token.

The app asks

It calls IsAuthorizedWithToken with the token, the action, the resource and the resource entities.

AVP checks the token

It checks the signature, the expiry and the allowed client ids or audiences.

AVP builds the principal

Id from the pool id and sub. Parents from the groups claim. Attributes or context from the other claims.

Cedar decides

The policies in the store are evaluated. The answer comes back with the deciding policy ids.

The app enforces

Only the app can hide the button or refuse the API call.

Batch calls for screens

A page shows a list of documents with buttons. You need many decisions for one user. That is the job of the batch calls. All requests in one BatchIsAuthorized call must share the principal or the resource. Bob on two photos is fine. Bob on one photo and Alice on another is not.

The AWS CLI commands look like this. We did not run them, because this book never uses real AWS credentials.

aws verifiedpermissions create-policy-store --validation-settings mode=STRICT
aws verifiedpermissions put-schema --policy-store-id PSEXAMPLEabcdefg111111 \
  --definition file://schema-definition.json   # {"cedarJson": "...contents of schema.json..."}
aws verifiedpermissions is-authorized --policy-store-id PSEXAMPLEabcdefg111111 \
  --principal 'entityType=DocShare::User,entityId=us-east-1_EXAMPLE|2222-bbbb' \
  --action actionType=DocShare::Action,actionId=view \
  --resource entityType=DocShare::Document,entityId=q3-report

The batch call has no IAM permission of its own. To allow BatchIsAuthorized, grant verifiedpermissions:IsAuthorized.

What it costs (checked on 2026-11-15)

These prices come from the AWS pricing page on 2026-11-15. The page says the prices are the same in all Regions. Prices can change, so check the page before you plan a budget.

Verified Permissions prices on 2026-11-15
What you callHow it is countedPrice
IsAuthorized, IsAuthorizedWithTokenPer call0.000005 USD (that is 5 USD per million)
BatchIsAuthorized, BatchIsAuthorizedWithTokenPer call, any number of decisions inside0.00015 USD for the first 40 million calls a month, 0.000075 USD for the next 60 million, 0.00004 USD above 100 million
