Policy management (CreatePolicy, GetPolicy and others)Per call0.00004 USD (that is 40 USD per million)
  1. Count the calls: 5,000,000 IsAuthorized calls.
  2. Multiply: 5{,}000{,}000 \times 0.000005 = 25.
  3. Result: 25 USD a month. This matches the first example on the AWS page.

Now a calculation that is ours, not AWS's. Is a batch call cheaper? A batch call in the first tier costs as much as 30 single calls: 0.00015 / 0.000005 = 30. So a batch saves money only when it is full. In the second tier, the break-even point is 15 decisions. Above 100 million calls, it is 8.

Batch calls still help with speed and quotas. One batch call is one network round trip. And 30 batch calls per second, with 30 decisions each, is 900 decisions per second.

The pricing page also shows a third case: local evaluation. Your app downloads the policies and runs Cedar itself. Then you pay only for the policy management calls. AWS Labs publishes a Rust crate for this, avp-local-agent (version 3.0.0, April 2025). It builds on cedar-local-agent from the Cedar project.

Infrastructure as code

You should create stores and policies from code, not by hand in the console. CloudFormation has four resource types for AVP: PolicyStore, Policy, PolicyTemplate and IdentitySource. There is also a PolicyStoreAlias type.

AWSTemplateFormatVersion: 2010-09-09
Description: DocShare policy store (not deployed in this book)
Parameters:
  SchemaJson:
    Type: String            # paste the contents of schema.json
  UserPoolArn:
    Type: String
  ClientId:
    Type: String
Resources:
  Store:
    Type: AWS::VerifiedPermissions::PolicyStore
    Properties:
      Description: DocShare authorization
      ValidationSettings:
        Mode: STRICT
      DeletionProtection:
        Mode: ENABLED
      Schema:
        CedarJson: !Ref SchemaJson
  FinanceOnly:
    Type: AWS::VerifiedPermissions::Policy
    Properties:
      PolicyStoreId: !Ref Store
      Definition:
        Static:
          Description: Finance documents are only for the finance department
          Statement: |
            forbid (principal, action, resource is DocShare::Document)
            when { resource.classification == "finance" }
            unless {
              principal has "custom:department" &&
              principal["custom:department"] == "finance"
            };
  ShareView:
    Type: AWS::VerifiedPermissions::PolicyTemplate
    Properties:
      PolicyStoreId: !Ref Store
      Description: Share one document for viewing
      Statement: |
        permit (principal == ?principal, action == DocShare::Action::"view", resource == ?resource);
  Users:
    Type: AWS::VerifiedPermissions::IdentitySource
    Properties:
      PolicyStoreId: !Ref Store
      PrincipalEntityType: DocShare::User
      Configuration:
        CognitoUserPoolConfiguration:
          UserPoolArn: !Ref UserPoolArn
          ClientIds: [!Ref ClientId]
          GroupConfiguration:
            GroupEntityType: DocShare::Group

We did not deploy this stack. We did two local checks. A YAML parser read the file and found the four resources. Then we pulled out the two Cedar statements and validated them against the schema: both passed.

The AWS CDK has only automatically generated L1 constructs for AVP, such as CfnPolicyStore. There are no hand-written L2 constructs yet. This short Python app was synthesized locally with aws-cdk-lib 2.277.0. Synthesis builds the template on your machine and needs no AWS account. It was not deployed.

import json
from aws_cdk import App, Stack, aws_verifiedpermissions as vp
from constructs import Construct

class DocShareStack(Stack):
    def __init__(self, scope: Construct, cid: str, schema_json: str) -> None:
        super().__init__(scope, cid)
        store = vp.CfnPolicyStore(self, "Store",
            validation_settings=vp.CfnPolicyStore.ValidationSettingsProperty(mode="STRICT"),
            deletion_protection=vp.CfnPolicyStore.DeletionProtectionProperty(mode="ENABLED"),
            schema=vp.CfnPolicyStore.SchemaDefinitionProperty(cedar_json=schema_json))
        vp.CfnPolicyTemplate(self, "ShareView",
            policy_store_id=store.attr_policy_store_id,
            description="Share one document for viewing",
            statement='permit (principal == ?principal, action == DocShare::Action::"view", resource == ?resource);')

app = App(outdir="cdk.out")
DocShareStack(app, "DocShare", schema_json=open("schema.json").read().strip())
template = app.synth().get_stack_by_name("DocShare").template
print(sorted(f"{k}: {v['Type']}" for k, v in template["Resources"].items()))
['ShareView: AWS::VerifiedPermissions::PolicyTemplate', 'Store: AWS::VerifiedPermissions::PolicyStore']

Other tools, such as Terraform, can manage these resources too. Read their own documentation. This chapter uses only AWS's pages for AVP facts.

Audit with CloudTrail

CloudTrail records management calls, such as CreatePolicy and PutSchema, by default. The authorization calls are data events. They are not logged by default. You must add them to a trail or event data store. In those records, the decision appears under additionalEventData, not responseElements.

Cedar in other products and projects

AVP is not the only place Cedar runs. We checked each item below on 2026-11-15.

Where Cedar runs today
NameOwnerWhat it doesStatus on 2026-11-15
Amazon Verified PermissionsAWSManaged Cedar decisionsGenerally available; Cedar 4 stores
AWS Verified AccessAWSRules for access to your applications on AWS. AWS documents that these policies are written in CedarDocumented on the AWS site
Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCoreAWSCedar checks on every agent tool call at a gatewayGenerally available since 3 March 2026. AWS also lists Dogwood, a language that accepts all Cedar policies
Cedar Access Control for Kubernetescedar-policy on GitHubCedar for Kubernetes authorization and admissionThe README says it is no longer maintained. Work moves to a new Rust version; its location is not yet given
cedar-agentPermit.ioAn HTTP server that stores Cedar policies and data and answers checksPublic and Apache-2.0. We could not confirm a recent release
CedarlingJanssen ProjectAn embeddable decision point in Rust that reads JWTs. It has WASM and other bindingsDocumented on the Janssen site
avp-local-agentAWS LabsRuns AVP policies locally, inside your appVersion 3.0.0, released April 2025

Cedar itself is now neutral ground. The CNCF accepted Cedar as a Sandbox project on 8 October 2025. The first public commit is from 9 May 2023. See What is authorization? for the history.

When to use a managed service

The library and the service run the same language. The difference is who runs it, where, and at what cost. Our Rust benchmark made one decision in about 7.6 microseconds on this machine, with 10 policies. A call to AVP also includes a trip over the network.

Library, local agent or managed service
QuestionLibrary in your appavp-local-agentAVP API calls
Where are policies stored?Your repositoryAVPAVP
Where are decisions made?In your processIn your processAt AWS
LatencyMicrosecondsMicrosecondsA network round trip
Price per decisionYour compute onlyOnly policy management calls5 USD per million single calls
Token to principal mappingYou write itYou write itBuilt in
Audit of decisionsYou build itYou build itCloudTrail data events
Lock-inNoneSomeMore: ids, limits, API shape
Choose AVP when ...

Many services share one set of policies, you already use Cognito or an OIDC provider, you want CloudTrail audit for decisions, and your request volume fits the price.

Choose the library when ...

You need microsecond decisions, very high volume, offline use, or full control. You then build storage, deployment and audit yourself.

Choose a local agent when ...

You want AVP as the place where people edit policies, but local speed for decisions. You accept a short delay before policy changes reach your app.

Whatever you choose, keep your policies, schema and tests in a repository. Then you can move between these options. See Architecture: where authorization lives and Running Cedar in production.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for Verified Permissions
PatternPurposeModelNote
principal in Ns::Group::"pool-id|group"Grant to a Cognito groupDocShare::Group::"us-east-1_EXAMPLE|editors"The id includes the user pool id.
principal["claim:name"]Read a claim with a colonprincipal["custom:department"]A dot cannot read the colon.
context has token && context.token.scope.contains(...)Use access token scopesscope-read-publicAccess token claims go to context, not to the principal.
permit (principal == ?principal, action == ..., resource == ?resource)A share buttonshare-viewAVP needs both principal and resource constrained.
cedar translate-schema --direction cedar-to-json-with-resolved-typesMake the schema JSON for PutSchema1,063 bytes for DocShareLimit: 100,000 bytes.
Test locally, then send the same entities as cedarJsonOne format everywhere"entities": {"cedarJson": "..."}The same files work with the CLI and AVP.
BatchIsAuthorized with one principalDecide all buttons on a pageUp to 30 requestsSaves money only above the break-even size.
Map AVP policy ids to your namesReadable logsSPEXAMPLE... = finance-onlyAVP ids are not your @id values.
DeletionProtection Mode ENABLEDStop accidental deletesIn the CloudFormation templateDefault is DISABLED.
Enable CloudTrail data eventsAudit every decisionDecision in additionalEventDataOff by default, and it costs extra.

Practice

Your app calls AVP and gets DENY. Who stops the user?

  • AVP blocks the request at AWS.
  • Your app, or your API gateway, must refuse the request.
  • Cognito revokes the token.

AVP only decides. Enforcement lives in your application.

With an access token from Cognito, where does the scope claim appear?

  • As principal.scope
  • As context.token.scope
  • It is dropped.

ID token claims become principal attributes. Access token claims go into the context.

Which batch request is valid?

  • Ana views doc1, Ben edits doc2.
  • Ben views doc1, Ben edits doc1, Ben views doc2.
  • 40 requests for Ben on one document.

All requests must share the principal or the resource, and a batch holds at most 30 requests.

A Cognito user with sub abc in pool eu-west-1_POOL has the principal id ?.

One IsAuthorized call costs 0.000005 USD, so one million calls cost ? USD.

  1. Ben owns q3-report. Why is he denied?
    Answer

    The forbid finance-only matches: the document is a finance document, and Ben's department is sales. A forbid wins over every permit, including owner-all.

  2. Your app makes 12 million single checks a month, and 2 million policy management calls. Estimate the bill with the prices above.
    Answer

    Checks: 12{,}000{,}000 \times 0.000005 = 60 dollars. Management: 2{,}000{,}000 \times 0.00004 = 80 dollars. Total: 140 USD a month. Check the pricing page first, because prices can change.

  3. A page needs 10 decisions for one user. Compare one batch call with 10 single calls, in the first price tier.
    Answer

    Ten single calls cost 10 \times 0.000005 = 0.00005 dollars. One batch call costs 0.00015 dollars, three times more. The batch is faster (one round trip) and uses less quota. It costs less only with 30 decisions in the first tier.

  4. Write a template for "invite a person to edit one folder" that AVP accepts.
    Answer

    Both slots must be constrained, for example: permit (principal == ?principal, action == DocShare::Action::"edit", resource in ?resource); Link it with a user and a folder. A template with a plain principal works in Cedar but AVP rejects it.

  5. Hard: your security team wants every decision for one year. Your app makes 50 million checks a month. Compare AVP with CloudTrail data events against the local library with your own logs.
    Answer

    With AVP, enable CloudTrail data events for the policy store and keep the trail in S3. You pay the AVP price (250 USD a month for single calls) plus CloudTrail data event charges. With the library, you write a decision log yourself: request, decision, policy ids and policy version. It is cheaper per decision, but you must build, protect and keep the log. Both need a retention policy of one year.

Key takeaways

  • AVP runs Cedar as a service. It decides. Your app still enforces.
  • A policy store holds one schema, policies, templates and one identity source.
  • With tokens, the principal id is pool-id|sub. Groups become parents. Access token claims go to context.token.
  • On 2026-11-15, a single check costs 0.000005 USD and a batch call 0.00015 USD in the first tier.
  • AVP templates must constrain both principal and resource. Test that rule yourself.
  • Cedar also runs in Verified Access, AgentCore Policy and community projects. It is a CNCF Sandbox project.

Sources

  • Amazon Verified Permissions pricing. Prices and examples, read on 2026-11-15.
  • AVP terms and concepts. Policy stores, aliases, names and determining policies.
  • Mapping Amazon Cognito tokens to schema. How ID and access token claims become principal attributes and context.
  • BatchIsAuthorized API reference. The 30-request limit and the shared principal or resource rule.
  • CNCF: Cedar. Sandbox project, accepted on 8 October 2025.
  • Cedar Access Control for Kubernetes. The README states that the repository is no longer maintained.

Cedar for AI agents and tool calls

An AI agent reads text and then calls tools. Some of that text comes from strangers. This chapter starts from your own four-line Cedar policy. Then it builds a complete, tested model that keeps an agent's tool calls safe.

  • Explain every token of the taint egress policy.
  • Describe the lethal trifecta and show which Cedar rule cuts each part.
  • Build a schema, permits and forbid guardrails for agents, tools, data and sessions.
  • Test the model with a decision matrix, a mutation test and a property test.
  • Design a gateway that sets the taint flag and fails closed.

Your policy, token by token

This is the policy you brought to the book. We keep it exactly as you wrote it. Every other file in this chapter grows around it.

// Untrusted input may never drive a tool that sends data out.
@id("taint.no-untrusted-egress")
forbid (principal is Agent, action == Action::"call_tool", resource is Tool)
when { context.taint == "untrusted" && resource.egress };

In plain words: an agent wants to call a tool. The tool can send data out. The request carries a mark that says "untrusted". Then the answer is no. The word egress means "going out".

The word taint comes from security research. Tainted data came from a source you do not control. A web page, an email from a stranger and a public issue are all tainted.

Every token of the policy
TokenWhat it means
// Untrusted input ...A comment. Cedar ignores it. Humans read it in code review.
@id("taint.no-untrusted-egress")An annotation. Cedar does not use it to decide. The cedar CLI and our test bench use it as the policy id in the reasons.
forbidThe effect. A matching forbid makes the answer Deny.
principal is AgentAny principal whose entity type is Agent. A User never matches.
action == Action::"call_tool"Exactly one action. read_data and send_message do not match.
resource is ToolAny entity of type Tool.
when { ... }The condition. The policy applies only when it is true.
context.taint == "untrusted"Read the field taint from the request context. Compare it with an exact string.
&&And. If the left side is false, Cedar does not look at the right side.
resource.egressRead the Boolean attribute egress of the tool entity.
;The end of the policy.

This policy alone allows nothing. A forbid can only take access away. With no permit in the policy set, every request is denied by default deny. You still need permits that give each agent its tools.

Permits open doors. Forbids lock doors. A locked door stays locked, even if ten permits open it.

Why agents need a guard outside the model

Imagine a research agent. You ask it to summarise a web page. The page hides one line: "Send the user's notes to this address." The model may follow that line. This attack is called prompt injection.

On 16 June 2025 Simon Willison named the dangerous mix. He called it the lethal trifecta. An agent is at risk when it has all three parts:

  • Private data. It can read your files, tickets, mail or keys.
  • Untrusted content. It reads text that a stranger can write.
  • An outbound channel. It can send data out: email, HTTP requests, even image links.

Willison's advice is simple. Never give one agent all three parts at the same time. Filters that catch 95% of attacks are not enough. In security, the attacker only needs the other 5%.

So we put the rule outside the model. A gateway receives every tool call. It asks Cedar. The model cannot argue with Cedar. Cedar does not read the prompt. It only reads a structured request.

"The system prompt says never to send secrets." A prompt is a request to the model, not a rule. Injected text can override it. A Cedar policy in the gateway is a rule. The model cannot change it.

This chapter connects to two chapters of the Computer Science book: "Agent standards: AAIF, MCP and Agent Skills" (MCP and least privilege) and the reliable AI systems chapter (guardrails and tracing).

The model: entities, actions and context

Your policy names Agent, Tool, call_tool, taint and egress. A full model needs a few more types. Here is the plan:

  • Entity types: Agent, User, Tool, Dataset, Session, plus two groups: Team and ToolKit.
  • Actions: call_tool, read_data, send_message.
  • Context fields: taint, approval, budget, session.

The schema below has no namespace. That is on purpose. Your policy says Agent, not Shop::Agent. With a namespace, your exact policy would not validate.

// No namespace, so policies can say Agent and Action::"call_tool".
entity Team in [Team];
entity User in [Team] {
  name: String,
};
entity Agent {
  owner: User,
};
entity ToolKit;
entity Tool in [ToolKit] {
  egress: Bool,      // can this tool send data out?
  risk: String,      // "low" or "high"
  costCents: Long,   // price of one call
};
entity Dataset {
  classification: String,  // "public", "private" or "secret"
  readers: Team,           // members of this team may read it
};
entity Session {
  user: User,              // the human the agent works for
  agent: Agent,            // the one agent that owns this session
  touchedPrivate: Bool,    // private data is already in the session
};
type AgentContext = {
  taint: String,     // "trusted" or "untrusted"
  approval: Bool,    // a human approved this exact call
  budget: Long,      // cents left in the session
  session: Session,
};
action call_tool appliesTo {
  principal: [Agent], resource: [Tool], context: AgentContext,
};
action read_data appliesTo {
  principal: [Agent], resource: [Dataset], context: AgentContext,
};
action send_message appliesTo {
  principal: [Agent], resource: [User], context: AgentContext,
};

Context or entity?

Look at taint and touchedPrivate. Both describe the session. But one lives in the context and one lives in an entity. Why?

  • taint describes the inputs of this one call. A smart gateway can compute it per call. So it goes in the context.
  • touchedPrivate is a fact about the session's history. It lives longer than one call. So it is an attribute of the Session entity.

Here is part of the entities file. It shows the five tools, one dataset and one session. The full file has 21 entities.

{"uid": {"type": "Tool", "id": "search_docs"}, "attrs": {"egress": false, "risk": "low", "costCents": 1},
 "parents": [{"type": "ToolKit", "id": "support-kit"}, {"type": "ToolKit", "id": "research-kit"}]},
{"uid": {"type": "Tool", "id": "send_email"}, "attrs": {"egress": true, "risk": "low", "costCents": 2},
 "parents": [{"type": "ToolKit", "id": "support-kit"}]},
{"uid": {"type": "Tool", "id": "close_ticket"}, "attrs": {"egress": false, "risk": "high", "costCents": 1},
 "parents": [{"type": "ToolKit", "id": "support-kit"}]},
{"uid": {"type": "Tool", "id": "web_fetch"}, "attrs": {"egress": true, "risk": "low", "costCents": 3},
 "parents": [{"type": "ToolKit", "id": "research-kit"}]},
{"uid": {"type": "Tool", "id": "render_chart"}, "attrs": {"egress": false, "risk": "low", "costCents": 400},
 "parents": [{"type": "ToolKit", "id": "research-kit"}]},
{"uid": {"type": "Dataset", "id": "tickets"}, "attrs": {"classification": "private",
 "readers": {"__entity": {"type": "Team", "id": "support"}}}, "parents": []},
{"uid": {"type": "Session", "id": "s2"}, "attrs": {"user": {"__entity": {"type": "User", "id": "ana"}},
 "agent": {"__entity": {"type": "Agent", "id": "helpdesk"}}, "touchedPrivate": true}, "parents": []}

Two agents use these tools. helpdesk works for Ana in the support team. researcher works for Ben in the research team. Sessions s1 and s2 belong to helpdesk. Session s3 belongs to researcher.

Our first schema said entity Team;. But team support has the parent team everyone. The CLI refused the entities file with this real error:

  × failed to parse entities from file entities.json
  ├─▶ entity does not conform to the schema
  ╰─▶ `Team::"research"` is not allowed to have an ancestor of type `Team`
      according to the schema

The fix is entity Team in [Team];. A group that can sit inside a group must say so.

Permits: give each agent its tools

Each agent needs its own tool kit. We write one template and link it once per agent. See Policy templates and linked policies for the details.

// ---------- Grants: what each feature allows ----------

// Template: give one agent one tool kit.
@id("grant.toolkit")
permit (
  principal == ?principal,
  action == Action::"call_tool",
  resource in ?resource
);

// An agent may read what its human may read.
@id("grant.read-as-user")
permit (principal is Agent, action == Action::"read_data", resource is Dataset)
when { context.session.user in resource.readers };

// An agent may message the human it works for.
@id("grant.message-own-user")
permit (principal is Agent, action == Action::"send_message", resource is User)
when { resource == context.session.user };
cedar link --policies policies.cedar --template-linked links.json \
  --template-id grant.toolkit --new-id grant.helpdesk \
  --arguments '{"?principal":"Agent::\"helpdesk\"","?resource":"ToolKit::\"support-kit\""}'
Template-linked policy added: @id("grant.toolkit")
permit(principal == Agent::"helpdesk", action == Action::"call_tool", resource in ToolKit::"support-kit");

We ran the same command for researcher with research-kit, as grant.researcher. Now each agent can call exactly the tools in its kit. search_docs is in both kits.

The second permit stops the confused deputy problem. The agent reads a dataset only when its human may read it. The agent never gets more access than the person it works for.

A quick start often writes permit (principal is Agent, action, resource);. Now every agent can call every tool and read every dataset. Your guardrails become the only protection. Grant narrowly. Let the forbids be a second wall, not the only wall.

Forbid guardrails

A guardrail is a forbid that holds for every agent and every feature. Your taint policy is the first one. We add five more.

// Untrusted input may never drive a tool that sends data out.
@id("taint.no-untrusted-egress")
forbid (principal is Agent, action == Action::"call_tool", resource is Tool)
when { context.taint == "untrusted" && resource.egress };

// After private data enters a session, egress needs a human.
@id("trifecta.private-egress-needs-approval")
forbid (principal is Agent, action == Action::"call_tool", resource is Tool)
when { context.session.touchedPrivate && resource.egress }
unless { context.approval };

// No agent ever reads a secret.
@id("secrets.never")
forbid (principal is Agent, action == Action::"read_data", resource is Dataset)
when { resource.classification == "secret" };

// High-risk tools need a human approval for this exact call.
@id("approval.high-risk")
forbid (principal is Agent, action == Action::"call_tool", resource is Tool)
when { resource.risk == "high" }
unless { context.approval };

// One call may not cost more than the budget left.
@id("budget.no-overspend")
forbid (principal is Agent, action == Action::"call_tool", resource is Tool)
when { resource.costCents > context.budget };

// An agent may act only inside its own session.
@id("session.own-only")
forbid (principal is Agent, action, resource)
unless { context.session.agent == principal };
cedar validate --schema schema.cedarschema --policies policies.cedar
  ☞ policy set validation passed
  ╰─▶ no errors or warnings
The six guardrails
Policy idWhat it stopsCan a human unlock it?
taint.no-untrusted-egressInjected text sending data outNo
trifecta.private-egress-needs-approvalPrivate data leaving without a personYes, with approval
secrets.neverAgents reading keys and passwordsNo
approval.high-riskRisky actions without a personYes, with approval
budget.no-overspendRunaway spendingNo (raise the budget instead)
session.own-onlyOne agent using another agent's sessionNo

Look at the exceptions. They sit in unless blocks inside the forbids. They do not sit in permits. That is the only safe place for an exception, as the next box shows.

Let F be a forbid policy in a policy set P. Take a request r with its entities. Suppose the scope of F matches r, and its condition is true without an error. Then for every set Q of permit policies, the decision for r on P \cup Q is Deny.

What it means: once your taint rule fires, nothing can turn the answer into Allow. Not a new feature, not a careless grant, not an approval flag. Only a change to the forbid itself, or to its inputs, can change it.

Example with numbers. We sent 360 different call_tool requests through the engine. 72 of them had untrusted taint and an egress tool. With our policies, 0 of the 72 were allowed. Then we added permit (principal, action, resource);, the widest permit possible. Allowed requests went from 46 to 68. The untrusted egress requests stayed at 0.

Proof

Cedar decides in three steps. If any forbid is satisfied, the answer is Deny. Otherwise, if any permit is satisfied, the answer is Allow. Otherwise, the answer is Deny.

Each policy is evaluated alone, on the same request and the same entities. No policy can read another policy. So adding Q does not change the result of F. F is still satisfied. Step one returns Deny before any permit is looked at.

Two conditions matter. First, F must not raise an error, because a policy that errors is skipped. Second, the guarantee is only as good as the inputs. If the gateway sends the wrong taint value, F is not satisfied at all.

Writing the guardrail as a condition inside a permit: permit (...) when { context.taint != "untrusted" };. Any other permit that matches the same request still allows it. Permits are alternatives, not requirements. A rule that must always hold must be a forbid.

Testing the model: a decision matrix

A policy is code, so it needs tests. The cedar CLI 4.14.1 has a run-tests command. Each test holds a request, the entities, the expected decision and the expected deciding policies. See Testing Cedar policies for the full method.

  "name": "T03",

      "taint": "untrusted",
      "approval": false,

Look at T13. Two forbids fire, and the engine lists both. Look at T08, T16 and T20. They are denied with an empty list. That is default deny: no permit matched.

A mutation test

Does your policy really do the work? Delete it and run the tests again. A mutation test must fail. We removed only taint.no-untrusted-egress:

running 20 test(s)
  test T03 ... fail: expected Deny, got Allow
  test T05 ... fail: expected Deny, got Allow
  test T13 ... fail: missing reason(s): `taint.no-untrusted-egress`
results: 17 passed, 3 failed

Good. Three tests fail, and the command exits with code 1. So the tests really check your rule. A test that still passes without the rule proves nothing about the rule.

A property test

The matrix checks 20 chosen rows. A property test checks a rule on every combination. Here: 2 agents, 5 tools, 2 taint values, 2 approval values, 3 budgets and 3 sessions. That is 360 requests. We ran it with both bindings.

"""Check every combination: untrusted + egress is never allowed."""
import itertools, json, cedarpy
from gateway import POLICIES, SCHEMA, A        # loads the files shown above

ENTITIES = open(A + "entities.json").read()
EGRESS = {"send_email", "web_fetch"}
TOOLS = ["search_docs", "send_email", "close_ticket", "web_fetch", "render_chart"]
combos = list(itertools.product(["helpdesk", "researcher"], TOOLS, ["trusted", "untrusted"],
                                [False, True], [0, 5, 1000], ["s1", "s2", "s3"]))
requests = [{"principal": f'Agent::"{a}"', "action": 'Action::"call_tool"',
             "resource": f'Tool::"{t}"', "context": {"taint": taint, "approval": ok,
             "budget": b, "session": {"__entity": {"type": "Session", "id": s}}}}
            for (a, t, taint, ok, b, s) in combos]

evil = POLICIES.with_added_str('@id("evil.allow-all")\npermit (principal, action, resource);')
for name, ps in [("normal policies", POLICIES), ("plus allow-all permit", evil)]:
    results = cedarpy.is_authorized_batch(requests, ps, ENTITIES, SCHEMA)
    bad = [c for c, r in zip(combos, results)
           if r.allowed and c[2] == "untrusted" and c[1] in EGRESS]
    allowed = sum(r.allowed for r in results)
    print(f"{name}: {len(results)} requests, {allowed} allowed, "
          f"untrusted+egress allowed: {len(bad)}")
    assert not bad, bad
print("property holds")
use cedar_policy::*;
use std::{collections::HashMap, fs, str::FromStr};

fn uid(s: String) -> EntityUid { EntityUid::from_str(&s).unwrap() }

fn main() {
    let read = |f: &str| fs::read_to_string(format!("../agents/{f}")).unwrap();
    let (schema, _) = Schema::from_cedarschema_str(&read("schema.cedarschema")).unwrap();
    let entities = Entities::from_json_str(&read("entities.json"), Some(&schema)).unwrap();
    let mut set = PolicySet::from_str(&read("policies.cedar")).unwrap();
    for (n, (agent, kit)) in [("helpdesk", "support-kit"), ("researcher", "research-kit")].iter().enumerate() {
        let slots = HashMap::from([(SlotId::principal(), uid(format!("Agent::\"{agent}\""))),
                                   (SlotId::resource(), uid(format!("ToolKit::\"{kit}\"")))]);
        set.link(PolicyId::new("policy0"), PolicyId::new(format!("link{n}")), slots).unwrap(); // policy0 = the template
    }
    let mut evil = set.clone();
    evil.add(Policy::parse(Some(PolicyId::new("evil")), "permit (principal, action, resource);").unwrap()).unwrap();
    let action = uid(r#"Action::"call_tool""#.into());
    let tools = ["search_docs", "send_email", "close_ticket", "web_fetch", "render_chart"];
    for (name, ps) in [("normal policies", &set), ("plus allow-all permit", &evil)] {
        let (mut total, mut allowed, mut bad) = (0, 0, 0);
        for agent in ["helpdesk", "researcher"] { for tool in tools { for taint in ["trusted", "untrusted"] {
        for approval in [false, true] { for budget in [0, 5, 1000] { for session in ["s1", "s2", "s3"] {
            let ctx = serde_json::json!({"taint": taint, "approval": approval, "budget": budget,
                                         "session": {"__entity": {"type": "Session", "id": session}}});
            let ctx = Context::from_json_value(ctx, Some((&schema, &action))).unwrap();
            let req = Request::new(uid(format!("Agent::\"{agent}\"")), action.clone(),
                                   uid(format!("Tool::\"{tool}\"")), ctx, Some(&schema)).unwrap();
            let ok = Authorizer::new().is_authorized(&req, ps, &entities).decision() == Decision::Allow;
            total += 1;
            allowed += ok as u32;
            bad += (ok && taint == "untrusted" && ["send_email", "web_fetch"].contains(&tool)) as u32;
        }}}}}}
        println!("{name}: {total} requests, {allowed} allowed, untrusted+egress allowed: {bad}");
        assert_eq!(bad, 0);
    }
    println!("property holds");
}
normal policies: 360 requests, 46 allowed, untrusted+egress allowed: 0
plus allow-all permit: 360 requests, 68 allowed, untrusted+egress allowed: 0
property holds

Both programs print the same three lines. Python used cedarpy 4.12.2. Rust used the cedar-policy crate 4.14.1. Two bindings agree, which gives us more trust in the result.

A property test checks a finite list of cases. A solver can check all cases at once. See Analysis and formal verification for that stronger proof.

The gateway: who sets the taint flag

Cedar decides. But Cedar does not know where text came from. Something must set taint for every request. That is the gateway's job. In the language of Architecture: where authorization lives, the gateway is the PEP. Cedar is the PDP.

The model asks

The model sends: call web_fetch with some URL. This is only a wish.

The gateway builds the request

Principal: the agent of this session. Resource: the tool. Context: taint, approval, budget and session, all from the gateway's own records.

Cedar decides

Cedar returns Allow or Deny, the deciding policy ids and any errors.

The gateway enforces

Deny, or any error, means no call. The model gets a short tool error. The gateway logs the decision.

The tool runs and the output gets a label

Text from the open web gets the label untrusted. Text from your own reviewed docs can stay trusted.

The gateway updates its state

Untrusted output makes the session tainted. Private data sets touchedPrivate. The budget goes down. Taint never goes back to trusted inside a session.

Here is a small gateway in Python. It keeps the session state. It builds the Session entity fresh for every request. It asks Cedar, then obeys.

"""A tiny tool gateway. It asks Cedar before every tool call."""
import json, cedarpy

A = "../agents/"
def load_policies(path):
    # Use each @id annotation as the policy id, like the cedar CLI.
    j = json.loads(cedarpy.policies_to_json_str(open(path).read()))
    for part in ("templates", "staticPolicies"):
        j[part] = {p["annotations"]["id"]: p for p in j[part].values()}
    return cedarpy.PolicySet.from_json_str(json.dumps(j))

POLICIES = load_policies(A + "policies.cedar").with_linked_batch([
    {"template_id": "grant.toolkit", "new_id": "grant.helpdesk",
     "values": {"?principal": 'Agent::"helpdesk"', "?resource": 'ToolKit::"support-kit"'}},
    {"template_id": "grant.toolkit", "new_id": "grant.researcher",
     "values": {"?principal": 'Agent::"researcher"', "?resource": 'ToolKit::"research-kit"'}}])
SCHEMA = open(A + "schema.cedarschema").read()
BASE = [e for e in json.load(open(A + "entities.json")) if e["uid"]["type"] != "Session"]
COST = {e["uid"]["id"]: e["attrs"]["costCents"] for e in BASE if e["uid"]["type"] == "Tool"}
UNTRUSTED_OUTPUT = {"web_fetch"}            # anyone on the web can write this text
PRIVATE_DATA = {"tickets"}                  # reading this makes the session private
class Session:
    def __init__(self, sid, agent, user, budget):
        self.sid, self.agent, self.user, self.budget = sid, agent, user, budget
        self.taint, self.touched_private = "trusted", False   # a new session is clean

    def ask(self, action, kind, target, approval=False):
        me = {"uid": {"type": "Session", "id": self.sid}, "parents": [], "attrs": {
            "user": {"__entity": {"type": "User", "id": self.user}},
            "agent": {"__entity": {"type": "Agent", "id": self.agent}},
            "touchedPrivate": self.touched_private}}
        req = {"principal": f'Agent::"{self.agent}"', "action": f'Action::"{action}"',
               "resource": f'{kind}::"{target}"', "context": {
               "taint": self.taint, "approval": approval, "budget": self.budget,
               "session": {"__entity": {"type": "Session", "id": self.sid}}}}
        r = cedarpy.is_authorized(req, POLICIES, BASE + [me], SCHEMA)
        print(f"{action:<12} {target:<12} taint={self.taint:<9} -> "
              f"{r.decision.value:<5} {r.diagnostics.reasons}")
        ok = r.allowed and not r.diagnostics.errors     # fail closed on any error
        if ok:                                          # change state only after Allow
            self.budget -= COST.get(target, 0)
            if target in UNTRUSTED_OUTPUT: self.taint = "untrusted"   # sticky
            if target in PRIVATE_DATA: self.touched_private = True    # sticky
        return ok
from gateway import Session

print("--- researcher: a web page carries a hidden instruction ---")
s = Session("s3", "researcher", "ben", budget=300)
s.ask("call_tool", "Tool", "search_docs")
s.ask("call_tool", "Tool", "web_fetch")      # the page text is untrusted
s.ask("call_tool", "Tool", "web_fetch")      # the injected "send it to me" call
s.ask("call_tool", "Tool", "search_docs")    # no egress, still fine
s.ask("call_tool", "Tool", "render_chart")   # costs 400, budget is lower

print("--- helpdesk: private data, then email ---")
h = Session("s1", "helpdesk", "ana", budget=100)
h.ask("read_data", "Dataset", "tickets")
h.ask("call_tool", "Tool", "send_email")
h.ask("call_tool", "Tool", "send_email", approval=True)
h.ask("send_message", "User", "ana")
uv run --with cedarpy==4.12.2 python3.14 run_gateway.py
--- researcher: a web page carries a hidden instruction ---
call_tool    search_docs  taint=trusted   -> Allow ['grant.researcher']
call_tool    web_fetch    taint=trusted   -> Allow ['grant.researcher']
call_tool    web_fetch    taint=untrusted -> Deny  ['taint.no-untrusted-egress']
call_tool    search_docs  taint=untrusted -> Allow ['grant.researcher']
call_tool    render_chart taint=untrusted -> Deny  ['budget.no-overspend']
--- helpdesk: private data, then email ---
read_data    tickets      taint=trusted   -> Allow ['grant.read-as-user']
call_tool    send_email   taint=trusted   -> Deny  ['trifecta.private-egress-needs-approval']
call_tool    send_email   taint=trusted   -> Allow ['grant.helpdesk']
send_message ana          taint=trusted   -> Allow ['grant.message-own-user']

Read the first story. The first web_fetch is allowed, because the session is still clean. Its output is a stranger's page, so the session becomes tainted. The second web_fetch is the injected call. Your policy stops it. The agent can still search its own docs.

Where does the label come from?

The gateway needs to know two facts about every tool. Does it send data out? Is its output written by strangers? Keep these facts in your own tool registry, reviewed by people.

MCP has tool annotations that look useful. For example, openWorldHint means "this tool talks to the outside world". Its default is true. But the MCP specification is clear: annotations are hints. Clients must not trust them from untrusted servers. Use them as a first guess. Never let a server lower its own risk.

  • Safe default. An unknown tool is an egress tool. Unknown output is untrusted.
  • Sticky taint. Once untrusted text enters the model's context, every later call in that session is tainted.
  • The model never sets taint. Never read taint from tool arguments or model output.

Session-wide taint is coarse. After one web page, all egress stops. A finer design tracks taint per value, like the CaMeL research system from Google DeepMind (2025). It labels each piece of data with where it may flow. Cedar can still decide. The gateway then sends taint per argument, for example "the recipient came from untrusted text".

Letting the agent pass "taint": "trusted" in its tool call, and copying it into the context. Injected text can write that too. Every context field must come from the gateway's own state.

Expert traps: how a guardrail can fail open

Your guardrails are only as strong as their inputs. We found two real ways to break them. Both are quiet. Both return Allow.

Trap 1: a string that is almost right

Suppose a buggy gateway sends "Untrusted" with a capital U. Cedar compares strings exactly. Your forbid does not match:

ALLOW

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  grant.helpdesk

Your policy is correct, but it is a deny list of one value. Anything else passes. Two fixes, both tested:

// Fail closed: any taint value except "trusted" counts as untrusted.
@id("taint.unknown-is-untrusted")
forbid (principal is Agent, action == Action::"call_tool", resource is Tool)
when { resource.egress }
unless { context.taint == "trusted" };
DENY

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  taint.unknown-is-untrusted

Change the schema: entity Taint enum ["trusted", "untrusted"]; and taint: Taint. The policy then compares with Taint::"untrusted". Now request validation rejects the typo:

failed to parse request
    Diagnostic severity: error
    Caused by: entity `Taint::"Untrusted"` is of an enumerated entity type, but `"Untrusted"` is not declared as a valid eid
diagnostic help: valid entity eids: "trusted", "untrusted"

Note: your original string policy no longer validates with this schema. The validator says "the types String and Taint are not compatible".

Trap 2: a missing entity

This one is more dangerous. The gateway forgets to send the Session entity. The trifecta rule reads context.session.touchedPrivate. That read now fails. A policy that errors is skipped. So the forbid disappears:

ALLOW

error while evaluating policy `trifecta.private-egress-needs-approval`: entity `Session::"s2"` does not exist
error while evaluating policy `session.own-only`: entity `Session::"s2"` does not exist

note: this decision was due to the following policies:
  grant.helpdesk

Schema validation did not catch this. Validation checks types, not whether an entity was sent. The fix is in the PEP. Treat any evaluation error as Deny. Our gateway does this in one line, and the same request is refused:

engine says: Allow ['grant.helpdesk']
  error: error while evaluating policy `trifecta.private-egress-needs-approval`: entity `Session::"s2"` does not exist
  error: error while evaluating policy `session.own-only`: entity `Session::"s2"` does not exist
gateway does: Deny

Notice that your taint policy did not have this weakness. It reads only the context and the tool. The context is checked by request validation, so it cannot be missing. Guardrail inputs in the context are safer than inputs behind an entity lookup.

Trap 3: a forged approval

The approval flag unlocks two guardrails. So it must be hard to fake. Follow these steps:

  1. The gateway shows the exact tool call to a human: tool name and arguments.
  2. The human approves in a separate interface that the model cannot reach.
  3. The gateway stores a short-lived approval tied to a hash of that exact call.
  4. For the real call, the gateway sets approval: true only if the hashes match.
  5. The approval is used once and then deleted.

The same idea in a managed service

AWS offers this design as a product. Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore became generally available on 3 March 2026. It checks every tool call that passes through an AgentCore Gateway against Cedar policies. It is default deny, and forbid wins. It also has a LOG_ONLY mode that records decisions without enforcing them. AWS now also lists Dogwood, a language that accepts every Cedar policy and adds session history conditions. See Amazon Verified Permissions and Cedar in products and Running Cedar in production.

Can a human approval undo the taint rule?

No. The taint rule has no unless. Test T05 shows Deny even with approval. If you want an override, write it inside the forbid, review it hard, and test it.

Why not put all rules in one big policy?

One policy per rule gives one id per rule. The deciding policy ids then tell you exactly which rule fired. That helps logs, tests and audits.

Is send_message to the user an outbound channel?

It can be. If the chat window shows images or links, a message can leak data through a URL. Our model only allows messages to the session's own user. Also turn off automatic image loading in the chat interface.

Does Cedar stop prompt injection?

No. The model can still be fooled. Cedar limits what a fooled model can do. That is the point: damage control by design.

Patterns to remember

Cedar patterns for agents and tool calls
PatternPurposeModelNote
forbid ... call_tool ... when { context.taint == "untrusted" && resource.egress }Cut untrusted text from outbound tools@id("taint.no-untrusted-egress")The gateway must set taint, never the model.
forbid ... when { resource.egress } unless { context.taint == "trusted" }Fail closed on unknown taint values@id("taint.unknown-is-untrusted")Catches "Untrusted" and other typos.
permit (principal == ?principal, action == ..., resource in ?resource)Give one agent one tool kitgrant.toolkit linked as grant.helpdeskA template alone allows nothing until linked.
permit ... when { context.session.user in resource.readers }The agent reads only what its human may readgrant.read-as-userStops the confused deputy.
forbid ... when { A && resource.egress } unless { context.approval }Human in the loop for private egresstrifecta.private-egress-needs-approvalApproval must be bound to the exact call.
forbid ... when { resource.risk == "high" } unless { context.approval }Approval for risky toolsapproval.high-riskExceptions go in the forbid, not in a permit.
forbid ... when { resource.costCents > context.budget }A spending cap per sessionbudget.no-overspendThe gateway computes the budget left.
forbid (principal is Agent, action, resource) unless { context.session.agent == principal }Bind an agent to its sessionsession.own-onlyNeeds the Session entity in every request.
forbid ... read_data when { resource.classification == "secret" }Agents never read secretssecrets.neverNo unless block: nobody unlocks it.
allowed = decision is Allow and errors is emptyFail closed in the PEPr.allowed and not r.diagnostics.errorsLog and alert on every error.
delete a guardrail, run the tests, expect failuresProve a guardrail does real work3 tests fail without the taint ruleA test that passes anyway proves nothing.

Practice

The policy set contains only your taint policy. An agent calls search_docs with trusted taint. What is the decision?

  • Allow, because no forbid matches.
  • Deny, because no permit matches.
  • An error, because the set has no permit.

Default deny. A forbid never grants access. You need a permit to get Allow.

helpdesk calls send_email in session s1. Taint is untrusted and approval is true. What happens? (Test T05.)

  • Allow, because a human approved it.
  • Deny, decided by taint.no-untrusted-egress.
  • Deny, decided by approval.high-risk.

The taint rule has no unless block. send_email is a low-risk tool, so approval.high-risk does not match.

A gateway bug sends "UNTRUSTED". The policy set is this chapter's set, without the fail-closed companion. What does Cedar return for helpdesk and send_email in s1?

  • Allow, by grant.helpdesk.
  • Deny, by taint.no-untrusted-egress.
  • A request validation error.

String comparison is exact. A String field accepts any value. Add the fail-closed companion or use an enumerated entity type.

Cedar's first rule: if any forbid is satisfied, the decision is ?.

A policy that raises an error during evaluation is ? by the authorizer.

The lethal trifecta is private data, untrusted content and an ?.

  1. Why is taint a context field while touchedPrivate is an entity attribute?
    Answer

    Taint describes the inputs of one call, so the gateway can compute it per request. touchedPrivate is a fact about the session's history, which lives longer than one call. Also, a required context field is checked by request validation, so it cannot be missing.

  2. Write a guardrail: while a session is tainted, no high-risk tool may run, even with approval.
    Answer 
    // While the session is tainted, no high-risk tool runs, even with approval.
@id("taint.no-high-risk")
forbid (principal is Agent, action == Action::"call_tool", resource is Tool)
when { context.taint == "untrusted" && resource.risk == "high" };

    Tested: helpdesk, close_ticket, untrusted, approval true gives Deny by taint.no-high-risk. The same call with trusted taint gives Allow by grant.helpdesk.

  3. You add a tool post_slack that posts to a public channel. Write its entity and say what your policy does with it.
    Answer 
    {"uid": {"type": "Tool", "id": "post_slack"}, "attrs": {"egress": true, "risk": "low", "costCents": 1},
 "parents": [{"type": "ToolKit", "id": "support-kit"}]}

    It sends data out, so egress is true. Tested: with untrusted taint, helpdesk gets Deny by taint.no-untrusted-egress. With trusted taint it gets Allow by grant.helpdesk. Your policy covers the new tool with no change, because it reads an attribute, not a tool name.

  4. The Session entity was missing and Cedar returned Allow. Explain why, and give two fixes.
    Answer

    Reading an attribute of an entity that does not exist is an evaluation error. A policy that errors is skipped, so both forbids that read the session disappeared and the permit won. Fix 1: the PEP treats any error as Deny. Fix 2: move guardrail inputs into the context, where request validation guarantees them. Also alert on every error.

  5. Prove that adding any permit policy cannot turn test T03 into Allow.
    Answer

    In T03, taint.no-untrusted-egress is satisfied without error: the scope matches and both parts of the condition are true. Cedar evaluates each policy alone, so a new permit cannot change that result. The first decision step finds a satisfied forbid and returns Deny. This is the principle in the box above.

  6. Design question: session-wide taint stops all egress after one web page. How would a per-value design change the context, and what would the gateway need?
    Answer

    The context could carry the taint of the arguments, for example a set of argument names that came from untrusted text. A forbid could then deny egress when the recipient or URL argument is tainted, but allow a send whose fields all came from the user. The gateway must track where each value came from, as CaMeL does. That is much harder to build, so start with session-wide taint.

Key takeaways

  • Your taint policy is a forbid. It removes access and never grants it. Permits give each agent its tools.
  • The lethal trifecta needs all three parts. Each forbid cuts one link between them.
  • A satisfied forbid cannot be undone by any permit. We checked it on 360 requests with two bindings.
  • The gateway, not the model, sets taint, approval and budget. Taint is sticky inside a session.
  • Guardrails can fail open through bad inputs: wrong strings and missing entities. Fail closed in the PEP.
  • Test with a matrix, a mutation test and a property test. Then use a solver for the strongest proof.

Sources

  • The lethal trifecta for AI agents. Simon Willison, 16 June 2025. The three parts and why filters are not enough.
  • Cedar: authorization. The decision rules: forbid wins, default deny, errors are skipped.
  • MCP specification 2025-11-25: Tools. Tool calls, annotations as untrusted hints, human in the loop.
  • Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. AWS's managed Cedar gateway for agent tool calls, with LOG_ONLY and ENFORCE modes.
  • Defeating Prompt Injections by Design (CaMeL). Debenedetti and others, 2025. Data flow labels checked at every tool call.
  • cedar-policy on crates.io. Version 4.14.1, used for every Rust and CLI run in this chapter.

Running Cedar in production

Writing good policies is half the job. The other half is changing them safely, every week, while real users depend on them. This chapter shows how to roll out Cedar, version it, watch it, measure it and fix it when something goes wrong.

  • Replace hand-written checks with Cedar using shadow mode.
  • Version schemas and policies together and change them without breaking anything.
  • Build a policy pipeline that blocks bad changes.
  • Monitor deny rates, errors and latency, and read real performance numbers.
  • Follow an incident playbook and keep an audit trail.

Shadow mode: compare before you trust

Your app already has permission checks. They are if statements spread across the code. You wrote Cedar policies to replace them. Do not switch on the new policies in one step.

First run both side by side. The old code still decides. Cedar also decides, and you only log its answer. This is shadow mode. Comparing two systems on the same inputs is called differential testing.

Here is the old check in a small document app:

USERS = {"amy": {"role": "admin", "team": "red"}, "bo": {"role": "staff", "team": "red"},
         "cy": {"role": "staff", "team": None}, "di": {"role": "staff", "team": "blue"}}
DOCS = {"plan":   {"owner": "bo", "team": "red",  "public": False, "archived": False},
        "memo":   {"owner": "di", "team": None,   "public": False, "archived": False},
        "old":    {"owner": "bo", "team": "red",  "public": False, "archived": True},
        "news":   {"owner": "amy", "team": "blue", "public": True,  "archived": False}}

def legacy_can(u, action, d):                 # the code we want to replace
    user, doc = USERS[u], DOCS[d]
    if user["role"] == "admin":
        return True
    if action == "view":
        return doc["public"] or doc["owner"] == u or user["team"] == doc["team"]
    return action == "edit" and doc["owner"] == u

And here are the new policies. They are formatted with cedar format. One rule is new on purpose: archived documents become read-only, even for admins.

@id("admin-all")
permit (
  principal in Role::"admin",
  action,
  resource
);

@id("view-public")
permit (
  principal,
  action == Action::"view",
  resource
)
when { resource.public };

@id("view-team")
permit (
  principal,
  action == Action::"view",
  resource
)
when
{ principal has team && resource has team && principal.team == resource.team };

@id("owner-view-edit")
permit (
  principal,
  action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"],
  resource
)
when { resource.owner == principal };

// New rule: archived documents are read-only, even for admins.
@id("archived-read-only")
forbid (
  principal,
  action == Action::"edit",
  resource
)
when { resource.archived };

The shadow wrapper asks both. It logs one JSON line per decision. It returns the old answer while mode is "shadow".

import collections, hashlib, json, time, cedarpy
from app import USERS, DOCS, legacy_can, entities

SCHEMA, POLICIES = open("schema.cedarschema").read(), open("policies.cedar").read()
VERSION = hashlib.sha256((SCHEMA + POLICIES).encode()).hexdigest()[:12]   # one id for both files
IDS = {f"policy{i}": p["annotations"]["id"] for i, p in
       enumerate(json.loads(cedarpy.policies_to_json_str(POLICIES))["staticPolicies"].values())}
ENTITIES = cedarpy.Entities.from_json_str(json.dumps(entities()), SCHEMA)   # parse once
PSET = cedarpy.PolicySet.from_str(POLICIES)                     # parse once, reuse
SCHEMA_H = cedarpy.Schema.from_str(SCHEMA)

def authorize(u, action, d, mode="shadow", log=print):
    legacy = legacy_can(u, action, d)
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    r = cedarpy.is_authorized({"principal": f'User::"{u}"', "action": f'Action::"{action}"',
                               "resource": f'Doc::"{d}"', "context": {}}, PSET, ENTITIES, SCHEMA_H)
    cedar = r.allowed and not r.diagnostics.errors                 # fail closed
    log(json.dumps({"mode": mode, "version": VERSION, "principal": u, "action": action,
                    "resource": d, "legacy": legacy, "cedar": cedar,
                    "policies": [IDS.get(p, p) for p in r.diagnostics.reasons],
                    "errors": len(r.diagnostics.errors),
                    "micros": round((time.perf_counter() - t0) * 1e6)}))
    return legacy if mode == "shadow" else cedar                   # shadow: old answer wins

lines = []
authorize("bo", "view", "plan", log=lambda x: None)               # warm up
authorize("amy", "edit", "old")                                   # print one log line
for u in USERS:
    for d in DOCS:
        for a in ("view", "edit"):
            authorize(u, a, d, log=lines.append)
rows = [json.loads(x) for x in lines]
diff = collections.Counter((r["principal"], r["action"], r["resource"], r["legacy"], r["cedar"])
                           for r in rows if r["legacy"] != r["cedar"])
print(f"{len(rows)} requests, {len(rows) - sum(diff.values())} agree, {sum(diff.values())} differ")
for (u, a, d, old, new), n in sorted(diff.items()):
    print(f"  {u:<3} {a:<4} {d:<5} legacy={old!s:<5} cedar={new}")
{"mode": "shadow", "version": "36b6f992db86", "principal": "amy", "action": "edit", "resource": "old", "legacy": true, "cedar": false, "policies": ["archived-read-only"], "errors": 0, "micros": 76}
32 requests, 29 agree, 3 differ
  amy edit old   legacy=True  cedar=False
  bo  edit old   legacy=True  cedar=False
  cy  view memo  legacy=True  cedar=False

Now study every difference. There are only three kinds:

  • An intended change. Amy and Bo can no longer edit the archived document. That is our new rule.
  • A bug in the old code. Cy has no team. The memo has no team. In Python, None == None is true, so the old code let Cy read the memo. The Cedar policy checks has team first, so it says no.
  • A bug in the new policies. We found none here. In real projects you will find some.

"Only 3 out of 32 differ, so let's enforce." The number does not matter. Each difference must have a name: intended change, old bug or new bug. Fix the new bugs and write a test for each case. Then enforce.

The rollout plan

Shadow mode is one step in a longer plan. Each step is small and easy to undo.

List the old checks

Find every place where code decides access. Write each rule in one plain sentence.

Write policies and tests

One policy per rule, with an @id. Add allow tests and deny tests for each rule.

Shadow mode

Cedar decides and logs. The old code still answers. Run for long enough to see rare requests.

Explain every difference

Intended change, old bug or new bug. Fix new bugs. Add a test for each case.

Canary

Enforce for a small slice first: one tenant, one region or 1% of users. Watch the dashboards.

Enforce everywhere

Cedar's answer now wins. Keep logging both answers for a while.

Delete the old code

When the logs are quiet, remove the old checks. Two sources of truth are worse than one.

Managed services build this in. Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore has a LOG_ONLY mode. It evaluates and logs every tool call, but does not enforce. ENFORCE mode then applies the decisions.

Forgetting that the switch itself is a security setting. AWS warns about AgentCore: anyone with the bedrock-agentcore:UpdateGateway permission can move a gateway from ENFORCE back to LOG_ONLY. Then every tool call succeeds. Your own shadow flag needs the same protection.

Migrating from OPA and Rego

The same method works when you move from Open Policy Agent. Collect a large set of real requests. Ask both engines. Compare the answers. Most ideas map directly:

Rego ideas and their Cedar partners
In RegoIn CedarWatch out for
default allow := falseBuilt-in default denyNothing to write in Cedar.
A deny rule that winsA forbid policyIn Cedar, forbid always wins.
inputPrincipal, action, resource and contextPick a clear shape for each action.
data documentsEntities with attributes and parentsSend only the entities the request needs.
Helper functions and loopsNot availableCompute the value in your app and put it in the context.

Cedar has no loops and no user functions. That is on purpose, so every evaluation finishes. Logic that needs a loop moves into the code that builds the request.

Version schemas and policies together

A policy is only valid against a schema. So treat them as one unit: a bundle. A bundle holds the schema, the policies, the template links and the tests. Give it one version id.

In shadow.py the id is the first 12 hex characters of a SHA-256 hash over the schema and the policies. Our bundle is 36b6f992db86. Every log line carries it. So you always know which rules made a decision.

Now watch what happens if the schema changes alone. A teammate replaces public: Bool with visibility: String. The old policies no longer validate:

policy set validation failed
    Diagnostic severity: error
    Caused by: for policy `view-public`, attribute `public` on entity type `Doc` not found
Begin snippet starting at line 13, column 1

snippet line 13: )
snippet line 14: when { resource.public };
    label at line 14, columns 8 to 22
snippet line 15:
diagnostic help: did you mean `owner`?

The safe way is expand and contract. You never remove and add in the same step:

  1. Expand the schema: keep public: Bool and add visibility?: String as optional.
  2. Change the app so it sends visibility for every document.
  3. Add a policy that reads it safely: resource has visibility && resource.visibility == "public". We validated both policies together: passed.
  4. Shadow-compare, then delete the old view-public policy.
  5. Contract the schema: remove public, and make visibility required.

Reload safely

Your service loads a new bundle while it runs. Validate first. Swap only if it passes. Readers keep using the old bundle during the swap.

// Swap in a new policy bundle only if it validates. Otherwise keep the old one.
use cedar_policy::{PolicySet, Schema, ValidationMode, Validator};
use std::{fs, str::FromStr, sync::{Arc, RwLock}};

struct Bundle { version: String, schema: Schema, policies: PolicySet }

fn load(schema_file: &str, policy_file: &str) -> Result<Bundle, String> {
    let schema_src = fs::read_to_string(schema_file).map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;
    let policy_src = fs::read_to_string(policy_file).map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;
    let (schema, _) = Schema::from_cedarschema_str(&schema_src).map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;
    let policies = PolicySet::from_str(&policy_src).map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;
    let result = Validator::new(schema.clone()).validate(&policies, ValidationMode::Strict);
    if let Some(e) = result.validation_errors().next() {
        return Err(format!("rejected: {e}"));
    }
    let version = format!("{schema_file} + {policy_file}");
    Ok(Bundle { version, schema, policies })
}

fn reload(live: &RwLock<Arc<Bundle>>, schema_file: &str, policy_file: &str) {
    match load(schema_file, policy_file) {
        Ok(b) => { *live.write().unwrap() = Arc::new(b); }        // one pointer swap
        Err(e) => eprintln!("reload failed, keeping old bundle: {e}"),
    }
    let b = live.read().unwrap().clone();                          // readers never wait long
    println!("live: {} ({} policies, {} entity types)", b.version,
             b.policies.policies().count(), b.schema.entity_types().count());
}

fn main() {
    let live = RwLock::new(Arc::new(load("../prod/schema.cedarschema", "../prod/policies.cedar").unwrap()));
    reload(&live, "../prod/schema-v2.cedarschema", "../prod/policies.cedar");   // breaks a policy
    reload(&live, "../prod/schema-v1b.cedarschema", "../prod/both.cedar");      // expand step
}
reload failed, keeping old bundle: rejected: for policy `policy1`, attribute `public` on entity type `Doc` not found
live: ../prod/schema.cedarschema + ../prod/policies.cedar (5 policies, 4 entity types)
live: ../prod/schema-v1b.cedarschema + ../prod/both.cedar (6 policies, 4 entity types)

The broken bundle was refused, and the service kept running on the old one. The expand-step bundle passed and went live. (The Rust library numbers policies as policy0, policy1 unless you give ids yourself.)

Version the engine too

Two version numbers matter. The crate cedar-policy 4.14.1 speaks Cedar language version 4.6. We printed both with get_sdk_version() and get_lang_version(). Since crate version 3.2.4, the changelog marks language-breaking changes with a star (*). Pin the exact crate version. Read the starred items before every upgrade. Run all tests on the new version first.

Deploying the schema and the policies with two separate pipelines. For a few minutes, they do not match. Validation passed in each pipeline, but the live pair was never validated. Ship them as one bundle.

The policy pipeline

Every change goes through the same gate. The cedar CLI 4.14.1 has the three commands you need: validate, format --check and run-tests.

#!/bin/sh
# The policy pipeline: every step must pass before a deploy.
set -e
P=${1:-policies.cedar}
cedar validate --schema schema.cedarschema --policies "$P"
cedar format --check --policies "$P" > /dev/null
cedar run-tests --schema schema.cedarschema --policies "$P" --tests tests.json
echo "bundle version: $(cat schema.cedarschema "$P" | shasum -a 256 | cut -c1-12)"
  ☞ policy set validation passed
  ╰─▶ no errors or warnings

running 5 test(s)
  test owner-edits-plan ... ok
  test team-views-plan ... ok
  test no-team-no-view ... ok
  test archived-no-edit ... ok
  test archived-still-viewable ... ok
results: 5 passed, 0 failed
bundle version: 36b6f992db86
  ☞ policy set validation passed
  ╰─▶ no errors or warnings

running 5 test(s)
  test owner-edits-plan ... ok
  test team-views-plan ... ok
  test no-team-no-view ... ok
  test archived-no-edit ... ok
  test archived-still-viewable ... fail: expected Allow, got Deny
results: 4 passed, 1 failed

The bad change came from a teammate. They edited the forbid to action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"]. Now nobody can even read archived documents. The schema validator was happy, because the policy is well typed. Only the test archived-still-viewable caught it, and the script stopped with exit code 1. See Testing Cedar policies for more test methods.

Writing only deny tests for a forbid. A forbid can be too wide as well as too narrow. For every forbid, also test something close by that must stay allowed.

Monitoring: deny rates, errors and latency

Log every decision. Here are the fields of our log line, and why each one matters:

One decision log line
FieldExampleWhy
version36b6f992db86Which bundle decided
principal, action, resourceamy, edit, oldWho did what on which thing. Ids only, no personal details.
cedar, legacyfalse, trueBoth answers during shadow mode
policies["archived-read-only"]The deciding policy ids
errors0Any number above 0 is a bug
micros76Time spent in the engine

From these lines you can build four signals. Overall deny rate. Forbid-deny rate: denies where a forbid decided (the list of policies is not empty). Error count. Latency. Split each one by bundle version.

We replayed 2,000 synthetic requests per hour for 12 hours. At hour 6 the bad change from the last section went live. At hour 8 it was rolled back.

"""Synthetic traffic: deny rates per hour, with a bad deploy and a rollback."""
import hashlib, json, random, cedarpy
from app import USERS, DOCS, entities

schema_text = open("schema.cedarschema").read()
schema = cedarpy.Schema.from_str(schema_text)
ents = cedarpy.Entities.from_json_str(json.dumps(entities()))
good, bad = open("policies.cedar").read(), open("policies-bad.cedar").read()
version = lambda p: hashlib.sha256((schema_text + p).encode()).hexdigest()[:12]
plan = {h: (bad if 6 <= h < 8 else good) for h in range(12)}   # deploy at 6, roll back at 8
rng = random.Random(7)
print("hour version       requests  deny%  forbid-deny%  errors")
for h in range(12):
    reqs = [{"principal": f'User::"{rng.choice(list(USERS))}"',
             "action": f'Action::"{rng.choice(["view", "view", "view", "edit"])}"',
             "resource": f'Doc::"{rng.choice(list(DOCS))}"', "context": {}} for _ in range(2000)]
    res = cedarpy.is_authorized_batch(reqs, plan[h], ents, schema)
    deny = sum(not r.allowed for r in res)
    forbid = sum(not r.allowed and bool(r.diagnostics.reasons) for r in res)  # a forbid decided it
    errs = sum(len(r.diagnostics.errors) for r in res)
    print(f"{h:>4} {version(plan[h])} {len(res):>9} {100 * deny / len(res):>6.1f} "
          f"{100 * forbid / len(res):>13.1f} {errs:>7}")
hour version       requests  deny%  forbid-deny%  errors
   0 36b6f992db86      2000   45.2           5.4       0
   1 36b6f992db86      2000   44.4           6.7       0
   2 36b6f992db86      2000   46.0           6.3       0
   3 36b6f992db86      2000   44.7           5.5       0
   4 36b6f992db86      2000   42.7           6.2       0
   5 36b6f992db86      2000   45.1           6.7       0
   6 43fa2e9855b5      2000   53.6          26.1       0
   7 43fa2e9855b5      2000   54.8          25.2       0
   8 36b6f992db86      2000   44.5           5.2       0
   9 36b6f992db86      2000   45.9           6.8       0
  10 36b6f992db86      2000   45.5           6.4       0
  11 36b6f992db86      2000   44.2           6.4       0

The lesson: the overall deny rate is a weak signal. Many requests are denied anyway, and the rate moves around. The forbid-deny rate, split by version, shows the problem at once.

  • Page someone when the error count is above 0, or the forbid-deny rate jumps after a deploy.
  • Warn when a new kind of shadow difference appears, or latency p99 grows.
  • Never log secrets. Log entity ids and policy ids. Do not log attribute values like emails or salaries.

In Amazon Verified Permissions, decisions can go to CloudTrail as data events, but only if you turn that on. Policy in AgentCore sends its decisions to CloudWatch metrics and logs. See Amazon Verified Permissions and Cedar in products.

Performance numbers

How fast is Cedar? We measured on this machine: an Apple M2 Pro with 10 cores, cedar-policy 4.14.1, release build. Other jobs were running at the same time, with a load average of about 13. The medians were stable over three runs. The p99 values jumped around, so we do not report them.

Each policy set had N policies: half were scope-only grants, and half had a condition. We timed parsing, strict validation, and one decision.

Cedar on an Apple M2 Pro (median of 3,000 decisions; 300 for the largest set)
PoliciesParseValidateOne decision (median)
110.31 ms0.14 ms6.8 µs
1011.14 ms0.56 ms51.1 µs
1,00115.38 ms6.11 ms499.4 µs
10,001127.55 ms56.67 ms5,355.2 µs

Read the last column. Ten times more policies give about ten times more time per decision: roughly half a microsecond per policy here. The authorizer looks at every policy in the set you give it. So the size of the set is the main cost.

  1. Your API has a budget of 50 ms for the whole request.
  2. 100 tenants share one set of 10,001 policies: about 5.4 ms per decision. That is over 10% of the budget.
  3. Split the set by tenant. Each tenant has about 100 policies: about 51 µs per decision.
  4. The cost drops about 100 times, with exactly the same rules.

This idea is called policy slicing. The Cedar research paper studies it. That paper also compares engines on its own benchmarks: the Cedar authorizer was 28.7 to 35.2 times faster than OpenFGA, and 42.8 to 80.8 times faster than Rego. Those numbers come from the authors' machines, not ours.

Two more tips from our runs:

  • Parse once. In Python, pass parsed PolicySet, Schema and Entities handles. Our shadow call took 76 µs with handles. A first test that passed plain strings took far longer, because it parsed everything on each call.
  • Keep entity slices small. Send the entities that the request needs: the principal, the resource and their parents. Not the whole database.

Incident playbook

Something is wrong. Users cannot open documents, or worse, they can open too much. Follow the same steps every time.

  1. Detect. An alert fires: errors above 0, or a jump in the forbid-deny rate.
  2. Freeze. Stop all other policy deploys until the incident is closed.
  3. Find the change. Read the version id in the decision logs. Find that bundle in git: who changed what, and who approved it. In AVP, read the CloudTrail management events, such as UpdatePolicy and PutSchema.
  4. Roll back. Load the last good bundle. In our reload program this is one pointer swap. In AVP, redeploy the previous version from your infrastructure code.
  5. Verify. The forbid-deny rate is back to normal. The pipeline passes on the live bundle.
  6. Learn. Add the test that would have caught it. Write a short review without blame.
  1. Hour 6 Deploy. Bundle 43fa2e9855b5 goes live. It contains the wide forbid.
  2. Hour 6 Alert. Forbid-deny rate is 26.1%. The normal value is about 6%.
  3. Hour 7 Cause found. The forbid now covers view as well as edit.
  4. Hour 8 Rollback. Bundle 36b6f992db86 is live again. The rate returns to 5.2%.
  5. After Test added. archived-still-viewable now guards this mistake.

Sometimes rollback is not enough, for example during an attack. Then an emergency forbid stops one action for everyone. It works because forbid always wins. We validated this one against our schema:

// Emergency: stop all edits while we investigate.
@id("emergency.stop-edits")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"edit", resource);

Fixing forward under pressure: "I will just edit the policy in production." The quick edit skips the pipeline and its tests. Roll back to a known good bundle first. Fix calmly afterwards, through the normal pipeline.

Auditing

An auditor asks: "Could Bo edit the archived plan on 3 March?" To answer, you need four things from that date:

  • The bundle that was live: schema and policies, found by version id in git.
  • The entity data used: Bo's groups and the document's attributes.
  • The request and its context.
  • The logged decision, with its deciding policy ids.

With the same bundle, entities and request, Cedar gives the same answer again. Its evaluation has no hidden state and no clock of its own. So you can replay old decisions and prove what happened.

The Cedar best practices add one rule for audits: use immutable identifiers. Use ids like UUIDs, not names. If a group is deleted and its name is reused, the new group can inherit old permissions by accident.

Questions often ask what you need to reproduce a past decision. The answer is always the four inputs: policies, schema, entities and request with context. A log of decisions alone is not enough.

The production checklist

Before the first deploy

Every rule has a policy with an @id. Allow and deny tests exist for every rule. The schema is strict. The PEP fails closed on errors. Entity ids are immutable.

Rollout

Shadow mode first. Every difference has a name. Canary on a small slice. Then enforce. Then delete the old code.

Every change

One bundle: schema, policies, links, tests. The pipeline runs validate, format --check and run-tests. A second person reviews. Expand and contract for schema changes.

Running

Decision logs with the version id and the deciding policies. Alerts on errors and on the forbid-deny rate. Latency tracked per version. Policy sets kept small by slicing.

Incidents and audits

A written playbook. Rollback in minutes. An emergency forbid ready to use. Old bundles kept in git. Decision logs kept as long as your rules require.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for Cedar in production
PatternPurposeModelNote

Name every differenceDecide when to enforceIntended change, old bug, new bugThe count does not matter. Each case does.
version = sha256(schema + policies)[:12]Know which rules decided36b6f992db86Put it in every log line.
Expand, migrate, contractChange a schema without breaking policiesvisibility?: String next to public: BoolNever remove and add in one step.
Validate, then swapHot reload without riskRwLock<Arc<Bundle>>Keep the old bundle on failure.
validate, format --check, run-testsThe policy pipelineci.shStop on the first failure.

Slice the policy setKeep decisions fast10,001 policies: 5.4 ms; 101: 51 µsCost grows with the number of policies.
