Practice

In shadow mode, which answer does the user get?

  • Cedar's answer.
  • The old code's answer. Cedar's answer is only logged.
  • Allow, if either system allows.

Shadow mode changes nothing for users. It only collects evidence.

Why did Cedar deny Cy viewing the memo, while the old code allowed it?

  • Cedar has a bug with missing attributes.
  • The old code compared None with None. The policy checks has team first.
  • The memo is archived.

This difference is a bug in the old code. Shadow mode found it.

A forbid was widened by mistake. The schema validator passed. What catches it?

  • cedar format --check
  • A test that expects a nearby request to stay allowed
  • Strict validation mode

The bad policy was well typed. Only a behaviour test sees the change.

With 10 times more policies, one decision in our benchmark took about ? times longer.

The safe way to change a schema is called expand and ?.

  1. List the four things you need to reproduce a past decision.
    Answer

    The bundle that was live (schema and policies), the entity data, the request with its context, and the logged decision to compare with.

  2. Your team wants to rename the attribute dept to department. Write the steps.
    Answer

    1. Add department?: String to the schema, keep dept. 2. Make the app send both. 3. Add policies that read department after a has check. 4. Shadow-compare, then remove the policies that read dept. 5. Remove dept from the schema and make department required. Validate and test at every step.

  3. The error count in your logs goes from 0 to 300 per minute after a deploy, but the deny rate does not move. What could be happening, and what do you do?
    Answer

    Some policies now raise errors and are skipped. If a skipped policy is a forbid, users may get access they should not have. That is why the deny rate can stay flat or even fall. Roll back first. Then find the cause, for example a missing entity or attribute. Make sure the PEP treats errors as Deny.

  4. Using the table above, estimate one decision with about 5,000 policies on the same machine.
    Answer

    About half a microsecond per policy: roughly 2.5 ms (2,500 µs). The real number depends on the conditions in the policies and on the machine, so measure it.

  5. Hard: design alerts for an agent gateway that uses the taint policy from Cedar for AI agents and tool calls.
    Answer

    Count denies by deciding policy id. Alert when taint.no-untrusted-egress suddenly fires much more: it may be an injection campaign. Alert when it drops to zero after a deploy: the gateway may have stopped setting taint. Page on any evaluation error, because a skipped guardrail fails open. Track decisions by bundle version.

Key takeaways

  • Shadow mode first. Give every difference a name before you enforce.
  • Schema, policies, links and tests are one bundle with one version id.
  • Change schemas with expand and contract. Swap bundles only after validation.
  • The pipeline runs validate, format --check and run-tests. Allow tests catch forbids that are too wide.
  • Watch the forbid-deny rate and errors by version. Errors can fail open.
  • Decision time grows with the number of policies. Slice policy sets. Roll back first in incidents.

Sources

  • Cedar: A New Language for Expressive, Fast, Safe, and Analyzable Authorization. The research paper with the engine comparison and policy slicing.
  • AgentCore policy enforcement modes. LOG_ONLY and ENFORCE, and the warning about who can switch them.
  • Logging Verified Permissions calls with CloudTrail. Data events are off by default.
  • Cedar best practices: avoid mutable identifiers. Why ids must never change or be reused.
  • cedar-policy changelog. Crate and language versions, and the star for language-breaking changes.

Practitioner exam

This chapter is your final check. It has 60 original questions, five modeling tasks with tested answers, a glossary of 60 terms and a 21-day study plan. Every answer points back to the chapter that teaches it.

  • Say honestly what official Cedar certificates exist today.
  • Answer 60 questions that cover all 19 other chapters, from basic to expert.
  • Build five complete models: schema, policies, entities and tested requests.
  • Explain 60 Cedar terms in simple words.
  • Follow a 21-day plan from your first policy to a full mock exam.

Is there an official Cedar certificate?

We checked on 2026-11-15. We found no official Cedar certificate. We looked at the Cedar documentation, the cedar-policy GitHub organization and the CNCF project page. None of them offers an exam or a credential for Cedar itself.

The closest official credential is from AWS. The exam guide for AWS Certified Security – Specialty (SCS-C03) lists Amazon Verified Permissions in its in-scope services. AWS Verified Access is listed too. That exam has 50 scored questions and 15 unscored ones. Scores go from 100 to 1,000, and you pass with 750.

So this chapter is a practice exam written for this book. It is not a certificate. Passing it shows that you understand Cedar at a practitioner level. The SCS-C03 exam covers much more than Cedar, so study its own guide if you want that credential.

Paying for a "Cedar certification" from an unknown website. On 2026-11-15, no such certificate exists from the Cedar project, the CNCF or AWS. Check the issuer before you pay.

How to use this exam

The 60 questions come in four parts. Each part matches one level of the book. Questions inside each part go from easier to harder.

Our pass mark is 45 out of 60 (75%). We chose this number ourselves. Also try the five modeling tasks. Write your own answer first, then open the model answer.

  1. Read the question twice. Find the key words: permit or forbid, has or no has, schema or no schema.
  2. For a decision question, apply the three rules: any satisfied forbid gives Deny, else any satisfied permit gives Allow, else Deny.
  3. Ask: could anything raise an error here? A policy with an error is skipped.
  4. Choose, then read the explanation and the chapter link.
  5. Write every wrong answer in a notebook. Reread its chapter the next day.

All policies, entities and requests in this chapter ran on the real engine: the cedar CLI 4.14.1, the Rust crate cedar-policy 4.14.1 or cedarpy 4.12.2. The outputs shown are real.

Part A: basic (Q1–Q15)

Q1. A user logs in with the right password. Which question has the system answered?

  • What may this user do?
  • Who is this user?
  • Which policies exist?

That is authentication. Cedar answers the other question: what may you do. Chapter: What is authorization?

Q2. Every Cedar request has a principal, an action, a resource and a ?. (Chapter: What is authorization?)

Q3. The rule is: "Doctors may read the records of their own patients." Which model is this?

  • RBAC
  • ABAC
  • ReBAC

The rule follows a link between a doctor and a patient. Links are relationships. Chapter: What is authorization?

Q4. When did the CNCF accept Cedar as a Sandbox project?

  • 10 May 2023
  • 8 October 2025
  • 3 March 2026

Cedar became open source in May 2023 and joined the CNCF Sandbox on 8 October 2025. Chapter: What is authorization?

Q5. The policy set is empty. A request arrives. What is the decision?

  • Deny
  • Allow
  • An error

Default deny: without a satisfied permit, the answer is Deny. Chapter: Your first Cedar policy

  1. Q6. Name the four files you always have in a Cedar project, and say what each one holds.
    Answer

    Policies: the rules. Schema: the types, attributes and actions. Entities: the facts about users, groups and resources. Request: who wants to do what on which thing, with the context. Chapter: Your first Cedar policy

Q7. The only policy is permit (principal == User::"alice", action == Action::"view", resource);. The entities file is []. Alice asks to view Doc::"d". What happens?

  • Allow
  • Deny, because Alice is not in the entities
  • An error, because Alice does not exist

We ran it: ALLOW. The scope only compares ids. No attribute is read, so no entity data is needed. Chapter: Your first Cedar policy

Q8. cedarpy returns Decision.NoDecision. What should your app do?

  • Allow, because nothing denied it
  • Deny, and log the error
  • Retry until it gets Allow

NoDecision means the request could not be built, for example a context key is missing. Never turn an error into Allow. Chapter: Your first Cedar policy

Q9. A permit and a forbid are both satisfied. What is the decision?

  • Allow
  • Deny
  • It depends on which policy comes first

Forbid overrides permit. Chapter: Policy syntax and how decisions are made

Q10. Which scope parses?

  • permit (principal, action is Action, resource);
  • permit (principal in [User::"a", User::"b"], action, resource);
  • permit (principal, action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"], resource);

Real errors from the CLI: "`is` cannot appear in the action scope" and "expected single entity uid or template slot, found set of entity uids". Lists are allowed only for the action. Chapter: Policy syntax and how decisions are made

Q11. A policy with unless { e } applies only when e is ?. (Chapter: Policy syntax and how decisions are made)

Q12. You move a forbid from the top of the policy file to the bottom. What changes?

  • The forbid now wins less often.
  • Nothing. Order does not matter.
  • The permits above it are checked first and win.

Cedar evaluates every policy and combines the results as a set. Chapter: Policy syntax and how decisions are made

  1. Q13. A forbid says unless { context.mfa }. A permit allows the same action. A request comes with an empty context, and no schema is used. What happens, and why is it dangerous?
    Answer

    Reading the missing key is an error, so the forbid is skipped. We ran it: ALLOW, with the error "record does not have the attribute `mfa`". The guardrail failed open. Write unless { context has mfa && context.mfa }, and use a schema. Chapter: Policy syntax and how decisions are made

Q14. What is the value of User::"a" in User::"a"?

  • true
  • false
  • An error

The in operator is reflexive: an entity is "in" itself. We evaluated it: true. Chapter: Entities and hierarchy

  1. Q15. Ana is in team red. Team red is in org acme. The policy is permit (principal in Org::"acme", action, resource);. The entities file forgets team red. What is the decision?
    Answer

    Deny, with no error. We ran it both ways: with team red, ALLOW. Without it, DENY. The in operator follows only the parents that are in the data. A missing link is silent. Chapter: Entities and hierarchy

Part B: intermediate (Q16–Q30)

Q16. A policy calls resource.getTag("dept") on a document with no "dept" tag. What happens?

  • It returns an empty string.
  • An error, so the policy is skipped. Check hasTag first.
  • It returns false.

getTag on a missing tag is an error. Chapter: Entities and hierarchy

Q17. What is the value of 1 == "1"?

  • true
  • false
  • A type error

Values never change type. A Long is never equal to a String. We evaluated it: false. Chapter: Expressions, types and operators

Q18. What is the value of "Report.PDF" like "*.pdf"?

  • true
  • false
  • An error

like is case sensitive. "Report.PDF" like "*.PDF" is true. Chapter: Expressions, types and operators

Q19. [1,2,3].containsAll([]) is ?, and [1].containsAny([]) is ?. (Chapter: Expressions, types and operators)

Q20. What happens with decimal("1.5") < decimal("2.0")?

  • true
  • An error: "<" works on Long, datetime and duration, not decimal
  • false

The real message: "type error: expected one of [long, datetime, duration], got decimal". Use .lessThan(...). Chapter: Expressions, types and operators

  1. Q21. Why does every Cedar evaluation finish? Name one error that can still happen during evaluation.
    Answer

    Cedar has no loops, no recursion and no user functions. Every expression is a finite tree, so evaluation ends. Errors can still happen, for example integer overflow: 9223372036854775807 + 1 gives "integer overflow while attempting to add the values". Chapter: Expressions, types and operators

Q22. Where does "the IP address of this request" belong?

  • In the context
  • In an attribute of the user entity
  • In the policy text

Facts about this one request go in the context. Facts about things, like a user's department, go in entities. Chapter: Context and requests

Q23. Where should context.mfa come from?

  • A request header that the browser sets
  • The server, from a token it has checked
  • Whatever the client app sends in the body

Cedar trusts the context completely. Your server must build it from trusted sources. Chapter: Context and requests

  1. Q24. In the schema, mfa is optional (mfa?: Bool). Write a forbid for the action pay that also denies when the key is missing.
    Answer 
    @id("pay.all")
permit (principal, action == Action::"pay", resource);

@id("pay.needs-mfa")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"pay", resource)
unless { context has mfa && context.mfa };

    Tested: empty context gives DENY, {"mfa": false} gives DENY, {"mfa": true} gives ALLOW. Without has, the validator refuses the policy: "unable to guarantee safety of access to optional attribute `mfa` in context". Chapter: Context and requests

Q25. cedar validate finds an error. Which exit code does it return?

  • 1
  • 2
  • 3

We saw exit code 3 for every failed validation. A CI script can stop on it. Chapter: Schemas and validation

Q26. The schema says dept?: String. A policy reads principal.dept == "sales" with no has. What does the validator say?

  • Nothing, it passes.
  • "unable to guarantee safety of access to optional attribute `dept`"
  • "attribute `dept` not found"

An optional attribute needs a has check before it is read. Chapter: Schemas and validation

  1. Q27. What does strict validation prove? Which three errors can still happen at run time?
    Answer

    Validated policies do not hit type errors with requests and entities that fit the schema. Three errors remain: a referenced entity is missing, an extension function gets a bad value (like a wrong IP string), or an integer overflows. Chapter: Schemas and validation

Q28. Which slots can a template use?

  • ?principal and ?resource, only in the scope
  • ?principal, ?action and ?resource
  • Any name, anywhere in the policy

There are only two slots, and only in the scope. Chapter: Policy templates and linked policies

  1. Q29. Why build a "Share" button with a template, not by gluing user input into policy text?
    Answer

    Glued text allows policy injection: a crafted id could close the string and add new conditions. A template is fixed text. Linking only fills the two slots with entity ids. Each link has its own id, so unlinking revokes exactly one share. Chapter: Policy templates and linked policies

Q30. You need tenant isolation: nobody may touch another tenant's data. How do you write it?

  • As a condition inside every permit
  • As one forbid with unless { principal.tenant == resource.tenant }
  • As a permit that lists the allowed tenants

A forbid cannot be undone by any permit. A condition inside permits fails as soon as someone adds a new permit without it. Chapter: The 15 Cedar patterns

Part C: hard (Q31–Q45)

Q31. A document has teams: [Team::"red"]. Ana's parent is Team::"red". Which policy allows Ana?

  • when { resource.teams.contains(principal) }
  • when { principal in resource.teams }
  • Both

We ran both: in gives ALLOW, contains gives DENY. contains checks exact members. in follows parents. Chapter: Modeling RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC

  1. Q32. An OpenFGA model says: "a viewer of a folder is also a viewer of every document in it". Write the Cedar version.
    Answer 
    // One link per (user, viewer, object) tuple.
@id("viewer")
permit (principal == ?principal, action == Action::"view", resource in ?resource);

    Make documents children of folders (entity Doc in [Folder]). Link Ana to Folder::"plans". Tested: Ana may view the folder and the document q1 inside it, but not memo, which is outside. A tuple becomes a link. "From parent" becomes resource in. Chapter: Modeling RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC

Q33. The rule is "all engineers may deploy, but not contractors". How do you write "but not"?

  • Leave contractors out of the permit
  • Add a forbid for contractors
  • Add a second permit with a condition

Exclusions are forbids. A missing permit breaks as soon as a contractor joins another group that has a permit. Chapter: Modeling RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC

Q34. What is wrong with permit (principal, action, resource) when { resource.public };?

  • Nothing, it is short and clear.
  • The empty scope covers every principal type and every action, even agents and delete.
  • It does not parse.

Populate the scope: name the principal type and the action. Chapter: Policy design and governance

  1. Q35. Who should own permits, who should own forbids, and why?
    Answer

    Permits are features, so product teams own them. Forbids are guardrails, so security owns them, with two-person approval. One wrong forbid can block everyone, and one removed forbid can open everything. Chapter: Policy design and governance

Q36. A policy's condition is principal has dept && 1 == 2. What does cedar validate do?

  • It fails with an error.
  • It passes with the warning "policy is impossible", unless you add --deny-warnings.
  • It removes the policy.

We ran both: exit code 0 with a warning, and exit code 3 with --deny-warnings. Use the flag in CI. Chapter: Policy design and governance

Q37. In a cedar run-tests case, the reason list names one policy. The real decision had two deciding policies. Does the test pass?

  • No, the lists must be equal.
  • Yes, the expected reasons must be a subset of the real ones.
  • The reason list is ignored.

We ran exactly this case: it passed. Chapter: Testing Cedar policies

Q38. You delete a guardrail policy. All tests still pass. What does that tell you?

  • The guardrail was not needed.
  • No test checks that guardrail. Add tests.
  • The test runner is broken.

This is a mutation test. A test that passes without the rule proves nothing about the rule. Chapter: Testing Cedar policies

  1. Q39. A property test checked 360 generated requests and found no bad Allow. Is the guardrail now proven?
    Answer

    No. It is strong evidence for those 360 cases only. A proof must cover every request that fits the schema. That needs a solver. Chapter: Testing Cedar policies

Q40. Why can a solver analyze Cedar policies exactly?

  • No loops, no I/O and no user functions, so validated policies become finite logic formulas.
  • Cedar policies are short.
  • The solver runs every possible request.

The language was designed for this. Chapter: Analysis and formal verification

Q41. You want to prove: "no agent can ever call an egress tool with untrusted taint". What do you ask the solver?

  • "Is there any allowed request with taint untrusted and an egress tool?" Unsatisfiable proves the claim.
  • "Is there any denied request?"
  • "Run 1,000 random requests."

Ask the opposite of your claim. If no example exists, the claim holds. A found example is a counterexample you can replay. Chapter: Analysis and formal verification

  1. Q42. What does differential random testing (DRT) in cedar-spec compare? Name the Rust crate for symbolic analysis.
    Answer

    DRT runs random generated policies, entities and requests through the Lean formalization and the Rust production engine, and compares the answers. The symbolic compiler crate is cedar-policy-symcc (version 0.8.1 on crates.io on 2026-11-15). Chapter: Analysis and formal verification

Q43. In the Rust crate, where do you find the errors of policies that failed during evaluation?

  • In the Err of a Result
  • In response.diagnostics().errors()
  • They are printed to stderr.

Policy errors are part of a normal response. Read them every time. Chapter: Cedar in Rust

  1. Q44. Give two rules for fast and safe Rust code with cedar-policy.
    Answer

    Parse the PolicySet, Schema and Entities once, and build only a small Request per call. Build ids from parts with EntityId::new, never with format! on user input, which could inject Cedar text. Chapter: Cedar in Rust

Q45. Which Python check is safe?

  • if r.decision == Decision.Allow:
  • if r.decision != Decision.Deny:
  • if not r.diagnostics.errors:

The second check lets NoDecision through. We saw cedarpy return NoDecision for a request with a missing context key. Chapter: Cedar in Python and other languages

Part D: certified (Q46–Q60)

Q46. Which statement is true?

  • cedarpy is the official engine from the Cedar project.
  • The official engines cover Rust, Java, JavaScript/Wasm and Go. cedarpy is a community binding.
  • Cedar runs only in Rust.

Bindings can lag behind the Rust engine, so pin exact versions. Chapter: Cedar in Python and other languages

Q47. Which box asks for a decision and then obeys it?

  • The PEP
  • The PDP
  • The PAP

PEP asks and enforces. PDP decides. PIP supplies facts. PAP manages policies. Chapter: Architecture: where authorization lives

  1. Q48. A decision cache uses (principal, action, resource) as its key. Users without MFA got cached Allow answers. Why, and how do you fix it?
    Answer

    The context was not in the key. A decision made with mfa: true was reused for a request with mfa: false, so the MFA forbid never ran. The key needs every input: the request, the context, and the versions of the entity data and of the policies. Chapter: Architecture: where authorization lives

Q49. The call to a central PDP times out. What should the PEP do?

  • Allow, so users are not blocked
  • Deny
  • Use the answer from yesterday

Fail closed. Every failure path denies. Chapter: Architecture: where authorization lives

Q50. A Cognito user has the sub abc in the user pool us-east-1_POOL. What is the principal id in Verified Permissions?

  • abc
  • us-east-1_POOL|abc
  • abc@us-east-1_POOL

The id is the pool id, a vertical bar and the sub. Groups get the same prefix. Chapter: Amazon Verified Permissions and Cedar in products

  1. Q51. Your app makes 10 million single IsAuthorized calls a month. What do they cost with the prices of 2026-11-15? How many decisions must a batch call hold to cost the same, in the first price tier?
    Answer

    10{,}000{,}000 \times 0.000005 = 50 USD. A first-tier batch call costs 0.00015 USD, the same as 0.00015 / 0.000005 = 30 single calls. Prices can change, so check the AWS page. Chapter: Amazon Verified Permissions and Cedar in products

Q52. Which template does Verified Permissions reject, although Cedar accepts it?

  • permit (principal == ?principal, action == A::"view", resource == ?resource);
  • permit (principal, action == A::"view", resource == ?resource);
  • permit (principal == ?principal, action == A::"edit", resource in ?resource);

AVP requires both the principal and the resource to be constrained in a template. Chapter: Amazon Verified Permissions and Cedar in products

Q53. An agent calls send_email (an egress tool). Taint is untrusted. A human approved the call. What is the decision under the agent model of this book?

  • Allow, because a human approved it
  • Deny, by taint.no-untrusted-egress
  • Deny, by approval.high-risk

The taint rule has no unless block, so approval cannot lift it. This is test T05. Chapter: Cedar for AI agents and tool calls

Q54. The lethal trifecta is private data, untrusted content and an ?. (Chapter: Cedar for AI agents and tool calls)

  1. Q55. An agent gateway forgot to send the Session entity. The engine answered Allow with two errors. Explain, and give two fixes.
    Answer

    Two forbids read context.session attributes. The entity did not exist, so both raised errors and were skipped. Only the permit was left. Fix 1: the PEP treats any error as Deny. Fix 2: put guardrail inputs in the context, where request validation guarantees them. Chapter: Cedar for AI agents and tool calls

Q56. Who sets the taint field in the request context?

  • The model, in its tool arguments
  • The gateway, from its own record of where text came from
  • The tool server, from its MCP annotations

Injected text can write anything into tool arguments. MCP annotations are only hints, and clients must not trust them from untrusted servers. Chapter: Cedar for AI agents and tool calls

Q57. During shadow mode, which answer does the user get?

  • Cedar's answer
  • The old code's answer. Cedar's answer is only logged.
  • Allow if either system allows

Shadow mode collects evidence without changing anything for users. Chapter: Running Cedar in production

Q58. A forbid was widened by mistake. Which signal shows it fastest?

  • The overall deny rate
  • The forbid-deny rate, split by bundle version
  • The average latency

In our synthetic run it jumped from about 6% to 26%. The overall deny rate moved from 45% to 54% and looked like noise. Chapter: Running Cedar in production

  1. Q59. You must rename the attribute public to visibility. List the steps.
    Answer

    Expand and contract. 1. Add visibility?: String, keep public. 2. Make the app send both. 3. Add a policy that reads visibility after a has check. 4. Shadow-compare, then delete the old policy. 5. Remove public and make visibility required. Validate and test at every step. Chapter: Running Cedar in production

  2. Q60. An auditor asks whether a user could edit a file on a past date. What four things do you need to reproduce that decision?
    Answer

    The bundle that was live (schema and policies), the entity data, the request with its context, and the logged decision to compare. With the same inputs, Cedar gives the same answer. Chapter: Running Cedar in production

Five modeling tasks

Each task gives requirements. Write the schema, the policies and the test requests yourself. Then open the model answer. Every model answer was validated with the cedar CLI 4.14.1. Its requests passed cedar run-tests, and cedarpy 4.12.2 gave exactly the same decisions and deciding policies.

Task 1: a clinic

  • Staff in a patient's care team may read that patient's records.
  • Patients may read their own records.
  • Sealed records: only a doctor, and only with a written break-glass reason.
Model answer for Task 1 
entity Role;
entity Staff in [Role];
entity Patient {
  careTeam: Set<Staff>,
};
entity Record {
  patient: Patient,
  sealed: Bool,
};
action read appliesTo {
  principal: [Staff, Patient],
  resource: [Record],
  context: { breakGlass?: String },
};
// Staff in a patient's care team may read the patient's records.
@id("care-team.read")
permit (principal is Staff, action == Action::"read", resource is Record)
when { resource.patient.careTeam.contains(principal) };

// Patients may read their own records.
@id("patient.read-own")
permit (principal is Patient, action == Action::"read", resource is Record)
when { resource.patient == principal };

// Sealed records: only a doctor, only with a written reason.
@id("sealed.break-glass")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"read", resource is Record)
when { resource.sealed }
unless {
  principal in Role::"doctor" &&
  context has breakGlass && context.breakGlass != ""
};
[
 {"uid": {"type": "Role", "id": "doctor"}, "attrs": {}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Staff", "id": "dr-kim"}, "attrs": {}, "parents": [{"type": "Role", "id": "doctor"}]},
 {"uid": {"type": "Staff", "id": "nurse-lee"}, "attrs": {}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Staff", "id": "nurse-ray"}, "attrs": {}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Patient", "id": "p1"}, "attrs": {"careTeam": [{"__entity": {"type": "Staff", "id": "dr-kim"}},
   {"__entity": {"type": "Staff", "id": "nurse-lee"}}]}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Record", "id": "r-visit"}, "attrs": {"patient": {"__entity": {"type": "Patient", "id": "p1"}}, "sealed": false}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Record", "id": "r-sealed"}, "attrs": {"patient": {"__entity": {"type": "Patient", "id": "p1"}}, "sealed": true}, "parents": []}
]
Task 1 results (6 passed)
RequestContextDecisionDeciding policies
nurse-lee read r-visit{}Allowcare-team.read
nurse-ray read r-visit{}Deny(none)
dr-kim read r-sealed{}Denysealed.break-glass
dr-kim read r-sealedbreakGlass: "ER: patient unconscious"Allowcare-team.read
nurse-lee read r-sealedbreakGlass: "curious"Denysealed.break-glass
Patient p1 read r-visit{}Allowpatient.read-own

Note: the principal can be Staff or Patient. So each permit starts with principal is .... The exception lives inside the forbid. It lifts the block but grants nothing: dr-kim still needs the care-team permit.

Task 2: a multi-tenant project tool

  • Three roles: viewer (view), editor (view, edit), admin (everything).
  • Every user and every project belongs to one tenant.
  • Nobody may ever touch another tenant's project, not even an admin.
Model answer for Task 2 
entity Tenant;
entity Role;
entity User in [Role] {
  tenant: Tenant,
};
entity Project {
  tenant: Tenant,
};
action view, edit, delete appliesTo {
  principal: [User],
  resource: [Project],
};
@id("role.viewer")
permit (principal in Role::"viewer", action == Action::"view", resource);

@id("role.editor")
permit (principal in Role::"editor", action in [Action::"view", Action::"edit"], resource);

@id("role.admin")
permit (principal in Role::"admin", action, resource);

// Guardrail: nobody ever touches another tenant's projects.
@id("tenant.isolation")
forbid (principal, action, resource)
unless { principal.tenant == resource.tenant };
[
 {"uid": {"type": "Tenant", "id": "acme"}, "attrs": {}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Tenant", "id": "globex"}, "attrs": {}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Role", "id": "viewer"}, "attrs": {}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Role", "id": "editor"}, "attrs": {}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Role", "id": "admin"}, "attrs": {}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "User", "id": "eva"}, "attrs": {"tenant": {"__entity": {"type": "Tenant", "id": "acme"}}}, "parents": [{"type": "Role", "id": "editor"}]},
 {"uid": {"type": "User", "id": "vic"}, "attrs": {"tenant": {"__entity": {"type": "Tenant", "id": "acme"}}}, "parents": [{"type": "Role", "id": "viewer"}]},
 {"uid": {"type": "User", "id": "ali"}, "attrs": {"tenant": {"__entity": {"type": "Tenant", "id": "acme"}}}, "parents": [{"type": "Role", "id": "admin"}]},
 {"uid": {"type": "Project", "id": "acme-web"}, "attrs": {"tenant": {"__entity": {"type": "Tenant", "id": "acme"}}}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Project", "id": "globex-api"}, "attrs": {"tenant": {"__entity": {"type": "Tenant", "id": "globex"}}}, "parents": []}
]
Task 2 results (5 passed)
RequestDecisionDeciding policies
eva (editor, acme) edit acme-webAllowrole.editor

Note: the global admin role plus one tenant forbid gives "admin of my own company". This works because each user has exactly one tenant.

Task 3: payments

  • Owners may transfer money from their own account, up to their daily limit.
  • Transfers above 1,000 need MFA in the current session.
  • No transfer ever comes from the blocked network 203.0.113.0/24.
Model answer for Task 3 
entity User {
  dailyLimit: Long,       // in whole dollars
};
entity Account {
  owner: User,
};
action transfer appliesTo {
  principal: [User],
  resource: [Account],
  context: { amount: Long, mfa: Bool, ip: ipaddr },
};
// Owners may send money from their own account, up to their daily limit.
@id("owner.transfer")
permit (principal, action == Action::"transfer", resource)
when { resource.owner == principal && context.amount <= principal.dailyLimit };

// More than 1000 needs MFA in this session.
@id("mfa.large-transfer")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"transfer", resource)
when { context.amount > 1000 }
unless { context.mfa };

// Never from a blocked network.
@id("net.blocked-range")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"transfer", resource)
when { context.ip.isInRange(ip("203.0.113.0/24")) };
[
 {"uid": {"type": "User", "id": "mia"}, "attrs": {"dailyLimit": 10000}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Account", "id": "acc-1"}, "attrs": {"owner": {"__entity": {"type": "User", "id": "mia"}}}, "parents": []}
]
{"amount": 500, "mfa": false, "ip": {"__extn": {"fn": "ip", "arg": "198.51.100.7"}}}
Task 3 results (5 passed): mia transfers from acc-1
AmountMFAIPDecisionDeciding policies
500false198.51.100.7Allowowner.transfer
5,000false198.51.100.7Denymfa.large-transfer
5,000true198.51.100.7Allowowner.transfer
20,000true198.51.100.7Deny(none: above the limit)
500true203.0.113.9Denynet.blocked-range

Note: the daily limit sits in the permit, because it describes what is granted. MFA and the network are hard rules, so they are forbids. Both IP ranges here are documentation ranges, not real networks.

Task 4: a coding agent

  • The agent may read files, open pull requests and push, but only in its own repositories.
  • It never pushes to a protected branch, even with approval.
  • After it reads untrusted text, pushing and opening pull requests need a human approval.
Model answer for Task 4 
entity Repo {
  protectedBranches: Set<String>,
};
entity Agent {
  repos: Set<Repo>,        // the repositories this agent works on
};
action read_file, open_pr, push appliesTo {
  principal: [Agent],
  resource: [Repo],
  context: { branch: String, taint: String, approval: Bool },
};
// An agent may read, open pull requests and push in its own repos.
@id("agent.repo-work")
permit (
  principal is Agent,
  action in [Action::"read_file", Action::"open_pr", Action::"push"],
  resource
)
when { principal.repos.contains(resource) };

// Agents never push to a protected branch, approved or not.
@id("guard.protected-branch")
forbid (principal is Agent, action == Action::"push", resource)
when { resource.protectedBranches.contains(context.branch) };

// After reading untrusted text, writing needs a human approval.
@id("guard.tainted-write")
forbid (principal is Agent, action in [Action::"push", Action::"open_pr"], resource)
when { context.taint == "untrusted" }
unless { context.approval };
[
 {"uid": {"type": "Repo", "id": "web"}, "attrs": {"protectedBranches": ["main", "release"]}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Repo", "id": "payments"}, "attrs": {"protectedBranches": ["main"]}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Agent", "id": "coder"}, "attrs": {"repos": [{"__entity": {"type": "Repo", "id": "web"}}]}, "parents": []}
]
Task 4 results (6 passed): agent coder
Action, repoBranch, taint, approvalDecisionDeciding policies
read_file webmain, trusted, falseAllowagent.repo-work
push webfeature/login, trusted, falseAllowagent.repo-work
push webmain, trusted, trueDenyguard.protected-branch
open_pr webfeature/login, untrusted, falseDenyguard.tainted-write
open_pr webfeature/login, untrusted, trueAllowagent.repo-work
read_file paymentsmain, trusted, falseDeny(none: not its repo)

Note: reading stays allowed when taint is untrusted, because reading does not send anything out. A stronger design also uses the fail-closed form unless { context.taint == "trusted" } from Cedar for AI agents and tool calls.

Task 5: tag-based document access

  • Users and documents carry tags. A user may read a document with the same "dept" tag.
  • Owners may always read their own documents.
  • A document tagged embargo = "true" is for its owner only.
Model answer for Task 5 
entity User tags String;
entity Document {
  owner: User,
} tags String;
action read appliesTo {
  principal: [User],
  resource: [Document],
};
// Same "dept" tag on the user and the document: read is allowed.
@id("tags.same-dept")
permit (principal, action == Action::"read", resource)
when {
  principal.hasTag("dept") && resource.hasTag("dept") &&
  principal.getTag("dept") == resource.getTag("dept")
};

// Owners may always read their own documents.
@id("owner.read")
permit (principal, action == Action::"read", resource)
when { resource.owner == principal };

// Embargoed documents: only the owner, until the embargo tag is removed.
@id("tags.embargo")
forbid (principal, action == Action::"read", resource)
when { resource.hasTag("embargo") && resource.getTag("embargo") == "true" }
unless { resource.owner == principal };
[
 {"uid": {"type": "User", "id": "fin-ann"}, "attrs": {}, "tags": {"dept": "finance"}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "User", "id": "sales-sam"}, "attrs": {}, "tags": {"dept": "sales"}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "User", "id": "fin-oz"}, "attrs": {}, "tags": {"dept": "finance"}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Document", "id": "budget"}, "attrs": {"owner": {"__entity": {"type": "User", "id": "fin-oz"}}}, "tags": {"dept": "finance"}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Document", "id": "results"}, "attrs": {"owner": {"__entity": {"type": "User", "id": "fin-oz"}}}, "tags": {"dept": "finance", "embargo": "true"}, "parents": []},
 {"uid": {"type": "Document", "id": "notes"}, "attrs": {"owner": {"__entity": {"type": "User", "id": "sales-sam"}}}, "tags": {}, "parents": []}
]
Task 5 results (5 passed)
RequestDecisionDeciding policies
fin-ann read budgetAllowtags.same-dept
sales-sam read budgetDeny(none)
fin-ann read results (embargoed)Denytags.embargo
fin-oz read results (his own)Allowowner.read, tags.same-dept
fin-ann read notes (no dept tag)Deny(none)

Note: our first schema put tags String before the attribute block. It failed with "error parsing schema: unexpected token `{`". The tags clause comes after the attributes.

Glossary: 60 terms

ABAC
Access decided by facts (attributes) about the user, the thing or the request.
ACL
A list on a thing that names who may use it.
Action
What the principal wants to do, like view or edit. Actions are entities.
Action group
An action that holds other actions, so one policy can cover many.
Annotation
A name and value before a policy, like @id. It never changes the decision.
Attribute
A named fact on an entity, like a document's owner.
Authentication
Checking who someone is, for example with a password or a token.
Authorization
Deciding what someone may do. This is Cedar's job.
Break-glass
An emergency exception with a written reason, written inside a forbid.
Bundle
Schema, policies, links and tests shipped together with one version id.
Condition
A when or unless block. All conditions must pass for the policy to apply.
Context
A record of facts about one request, like the IP address or MFA.
datetime and duration
Extension types for points in time and lengths of time.
decimal
An extension type for numbers with up to four digits after the point.
Default deny
Without a satisfied permit, the answer is Deny.
Determining policies
The policies that decided: satisfied permits for Allow, satisfied forbids for Deny.
Diagnostics
The extra part of a response: the reasons and the errors.
DRT
Differential random testing: random inputs, two engines, compare the answers.
Effect
Permit or forbid: what happens when a policy matches.
Egress
Data leaving your system, for example by email or an HTTP call.
Entity
A thing Cedar knows about: a user, group, file, action or session.
Entity tags
Labels on an entity whose names are not fixed in the schema. Read with hasTag and getTag.
Entity UID
The full name of an entity: a type plus an id, like User::"alice".
Enumerated entity type
An entity type whose ids are a fixed list, so typos are rejected.
Evaluation error
A failure while a policy runs, like a missing attribute. The policy is skipped.
Expand and contract
Change a schema by first adding the new thing, later removing the old.
Extension type
A built-in type beyond the basics: decimal, ipaddr, datetime, duration.
Fail closed
Every error or timeout gives Deny, never Allow.
Forbid
A policy that denies when it matches. No permit can undo it.
Guardrail
A forbid that sets a hard limit for everyone.
has
The operator that tests whether an attribute exists before you read it.
Hierarchy
Entities connected by parents, like user, team and organisation.
Identity source
In Verified Permissions, a link to Cognito or an OIDC provider that issues tokens.
in
The operator meaning "is, or is below, in the hierarchy".
ipaddr
An extension type for IP addresses and ranges.
is
The operator that tests an entity's type.
Lethal trifecta
Private data plus untrusted content plus an outbound channel in one agent.
like
String matching with one wildcard, *. It is case sensitive.
Namespace
A prefix that groups type names, like DocShare::User.
PAP
Policy administration point: where people write and manage policies.
Partial evaluation
Evaluating with some values unknown, to get a residual policy.
PDP
Policy decision point: the engine that answers Allow or Deny.
PEP
Policy enforcement point: the code that asks and then obeys.
Permit
A policy that allows when it matches, unless a forbid also matches.
PIP
Policy information point: where the facts about entities come from.
Policy
One rule: an effect, a scope and optional conditions.
Policy set
All policies that are evaluated together for a request.
Policy store
In Verified Permissions, the container for one schema, policies and templates.
Principal
Who is asking: a user, a service or an AI agent.
RBAC
Access decided by roles, like "editors may edit".
ReBAC
Access decided by links, like "the owner of this document".
Resource
The thing the principal wants to act on.
Schema
The list of entity types, attributes, actions and context shapes.
Scope
The principal, action and resource part of a policy.
Shadow mode
Cedar decides and logs, but the old code's answer is used.
Slot
An empty place in a template: ?principal or ?resource.
SMT solver
A program that searches for an input that makes a logic formula true.
Taint
A mark that says data came from a source you do not control.
Template
A policy with slots. Linking fills the slots and makes a new policy.
Validator
The tool that checks policies against a schema before you deploy them.

Learn ten terms a day for six days. Say each one aloud in your own words.

A 21-day study plan

About one hour a day. Each week ends with questions from this exam.

  1. Week 1 The language. Days 1 to 7: requests, syntax, entities, expressions and context. Then Part A.
  2. Week 2 Modeling. Days 8 to 14: schemas, templates, patterns, modeling, governance and testing. Then Part B and Tasks 1 and 2.
  3. Week 3 Expert and production. Days 15 to 21: analysis, code, architecture, Verified Permissions, agents and production. Then the full mock exam.
Day by day
DayReadDo
1What is authorization?Write five rules from your own job as principal, action, resource.
2Your first Cedar policyBuild the bench in Rust or Python. Change one thing at a time.
3Policy syntax and how decisions are madeWrite one permit and one forbid that both match. Predict, then run.
4Entities and hierarchyBuild a user, team and org chain. Remove one link and run again.
5Expressions, types and operatorsEvaluate ten expressions with cedar evaluate. Predict each first.
6Context and requestsAdd an MFA forbid with a safe has check. Test a missing key.
7Review week 1Answer Q1 to Q15. Reread the chapter of every wrong answer.
8Schemas and validationWrite a schema for your day 1 rules. Make the validator fail on purpose.
9Policy templates and linked policiesBuild a share button: template, link, unlink.
10The 15 Cedar patternsWrite all 15 patterns from memory. Compare with the table.
11Modeling RBAC, ABAC and ReBACModel one app three ways. Write the expected matrix first.
12Policy design and governanceRename your policies with area prefixes. Write a review checklist.
13Testing Cedar policiesWrite run-tests cases. Delete one policy and watch tests fail.
14Review week 2Answer Q16 to Q30. Do Tasks 1 and 2 without looking.
15Analysis and formal verificationWrite three "can anyone ever ...?" questions for your model.
16Cedar in RustLoad files once, decide many times, and read the diagnostics.

18Architecture: where authorization livesDraw PEP, PDP, PIP and PAP for your own system.
19Amazon Verified Permissions and Cedar in products and Running Cedar in productionPrice your traffic. Plan a shadow-mode rollout.

21Mock examAll 60 questions in 90 minutes, then Tasks 3, 4 and 5.

Patterns to remember

Patterns for answering exam questions
PatternPurposeModelNote
Forbid, then permit, then default denyDecide any requestQ9, Q53Errors remove a policy before this step.
Look for a read without hasFind hidden errorsQ13, Q24An erroring forbid fails open.
Scope compares ids onlyDecide without entity dataQ7Conditions that read attributes need entities.
in follows parents, contains does notGroup membership questionsQ15, Q31A missing parent gives a silent Deny.
Exact types, exact stringsExpression questionsQ17, Q181 == "1" is false. like is case sensitive.
"But not" is a forbidModeling exclusionsQ30, Q33A permit with a condition is not a guardrail.
Exception inside the forbidBreak-glass and approvalsTask 1The exception lifts a block but grants nothing.
Ask the oppositeSolver questionsQ41Unsatisfiable proves the claim.
Every failure deniesArchitecture questionsQ45, Q49NoDecision, timeouts and errors are all Deny.
Calls times priceVerified Permissions cost questionsQ51Batch calls are priced per call, not per decision.
Name every differenceRollout questionsQ57Intended change, old bug or new bug.

Practice

The 60 questions above are the main practice. Use these flashcards on the last three days.

The three decision rules?

Satisfied forbid gives Deny. Else satisfied permit gives Allow. Else Deny.

What happens to a policy that errors?

It is skipped. The error appears in the diagnostics.

Which scope operators exist?

==, in, is and is ... in. Lists only for action. No is for action.

Which template slots exist?

?principal and ?resource, only in the scope.

Four extension types?

decimal, ipaddr, datetime and duration.

Exit code of a failed cedar validate?

3.

Cedar crate and language versions on 2026-11-15?

cedar-policy 4.14.1, Cedar language 4.6.

Cognito principal id in Verified Permissions?

user-pool-id|sub.

The lethal trifecta?

Private data, untrusted content, an outbound channel.

Official Cedar certificate on 2026-11-15?

None found. AWS SCS-C03 lists Verified Permissions as in scope.

  1. Final self-check: explain to a friend, in five sentences, why a forbid guardrail is safer than a condition in every permit.
    Answer

    A satisfied forbid always gives Deny. New permits cannot undo it. A condition in each permit protects only those permits. The next permit someone adds may forget it. One forbid covers every feature, now and later. Chapters: Policy syntax and how decisions are made and Cedar for AI agents and tool calls.

Key takeaways

  • On 2026-11-15 there is no official Cedar certificate. AWS SCS-C03 lists Verified Permissions in scope.
  • Most decision questions need only three rules plus one question: could this policy error?
  • Guardrails are forbids. Exceptions live inside them. Missing data can switch them off.
  • Every modeling task needs a schema, policies, entities and tested requests with expected reasons.
  • Study one chapter a day. Write every wrong answer down and reread its chapter.
